&raquo;,&ldquo;We do not serve the Dead, the past is past :,God lives, and lifts his glorious mornings up Before the eyes of men, who wake at last,,And put away the meats they used to sup,,And on the dry dust of the ground upcast The dregs remaining of the ancient cup,,And turn to wakeful prayer and worthy act !,.Soon shall we,Be the dead too, and that our periods Of life may round themselves to memory As smoothly as on our graves the funeral sods,,We must look to it to excel as they,,To hear our age as far, unlimited By the last sea-mark, so to he revered By future generations as the Dead.&rdquo;,Elizabeth Browning.
PREFACE.,This volume is a record of a twelvemonth&rsquo;s work in and among Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s Colleges. As we reckon up our Christmas account we can justly say that the year has been a prosperous one. Any one who turns over these pages will see that we have had difficulties and anxieties to con&not; tend with ; but even in the way there has been met we can trace evidences of increased stability and promise of further progress. New Colleges have been started, the old have been advancing. The accounts of the new in&not; stitutions at Birkenhead and Ayr will be read with all the greater pleasure by the friends and founders of the older colleges, inasmuch as they show how actively the movement is being carried on in fresh districts by men having no personal connection with themselves. The success of any at&not; tempt must depend upon the amount of personal effort engaged in it. A mere scheme, however carefully planned, will break down unless some set of men can be found who will determine to give their hearts to its accom&not; plishment. But however essential is this personal element, no permanence can be looked for unless it is obtained from a wide area. A knot of per&not; sonal friends must in time be broken up. Death, to say nothing of the lesser forms of separation in this changeful world, dooms it to dissolution. It is the more pleasant therefore to find that the principles which instigated the foundation of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; are asserted no less firmly by the originators of new institutions. Never, for instance, has the differentia of a college been insisted on more eloquently than in the addresses at the first annual meeting of the Ayr College.,Little or great as may be the good done by each College within its own particular circle of action, it is still unfortunately true that the main rea&not; son for a College existing at all, is that it may be a living protest against the popular doctrines current about education. The declaration that it is intrinsically a better thing to be rational rather than irrational, wise rather than unwise, instructed rather than ignorant, might seem worthy of being classed among the truths which, as the &ldquo; Times &rdquo; says, &ldquo; have by perpetual repetition arrived at the dignity of platitudes.&rdquo; And yet no year, no month passes over us without our being reminded in a hundred ways that the thing is after all not only not believed, but that the very opposite is held and preached by the chief oracles of opinion. Lest we should get weary of saying that the end of all human existence is not to make so many shil&not; lings a week, we have just had one of the greatest of living orators in effect declaring that it is. Mr. Bright, at a late meeting of a popular insti&not; tute, could find no better plea for recommending lads to learn arithmetic and not to learn grammar, than that the former would perhaps add five
shillings a week to their wages, and the latter would not. And then the next day we have this doctrine indorsed by the leading organ of Commer&not; cial Secularism, recommended to the million readers of the &ldquo; Times &rdquo; as the true rational common sense of the question. A few weeks before, the re&not; presentative of the &ldquo; scholar and gentleman &rdquo; class of English readers had mentioned, quite parenthetically, as if no doubt could exist on the question, that, &ldquo;as a rule a superior education should be confined to those who can afford to pay for it.&rdquo;* Finally, we have the Government of the country acquiescing in, if not adopting, the money rule as the guiding principle of national action in the recent Educational Minute, which in effect says that as trade interests and the personal selfishness of parents and employers make children leave school at eleven years old, therefore we will cease to hold out the offer of any education better than a knowledge of the alphabet and multiplication table ; we will accept as a necessary law of social life what we know in our hearts is really a hideous disease. If these tre&not; mendous authorities are right, a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; is a hopeless absur&not; dity. Either Mammon (by some called the Devil) is the rightful ruler of this universe, or he is not ; if we still continue to think not, we must make up our minds to be Colleges militant. &ldquo; Popular judgments, as Lord Bacon says, &ldquo; continue as they have been : but so will that also con- &ldquo; tinue whereupon learning hath ever relied and which faileth not : Justi- &ldquo; ficata est sapientia a filiis suis.&rdquo;,A bit of protest like this is in truth a sorry sort of Christmas greeting ; but it is hard to forget, even in a time ol feasting, that the piincipal busi&not; ness of life is after all (as &ldquo; Tom Brown&rdquo; says somewhere) fighting. Least of all can we forget it when the advent of a new year is shadowed by a dark cloud of national anxiety. At such a time, it is scarcely possible to think of any work that we may be about without also thinking of its bearings on our national life. A terrible example is teaching us how a certain kind of mock democracy can plunge a people into a war more awful than the history of &ldquo;rotten despotisms&rdquo; has recorded. A few days or hours will let us know whether we ourselves are to be presently involved in a conflict only one degree less fratricidal. To attain a condition in which a real democracy should be not only a possible, but the best possible form of government, should be the highest ambition of a nation. This goal can never be reached until a considerable proportion of the people become rational, intelligent, and sober-minded. If ever the prospect is to be realized, I know not what can contribute more to it than the work which our Colleges have set themselves to do. Whatever betide, there&not; fore it will be something to know that in sinking our class jealousies m a common effort, of which this is the end, and in taking part in the move&not; ment which has made our shores inaccessible to an invader, we have to the best of our ability been widening the foundations of our national,strength. ^ ^ l ITCHFIELI)-,Christmas, 1861.,* &lsquo; Saturday Review.&rsquo;

JANUARY 1, 1861.,WORK AND PLAY ; OR, HEADS AND HEELS.,Christmas and the New Year bring with them festivity to &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; as to the rest of the world. Work, and play, make up the most of all our waking lives. About work, and how to do it wisely, we are incessantly talking in these pages ; what better time than Christmas for a little speculation on the art of doing the other wisely too ? What is amusement ? Neglecting the case of idle people, I should say it is any&not; thing by which the mind is pleasantly diverted from fatiguing work. When we are tired of doing one thing, we take to doing something else, and that we call our &ldquo; amusement.&rdquo; But here we come upon a note&not; worthy distinction. The something else to which we &ldquo; divert &rdquo; our minds may have positive interest and advantage of its own, or it may not, but may be simply pleasant because it is an occupation, and is a change from what we have been about. Such occupations as this I should call amuse&not; ments pure and simple. Under this head come all purposeless games, such as dominoes, draughts, backgammon, chess, solitaire, cards, and the like. These may all have their virtues, but no one will pretend to say that any&not; thing can ever come of them : and, in short, the thesis I have to propound is that amusement pure and simple is never needed by a rational being under any circumstances whatsoever. This may sound a barbarous doc&not; trine, but it is practically true. For the question is not, are ive to have all work and no play ? but, what sort of work can we find pleasant enough to be made play of?&mdash;and to that there are happily a thousand answers. A man says, for instance, he takes delight in working a chess problem : well, I should say to him, if you enjoy that severe exercise, why not try to get some equally hard work of a kind which leads you to something of higher interest ? Suppose you play a game blindfold,&mdash;a fine piece of brain-gymnastics certainly,&mdash;but would not a tough differential equation be just as amusing ? It would give you quite as much trouble, and might reveal besides some strange fact about the world, the perturbations of a planet, peihaps, or something about the rotation of the earth. Or if you want gentler exercise, try a problem about the path of a projectile, or, better still, investigate the physiology of an ammonite. As a matter of fact, people do find these things amusing as well as interesting, and they can be made to combine, some cannot be carried on without, all the social advantages which give a zest to your enjoyment of your games. Of all relaxations the most effective, I believe, is society. Taking the word in its simplest meaning, apart from all notion of improving the mind by inter&not; change of ideas, the mere companionship of human beings, sitting round a fiieside, or fraternizing over a jug o&iacute; beer and a pipe, is a thing which pi o uces actual good, and is at the same time the most complete restoration a ter i&aacute;id work, I once knew a great Cambridge scholar who was heard to say t at he did not care to talk to a man unless he could get some vol. in. 5
information from him.&rdquo; Plausible but hateful sentiment. As if one may never come alongside a human being but one must set to pumping useful knowledge out of him. No, the interest which every man must take in every other man,&mdash;in humanity &ldquo; absolute,&rdquo; as our German friends would say,&mdash;ought to be enough to make us care for each other&rsquo;s company, if we do no more than look each other in the face, A student said to me a week ago, &ldquo; Those Wednesday evenings were very pleasant : we talked when we had anything to talk about, and when we hadn&rsquo;t we could look at each other.&rdquo; But superadd to this mere companionship a common employment, in some interesting or beautiful pursuit, and you get the best conceivable class of amusements ; and the relative advantage of each sort is, I think, to be weighed by the amount of collateral interest or incidental advantage connected with it. This brings me to a topic which is in fact the occa&not; sion of this paper.,Some of the students of the London College have, during the past twelve months, instituted the custom of having periodical dances, to which mem&not; bers and non-members invite their female friends. These have not been held in direct connection with the College, or recognized in any way by its authorities, but they have been promoted chiefly, and managed entirely, by one of the Council of Teachers, and have practically been College parties. It w*as at first proposed that there should be four in the year. The first, in October, 1859, was a success; everybody was very happy; and a certain number liked it so well, that they could not wait till Christmas for the next, so an intermediate dance was organized. Others were added in like manner, and by June, nine had been held. This year the same course has been followed ; much objection had been made to the frequency of last year&rsquo;s entertainments, and it was announced that there would be four in the season, and, as was generally understood, four only ; but again another was added a few weeks after the first. A grand &ldquo; Eifle Ball,&rdquo; fixed for December 37th, will have been the third within two months. As this sort of entertainment thus threatens to become a systematic appendage to the life of the College, it is worth while to consider whether the net result has been, or is likely to be, good or bad. After balancing all the circum&not; stances of the case, I, for one, am clearly convinced that it has been bad. The agreeableness of the parties it would not be easy to overrate. A dance is a very pleasant thing to be at, but thought of in cold blood, it must, I fear, take rank as among the least useful forms of social enjoyment. The, flutter and whirl of the ballroom, with the dash of many twinkling feet, the buzz of soft voices, and gleaming of bright eyes, etc. etc., make up an ensemble which is delightful enough when you are in it ; none but an ass or a philosopher could be unmoved thereby : but when it is all over, isn&rsquo;t it something like the fall of a rocket, or the subsidence of an efferves&not; cing draught ? You have danced with a variety of agreeable partners ; you have had a certain number of pleasant faces looking over your shoulder ; you have seen no end of pretty coiffures ; you have spun through clouds of tarlatan and crinoline ; and you have been immersed for about seven hours (according to our present practice, adopted, no doubt, in imitation of the fashionable world) in a general torrent of fizz and excitement. You have, besides this, done an amount of physical work equal to a fifteen-mile walk (in an atmosphere however very unlike that of the Surrey downs), and you
get to bed at four in the morning, to be prepared for the business of the same day by from three to five hours of dreaming over the ecstasies of the past evening. Well, and what comes of all this?&mdash;why, nothing. What conceivable way are you and the hundred other human beings, presumed to be intelligent, who have thus been busily employed for seven hours at a stretch, the better for the affair. You have increased your acquaintance perhaps ; that is, you will be able to recognize the faces of a dozen or more people you did not know before ; but you have not exchanged a dozen words of rational conversation with any one of them, and they know no more of you except as to the colour of your hair, whether or not vou are a good dancer, and such-like important features in your character&mdash;than you do of them. But, you say, this is a denial of the principle stated above, that society pure and simple is the most effective kind of relaxation. Granted that a dance is one way of seeing your fellow-creatures ; but it is just that way which most effectually cuts off the chance of such associa&not; tion bearing any useful fruit. For the question is not between dancing and nothing at all, but between dancing and other modes of association. For instance, a Sunday afternoon walk, such as so many of us have enjoyed within the last six months, is productive of infinitely more genuine socia&not; bility than a dance. If &ldquo; grace and beauty &rdquo; is the attraction, surelv twenty minutes by the side of a &ldquo;partner&rdquo; in a country ramble, is better than meeting her at twenty balls. And as to the other enjoyments in each case, who dare compare them ? Who would exchange the breezy freshness of the Addington hills for the stifling swelter of a city dancim&raquo;- room, the glow of sunlight and scent of heather for the flare and smell of car- buretted hydrogen P For an occasional festival a dance may be well enough, and the rarity of the pleasure would give increased relish to enjoyment Our Christmas and summer parties, for instance, might well, if we had space tor it, be wound up with an hour&rsquo;s dancing ; or if it was possible to have an open social meeting once a fortnight, a quadrille or polka might be thrown in as a hors-d&#39;&oelig;uvre in an evening&rsquo;s entertainment. But the sal&not; tator} art seems to have an all-absorbing and tyrannous tendency to over&not; ride other quieter modes of enjoyment. It gains irresistible sway over light heads and nimble heels ; it makes us all children for the nonce. Pleasant enough it is to shake off the burden of daily work by an occasional return con f lti y i &deg;f babyhood. None can enjoy more than myself such a mode of celebrating Twelfth-night or Christmas Eve, with blind man&rsquo;s buff, hunt-the-slipper, and all the rest of it; but to repeat the metamor- pho,usance a month, and for a period of seven continuous hours, and this as one s regular and most precious amusement, seems to be taking far too large doses of an innocent restorative,&mdash;turning, in fact, a useful tSnic into a pernicious drug.,All this assumes that the amusement, taken by itself, is perfectly harm&not; less ; but there are one or two points in which positive harm certainly arises from these entertainments They give rise to a great deal of emense The admission is one shilling, but practically every one spends at least five. Supper, bd gloves ices, cabs home, and such-like etcľteras make up a total which, to people who have not much money to throw away is no tiifle. And at the risk of forfeiting the favour of my fair friends I mist, if only m the interest of the more reasonable among themselves, pro-
test against the reckless display of millinery which these parties have called forth in an ever-increasing ratio of gorgeousness. By all means let every&not; body&rsquo;s costume be as rich and beautiful as possible, that is, as each can afford to make it.* But if this condition is broken, can such display be called anything else than folly, or worse ? Men who have wives or sisters, know that gay wreaths and dashing opera-cloaks are not bought for no&not; thing, and they also know the little pleas by which such indulgences are insisted on as necessaries. Given a set of quiet young ladies, but one of whom appears &ldquo; en grande toilette,&rdquo; and at their next meeting you shall see that about half the whole number have entered on a rivalry for the honour of outblazing their competitors. Miss A. &ldquo;wore a wreath of roses,&rdquo; Miss B. cannot think of tamely accepting that assumption of superior dignity. The native ripple of Miss B.&rsquo;s tresses is accordingly hidden beneath a cluster of fade camellias, with an addition of six-inch radius to the crinoline by way of defying competition. Miss A. feels crushed, but bravely determines to renew the struggle at the earliest opportunity. To what trials, in the interim, the good-nature of some papa, brother, or husband has been put, an admiring public never knows : but we can guess : and if the fair aspirants are sacrificing their own pockets, how is the case improved ? These are, I am afraid, very insolent remarks ; 1 al&not; most tremble while putting them on paper. But if my young lady friends are as honest as 1 take them to be, they will confess that these little weaknesses (only the counterpart of other vanities in the male sex) are signally en&not; couraged by the gay parties in question. This however is but the smallest part of the case. If the dances were productive of nothing but good, and free from all objection on collateral grounds, the irresistible plea would still remain that dancing is not the work for which our College exists. Suc&not; cess in a work so full of difficulties as ours, can only be attained by simple singleness of aim. We must do only those things which directly bear on our main purpose. To say that dancing comes under this head would be an impertinence. We must avoid all distractions, especially noisy, clat&not; tering distractions, and, more than all, distractions which tend to draw round us men having tempers and tastes different to those which we desire to find prevalent among our students. As a rule, dancing men are cer&not; tainly not working men : if we could get the latter only, and let them dance to amuse themselves, it would not be so bad; but if we surround a College with amusements of a sort to draw a non-working class, we gradually but certainly alter its whole tone and character. For fools, unfortunately, are in a decided majority in this world ; not that all dancers are fools, but at least a large proportion are silly people, it being a diversion &ldquo;suited to the meanest capacity.&rdquo; A casino will fill any night better than a class-room ; we cannot, systematically, fulfil the purpose of both. We have made a certain thing our business. Let us stick to that. Other things may be excellent to be done, but they are not our business. A pastry-cook will have more customers than a watchmaker, but the watchmaker will gain nothing by setting up to sell tarts. If we go wrong in this way, we shall fully deserve the consequences, for we have had abundant warning in the fortunes of the mechanics&rsquo; insti&not; tutes. They have failed, or have at least been revolutionized, by yielding to this very same temptation. By letting play carry the day against work, for,* See Mr. Ruslan&rsquo;s excellent chapter on * Vulgarity,&rsquo; Mod. Painters, vol. v.
the alleged benefit, at first, of the workers, they have at last almost ceased to attract any but those who want play and nothing else. The warning given us by Mr. Eossiter* is, I believe, thoroughly justified by our past year&rsquo;s ex&not; perience : &ldquo; Play founded on nothing but itself is a delusion.&rdquo; Those of our students who care seriously about the serious objects which bind us to&not; gether, have now to consider whether the kind and the quantity of our amusements has not already somewhat damaged our work, and to make up their minds to support by their example and their influence one side or the other. Work vei&#39; sus play, heads versus heels : for which d&ccedil;&raquo; they give their verdict ?f E. B. Litchfield.,ON SOME OF THE USES OF LEAENING GEEMAN.,[Mr. Sonnenschein is about to take a German Class in the London W. M. C,, com&not; mencing next term.],In compliance with the request of the Editor to make a few remarks on the study of German, I gladly do so, and though these may have nothing original in them, and will by no means exhaust the subject, yet I believe they will prove true and perhaps not altogether uninteresting.,Next to Mathematics, no study that I am aware of affords such mathesis &mdash;discipline to the mind&mdash;as the study of one&rsquo;s own language, especially when compared with one or more foreign tongu&eacute;s; for by this means only can we distinguish between universal and local truths. Comparative Grammar is to the understanding of an individual language what Comparative Ana&not; tomy is to the correct appreciation of the frame of any one animal ; and I hope the day is not far off when teachers will as little attempt to teach that most composite of all languages, English, without the help of a foreign standard of comparison, as naturalists would think of investigating the structure of any one animal, otherwise than as a link in a whole chain.,Now, of all living languages I believe German to have the greatest claim upon Englishmen, lor it is the stock from which the choicest portions of the English language has been drawn ; and as a mere subject of historical investigation its importance is invaluable.,Fashion says Thou shalt first learn French and then German. This is reversing the natural order of things, for the language of the English nursery and earlier years is mainly Saxon, whilst the words of Latin origin come in at a later age; thus, we force on the mind that which at the time it is least ready to receive, and the loss, therefore, is very great.,Let us make a few comparisons between the two languages, and we shall see that both the analogies and differences are suggestive of the respective national characters.,* See his letter in last number. t I am sorry to find myself directly opposed in this matter to one of the most active members o&iacute; the Council, with whom tiff about six months ago I scarcely ever had occasion to diner on a serious question. But the case is critical, and it is right that the students should know that Mr. Furmvall is here acting in opposition to the convictions of all lus tnends, and of those especially who are most inclined to think with him on all other points.

The Letters preferred by the two nations respectively:&mdash;,German.,English.,G&uuml;rtel,girdle ;,t changes into d.,Bad,bath ;,d into th.,Kinn,chin ;,к into ch.,Brech,break ; I,Nacht,night ; &fnof;,ch into к or gh.,Kessel,kettle ; Ί,Malz,malt ; &amp;gt;,s, z, or tz, into t.,Letzter,latter ; J,Such instances might be,quoted literally by thousands, but these are,enough to show that the Englishman prefers the softer and easier, the.,German the stronger and fuller utterance.,Now, this same feature shows,itself also in&mdash;,2ndly. The Length of the words :&mdash;,Greek.,German.,French. English.,ΐ - &lambda;&zeta; - &psi; / &chi;&omicron; - &sigma;&nu;-&nu;&eta;,Al-mo-sen,au-m&ocirc;ne alms,And as such instances can also be multiplied, we see that to the gradual detrition of words in the course of ages the German has yielded consider&not; ably less than the English.,3rdly. The Pronunciation of words, wherein, especially in the long ones, we find that the impetuous Englishman lays the stress on an early syllable of the word, thus hurrying more rapidly over the remainder ; for example, let the accent in &ldquo;repository&rdquo; be shifted to the third or fourth syllable, and see how much more time it would take to utter the word. The German takes little heed of the time consumed, but emphasizes that part or those parts which he believes give it its distinctive meaning.,4thly. The Grammatical peculiarities. I often see advertisements run&not; ning somewhat in this style :&mdash;,&ldquo;Brown and Jones, pickled herring merchants, beg to,&rdquo; etc. A Ger&not; man would here profess himself utterly unable to determine which Avere pickled, the herrings or the merchants, and therefore would say, &ldquo; mer&not; chants of pickled herrings.&rdquo; Now, in this case the difference may appear small ; but take the expressions, &ldquo; Hot Water Oven Biscuit Company,&rdquo; or &ldquo; New Cross Cut Signal Station,&rdquo; and paraphrase them into the German constructions.,A still more striking grammatical difference we find in the arrange&not; ment of words ; for example, an Englishman would say, &ldquo; Ered will take you home with pleasure on Thursday next at five o&rsquo;clock ; &rdquo; whilst the German says, &ldquo; Ered will you on Thursday next at five o&rsquo;clock with plea&not; sure home take.&rdquo; On comparison, we find that the English sentence be rins with force, and dies off in importance towards the end, whilst the German gains in intensity. The English sentence might be broken off at any of the words&mdash;you, home, pleasure, Thursday, next, or clock,&mdash;and still make sense ; the German phrase must be completed to be understood at all. In one word, the English utterance is hurried and business-like, the German circumspect, and, it must be confessed, at times pedantic.,Now, these peculiarities a good teacher will not fail at every turn to
point out, develope, and illustrate. But I have left a still more important item of instruction to the. last, viz. the Derivation of words. This is a study of inexhaustible beauty and instruction, and I cannot under&not; stand how a teacher can forgo dwelling on it, seeing, to say the least of it, the great help learners derive from it in remembering words ; for example, it is easy to remember that Schnee is snow, well woe, geh go, and so on ; but where would the uninitiated detect any resemblance between unverg&auml;nglich and imperishable P Nevertheless the two words are indentical,&mdash;thus, geh &mdash; g&deg;&amp;gt; gegangen = gone (Latin for c to go&rsquo; is ire), hence vergehen , perire , perish ; vergangen &mdash; perished ; verg&auml;nglich = perish-like, perishable ; and unverg&auml;nglich &mdash; imperishable. All we have to do is, once for all, to clearly understand the meaning of the German and the Latin prefixes and suffixes un, ver, and lich, and in, per, and able, and the connection is evident. Again, what trace of similarity is there between Bach and roof, Dorf and village, Holz and wood, Thai and valley ?&mdash;but translate these German words respec&not; tively by thatch, thorp, holt, and dale, and not only is the connection evi&not; dent, but in many instances beautiful and highly useful old English words, that are partly buried out of sight, are resuscitated. Is that small gain to a language ? I may be too enthusiastic, perhaps foolishly so, but I be&not; lieve it an act of patriotism in an Englishman to learn German.,I have purposely avoided speaking of the claims of German Literature, as these are nowadays sufficiently established, but only wish, in conclusion, to refute the objections made by some parties, that this method of instruc&not; tion is &ldquo; too deep for young students.&rdquo; Erom ray own experience I can say that it is never too early, but, on the contrary, a great help to stu&not; dents even of the tenderest age, to reduce the multitude of facts that meet them under the fewest possible heads or principles.,A. Sonnenschein.,3 , Clarence Place, Clapton Square, December 34 , 1860 .,INVICTA.,A SONG OF 1860.,(Written for the 19th Middlesex.),Men of our own free England !,Sons of undaunted sires !,Whose fathers moved, for the land they loved, Through battles and through fires ;,We too have arm&rsquo;d for Freedom ;,We too can fight for her;,For who but we have the mind to be free. And the soul that fears a slur ?,Foul fare the fools who tell us That manhood breeds but gold ;,That the ancient name of a realm of fame Is a thing to be bought and sold ;
That Peace is the coward&rsquo;s portion,,Who would cringe to a Man of Sin So his mills might whirl, and his bobbins twirl,,And himself have leave to spin !,No&mdash;Peace, to the wise, for ever With Courage stands involved,,And calmly shows to friends or foes She&rsquo;s ready, and resolved :,Resolved, as the forest-lion,,Who trusts, and not in vain,,To the grasp of his paws, and the gripe of his jaws, And the terror of his mane.,Should we, whose ears are tingling While the cannon booms afar&mdash;,Who have seen the affright, and the shocks of might, And the awful joys of war&mdash;,Who still, &rsquo;mid a quaking Europe,,Keep steadfast, but alone,,While over the waves is a land of slaves,,With a liar on the throne&mdash;,Should we forsake or alter Ош* brave ancestral creed,,That only they who can stand at bay Are Englishmen indeed ?,No ! INTRODUCTORY NOTE ON A COURSE OF MENTAL PHILO&not; SOPHY AND LOGIC.,(To be given at the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, January, 1861, by A. W. Bennett, Esq.),The main object which I propose to keep in view in the carrying out of this course, is to endeavour to interest my hearers in the consideration and discussion of those important questions which relate to the constitution of the. human mind, to the origin of their own thoughts, and the source of then own mental powers. With this end, the course will be commenced, after the needful introductory explanations, definitions of terms, etc., by a review and classification of the different powers and faculties of the mind, including also a description and analysis of the bodily powers ordinarily included in the term &ldquo;the five senses,&rdquo; especially those of Sight and Hearing, as the medium through which we derive the greater part of our sensations and emotions. Among the various mental faculties which will come under consideration, two will demand special attention,&mdash;the Memory and the Reason. Under the former head will be given an account of the various views held by different writers of this most wonderful of the powers of the mind, with some hints as to the best mode of cultivating it. Under the term Reason will be included the subject of Logic, which will form an im&not; portant poition of the course. Logic will be considered as the science of the laws of thought ; that which enables us to distinguish sound reasoning from fallacious sophisms. The work of Archbishop Whately will be used as the text-book, while reference &iacute;v&uuml;l frequently be made to4hose of Mill and Thomson, The position of Conscience among the faculties of the mind, and the exceedingly interesting questions touching the Freedom о the Will, and other kindred subjects, will also claim some attention, throughout the course my object will be rather to elicit the thoughts and difficulties of the members of the class, and to induce them to form their own ideas on the subjects which mav come nndp.r nrvn s&iacute;riprat&iacute; nn than +&amp;lt;-.,CORRESPONDENCE.,. that we have no right to use the Magazine as a means for the propagation of our special opinions on other subjects than that of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, except so far as we view them as necessarily connected, or as they become necessarily mixed up, with Col&not; lege work. Our friend Mr. Newton was quite right in giving us the account he did, of the establishment of the &ldquo; Gilders&rsquo; Association,&rdquo; seeing that it sprang directly from the College ; and I could only wish that he had given that account at greater length, and said something also of his brother&rsquo;s attempt at association in the chair-making line, which probably might meet with easier support from members of the College than can be supplied to the gilders. Mr. Furnivall, too, had the full right to show the connection of our own College with the co-operative movement of 1850-52 ; although I should lake great exception, in several instances, to his mode of presenting facts, and indeed consider his narrative, from the unaccountable overlooking and undervaluing of one car&not; dinal influence and example (not to dwell on preposterous overpraise of myself), as equally unsatisfactory and, in its general effect, untrue. But beyond this I do not well see how we can go. I am not aware that the subject of co-operation (in its technical sense) is one exciting any special interest among our members, or those of any of our sister Colleges, or that any special relation is felt to exist between it and College work. I may fancy 1 see such a relation myself,&mdash;I may even feel in it the main source of my interest in &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. But knowing, as I do, that many of the most earnest friends of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; hold directly opposite views, I should deem it wholly premature, to say the least, to dwell upon the subject, whilst there should be so many others of common interest to all of us.,But again,&mdash;I do not well see how one whose connection with the College is at pre&not; sent barely more than nominal, has any extensive range of subjects whatsoever on which he has the right to speak, however much he may desire it, through these pages. Th&eacute; c &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Magazine,&rsquo; should be, as it seems to me, the moral mirror of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. It should show, as far as possible, what is taught, done, felt, spoken of, thought of, in the various Colleges, or in connection with them, or as the re&not; sid&iacute; of their establishment. One who is neither teaching nor studying there can have very little to say, unless he can tell us what influence they have had upon his life, both outward and inward. That, I take it, is a most interesting field of observation, and the one or two gleanings from it which we have had already, only make one greedy for more. Many of our students who have gone away from us might, I believe, do essential service to their fellow-workers by simply narrating their experiences in their several call&not; ings, from their point of view as trained students of our Colleges. Our tutors and teachers of preparatory classes have also, I am convinced, treasures of useful experience acquired in those offices, which I hope they will not be always too modest to unlock. For I have always been convinced that the teaching of working men by working men is the main ultimate work of these Colleges ; that the most imposing staff of teachers laden with university degrees and honours is worthless, except so far as it conduces to that end. Only the working man can thoroughly understand the working man&rsquo;s difficulties, puzzles, bewilderments ; only he can place knowledge precisely in that point of view from which his fellow can best grasp and handle it. Which does not in the least clash with another conviction of mine, that a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; with only &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; for teachers, can never do all that it ought to do. The two elements,&mdash;of strict theoretic training, such as can till now be enjoyed in youth only by the more leisurely and well-to- do classes,&mdash;and of practical experience, must be kept married together in harmonious fellowship of work. Nay, our youngest and rawest teachers, those who come to us most fresh from the schools, most unused to working men&rsquo;s ways and modes of thought, are in one sense the most valuable of any. It is they who do most to save us from becom&not; ing merely a class College, using special methods of class-teaching ; who keep us afloat in the general current of English education, when we might otherwise lazily go stranded in some snug cove ; who maintain and stimulate our efficiency in the work of bridging over the gap between class and class, by continually reminding us how deep and wide it yet is. Such men too should also have something to say to us from the point of view of their experience of our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; teaching, as compared with that to which they have been used ; though many of them may fairly feel that they have more to say upon such matters to others, than to ourselves.,I have now said, I trust, at sufficient length, why I have little right to wish to say anything. Mind, I do not mean to say that I never do wish to say something, because every now and then I read something in your pages which I feel a violent wish flatly to
contradict, but (beyond mincing a little in that way above, now that you have drawn me, like a badger, from my silence) I have hitherto managed to keep down that wish. For what matters it, after all, in the great work of working men&rsquo;s education, whether A. says black or B, white? The further we keep from mere personal opinion, and, above all, mere personal gossip and chatter, the better,&mdash;things as different from honest personal experience and serious personal observation as the hiss of escaping steam is from the steam-engine.,But there are two classes of persons of whom it should always be possible to speak without littleness, the iar-oif absent, and the dead. Since I have pen in hand, I am sure that I shall give pleasure to many of the older students of our College by telling them that Robert Curtis, one of our best men in years gone by, whose &ldquo; duck&rdquo; was one ot the earliest pictorial triumphs of our drawing-school, but whom his health compelled to leave us for Australia, has, after very various experiences, settled down into what seems a comfortable berth at the Melbourne Public Library ; that he has always conti&not; nued to take the deepest interest in onr College, and has carried on its work by taking- part in teaching himself. And there are a few&mdash;a very few now&mdash;of our oldest members who were connected with that co-operative movement to which 1 have referred, who will learn with grief that Joseph Millbauk, once joint secretary to the Society for Promoting Working Men&rsquo;s Associations, died at Melbourne on the 6th October last.
