THE,ffl&oslash;rkmg ЩЬи C&oslash;llege Щадеше&raquo;,No. XXVI. FEBEE AEY 1, 1861.,PEESONAL EXPLANATION.,By F. D. Maurice.,Dear Mr. Litchfield, &mdash;You have told me that my position in the College is not understood either by the students or by some of the Council. I thank you for your frankness in saying this. I thank you also for inviting me to explain myself in the Magazine. No good ever comes from feign&not; ing, or from compliments. They are the destruction of all Societies which indulge in them. Much may come from the effort&mdash;even if it be not an altogether successful effort&mdash;to show one another what we mean, and to bring our sayings and meanings more closely into accordance. A Prin&not; cipal ought never to be a merely ornamental appendage to a College. To such a College as ours he cannot be. If he is content to be only that, he will be an ugly excrescence in it.,When I say this, I do not shrink from the shame and disgrace which are implied in your statement. If I have not made myself intelligible to the students and teachers of the College, it must have been through some very grievous failure on my part. I have found them willing enough&mdash;ra&not; ther too willing&mdash;to give me credit for good intentions and for interest in the cause of the working men. It behoved me to make it very plain by acts and words what my intentions were, by what means alone I suppose that I can serve the working men. Each would then have been able to answer for himself whether he could honestly act with me or whether you ought to seek another leader.,At the commencement of our work, there was no doubt upon these points ; I cannot charge myself with any concealments then. I was known to a few friends who had frequented a Bible Class at my house. Our in&not; tercourse was of the frankest kind, but this was the foundation of it. They found that I was far less accomplished in general science and litera&not; ture than numbers of my order, than most of themselves, but that I re&not; garded the knowledge of God as the key to all other knowledge, as that which connected knowledge with life. They found that I accepted the Bible as the interpretation of the history of mankind. They found, as they might have expected, that I did not demand that they should bring that conviction with them. They might, if they pleased, bring just the opposite conviction. They might think that the Bible was no better than any other book, or that it was a worn-out book. The truth, it seemed to me, would establish itself. I at first was not afraid to subject that belief which had possession of me, to any test. I thought that it would only be proved and spread if it was brought to the severest test. If the Bible shrank from difficulties, if it needed some preliminary evidence to show that such difficulties ought not to be fairly met, if it had not a stronger evidence in itself than all the ingenuity of apologists could supply,&mdash;it was not the,. c
book which I took it to lie, it had not the power for which I had given it credit, I desired therefore that all who read it with me should express their thoughts of what they read with entire freedom. I had no confidence that any wit or reasonings of mine would be able to change their thoughts. I had the greatest confidence that a Wisdom higher than theirs or mine could impart itself to them.,This may seem to many a most foolish opinion. I am not arguing on behalf of it. 1 am telling you what was my own course ; those who were present at the meeting to which I have alluded, can confirm or contradict ray statement. But 1848 was a time in which no one could merely talk of past history or seek an interpretation of that. History was making itself very fast. We needed something to explain what was going on before our eyes. Men in all lands were declaring that their visible rulers had failed to govern them. These visible rulers had invoked the help of in&not; visible powers to sustain their own weakness, and to make the people yield to them. Evidently there was a protest throughout Europe against the notion that thrones could be upheld by appeals to mere terror, either of the seen or the unseen, either of the present or future. Evidently these terrors were losing their influence. Was it true then that all invisible power was falling to pieces? Was there nothing to keep the world from Anarchy? Was there nothing that could make the world free?,It had seemed to me, ever since I began to think earnestly of these things, that the manifestation of Christ as the actual King of Men, whrch I found set forth in every page of the Nerv Testament, which appeared to be the fulfilment of all that was promised in the Old, had been sti ange y overlooked, often almost denied, by modern divines of all schools. Roman&not; ists had put a visible Pope in His place ; Protestants appeared 1&deg; re &quot; gard Him as only the head of their schools or sects. The events which were happening &lsquo;in France, Italy, Germany, throughout Europe threw, it seemed to me, the strongest light upon these assertions of the Bible, upon these omissions of its interpreters. If it was so ; and if Christ was, as our creeds affirmed, the very Son of God, the perfect Image of His Person, then it must be the highest of all duties to proclaim Him to men in just the opposite character to that in which He had been proclaimed by those who reo-arded His name as the authority for any injustice or tyranny. God must be set forth as He is set forth throughout Scripture, as the Re&not; deemer of Mankind. All rulers must be told they exist to bear witness o&iacute; Him as such. They must be told that their power, if it is like His, it it is derived from His, if He is to uphold it, must be used as a power for deli&not; vering their subjects, not for crushing them. They must rule ; there must be no weak, cowardly tolerance of crime, no shrinking from the use о authority. But just so far as they use it for partial ends ; just so tai as they claim an absolute irresponsible authority ; just so far is it a Godless authority ; just so far may they be sure, from all Divine testimonies, as well as from the evidence of history, that it will be put down.,I should have no right to tell you in the Magazine what convictions i held in 1848, if these had not been the convictions which led my Inends to choose me as the Principal of their College. They not only knew me to be such a theologian as I have described, but they knew me in no other character. But for this one belief, I was the unfittest person m all Englan
for such an office. I had a bad reputation with religious journals and with liberal journals. I had less knowledge of poor men than most of my bre&not; thren. I had, as you have all found too well, little power of acting upon others, or even of making them understand my purposes. I repeat it there&not; fore ; my belief that the Revelation of God in Christ is the great power for the renovation of society, and especially for the elevation of the working class of Englishmen, is the only claim 1 ever had or ever shall have to take part in such a work as yours.,I can easily understand, however, that those who have joined the Col&not; lege recently may have given me credit for intentions the very reverse of these. Perhaps you will allow me, in the next number, to explain a little more fully how I fancy these suppositions have originated, and whether I have changed any of the principles with which I started.,Very truly yours, F. D. Maurice.,A TRAVELLER&rsquo;S RECOLLECTIONS AND THOUGHTS ON EDUCATION IN THE UNITED STATES.,My dear Editor,&mdash; There are books on the subject of American Edu&not; cation, and there will soon be a report on it from a barrister who has been engaged during the past fall in studying it for the Education Commission. I pretend neither to rival the one nor to forestall the other, but to give you a tew impressions and reflections from a pleasant time of nearly two months which I spent in the north-eastern States last long vacation. If they are mostly trite to those who have in any degree taken up the ques&not; tion, still I hope there are many in the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; to whom it will be new ground, as it was to me before I landed at Boston ; and interesting as it will be to me henceforth, and though I cannot claim for the subject any peculiar connection with the College further than as all educational inquiries are mutually akin, yet that seems to me enough to entitle it to a place in your correspondence, though not perhaps to figure at the head of your number.,The first thing, then, relating to education which strikes a stranger in the northern States, is the existence of a general system of what we should call in England secular schools, supported by rates, and managed by local committees, which I need scarcely say are elected. It includes both sexes, and nearly the whole young community of each sex, the exceptions being tound among the richer classes ; for although it is only at Boston that instruction is compulsory, there are few anywhere who are too stupid to desire it for their children, none who are too poor to afford it,&mdash;for it is absolutely free,&mdash;and none whose independence is shocked by its being tree, for all contribute to the rates which maintain it. Add to this, that the efficiency of the public schools beats all but expensive private ones out of tfie field, and you will understand how nearly they sweep the entire juvenile population into their net. Even the rich formerly, when they were less rich, were often brought up in the public schools, as in Scotland, and there aie many o^s and gills at them still, whom an Englishman is surprised to see iere, partly fiom the persistence of old habits, and partly from the money which of late years has been spent on the superior grades of the,c 2
school hierarchy ; for it is by no means the American idea that free public instruction should be limited to the amount attainable by all classes. The system culminates in free academies, where there is given what in any country would be thought a competent education for a merchant, leaving only the higher branches of a thorough scientific and classical education to be the exclusive property of the Universities and Colleges,&mdash;and in girls&rsquo; normal and high schools, where all knowledge necessary for a teacher or governess may be acquired, and is acquired, by many of the daughters of the middle classes, by some to be used in teaching, and by others for the mere sake of education, without any intention of so using it. In the girls&rsquo; nonnal and high school at Boston, containing about two hundred girls, the mathematics, pure and applied, are carried higher than for the ordinary degree at my own University, at Cambridge ; but, as ray visit happened not to be in class hours, I had no opportunity of seeing how solidly they had been acquired. Those who call at the American schools during class hours with a proper introduction, are readily admitted to witness the instruction, from which, as the children are drawn out by question and answer much more than in England, it is not difficult to judge of their advancement. Education is made compulsory at Boston by means of truant-officers, who patrol the streets during school-time, and send truants found there to the house of correction, a mode which leaves parents at liberty to send their children to private schools if they please, or to keep them safe at home, so only that they are not caught idling in the forbidden hours. Something of the same kind is certainly needed at New York, and perhaps in one or two other large cities, but from what I have said, it will be apparent enough that there is no general necessity for any such measure.,The stranger is next struck with the faith all parties in the States display in this system of education, and the accord which there is among them on some of the questions most agitated among us. Any expression of surprise or admiration at the completeness of the system, and the freedom with which the ratepayers supply the money which the school committees ask for, is met by the remark, &ldquo; Why, we could not do without our public schools. They are absolutely necessary to the working of our Constitution. The power being in the hands of the masses, we could never venture to leave it to chance whether the masses should be fit to exercise it. Every one in this country must for his own safety see that the people are edu&not; cated.&rdquo; Such language I have heard both from democrats and republicans, the two great parties which now divide the Union, and with equal earnest&not; ness from each. It is usual, or at least frequent, to open school by read&not; ing some verses of Scripture, singing a hymn, and repeating the Lord&rsquo;s Prayer ; but before the acknowledged necessity, the denominational jea&not; lousies, which in the United Kingdom we see wrangling over the field they omit to occupy, are silent ; so much so, that there are even Eoman Catholic masters at the head of some of the public schools. Yet religious secta&not; rianism is at least as strong in the United States as in England, and per&not; haps more general. It is not then from any absence of the jealousies in question that they have concluded an armistice on that which in England is one of their chief battle-grounds. In saying that it is from the acknow&not; ledged necessity of the case, I have wished to reproduce faithfully what appears to be the American opinion of such necessity ; but one may con-
jecture that the silence of denominational jealousy on this subject is at least as much derived from the discovery that there is nothing in secular education which it need fear, capable as American experience shows it to be of thriving amply among the human material which a fair secular edu&not; cation presents to it.,On examining the nature of the instruction given at the public schools, it is found to possess certain characters in common with that at the Ame&not; rican Universities and Colleges, and which must therefore flow from the peculiar institutions and society of the country. One of these characters is so familiarly known to, and distinctly avowed by, the Americans them&not; selves, that an intelligent citizen will seldom fail to call your attention to it in showing you over a school or a college. It is that a generally good standard is aimed at, and not the production of some brilliant pupils. Of course, the laissez faire system of education, if applied in America to the same extent as even at Oxford, not to mention the English Cambridge (my friends will remember that there is another and a very worthy Cam&not; bridge in Massachusetts), would lead to very different results from those we see here. There being no rich foundations to stimulate competition by fellowships, and the highest finish of scientific and classical attainments being, perhaps in any country, and certainly in a new one, rather a hin&not; drance than a help in the race for making money, laissez faire in America would turn out our bad specimens of education without our good ones. Besides this, however, there is a real recognition of the average, in the genuine spirit of democracy, as the true aim : a deep and confessed feeling that not only would the prosperity of the countiy be impaired, but much more&mdash;its honour tarnished, by any wide defalcation from the average, in a far higher degree than either could be enhanced by the prominence of a few above it. There are instances of Americans, distinguished in their own colleges, who have failed to attain distinction on coming over to the English Universities. You hear these freely admitted, and cited when an Englishman would have avoided their mention, but never with any appear&not; ance of concern.,Another character to which I alluded is discursiveness. On this point the Americans appeared to me to be less aware of their own peculiarity ; and indeed the limited selection of subjects for general education, may be rather described as an English peculiarity, although I do not believe that even on the continent of Europe the encyclop&aelig;dic introduction of youths to all the sciences is carried to such an extent as on the other side of the Atlantic. This characteristic of their education stands, I imagine, in close connection with the well-known versatility of the American in life. The enjoyment of new sensations is their popular idea of happiness, and the search for such in new fields of employment must be greatly facilitated by even a slight knowledge of many subjects ; while the man whose first choice proves unsuitable or unpromising, must find the benefit of such a knowledge in suggesting, and partially fitting him for, many a change of avocation. But as population and capital increase, labour becomes more subdivided and settled ; and in the Atlantic States, the cases are rarer in which the same man has been soldier, lawyer, merchant, politician, farmer, nor abandoned any of those careers, except that of politics, for want of success in it. Still, however, the discursive education remains in vogue.
On no other plan is it easy to aim steadily at an average ; for if the sub&not; jects were few, and further pursued, the superiority of the few in them, would inevitably become apparent. Also a nation of public speakers, and in which youths are taught to declaim as a necessary part of their prepara&not; tion for life, appreciates the power of display, which results from knowing enough of every subject to talk about it, and draw illustrations from it ; while a generation which has been educated in a systematic smattering itseli, is slow to feel the want of fuller and sounder knowledge for its sons. f he allied principles of average and encyclop&aelig;dism, receive their fulfilment in the class-lists of the American Universities, which are arranged from the combined performance of the students in all departments, without special lists for special subjects, as mathematics : and on making particu&not; lar inquiry at Harvard University, as that of the Massachusetts Cam&not; bridge is called, I was assured that little or no connection could be traced between men&rsquo;s rank in the class-list and their success in after life. Un&not; doubtedly some subsidiary reasons may be brought to account for this fact, and I would name as a leading one among them, the youthful age at which the students leave the Universities; an age which, lying between the extreme limits placed by the human constitution for the ripening of the faculties, pits some in still lingering boyhood against others in incipient manhood. A Cambridge man* knows that if those whose opportunities and diligence have been equal were to be examined at the end of their freshman&rsquo;s year, their order then would be different from that in which the unequal subsequent development of their powers will place them at their degrees. But the fact remains at bottom, a result of the peculiar cha&not; racter of American education, which uses no stimulus to call out the energy that success in life so much depends on, but seeks to place the youth on a generally accepted level of moderate preparation, relying for the rest on the boundless reward of energy, which, when he enters on life, the Ameri&not; can student sees opening around him in every direction.,I have alluded to instruction in declaiming or public speaking as a usual part of education, and indeed I have witnessed the surprise of in&not; telligent Americans at hearing that it is not so in England. A stranger quickly gets some notion of the difference between the nations in this respect, by observing that at the public schools, the term recitation is used as almost synonymous with lesson; and from recitation by heart, the pupil proceeds to that of his own compositions or harangues. It will be easily understood from this single, but typical, instance, that display is the chief vice of the American educational system. The effects of con&not; verting instruction into one prolonged preparation for examination, bad enough when the examination expected is private and in writing, are ag&not; gravated when an oral triumph before the school committee, the friends of the children, and strangers, is the object kept in view ; and there is reason to believe that in America, there not only results from this cause the unsoundness which might be expected from it in the teaching of what is actually taught, but that it even influences for evil the choice of sub&not; jects for teaching. There are public schools in which the children can an&not; swer brilliantly on many topics, but in which writing is not taught.,And yet I cannot say that in the demeanour of the children any traces are apparent of the bad effects we should look for from a system tending
so much to display; and this is perhaps attributable to the circumstance that individual self-assertion is not more fostered on the one hand, by the mode and nature of the instruction given, than it is checked on the other by what I must call the drill, which reigns throughout, and which has been adopted to bring great numbers more easily under the control of a few teachers. Sometimes this drill is presented in pretty and pleasing aspects. I have seen five hundred boys, not very widely differing in age and size, having first assembled downstairs, march up to the schoolroom, and take their places in it, to the sound of the piano, with such order and despatch as readily assured me that the one master with his small number of assistants would have them all well under command throughout the day ; similarly I have seen three hundred girls galopading into and out of their room : and as I have incidentally mentioned the uniformity of the boys in age and size, I must explain that this resulted from the grading, as they call it, applied to the schools at New York, by which each school becomes in a sense a single class, deriving its pupils from an extensive area of the city, over which other schools standing higher or lower in the scale concurrently extend&mdash;again an instance of plans enabling a small staff to effect a great deal. Such spectacles bring forcibly home to one an idea of the Americans, which however may be derived with sufficient evidence, even from the adult population, wherever the struggle with Nature in the backwoods is not calling forth and perpetuating individuality, the idea, namely, of a great army marching with uniform characters and aims, and taking ground to the right or to the left with a telegraphic instanta- neousness throughout its whole line. The missing thing in the comparison is the officers : are they therefore the coming thing P One of their own writers has likened his people, &rdquo;surging&rdquo; towards this or that definite point, to those herds of rushing buffaloes, which cover the prairies, so that the ground cannot be seen for miles, and before which no living thing can stand. I have, sincerely, too good a hope for the Americans to desire to press this comparison close, else one might remark that the Indians kill those buffaloes by hundreds, by leaping on their backs, and so crossing the herd from end to end; also that the whole herd sometimes perishes by being driven into one of those deep valleys with precipitous sides, which are known by the Spanish name of canons. Seriously, however, I take the great danger of the States to be the universal preparedness for follow&not; ing. impulses, which are often genuine, but as often got up by the wire&not; pullers and makers of stage thunder behind the scenes : the total want of individual power of resistance to such impulses.,One word on politics, because at this crisis I cannot help it, and in re&not; lation to my last remarks : then I have done. At the recent election, every Bell or Douglas voter was thereby a pronounced man against seces&not; sion, and the Breckenridge vote had in most of the Southern States but a narrow plurality, in Georgia so narrow a plurality over the Bell vote, that it had-no majority at all over the Bell and Douglas votes combined, and the election in that State went consequently to the legislature. Yet the whole South seems now going for secession, and Georgia, after South Carolina, among the foremost. Is not this an instance ? Are the Bell and Douglas men at the South but buffaloes ? The most hopeful part of the States, to me, morally, is the great West, Lincoln&rsquo;s Illinois lor the
centre, the flanks reaching far away beyond Minesota one way, and even to the country part of New York the other. There you have the cultiva&not; tion and moral sense of New England, filled as the country is with the sons of New England and New York, who have emigrated after receiving an education at home, but with the energy and individuality of the back- woods. Lincoln&rsquo;s election is in fact the advent of the Great West in American politics : that part used to be too much filled with backwoodsmen of a rougher and less principled sort, the men of &lsquo; Martin Chuzzlewit,&rsquo; from whom the swampy southern counties of Illinois are still called Egypt, though light is now penetrating even the Egyptian darkness. Being less developed and more distracted, it was of small account : now it has spoken out, while the election returns of Massachusetts and Connecticut last No&not; vember show no advance in public feeling beyond the standard of Fre&not; mont&rsquo;s candidature in 1856, which was unsuccessful.,God speed the American people, and long keep them up to the high moral tone of the great election of 1860, glorious exercise as it was of the distinctive human faculty of choice, choice of the right with the dangers seen and faced ! Yours truly, my dear Editor,,John Westlake.,&ldquo;HEADS versus HEELS;&rdquo; THE DEFENDANT&rsquo;S ANSWEE.,Having been named by our Editor, Mr. Litchfield, in his article or at&not; tack, in the last number, on the dances in which 1 have taken a leading part, it seems incumbent on me to say a few words in defence of them. They grew out of some walks that we&mdash;friends, male and female&mdash;had had together. These joint walks arose on this wise. One summer afternoon in 1859 a party of us amateur geologists, members of the College, were (as we had often been before) in the chalk-pit in Eiddlesdown, below Croydon. A student-friend and I were chipping rhynconellas and tere- bratulas out of the broken chalk around, and were talking about the College, of what it had done, and had not done for its students, when presently he said to me, &ldquo; There is one thing above all others that some of us want, which the College hitherto has not helped us to.&rdquo; &ldquo;What is that?&rdquo; &ldquo; Why, the society of pure and good women. Some of us, who know no families in London, can&#39;t get it.&rdquo; As one looked at the stains on the white chalk round one, and then up to the pure blue heaven above, one could not but be touched by the words and the scene ; they are as present to me now as eighteen months ago ; and when I recollected the life of most men round me at College, and how often they had desired the same help, I saw how real the want was, and after talking it over, was compelled to promise that I would try to get the aid asked. The wife of one of our members most kindly gave it. Our first joint party met at the Crystal Palace, and thenceforward through the summer, young ladies formed part of all our old fortnightly excursions, whether walking or rowing. How their presence was enjoyed, let the short notices of the excursions in the Magazines for the autumn of 1859 witness. While it gladdened and refined all, ft worked a deep and happy change in a few. As the leaves began to redden, and the days draw in, we feared that our association, so pleasant
and profitable as it bad been too, must end. But as we talked this over one evening on the Epping Forest fern while we watched and sang to the the sunset, some one said, &ldquo; We cannot give up our meetings ; as we cannot have them out-of-doors, we must have them in ; and we will have some dances.&rdquo; All liked the notion. A committee was formed (of six students and three teachers of the College, and two compositors, and two married women, and two single ones, one of each once members of the women&rsquo;s classes), and the following circular was issued :&mdash;,&ldquo; All Working Men and Women find that they have neither the rooms nor the money to get together a party of their friends for a merry Dance or Social Meeting ; the plea&not; sant evening &lsquo; At Homes &rsquo; of richer people are out of their reach, though they, after their work, desire and need the refreshment of social intercourse, and the gaiety of music, even more than wealthier persons. But as union of many can do what one cannot, some Members of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; and their Women&rsquo;s Classes, and of Messrs.,- &rsquo;s Printing Offices, have resolved on trying a series of Four Dances at Salters&rsquo;,Hall, under the management of a Committee of Members of the Classes of both sexes, and of Teachers, so as to secure both the freedom and the decorum of a private party.,&ldquo;The cost of the Tickets will a little more than cover the hire of the Hall and the Music ; simple Refreshments will be supplied to those who want them, between the hours of 10.30 and 12 each evening, by the landlord of the hall, at moderate prices. The sur&not; plus from the Tickets will be applied in giving a Tea to some of the poor of the Parish The Singers among the Dancers will no doubt help to entertain them , as well as give th&egrave; rest of the Dancers some Songs and Choruses on the regular Dance-evenings. To avoid unnecessary expense, regular Evening Ľress will not be expected. It is understood that Ladies will dispense with introductions of partners.,&ldquo; Only four Dances have been fixed on at first, on account of the Terms at the College&middot; but one a month might well be held; and if, at or before any Dance, a hundred person&eacute; or more will put their names down and pay for Tickets for that night four-weeks or any other evening, the Hall and Music will be engaged for them.&rdquo; &rsquo; 3,The first dance was held on Friday, October 28th, at Salters&rsquo; Hall, passed off so happily that one of our teachers and some of his friends said they could not wait two months for another. I declined to take any part in making arrangements for an extra dance; but let my name be on the Committee ; and we had a very pleasant little dance of seventy or so on December 1st, at some new rooms, which were not liked so well as the Hall. For our Christmas dance, all the tickets (almost eighty) were sold and fifty people at least excluded, who wished to have been present. A &laquo;min many of us did not wish to put off our next bright meeting for two months&rsquo; and another dance was held on February 10th. The next two took plac&eacute; on the days originally announced, March 9th, and Easter Tuesday, April 10th. So our four dances had turned into six. Meantime our Biffe Corp Sj had got under way, and on May 14th and June 1st we had Biffe Corps dances, chronicled in the Magazine as such. As the original Circu- lar had said one dance a month might well be held, and one was so held And really the people taking part in them were neither boys and girls at school, who needed telling what was reasonable and lawful for them and what was not nor were they fools, as has been half suggested; but&rsquo;men and women able to judge for themselves, attending such dances only as smted them, knowing what their purses and occupations could afford On he 15 h of April, we gave our dance-tea to two hundred of the p&eacute;or of the College parish, the first time that any number of College-men and their mends had entertained their poorer neighbours. And a tmlv harmv even mg it was. Part of Mr. Litchfield&rsquo;s record of it is in tLsľword&trade; - The
entertainment was paid for out of the surplus proceeds of some private subscription dances, the promoters of which thought they could not better employ this little fund than in providing some amusement for those to whom an evening of pleasant recreation is but too great a rarity.&rdquo; The programme, as summed up on the invitation-tickets, ran thus :&mdash;&ldquo; Tea at 6 ; music at 7 and 8.30; &lsquo;Box and Cox,&rsquo; a farce, at 8; a sandwich, beer, and a pipe at 9.15.&rdquo; 1 hope Cox, from his Principal&rsquo;s chair in India, looks back to the evening with as pleasant recollections as we who heard him. The previous rehearsals too, with Corporal R. as Mrs. Bouncer, the cutting the sandwiches, brewing the tea, tapping the beer-barrels, etc., indeed the whole of the dances, is wound round with a net of pleasant memories of friends ministering to the innocent enjoyment of one another, and of the poorer friends of all. Bright spots in the year, for all who took part in them, were the evenings of our dances, and our tea, I am sure.,F, J. Furnivall.,{To be continued .),CONFERENCES ON THE AMUSEMENT QUESTION.,The topic handled in the first article of the last number has occasioned much discus&not; sion in the College, and given rise to conferences and meetings of the Council and stu&not; dents. A short report of these is given here, as the subject is one which, though practically settled as far as this College is concerned, wall always he of critical interest whenever and wherever it is mooted in connection with the work of similar institutions. At the usual General Meeting on January 7th, Mr. Maurice, who presided, said that with a view of rendering such meetings more useful and interesting, it had been sug&not; gested that they should occasionally take the form of conferences on any special topic on which it might be desirable that all the members of the College should interchange opinions. They proposed, therefore, to devote that evening to a discussion of the ques&not; tion of amusements, with especial reference to the dances treated of in the Magazine. It seemed to him that whenever we thought anything was going wrong, the best thing to do was to discuss it openly, and not be afraid of letting the world know we had diffi&not; culties to fight against and sometimes sins to confess. If we only met to tell each other what fine people we were, what a noble work we were doing, etc. etc., we should be making our College (to use an expression once applied to certain charitable associations) a &ldquo; benefit club for mutual flattery.&rdquo; With this remark Mr. Maurice passed on to the special question, and gave his reasons for concurring in the general views expressed by the Editor of the Magazine. This he did most heartily, without of course pledging him&not; self to every word of the article. But the object of that evening&rsquo;s conference was to elicit the feeling of the Students, and he invited any one who so desired to give them his thoughts on the matter. After a few minutes&rsquo; silence,,Mr. Furnivall, in obedience to what seemed the wish of the meeting, gave an ac&not; count of the origin of the dances. [See his article in the present number]. With regard to these alleged abuses, he said that a certain tone of pleasant exaggeration or editorial glow pervaded Mr. L.&rsquo;s article, and that the plea of expensiveness was much overstrained. The Committee had done what they could by a hint in their first programme, and by personal advice, to check over-dressing, and he thought that this was a matter in which to attempt to enforce their notions by any stronger means would be an impertinent in&not; terference with what did not concern them. The parties had been studiously kept sepa&not; rate from the College, and latterly, perhaps, half of those who came were &ldquo; outsiders,&rdquo; and many students were quite ignorant of the whole thing. He was not surprised that the proceeding should meet with opposition from some of the Council and at that con&not; ference. Dancing was not a thing which he should expect clergymen to take much in&not; terest in.,Mr. Newton, as an old member of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, and as a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;man&lt;/span&gt; who had seen much in the course of his life of the working of various institutes and societies, wished
&raquo;.о state his strong conviction, that the introduction of amusements into the regular life of the College would he a most fatal step. He had always observed that when amuse&not; ments were introduce! into a place meant for work, they invariably became the dominant feature, and in a little while the serious purposes of the institution were overridden and neglected.,Mr. Roebuck and Mr. V. Lushington spoke against the dances, expressing their full concurrence in Mr. Newton&rsquo;s views.,Mr. Ludlow thought that in this question nothing less than the very existence of the College was at stake. We have simply, he said, to choose between seriousness and frivolity. Let us never forget for what and for whom we founded the College. It was to give the working men of London&mdash;those, that is, who work with their hands other&not; wise than with a pen&mdash;such help as education can give towards raising the condition of their entire class, towards conquering some of the evils with which the masses of the English people are fighting. Can any work be more serious than this ? And could any time remind us more strongly than the present of its intense solemnity ? In the pre&not; sence of all that we see and hear, day after day, of the destitution and ignorance around us, is it rational, is it decent, for a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; to be amusing itself with wieaths and white waistcoats ? For if these entertainments are to go on at all, surely they ought to be held in this room, and then let our common sense answer the question, Is this the work for which we exist ?,Mr. Hansard wished to protest against the notion that a clergyman, as such, was a foe to dancing or any other innocent amusement. The choir of his church used to have Christmas gatherings, at which they danced nearly all the evening, and at which he him&not; self was present, and very much enjoyed himself. Neither Mr. Maurice nor Mr. Litch&not; field objected to dancing in itself ; Mr. L. probably enjoyed it as much as anybody ; all they said was, that as a systematic amusement it produced grave incidental evils, and was, in any case, not the business of our College.,Mr. Litchfield said, that the feeling of the meeting was too conclusively in accord&not; ance with his own, to make it necessary to argue the matter further. He wished chiefly to explain one or two expressions in his article which he found had given offence. A friend had noticed that his remarks on the female attire prevalent at the dances seemed to imply a &ldquo; sneer at poverty.&rdquo; All he could say was, that to sneer at anything, least of all at poverty, was the last thing he should be guilty of, and he did not think the persons most concerned, among whom he had the pleasure of reckoning many friends, would dis&not; cover m his words such an unworthy meaning. A certain word, too, had been inter&not; preted as conveying a slur upon the character of the parties. When he said that a &ldquo; casino always filled better than a class-room,&rdquo; he chose these two things, hastily perhaps, as the most obvious types to give the antithesis between work and play, without, of course, the remotest intention of alluding to the character of the dancers. He was sorry to find that a careless expression of his had given pain to any one, and wished to disclaim most strongly having used it in the sense suggested. He did not the least agree with the doubts expressed by previous speakers as to the mode in which the dances had been ma&not; naged ; he thought that m this respect no private party in a lady&rsquo;s drawing-room could,te, .Tw a r 0Ue L &iacute;re i fr0m 0bj 1 T tl0n &middot; But the allegations of expense and excess seemed, to him, to have been virtually admitted by all ; and even if they were disproved the simple plea that amusement, if systematized, attracts men of a different kind to those whom the College seeks to attach to itself, ought to be sufficient to decide the ques- tion. It would not do to listen to the arguments urged on behalf of the young women for the simple reason that we are a College for men, not for women. If we could take in hand the entire question of female education, we might also deal with that of female amusement. Го touch just that part alone of this great problem which least concerns us, while we shrink from the attempt to bring women within the action of the serious influence of the College would be plainly absurd. Till they are allowed to share our work, let us at least keep them clear of our frivolities.,The Conference then finished.,A Meeting of the Council was held on January 17th, at which the same subiect was discussed. Sutton said : I, as a member of the Committee, cannot leave Mr. Furnivall to bear the whole weight of defending the course taken by the promoters of the dances.,I like the dances, but I like the College very much more ; if the two will not work to&not; gether, and we must make a choice between them, let the dances go, by all means. I believe they did some good, and so regret their loss ; but between such an alternative, one of course cannot hesitate. I liked the article in the Magazine, but I must say, with the permission of my friend Mr. Litchfield (for he is one of the best friends I ever had), that in reading it I had a sensation very like that of being skinned. Might not the pro&not; cess of putting down the dances have been carried out in a quieter way ? If the Council had said they thought the dances were doing harm, I and others would at once have withdrawn our names from the Committee, and have done what lay in our power to stop them. To have dances in the homes of such men as myself, as a previous speaker had suggested, is an impossibility. These parties have given us good opportunities for meet&not; ingai 611 ^, and for real social intercourse, and if any other means can be devised for affording such opportunities, we should have less reason to regret their cessation. It is as social meetings, and not for the actual dancing, that I have valued them. As to the question of expense, I think (though I appear to differ from some of my friends) that five shillings a month could be screwed out of a man&rsquo;s ordinary expenditure without much difficulty. As to dress, in the original prospectus a notice appeared that &ldquo;evening dress was not expected,&rdquo;and the promoters of the dances have for the most part set a reasonable example in that respect. For myself, I have always gone direct from my business to the dance. But there is no denying that a more expensive and elaborate
style of costume has gradually become prevalent. Gentlemen will wear tail-coats, white kids and chokers ; ladies will disport themselves in low-bodied dresses, opera cloaks, and extensive crinoline. But with respect to the ladies, we ought not to forget that many of our female relatives and friends have had an opportunity, through these parties, of enjoying a kind of social pleasure which they can scarcely have in any other way, I do not object to the jurisdiction of the Council in the matter ; on the contrary, I receive their opinion very gladly, as that of men for the most part older than myself, and certainly well fitted to give advice on the subject. I believe them to he wrong in this instance. But I would say in conclusion, dancing can be found anywhere ; in London we have only one &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ; and if the one or the other must be given up, by all means let it be the dancing.,Mr. Thrower said, as a committee-man he too must say a word in his defence ; for it appeared they were all on their trial (No, no). He desired to know what was in&not; tended by the resolution of the Council, and how far it was likely to affect Mr. Furni- vall&rsquo;s connection with the College. He (Mr. Thrower) had attended several of the dances, having been accustomed to dance more or less for a number of years ; hut he had throughout been of opinion that the four dances originally announced were quite sufficient for one season, and at those only had he attended. He believed, as to the question of expense, that the men who attended the dances were as able to judge of their capability to meet the expense attending them, as any other class of men. He himself belonged to one of the worst-paid trades, and he always found himself able to indulge in a dance when he wished. He knew many working men who spent as much ш a week in other indulgences, as it was said one of these dances cost. Ц(Ы the dances &rsquo; wished t0 bear testimony to their pleasant nature, and to say that he had met many of his friends there, and enjoyed more social intercourse with them at the dances than at the College. He hoped, however that,he cT T damned and that the good feeling which had always existed in the College between Council and students would not be interrupted. He hoped this because of his strong feelings of attachment towards the College and the Council,the oh ľ?? T at &pound; d V ! hat h ^ had Said at the last meetin S as to the meaning of rinuaneľfo? U .&deg; ted Ьу Breston &rsquo; addin S&amp;gt; that his own presence at the dances, and fon- tmuance for a year on the Committee, was a sufficient proof that he had nothing to allege
against the character of the dances. But before the discussion closed, he wished to recall the main points, which had somewhat slipped out of view. The allegation, he said, of undue expense is not the chief ground on which I rest my opposition to the dances. That they have a great deal of good in them, and are very pleasant things, I do not for a moment deny. I fully concur with almost every word iu a letter about to be published in the Magazine, from a lady who takes a great interest in the College. What we have to do is to strike the balance between a certain amount of undoubted good on the one side, and an equally certain but indefinite amount of risk on the other. Now if this was a purely speculative problem, if we were dealing with a new case, we might fairly hesi&not; tate. But it is not so, for in fact we are now forced to this alternative : those parties must be either disavowed or adopted by the College ; their present half-and-half con&not; nection with us must clearly be put an end to somehow. Are we then prepared delibe&not; rately to make dancing a part of our College business ? If we say we are, we shall be to that extent sinking the very purposes for which we exist; we shall be destroying that atmosphere of work which is the first condition of our existence ; and more than that, we shall be flying in the face of facts : facts which no one pretends to deny or dispute. Have we not before our eyes the spectacle of hundreds of institutions ruined by the very course we are now told to take P For who can deny that the present condition of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes is, with reference to the grand views with which they started, one of simple failure ; and failure brought about mainly by the intrusion of this pretty, plau&not; sible thing, &ldquo; Social Amusement&rdquo;? And if this is so, how can we, as rational beings, quietly put aside, on the strength of a speculative opinion, a great broad fact of every&not; day experience ? What right have we to stake the future of the College on the chance of its turning out the one lucky exception to a universal rule ? For seven years we have been carrying on this College as an experiment. Its success or failure is an issue for which we have made ourselves responsible. If it fails in our hands, the failure will be quoted for perhaps a whole generation, as a standing reproach to the working men of Loudou ; and whoever follows us in the attempt will be told, &ldquo; Oh, you can do nothing serious with working men : it has been tried, and it doesn&rsquo;t answer. Give them dancing or any other nonsense, and they will come to you ; not otherwise.&rdquo; Let our College be a living contradiction of this stupid bigotry, and we can afford to laugh at it: but let us see that we don&rsquo;t recklessly justify the taunt.,The conference closed with a unanimous vote of thanks to the Chairman, whose ready tact and genial good-humour had been conspicuous throughout the discussion.,NEWS. 9,Wolverhampton .&mdash;The students of our College could not reconcile themselves to the idea of Christmas passing away without some festive gathering; so, on Thursday, Jan. 10, they, and a few of the teachers and Council, took tea together in the Athen&aelig;urn Room, &Oacute;neen Street. The room has just been very nicely decorated, and looked very inviting. On a small platform was placed a piano&mdash;a clear indication of a pleasure in store. After tea, the Rev. I. H. lies, the rector of the Collegiate Church, a firm and fast friend of the College, took the chair, or rather was supposed to do so, for the whole of the platform was speedily monopolized by the members of our Glee Class. Mr. lies briefly reviewed the past year&rsquo;s College history, referring especially to the visit of Mr. Maurice to us in September, and the pleasure it gave to him and to us all, to see amongst us one who might be called the Father of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, and to receive his kindly counsel and sympathy. Mr. Langley, the Secretary of the College, also dwelt upon Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s visit, and expressed his special delight to hear that some of the students were anxious to have Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s portrait hung up in the principal room of the College, which had lately been re-decorated and re-papered, not only at the sole expense of the students but by the labour of some of them. He adverted to the social meetings which the students had themselves instituted, as filling up a very important element in their College life. The College, he added, was now in the fourth year of its existence, and up to the present time not one jarring word or suspicious look had caused a moment&rsquo;s interruption to the harmony and confidence of both students and Council. Mr. Langley then described a visit he had just paid to Messrs. Chance&rsquo;s Institute at Spon
Lane, which had furnished the greatest number of successful cand dates for the Society of Arts certificate, of any institution in South Staffordshire in the last year. On the behalf of the College, though without any authority, he had actually challenged them to a further trial of skill iu the ensuing examinations, and though he believed we should be beaten, he had no tear for the result ; for when carried on iu the right spirit, such a con&not; test would only bind them closer together, and whoever was vanquished would be the fust to hail the victors. Mr. Farnworth, an old student, who had been with us from our commencement, gave us an admirable speech, and, without asking anybody&rsquo;s permission, ventured to introduce politics. He believed we might call ourselves a &ldquo; Reform Club,&rdquo; for teachers, students, and all, are thorough Radical Reformers. &ldquo; Though,&rdquo; he added, we do not carry on our reforms just in the same way as many so-called reformers do, ve want to raise one another, by kindly help, up to the franchise ; they want to pull the franchise down to the people ; and ours, I for one firmly believe is the truest reform&mdash;far better than Mr. Bright&rsquo;s or Lord John&rsquo;s!&rdquo; I do not know what some of our Radical friends will say to this ; I am only bound to give a faithful report of whąt was actually said by a genuine working man, and, I must add, it was heartily applauded by a meeting о woiking men. Conservative as I happen to be, I must just confess, I joined most uliy in the applause. Mr. lies being obliged to leave us, first invited us all to discuss some oranges which had been provided, and then summoned another of our old students, Mr. 1 bomas Morris, to succeed him in his imaginary chair. Mr. Hough, a valuable cacher, who has recently joined us, gave the men some admirable advice about , their woi , it they meant to accept Mr. Langley&rsquo;s challenge. Mr. Green gave a hearty testi- mony to the steady improvement of his Writing Class. Mr. Bradshaw, in a speech hrim- u о inmour, appealed to the young ladies present, and urged them, when &ldquo; some- body popped the question&rdquo; to them, to pop another iu return: &ldquo;Do you go to the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt; s &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ? If not, I&rsquo;ll have nothing to do with you ! &rdquo; Mr. Caddick another old student, added the result of his three years&rsquo; experience. But I must not forget what was after all, the chief pleasure of the evening. Mr. Tilley, the energetic master of the Glee Class, marshalled his forces on the platform. Mr. Jones gave us his admirable help at the piano, and songs, duets, and glees alternated with the speeches in capital style. Our friend Mr. Furnivall will remember how he shrugged his shoulders at our musical performances at a meeting similar to this, at which he was present in beptember, 1859 ; but he would not have shrugged his shoulders at this meeting. CORRESPONDENCE.,Dear Mr. Editor, &mdash;I am not a student of your College, and am not called upon there&not; fore to make the decision you urge ; but I am much interested in the subject of the dances, and shall be glad if you will permit me to say a few words about them.,Were their continuance involved in the question of &ldquo; Work v. Play, Heads v. Heels,&rdquo; I, for one, should have no hesitation in deciding what reply to give : but I do not feel the subject should be quite so summarily dismissed.,It seems to me rather hard that I, for instance, because I need amusement &ldquo;pure and simple,&rdquo; should be voted &ldquo; irrational for the need only comes to me (setting illness aside) at times when I am working most hard, and in all humility, I think most use&not; fully,&mdash;when, under the pressure of work, and of interest and anxieties for others, I get weary of thinking, feeling, talking, and I only long for rest. Such rest one can some&not; times get in pure and simple amusement. And if / feel this, may it not be true of other and harder workers than 1P,I, for my part, do not rank dancing high in the scale of amusements, nor should I seek the rest / need in the exercise ; but others may ; and it seems reasonable to me to suppose there may be many, even among the privileged workers of the College, who might do so under certain conditions. There is certainly a numerous class who have not, unfortunately, sufficient education to find amusement in study, and whose daily life does imperatively need change and refreshment. To such it is a great good to share amusements they can enjoy with those of higher cultivation and taste. There is much need of social pleasures which form such a platform of meeting, and when the dances first commenced, 1 rejoiced in them for their use in supplying such a need.,I can readily understand, however, it may be a serious and most important question whether the college should have anything to do with the promotion or encouragement of dances at all. Standing aloof from it, though promoted by men of position and educa&not; tion, the dances, with a few reforms, might be made, I believe, one means, though a small one, by which the students of the*College might exercise a beneficial influence over men and women of their own class who have not their taste and refinement. I have been pre&not; sent at several of the dances, and have had great pleasure in seeing them yield so much hearty enjoyment. I never saw dancing made elsewhere such a genuine delight, with so little flirting and affectation attending it.,I feel strongly however the evils you complain of in your paper,&mdash;the long and late hours of the dancing, and the overmuch dressing. I cordially agree that any amusement which unfits one for the work of the next day should be most carefully indulged in, if it should not be altogether avoided; but dancing does not necessitate late hours, nor ex&not; pensive toilettes, I know. Would not the dances gain over to themselves many oppo&not; nents if they took place at rarer intervals, began really punctually, and ended no later than 12 o&rsquo;clock? If the young girls too would remember your rule of dressing only as prettily as they could easily afford, and go in dresses which would allow their passing through the streets with warm cloaks over them, bonnets on their heads, and goloshes on their feet, independent of cabs and white kid gloves, and conscious of being really more becomingly dressed, in an everyday simple costume which is in keeping throughout, than in attempting a style of dress which must be expensive if beautiful.,Of course I do not compare a dance in Salters&rsquo; Hall with a walk on the Surrey downs.,1 would never vote for a ball when any better social pleasure is to be had. I would never jeopardize any principle or earnest work by upholding a mere amusement. If work and play can not go on side by side, the play should stop and be replaced by some other kind of recreation with less danger in it. But the dances in question have been pleasant and useful to many with whom my life is in contact, and 1 feel almost bound therefore to give ray testimony in their favour. THE,No. XXYIL MAECH 1, 1861.,THE BIBLE AND THE COLLEGE.,By the Rey. F. D. Maurice, M.A.,Deau Mr. Litchfield, &mdash;There is an error of the press in my last letter, which is very innocent as far as you and the printer are concerned, but which seriously affects my meaning. I am made to say, &ldquo; I at first was not afraid to subject that belief which had possession of me to any test.&rdquo; I did say, &ldquo; I, at least , was not afraid, etc.&rdquo; I correct the mistake at once, because it brings me straight to the subject of this letter. My purpose in writing is to declare that what I thought &pound; at first,&rsquo; I think now ; that what I was not afraid to do c at first,&rsquo; I am not afraid to do now.,I said that the new students and the new Members of Council might have good excuse for suspecting that it was not so. My name has been associated, even in penny newspapers which all people read, with the names of very eminent men who think that the English reverence for the Bible is exaggerated. These men are so superior to me in all intellectual and moral qualities, that I am sure those who brought us together intended to pay me a compliment. It is a compliment which I should be less disposed to decline at this time than any other, because the writers to whom I al&not; lude are in disgrace with the religious world and with the authorities of the Church. Were I addressing that world or those authorities, I should let the suspicion go for what it is worth. As I am writing to those by whom for practical purposes it is needful that I should be understood, 1 will say at once that I am more convinced in the year 1861 than I was ten years ago, that the only cure for the mischiefs which are current among religious men of our day, and for the most deep-seated corruptions of the Church, lies in a more hearty and thorough appreciation of the worth of the Bible than any which prevails amongst us. 1 must go further, and say that the high character and learning of those who adopt the opposite opinion to mine, so far from shaking me in it, have led me to consider it more carefully, and have helped very much to make it a fixed and abiding conviction. 0,There seems to be some arrogance in the last assertion ; I will endea&not; vour to explain it.,Men at the Universities and in places where leisure is found for such inquiries, debate long and learnedly about the Bible ; what evidences there are for its authority ; what amount of authority it has ; how far it can be shown to proceed from above ; how far its words are affected by the hu&not; man agents through whom they come to us. Those who enter into these controversies, m their pleadings for one side or another, in the efforts to leeone e different sides, in their pursuit of some safe result whatever it may be, exhibit all degrees of earnestness, of moderation, of fidelity. But,D
the onlookers feel more and more as the strife proceeds, that the issue ought to be one of immense importance to them ; and yet that if there is so much to be said for every possible opinion respecting it, they can never arrive at any issue. They have other work to do in the world than to follow divines into all their intricacies of argument ; if that is neces&not; sary,&mdash;if there is no safe or honest conclusion to be got without it, they determine, for their part, to let the whole subject alone. And so the question will be practically decided ;&mdash;decided not against a particular theory about the Bible, but against all interest in it whatever. Notoriously it is so decided in a multitude of cases. And no one can say that the reas&ouml;ners on one side have had more to do with this result than the rea&not; s&ouml;ners on the other. Often the asserters of the authority of the Bible pro&not; duce a violent reaction against it by their assumption and ignorance ; often the doubters, after holding out hope for awhile of some more easy solution, hasten the same consequence by the disappointment which follows when his solution proves unsatisfactory. Now here is proof enough, I think, to all manner of people, if they would see it, that this kind of disputation never will bring us to any honest conclusion. I use the word honed deliberately. I believe the persons who are fighting may be most honest. But does it not strike those who wish for earnest in&not; quiry, that earnest inquiry is ceasing amongst us&mdash;that the negative con&not; clusion is taken for granted &mdash;by those whose minds are least embarrassed with what are called religious prejudices, just as a different conclusion is taken for granted by those who are impatient of inquiry ? And does it not strike those who are most eager to defend the authority of the Bible that they are resting its authority on the cleverness and force of the ar&not; guments for A; and that these are proving most ineffectual, as they ought to do, if it /Шап authority which is higher than all such arguments.,These considerations might have forced me, if there had been no others, to the practical conclusion which I announced in my last letter, viz. that nothing whatever can come of arguments about the Bible, of arguments to prove that it has an authority ; that we must let it speak for itself, and show what it is ; instead of trying by our skill and wisdom to make out its claims beforehand. If I have faith in it, I dare put it to that proof. Those who have not faith in it may be at least willing to learn what it con&not; tains. I believe an immense majority of the most religiously educated people in the land do not know what it contains. They have read a great deal about the Bible. They remember that their fathers prized it very dearly. They have been crammed with arguments to prove its inspiration. But what it actually has to say about any of the facts of the world, about any of the thoughts in which they are most interested, they have only the vaguest, haziest impression. In general, their notion is that it has a sort of left-handed connection with the history of the world, and with what is nassino&middot; in the world ; but that the purpose of its writers is to impose a certain&rdquo;religious theory upon us which we are bound to accept under ter&not; rible penalties, though it may happen to contradict all history, though it may be especially at variance with the thoughts which most occupy us in,&deg; U Now it appeared to me, when we commenced our work, it appears to me far more clearly now,&mdash;that the great hope of this age lies in those per-
plexities which are driving so many to despair. I rejoice that our argu&not; ments to prove the Bible the word of God have broken down under us ; that they have failed to convince doubters. For I believe those argu&not; ments obliged us to assume a divine Person whom we made out of our own thoughts and imaginations, and whom we often endowed with some of our own evil qualities ; with those qualities which the Bible ascribes to the false gods whom the heathens worshipped. Whereas the Bible tells us that the Eternal God reveals Himself to men ; that He discovers to their hearts and consciences, the Righteousness which is the ground of all their righteousness ; that He scatters the dark notions and fancies and conceptions of our minds, which we try to enthrone in His place. We who think that it speaks truly in this matter,&mdash;we who know from our own experience what a number of dark thoughts we have had in ourselves ; and from history what influence such thoughts have exercised upon the lives of men and nations, must desire that God Himself should come and confound them. And we must think no instrument so effectual for that work as the book which sets forth His revelations of Himself from genera&not; tion to generation ; which records what we believe to have been the final and perfect Revelation in the Son of God and the Son of Man. We who have no question that the Bible does speak the thing as it is, and does ad&not; dı ess itself to all those thoughts and cravings in men which are not of one age but all ages ; we who hold it to be, what it assumes to be, a book of Ethics and Politics, laying down the foundation of moral life and of human Society, explaining the causes which have corrupted moral life and human Society, setting forth these especially in the nation and the men whom it declares to have been chosen by God as witnesses of His Name ; we who hold it to be an honest and true book, most honest and true in those particulars which have provoked the most plausible animad- veisions upon it; we must desire not to put ourselves between it and the readeis of it, but to let it bear with all its force upon us and them, to let it expose our follies and ignorance as well as theirs.,I am certain that I am not deceiving myself, when I say that it is possible to read the Bible in this spirit; and that a clergyman is never so likely to read it in this spirit as when he reads it with a class of men who see things from a point of view altogether unlike his; when he hears all their objections, difficulties, unbeliefs, fairly and broadly stated; when he comes provided with no stock of arguments to overthrow them ; when he acts as it the Book were wiser than he is, and as if God were greater than the Book, and wished us all to understand what He means, and as if He would humble the arrogance of the priests and meet the wants of the lav- men. I did believe, and I do believe, that a Bible Class, instead of beino&middot; extraneous to the other studies of our College, is that which interprets them all ; that which might speak most directly to the mind and heart of the student ; that which might tell him who is educating him, and for what ends I am well convinced that if there is not a message to him concerning himself, he will very soon cease to care for all his other lore ; that it will become dry and worthless to him ; that he will not be able to connect it with his common work; with his family life; with what he reads in the newspapers ; with the puzzles of the world. 1 say this deli&not; berately , though I do not think that I am apt to exalt my own special fune-
tions, and though I desire heartily that they should cease altogether, and that our common prayers should cease, if they are only shams and cere&not; monies, instead of making all that could degenerate into sham and cere&not; mony, real.,I am not writing only for the students. What I have said has far more to do with the teachers. I have felt for years, that the men who come from our Universities must lose the interest in all that which they have learnt there, if they cannot bring their learning into connection with life, if they cannot make it bear upon the condition of the toiling, suffering classes. This was the thought with which we started. I confess I was still more occupied by another. It seemed to me that they would sink into moral indifference and heartlessness,&mdash;into a contented atheism&mdash;- if they were not forced to confront the actual problems by which men are tormented. And, on the other hand, I was very confident that if they did fairly meet these problems, they would find a necessity for that faith which evaporates in the places that appear professionally appointed to nourish it. But I was sure that no mere notion that religion is good and necessary for the poor would ever enable any honest man to recover his faith. All atheism lies in that phrase and that thought. One law for the rich and one for the poor is bad enough. A God for the rich and a God for the poor is damnable. It was only those who were brought to feel that what the poor man wants they want; that they are men and must have all that men need,&mdash;it was only these who could ever leam whether there is any revelation of God or not.,Our first Bible Class, therefore, was, as I have said, among ourselves. The loss of the intercourse which it produced has been, to me, the greatest of all losses. I have the truest regard and affection for my colleagues, But I do not feel that I know them, or that they know me. Our con&not; nection is cold and professional, not the least what it ought to be. I know what they will suspect that I intend by such words. They will suppose that I want to find out those who agree with me, and to get rid of the rest.,I mean just the reverse. Let there be a thorough understanding ; let every one speak out just what he thinks, with that reverence and courtesy which are never wanting among friends to each other, and I say it boldly, the less they agree with me, the more I shall be pleased to meet them, and work with them. I shall think they are doing their own work truly and manfully in their own classes. I shall feel that we are seeking for a common bond, a real fellowship. Every one in his lower nature craves for those who echo his words and swear by his opinions. To learn from the Bible, to feel its power of addressing all states of mind, we must be content to meet those whose education, tempers, and opinions are altogether unlike our own, who do not accept us as oracles, who try us with hard questions.,I have seldom been so much pleased with anything as with your frank confession that you and others did not understand me, and wished to un&not; derstand me. The fault is mine ; may we all strive together for the remedy&not; ing of it.,Very truly yours,,F. D. Maurice.
LONDON &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;. Sixth Annual Report by the Principal.,^ The &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; has now been established above six years. There has been nothing in the experience of those six years to make us distrust the principle on which it was founded. Many students have been thankful that they joined it. The teachers have had at least as much reason to be thankful. Nor does the condition of the classes or of the tunds at the present time oifer any excuse for despondency. The attend&not; ance has been better since the first excitement of the Volunteer movement has ceased and the demands for attendance at drill have been less exact&not; ing. The real benefit of the corps for social purposes, and even as giving an object to the work of the College, is, we trust, beginning to appear&deg; And the steady exertions of the Finance Committee, the liberality of old friends, and the increased payments of the students, have enabled us to meet our current expenses and to invest a little.,It would be ungrateful not to record these facts. They afford, how&not; ever, no plea for self-laudation. In six years much more should have been done than has been done. If the principle which we have asserted is right, we ought to have made its power far more widely and deeply felt than it has been felt. Agitation to establish Colleges, either in London or in the country, would not have effected this object. The noise and clatter of platforms does little for the elevation and reformation of classes or indi&not; viduals. What is wanted is more energy and purpose among ourselves ; a clearer and fuller understanding in the minds of us, the teachers, of the ends foi which we are working ; a more distinct and vigorous determination to impress the students with our own convictions. In every case the shame of any failures to Ъе what we ought to be, and do what we might do, should be borne rather by us who teach, than by those who learn. We have had little cause to complain of a want of response to any proposals we have made to them, even to appeals which have demanded of them a con&not; siderable sacrifice of time and labour. Very recently several of them have come forward with great alacrity to assist a neighbouring institution, and have continued steadily to teach in its classes.,To more frequent and open communication between the teachers themselves, and between the teachers and the students, and to the influence which may be exerted upon us by Colleges which have been formed else&not; where m imitation of ours, we look mainly for a stimulus to make us moie earnest in fulfilling the task we have undertaken. We are endeavouring to change our mere formal meetings into conferences on sub&not; jects connected with the well-doing of the College, in which teachers and students will take a part. We are very anxious that there should be the fieest communication, through the Magazine, of thoughts which occur to one 01 another, for the improvement of our methods or of our social life. We especially invite the hints and animadversions of the members of other ol eges, who may have hence made many discoveries which we have not made, and corrected errors into which we have fallen.,&quot;snal Reports of classes and of funds are subjoined.
