WORK AND PLAY, OR HEADS AND HEELS.,January 25, 1861.*,** To hear an opeo slander is a curse ;,But not to find an answer is a worse.&rdquo;&mdash; Dryden.,Dear Mr. Editor, &mdash;I should have said a word or two on this subject under any circumstances, but as I am on the Dance Committee, I feel per&not; sonally bound to notice the acrimonious invective which the writer of the article in the January number gives vent to, in stigmatizing our endea&not; vours to enable our friends,&ldquo; To chase the glowing hours with flying feet.&rdquo;,Of course Mr. Furnivall will be able to show the fallacy of three-parts of the argumentation, and anything that I may attempt with a similar inten&not; tion, is likely rather to damage than to help his cause ; nevertheless, I must openly express my repugnance to the uncalled-for insinuations in the article. Such, for instance, as that the ladies could not afford to pay for such costume as they appeared in. Now this is an assumption which no one has any business to make with respect to his neighbour&rsquo;s apparel, or anything else that is his neighbour&rsquo;s, and is, to use a mild word, inde&not; fensible. As to his complaining that the dancing has been taken in far too large doses, this is certainly true, and I am glad attention has been called to it ; but I must remind him that not more than a fifth of the per&not; sons who attended the dances last year, were at all of the nine. Some only attended one, and very few indeed more than four. Nor had several of the Committee, myself included, anything to do with the extra dances ; in fact, there was opposition to any more than the four. Again, on the score of expense ; the writer knows that the Committee, both ladies and gentlemen, at the first set themselves, both by example and influence, against &ldquo;costly finery.&rdquo; But when one comes to look at this question of cost, I am led to ask myself, do not all &ldquo; gay parties,&rdquo; does not, in fact, every kind of enjoyment (except perhaps that of the sage member of our College, who is satisfied with looking at a fellow-student) cost something ? Does not meeting together to practise music, the sister art of dancing, also entice the singers into petty but pardonable acts of extravagance ? Music costs money ; so much a quarter to start off with ; so much for white kid gloves, cabs, refreshments, tickets for friends, for concerts, etc. Then, too, this cultivated taste for music and singing leads to the spending of money in going to see new operas, and paying for cabs and white kids again, to the extent of more than the quiet five-shilling dance. Hearing the songs at the opera induces the purchase of the music, and so on. I should be very glad to cast accounts as between the concert-goer and the temperate votary of Terpsichore. Then the Committee are charged with having a grand &ldquo; Rifle Ball.&rdquo; Now no one could presume to call such a quiet dance as the one of the 27th December a grand affair. The Committee,[* This letter was not printed last month, as it was thought that the two Conferences had given members of the College a sufficient opportunity of discussing the question. It now appears in accordance with a special request. The College having come to a practical decision last month, it does not seem necessary to restate the case to which Mr. Preston&rsquo;s letter is an answer.&mdash;E d.]
styled it a &ldquo; Rifle Corps Dance,&rdquo; and its grandness may be estimated by the readers of the Magazine, when I tell them that the tickets were a florin each, and a breakfast was provided at a shilling&middot; a head.,The advantages of a Sunday excursion are compared with those of a dance. As to the propriety of the latter, in comparison with the former,,I need not say a word ; but we all know that getting to Addington Hills costs something, and tea at a roadside inn something more. The ex&not; penses, too, are increased when, as I understand, these Sunday outings occur rather oftener than the much vilified dances. I think, too, I can put another version to the writer&rsquo;s account of the introduction of finery and expensive dresses which he would seem to deprecate so much. As I have already said, at the first dances all the Committee dispensed with evening dress, but there were unfortunately present two or three ladies en yrande toilette. The result was that their company was the most courted all the evening, there was an immense demand for their programmes, and it was observable that those gentlemen of whom Horace had well said,,&ldquo; Their folly pleads the privilege of wealth,&rdquo;,were the most assiduous in their attentions to these bedizened fair ones. How can it be wondered at, then, that on the next occasion there were &ldquo; clouds of tarlatan and crinoline&rdquo; ? It is all very well to try to appear insensitive, but a man is as pleased to see a lady prettily dressed as ladies are to dress themselves out to advantage. For whom, pray, do the ladies dress ? Certainly not to please each other. Why do they vie with each other, as the writer of the article hints? Hardly to gratify themselves? So man, after all, is the cause of the &ldquo; reckless display of millinery,&rdquo; and its &ldquo; ever-increasing ratio of gorgeousness.&rdquo; Then let man be candid, and not quite so querimonious about what he knows inwardly pleases him. This leads me say a word with regard to the &ldquo;guess &rdquo; as to the &ldquo;trials the good-nature of some papa, brother, or husband has been put to,&rdquo; and the &ldquo; little pleas &rdquo; which extorted the &ldquo; gay wreaths and opera cloaks.&rdquo; All good-natuied papas, brothers, and husbands, too, will agree with me, X am sure, when I say that few things in this life please them better than to see their fair dependants attired &ldquo; fit to be seen&rdquo; and of the fitness of the attire, it would be impertinence not to allow the ladies to be the best judges.,Before I conclude, I must demur most strongly to the introduction of the word &laquo; casino&rdquo; into the article ; it is entirely out of place there &middot; and when the writer plainly says, we cannot fulfil the purposes both of a casino and a class-room, he casts an irr&eacute;missible slur on the character of the danceis, and the natuicd inference is, that we have been giving opportuni&not; ties for, and have revelled in, licentiousness of the worst kind. 0 The asser&not; tion is as false a one as was ever made, and should be as publicly recalled as it has been published. I also demur to his polite hint that dancers are not workers,&mdash; and &ldquo; if not fools, at least silly people,&rdquo;&mdash;dancino&middot; being &ldquo; suited to the meanest capacity.&rdquo; I do not know the author of this last quotation, but peihaps I may be permitted to give the opinions of such a man as Lucian, who says that &ldquo; dancing has been approved by the greatest men m all ages ;&rdquo; and Homer, who thought it a creditable thing in Merton 1 i&auml;t he was a fine dancer, and further says, &ldquo;that the graceful mien and,&Eacute;
great agility which he, Merion, had acquired by that exercise, distinguished him above the rest in the armies* both of Greeks and Trojans.&rdquo; Hesiod too, compares valour and dancing together, and says that &ldquo;the gods have bestowed fortitude on some men, and on others a disposition for dancing.&rdquo; And again, &ldquo; the wise Socrates was not only a professed admirer of this exercise in others, but learned it himself, even when he was an old man.&rdquo; Then, to come to modem times, Addison calls a man who had been chiding his friend for being a lover of dancing, &ldquo; a morose philosopher ;&rdquo; and after saying that he does not think it below the dignity of his &ldquo; spe&not; culations&rdquo; to notice the subject of dancing, says, &ldquo; I am of Mr. Cowley&rsquo;s opinion, that so much of dancing at least as belongs to the behaviour and a handsome carriage of the body, is extremely useful, if not absolutely necessary.&rdquo; But need I go any further to prove that dancing is, in the opinion of many wise men, a desirable accomplishment and a useful acqui&not; sition ? I hope not.,I should now like to go on and discuss the thesis, which I certainly never heard propounded before, namely, that &ldquo; amusement pure and sim&not; ple is never needed by a rational being under any circumstances whatever&rdquo; ! / am certainly &iquest;/rational if this is the case, being prepared to contend for a thesis just the reverse of it, viz, that the mind should now and then be diverted by rational amusements and games of all kinds, that it may return the better to thinking. What is the difference, pray, between brain- sweat and brow-sweat ? Each is work in the fairest and fullest sense of the word. And if the only kind of rest a man is to get is an alternation between one or the other kind of labour, then indeed this life would be but a burden.&middot; It is the fact of having nothing to worry us, nothing to trouble our thoughts, that gives zest to relaxation, and hence it is that the meanest capaciti&ecirc;s as well as the most cultivated minds can alike enjoy the various sports and amusements invented for their pastime. I should like to say more, but do not want the length of this letter to be an excuse for depositing it in the Editor&rsquo;s waste-paper basket, so will conclude by askino&middot; Mr. Litchfield to be assured that I believe he is actuated by the best possible motives in taking up the position he has, and will say to him,,&ldquo; In all thy humours, whether grave or mellow,,Thou&rsquo;rt such a touchy, testy, pleasant fellow ;,Has so much wit, and mirth, and spleen about thee,,There is no liviug with thee, nor without thee.&rdquo;,I am, dear Mr. Editor, yours faithfully,,Thomas Preston.,THE CHOICE OE HEECULES.f,A BIT OF THE WISDOM OF THE ANCIENTS.,When Hercules, leaving boyhood, was entering upon that season of youth, m which the young, becoming their own masters, presently show whether they will enter upon,* A hint for Volunteers, f Freely translated from Xenophon&rsquo;s Hercules was to the ancients the pattern Recollections of Socrates (Book II. c. i.).&mdash; hero of victorious labour. Socrates tells the
life by the path of virtue or vice, he strolled forth one day for pastime, and sat down, at a loss which of the two paths he should take. All at once there appeared coming towards him two tall damsels. One of them was comely to see ; her countenance open ; her form adorned with purity, her eyes with reverence, and her hearing with discretion ; she was clad all in white. The other seemed full-fleshed and luscious with good feeding, her skin embellished to look whiter and redder than in truth it was, and her carriage more lofty than was natural ; her eyes were wide open, and her apparel so chosen that her heyday form might shine well through it ; moreover she glanced often at herself, aud looked to see if any one else was watching her, and often she looked attentively at her own shadow. Now when they came a little nearer Hercules, the first damsel kept on her own way, but the other made eager haste to run up to Hercules, and said :&mdash;&ldquo; I see you, Hercules, at a loss which way of life to take. Now, if you will have me for friend, I will lead you the sweetest and easiest road ; you shall taste everything pleasant, aud go through life untried by any hard things. Thus you shall not trouble yourself with wars or business, but choose at your leisure the food and drink you find agreeable, the things which it shall most please you to see and hear, and smell and touch, the paramours you shall most fancy, how you may sleep most luxuriously ;&mdash;yea, and how you shall enjoy all these things with the least trouble in the world. Moreover, if you should ever sus&not; pect &auml; want of means to obtain these things, there is no fear that I shall lead you to win them by toiling and suffering in body or soul, for you shall use what others earn by labour, without lack of anything you may turn to profit. I give power to those who hold by me to gain from all quarters.&rdquo; Hercules listened to this, and then said, &ldquo; What is your name, Lady?&rdquo; She replied, &ldquo;My friends call me Goodfortune, but those who hate me nickname me Vice.&rdquo; Meanwhile the other damsel came up and said :&mdash;&ldquo; And I am come to you, Hercules, well knowing your parents, and having observed your cha&not; racter in your bringing-up ; whence I hope, if you will take the road to me, that you will become a right good doer of high and noble deeds, and that I shall appear far more honourable and lovely in the sight of good men. I will not dupe you with promises of pleasure, but as the gods have appointed the things that are, so will I ever guide you in truth. For of things that are good and beautiful, the gods give nothing to men without toil and care. If therefore you would have the gods gracious to you, you must pay worship to the gods ; if you would be beloved by friends, you must do good to your friends ; if you wish to be honoured of a State, you must do good service to the State ; if you seek to be admired by all Hellas for manly virtue, you must strive to do good to Hellas. Would you have the earth bear you abundant fruit, you must till the earth ; would you grow rich by herds, you must care for your herds ; would you grow great by war, and win power to deliver your friends and subdue your foes, you must learn the arts of war from those who know them, and practise how to use them well ; and if you would be able in body, you must train your body to obey discretion, and exercise it with labours and sweat.&rdquo; Vice thereupon broke in and said :&mdash;&ldquo; You see, Hercules, how rough and long a road to pleasure this lady would lead you ! But I will take you a short and easy road to good fortune.&rdquo; Then Virtue said :&mdash;&ldquo; Poor wretch ! what good thing have you to give, or what pleasant thing, when you would do nothing for the sake of them ? You do not even wait for the natural desire for sweet things, but before desire comes, fill yourself full of everything,&mdash;eating before you hunger, drinking before you thirst&#39;, procuring cooks in order to eat pleasurably, preparing expensive wines, and running about in summer to find ice in order to drink pleasurably, and in order to sleep pleasurably, preparing not only soft beds but fine coverlets and quilts,&mdash;for you desire sleep, not because of toil, but because you have nothing to do. You are always seeking greedily to satisfy the lusts of the body. And thus you train your friends ; spending the night in revelling, and the most precious part of the day in sleeping. Immortal though you are.,story to one of his disciples, borrowing it from one of the so-called Sophists, then in high repute. Subject only to the characteristic remark of Mr, Maurice, that it may make too much of the praise of men and self-praise, this beautiful tale may stand as a specimen of what the sturdy-hearted Socrates, in a time much like the present, taught the youth of Athens two thousand two hundred years ago. &ldquo; Great; ever fruitful; profit&not; able for reproof, for encouragement, for building up in manful purposes and works, are the words of those who in their day were men.&rdquo;&mdash;V. L.
yon are rejected by the gods and dishonoured by good men. That sweetest of all things to hear, praise of yourself, you can never hear ; and the sweetest of all things to see, you can never see,&mdash;for never yet have you seen a good deed of your own. Who, moreover, would believe you when you speak ? If in need, who would come to your aid ? Or what man of sense would be of your soul ? Your young men are impotent of body, and when they have grown old, senseless in mind : toil-lessly carried smoothly through youth, they have to pass toilsomely and drearily through old-age ; ashamed of what they have done, burdened by what they do, having run through their sweet things in their youth, and put off their hard things to old-age. But I live with the gods, I live with all good men, and without me no noble work of gods or men is done. And I am honoured above all, both by gods and men. To craftsmen I am a dear fellow-worker, to masters of families a faithful guardian, a kindly companion to servants, a hearty sharer in the toils of peace ; a stout comrade in works of war, and the best mate of friendship. My friends have sweet enjoyment of food and drink, and with no anxiety, for they refrain till they naturally desire them. And sleep comes sweeter to them than to those who toil not, and even if they have to give it up, they do not mope or quit what they are called to do. And the young rejoice in the praises of their elders, and the elders exult in the honours of the young, and sweetly they remember their former deeds, and gladly they perform their &quot;daily work, through me being dear to the gods, and beloved by their friends, and honoured by their fellow&#39;-countrymcn. And when the appointed end comes, they lie not dishonoured in forgetfulness, but flourish in the after-time, remembered with honour. Such things, thou sou of noble parents, thou mayest have for brave toil, and w&#39;in the most blessed happiness.&rdquo;,NEWS.,London.&mdash; Rifle Corps&mdash; Lieut.-Colonel Bathurst, late of the Coldstream Guards and of the British German Legion, has accepted the command of the 19th Middlesex, Captain Hughes taking the rank of Major. Tickets, l&iacute;., 2s., and 3s., to be obtained at the head-quarters of the corps, or of any of the ten Companies.,Lancaster. _The &lsquo; Lancaster Observer&rsquo; has the following remarks on the First Report,of the Working Men&rsquo;s Mutual Improvement and Recreation Society :&mdash;&ldquo; From this do&not; cument we learn that the predictions of those who prophesied a speedy end to the Society &lsquo;as soon as the Recreation Field closed,&rsquo;&mdash;have proved false. Not less than &iquest;18 ot those who attended the field in the summer months, have banded together in the winter for mental improvement. These are the men whose interest it has before been impossible to excite, to whom the friends of progress and education in vain addressed themselves. They refused to listen to the voice of the charmer &lsquo; charm he never so wisely. Lec&not; tures were given to them on the advantages of intellectual cultivation ; their employers, many of them, were anxious to pay the necessary subscriptions; they were pressed pub&not; licly&quot; and privately to &lsquo; consult their own interests&rsquo; and join the Mechanics Institution but always with the same result. Now, whatever there may be in this phenomenon (and it is certainly marvellous, aud must have some reasonable explanation), it does not seem that the men are indisposed to make an effort to improve themselves. M hat is there so favourable in the atmosphere of Brock Street which is not found in Market Street ? is the former locality peculiarly beneficial to the mental appetite r Be that as it may, all true friends of the industrial population of Lancaster w&#39;ill be delighted with the results which have been obtained in that place. During the past half-year the receipts ot the Working Men&rsquo;s Society have amounted to &pound;79. 19i. 2d., and including five pounds ( ue from Subscribers, the income at the present rate may be roundly set down at &pound;160 per^ annum The money received has not been thrown away m the purchase ot toys, billiard-boards, or card-tables. But a moderate amount has been expended for recreative purposes ; and a library of above 400 volumes, in this short space, has been collected,
classes have been carried on, a series of lectures are now in the course of delivery, which have, up to the present time, been well attended, and have excited great interest among the members ; a news-room has been maintained, and a most pleasing spirit of mutual help has been developed among the members. Yet these are the men who would have turned everything upside down, we were told, if permitted to assume the reins of power at the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute.,&ldquo; Those who look into the future may each be curious to know the result of the new movement. Will the present state of things continue P Must we have two societies in the town with professedly the same objects in view ? Is there any advantage in the se&not; paration ? Certainly, it is strange, and passing strange, that two or three hundred intel&not; ligent men should prefer being cooped up in a place far too small, with a supply of books quite insufficient, and with many other disadvantages which might easily he enumerated, rather than unite with another establishment, where every facility already exists, or may quickly be provided, tor the pursuit of the objects which they have in view. And what&not; ever may be urged on the other side of the question, aud though we have no intention of renewing the controversy of last autumn, we may yet be permitted to say, that the dogmatic manner in which the propositions for amalgamation were treated, and the paltry motives which were imputed to those who were striving after better things, were suffi&not; cient to create a feeling of disgust which time only can obliterate.,The Report which we are now reviewing is the best answer to the aspersions above referred to. It is encouraging in its character, and shows that there has been good earnest woik done; that there are willing heads to think and hands to labour among the mem&not; bers ; and it also indicates that the society is rapidly gaining the good wishes of those who are able in a pecuniary way to assist in the instruction and improvement of their less fortunate fellow-townsmen.&rdquo;,A private note says:&mdash;&ldquo;We are going on satisfactorily on the whole. We get about twenty veritable sons of toil to the English History Class every Tuesday night, and about fifty or sixty (as many as the room will hold) to the Lectures, by native amateurs, every Thursday. A good Elocution Class, numbering fourteen, has recently been formed ; also a capital Glee Class. But the Reading, Writing, and Arithmetic Classes have lan&not; guished ever since the cold weather set in. The Recreation and the News Rooms are well attended, but the great success has been the Library, from which we have been able to begin lending out, owing to the munificent gift of &pound;10 from Messrs. Storey, Brothers, for hooks. . This has proved a great attraction. One promising fact is that he men have established a Discussion Class, which is kept up with considerable spirit. 1 have long felt convinced of the great value of such a class in an Institution like ours.,has only been started this year, as we had too much on our hands before.&mdash; H. Litchfield.,Fee for Course of Two Terms, 16 lessons, l&iacute;.,New Courses begin in April and October.,The Council has adopted the following Resolutions :,1. The College will be ultimately divided into Six Classes.,2. The First will consist of the general body of merely Matriculated Students.,3*. The Second Class will consist of Certificated Students, or those to whom at the end of any course shall be awarded a Certificate of Competency in the subject of the course.,4. The Third Class will consist of Scholar (in Art, History, Language, Literature, Mathematics, or Physics, etc.), or those who prove themselves competent in any one branch of the College Studies.,5. The Fourth Class will consist of those who are thought worthy to be Associates, or permanent Members of the College. From these we require competency not only in some specific study, but in the most important studies of each department of the College. Each student will have an opportunity of answering questions in those subjects iu which he takes most interest ; but he will be expected to prove that he is acquainted with (1) the Bible History, (2) the History of England, (3) the Principles of English Grammar, and the Elements of (4) some pure Mathematical, and (5) some Physical study, or some branch of applied Mathematics, as Mechanics. The object will be to ascertain that he has the knowledge of those subjects which every person educated in a College ought to have; not to elicit his opinions either in Theology or Politics. The Associate must have attended the College for Eight Terms.,6. The Fifth Class will consist of Fellows : these will be chosen out of the Associates. The Fellows will take part in the education of this College, and may assist in establishing and carrying on other Colleges. They will be chosen by the Council, alter due con&not; sideration of what they have done in the Associate Examination, and o&iacute; what they have been since they entered the College. Moral qualities, a capacity for teaching, and a will&not; ingness to teach, will be at least as indispensable as proficiency in any branch of study.,7. The Sixth Class will consist of the Council. It is intended that the Council be hereafter recruited from the Fellows, to the extent of at least a third of its members. The Council will be prepared to assist in the formation of new Colleges, in London or other parts of England.

APRIL 1, 1861.,A FEW THOUGHTS ABOUT THE FRANCHISE.,To venture upon politics in a magazine devoted to the cause of Educa&not; tion is generally deemed a gross violation of prudence and propriety. To my. mind, it has long been painful to contemplate the absolute necessity which compelled every Literary or Educational Society to inscribe on its very threshold the suggestive prohibition, &ldquo;No politics allowed here. 5 &rsquo; It is as though the members of such societies said, &ldquo;We,meet here to think, to read, to learn, to do all we can to gain a deeper insight into the men and things around, above, beneath us ; we meet not as members of this 01 that party, but simply as men who believe that God&rsquo;s voice is calling us ever onward, bidding us use every faculty in the search for wisdom ; and therefore , because this is our aim, we must exclude politics.&rdquo; Surely this is a humiliating position to occupy. Politics&mdash;all that which concerns me as. a citizen, as a member of a nation&mdash;must be banished, like an evil spirit, to dark and dingy back apartments, where no fresh breeze of air, no pure ray of sunlight can ever penetrate. And I believe the narrow party-spirit of politics alone must bear the blame of this disgrace. We have no political science amongst us ; I mean no systematic study of the laws and principles upon which a true polity must be based, or of the methods by which it must be secured and extended ; and in the absence of this we have unions, leagues, and parties of every shade and hue ; every&not; thing which hides the true nature of politics from our sight, which fosters the notion that education can have and ought to have no possible connec&not; tion with the angry feelings and sectarian strife which politics are so apt to pioduce, and therefore no connection with politics themselves. So far as am aware, the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ivas the first institution o&iacute; the kind that formally recognized the principle that politics should form an essential element in all adult education. Our men come to us with some kind of political creed ready formed : surely any education which is to be of any real service in the daily battle of life must furnish them with some test by which they may examine that creed,&mdash;may see on what prin&not; ciples or on what facts it is based and to what consequences it leads i hope then, I may give my opinion about the Franchise without com- nnttmg the Magazme to any pobtical party; and without weakening that bond of feUowship which unites the teachers and students of our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt; s &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; m one common work. I am no advocate for lowering the franchise ; I believe such a step might do serious harm,-could do no pos&not; sible good I should like to see it extended by what Mr. Bright con-,rt UO v y m &ldquo; es &rdquo; Tn balding the conviction that,ou &pound; ]lt not *0 be reduced, I am open to certain grave charges i the advocates of the proposed reduction invariably assume as true ot all their opponents. They style themselves the friends of the working- classes, thereby assuming that we are their enemies. Nor can T, speaking,VOL. III. &rsquo; 1 **,E
for myself, refute this assumption by the recital of any great deeds proving myself the friend and well-wisher of working men : I can only take shame to myself that I have not known more of them and learnt more from them. But all I have seen and learnt from my experience of our College has strengthened this conviction most deeply. What was previously an opi&not; nion which might derive its strength from prejudice or from ignorance of working men, is now a conviction which I hold the more strongly, because I find it is shared to a very great extent by the working men themselves. They have told me, over and over again, that the &pound;6 franchise would let in a great many good men of their class, but a great many more bad ones. Of course, it is always the fashion of the advocate of a reduction to lavish the most flattering epithets on the class Avhose wrongs they so loudly deplore. The aristocracy may be thankful to be called by no worse name than &ldquo; the tax-consuming &rdquo; class ; the middle class are treated with silent contempt ; but for the working class no string of adulatory adjectives is too long or too unctuous. If flattery is the truest test of friendship, then these politicians are their friends indeed. Which is really the kinder course to take, which the likelier to elevate the working classes : to help them by providence and industry to raise themselves to the enjoyment of the franchise, or to bring the franchise down to them, and thus take away one healthy stimulus to honest exertion ; to give, as a bribe to improvidence and intemperance, the very prize which the &ldquo; honest, intelligent, and in&not; dustrious &rdquo; working man would have earned for himself? While fully admitting the grave sins to which the middle class are so fatally liable, I must assert that improvidence and intemperance are the special sins of the working classes ; these are the wholesale disfranchisers of the great &ldquo; unre&not; presented&rdquo; class; and that improvidence, which in so many cases keeps a man at the very brink of starvation, would be, I believe, the very greatest- hindrance to the right use of the franchise, if possessed. Present advan&not; tages and present relief would be the mightiest argument to gain the votes of such a constituency. Our legislation would be&mdash;nay, it is now&mdash;in danger of becoming, with the growth of democracy, a hand to mouth process, a temporary provision for immediate emergencies, or a yielding to clamour for relief from present burdens.,It is a comparatively easy task to draw upon paper the sketch of an ideal constituency. Those who maintain that the elective franchise is the in&not; alienable right of every man, draw up a plan which one stroke of legislation could&mdash;I earnestly hope, for the sake of all true manhood and liberty, it never wih&mdash;convert into a reality. Every man above twenty-one years of age who is not labouring under legal restraints should have a vote. Those who look upon the franchise as a sacred trust to be used by those who are the most competent to use it wisely and beneficially for the common weal, are all agreed that the proper thing to do is to give it to the right men from all classes. As to who the right men are, there would probably be but little difference of opinion ; the definition would not and ought not to be narrowed by any arbitrary lines of social distinction ; but when we come to the question. How are the right men to be got at P we are met with great difficulties and endless varieties of opinions. That the present franchise of a &pound;10 rental (for simplicity&rsquo;s sake I confine myself to the borough franchise) secures all the competent men and excludes all the in-
competent no man would affirm ; but probably the crudest, most unsatisfac&not; tory way of mending the matter would be simply to lower the qualification from &pound;10 to &pound;6 rental. Some method that is really practicable for admit&not; ting all the working men who really care for the franchise, and who would strive to use it wisely, is imperatively required. I cannot but think the great idea of co-operation, which has been so nobly translated into fact at Rochdale and many other towns in the north, may at least help us to solve this problem. The House of Commons has shown itself by no means unwilling to throiv around the Co-operative Societies the &aelig;gis of legisla&not; tive sanction. No one can doubt that men who have so throughly approved themselves capable of surmounting difficulties, of compelling success to crown their efiorts, would be invaluable additions to any constituency ; but I, for one, very seriously doubt whether it would be wise for the country&rsquo;s interests or kind to those very men to open a door by which not only they, but vast crowds of those who have proved themselves in every possible way totally unfit, may swell the list of our &ldquo; free and indepen&not; dent &rdquo; electors. Having no statistical evidence on the point, I am strongly inclined to think that many of the Rochdale co-operatives have already solved this problem for themselves in the most satisfactory way ; they have already gained the franchise. The so-called political friends of the work&not; ing classes delight to refer to the Rochdale co-operatives, and, by way of demonstrating the gross injustice done to them by our present represen&not; tative system, they attribute to them almost every virtue under the sun, except that of being able to raise themselves to the possession of the fran&not; chise. I think facts would demonstrate the possession of this virtue also. But any rental franchise will always exclude very many deserving men of all classes, and any well-considered and practical scheme for including these men would be deserving of the best attention. Tories, Whigs, Radi cals, all are agreed that any men who prove themselves really men by their habits of order, obedience, government, must possess the suffrage ; that by their addition the constituencies would be strengthened and purified. A large majority of the country, I fully believe, entertain very serious doubts whether the best way to gain such an addition would be by the in&not; discriminate reduction of the franchise from a &pound;10 to a &pound;6 rental. To speak of giving every male above twenty-one years of age a vote as &ldquo; man&not; hood suffrage. is simply a gross prostitution of the word manhood. It is the enthronization of mere power and numbers in the place of real man&not; hood ; the consecration of ignorance and passion .as the rightful lords of humanity.,. Iť is a terrible conclusion to arrive at, that the highest results of poli&not; tical science are to be aimed at by a process of simple addition, a mere counting of heads. If we have learnt nothing more in our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, surely we have learnt to form a much deeper, more awful esti&not; mate of the sacredness of human worth than that. As a teacher in one of these Colleges, I have hastily penned these thoughts about a subiect which is occupying the attention of many working men. I know many will differ from me, but I also know that some will agree with me I have no intention of turning our Magazine into a vehicle for expressing any particular opinions, nor have I any intention of continuing the subject. 1 simuld like to hear one of our students on this question, and I should,E 3
be none the less pleased if he opposed all my conclusions, and converted me from the error of my ways. The question is an important one on which surely we are bound to speak openly and without fear.,Wolverhampton. J. N. LANGLEY.,ON THE STUDY OF PHYSICAL SCIENCE.,The last number of this Magazine contained a statistical table, from which may be gathered an estimate of the general feeling of the students of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; as to the relative importance of the various subjects of study which challenge their attention. The statement of the class-attendances at the London College for the year 1860, will be found on examination to differ little, if at all, from those given in previous Re&not; ports, and may, therefore, fairly be taken to represent the result of several years&rsquo; experience. The information thus given is important enough to deserve a detailed examination, the various points of which would open general questions of the highest interest. Of such questions 1 propose now to discuss one only, feeling sure that it is at least as important as any which can be raised in connection with the subject of College studies. Among 394 students whose entries were registered during the first term of the present year, the numbers attending the various classes were these :&mdash; French, 79; Drawing, 53; English Grammar, 37; Latin, 34; Book&not; keeping, 35 ; Arithmetic, 33 ; Geometry, 30 ; Algebra, 16 ; English Com&not; position, 14 ; History of England, 13 ; English Literature, 10 ; Geology, 7. Different people will gather different impressions from these figures, but I suppose that no one will pretend that they represent a satisfactory state of things. At the foundation of the College, and at intervals from time to time, its founders and teachers have, in more or less definite forms, given their impressions as to the relative importance of each study, the general drift of which will be best described by saying that the table just quoted, read backwards, would nearly represent the realization of our ideas. The subjects which do not appear at all (and this chiefly because they are never asked for, or by so few that classes can only be formed at intervals), such, for instance, as Political Science, are those which we are always saying should occupy the foremost place in a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. Natural Science and History, the two last on the list, are what we have been constantly urging as the.next in importance ; while the study of language, which seems to sweep into the French and Latin Classes about a third part of our entire number, is that which we have done least to encourage. To this pleasant rule of inversion there stands, indeed, one happy excep&not; tion. The Art department comes out second on the list. But even this small consolation is a little weakened by the recollection of the special causes which have made this the most effective and significant branch of the College work. We know that we owe this result in great measure to a personal cause ; to the presence of one who has now for several years been devoting to the regular weekly task of teaching a class of working men to draw, that same genius and energy which has left its mark on our generation in the shape of a great Art-revival. But the contradiction, violent as it is, between the hope and the reality, is no proof that our esti-
mate of the chief uses of a College and of what its teaching ought to be, was a wrong one. On the contrary, if any one says, &ldquo; The facts are against you,&rdquo; I am ready to reply in all seriousness with the French phi&not; losopher, &ldquo; So much the worse for the facts ! &rdquo; It is not experience that is against us, but want of experience.,That out of 394 persons presumed to be rational Englishmen and to be bent on doing themselves some good, 79 thought it desirable to learn French and 34 Latin, while only 7 cared to know anything of the world they live in, and only 13 exhibited any interest in the history of their country, seems to me the strongest argument for the existence of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. But it shows that our destiny is to be a &ldquo; Col&not; lege Militant.&rdquo; If we cannot alter the eternal law of human nature, by which men like better to follow blindly a traditionary custom and conven&not; tion than to ask themselves the reason for what they are about ; we can at least do some little towards establishing another custom more intrinsically rational, and adapted to the real demands of our time. Most of all, we should never cease to urge our students to think for themselves on the first ques&not; tion, What to learn ? Some, no doubt, of the crowds who flock to the Latin and French class-rooms would give, if asked, an intelligent reason foi their choice. But it is quite certain that the great majority would say, if they answered honestly, &ldquo; Oh, we understand that &lsquo; educated people,&rsquo; so called, learn those tongues, and so we must learn them too. They are, in fact, the &lsquo; correct thing.&rsquo; &rdquo; The system of laying out re&not; gular courses of reading for our students, telling them what to learn, and how much, would perhaps be in the long-run less healthy than the present regime of absolute freedom. Perhaps, too, the experience of other Colleges, which have always adopted some such plan, is not altogether conclusive in its favour. But at any rate the liberty we allow on this point entitles us to insist the more strongly on the necessity of our students settling seriously for themselves those questions which* in most Colleges they would find settled for them. &ldquo; Do what you like,&rdquo; we should say to every man, t&pound; but be certain that you have a reason for doing it. And not only a reason, but a better reason for doing that than anything else.&rdquo; It may be very convenient, for instance, to be able to read French books, to understand scraps of French dialogue in a play or a novel, and to be able to sputter a few words to a Palais-Royal waiter, during a week&rsquo;s trip to Pans ; it may be also very useful to go through the mental discipline incident to studying a foreign tongue; but what you have to consider is not merely the desirableness of these objects, but whether they are worth the devotion of long and precious hours of study, and whether they are positively more valuable when attained, than any other results which could be achieved with the same outlay of time and trouble. A decision on these points can of course only be approximately sound; for no one can iudg-e lully of the merits of what they only know a little about. But still ^a rational man can ascertain pretty well the bearings and purpose of a study without going very deeply into it, and thus gather sufficient materials for an intelligent comparison of the value of various pursuits. For the ques&not; tion, be it observed (and this is the point most often forgotten), is always one ot comparison. No one denies that any employment of the intellect, on any subject, touching man&rsquo;s life however remotely, must be capable of
serving some good end. The problem which every man has to solve, and most of all every &ldquo; working man,&rdquo; is, &ldquo; How can I make the most of the very few hours I can spare in the course of a year ? Whąt study will put most fresh interest into my life ?&mdash;which opens the largest field of thought ? &mdash;which bears most closely on the great questions which are stirring the world, on the social and political life of the time ?&rdquo; To all these queries there should be an answer, or an attempt at an answer. As applied to certain subjects, some of them have already been discussed in these pages. Some&not; thing has been said about Mathematice and the study of Language; something, too, about one great branch of Physical Science, Chemistry. But the general neglect of the study of nature in our College seems to me, after making all allowances, quite a monstrous anomaly. We may plead indeed the examples of other and older Colleges. Physical Science was almost as much ignored at Oxford, till a few years back, as if Bacon and Newton had never existed. It was assumed that the education of an Englishman was to be framed on a model established twenty centuries ago in Greece. Becauşe Aristotle was an immortal genius, the human intellect was to be treated just as if it had been stationary since the time when he wrote his Logic. Cambridge again, though she was the nursing mother of modern science two centuries ago, has devoted all her rewards and honours to the encouragement of one branch of it, neglecting almost entirely the extra-mathematical parts of the investigation of Nature. But a Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College had no need to be bound by these traditions. It was our business to adopt a scheme of education based on the study of those subjects which are found to be the instruments best adapted for the purpose : to show that Physical Science is one of these, ought to be a quite superfluous task. To prove it at large would be to repeat a string of truisms. It is, in fact, one of those points which is not so much to be enforced by argu&not; ment, as by that instinctive perception of its reasonableness which comes into the mind when once the matter is attentively considered. We may have very various notions as to the chief use of cultivating our minds, but none caii help seeing that the very alpha and omega of study must be the ascertainment of truth in some shape or other. Even if we think more of action than of knowledge as the end of &ldquo; training,&rdquo; we must see that right action can only follow on right knowledge. Let us than ask the question : Where has mankind as yet succeeded in getting any true knowledge ? Where is there a great accumulated store of ascertained fact? Where does the result of human thought and labour appear in the shape of a solid indestructible mass of positive truth ? The answer is in Physical Science, and as yet, we may almost say, there alone. Are we not obliged to con&not; fess that outside of the domain of Science, the world of human thought ip at present little better than a chaos ? What has Metaphysics ever been but an incessant conflict among elementary theories ? every fresh thinker spending his life in discovering and establishing a new starting-point, to be thrown aside by his successor, perhaps by his disciple. The labour of hundreds of acute intellects for two thousand years in this field of thought, has not produced a single proposition which has commanded the assent of mankind. What even has history done towards making itself a science ? Who can point to a single well-ascertained law by which we dare say what will follow on a given conjuncture of political events? And as to Theo-
never was it more evident than at the present moment, that the re&not; ligious beliefs of the world are trembling on the border of some great con&not; vulsion. The hitherto unspeculative middle-classes of the country are now standing aghast at a discovery which devout people had made some time back, A mere literary accident has revealed to the Mudie-reading public, the fact that some of the greatest minds in England, some of the most honoured of her public servants, are deliberately occupied in tearing to pieces the groundwork of the national faith, while the united force of authority is concentrated not on the defence of that faith, but on the at&not; tempt to expel from the Establishment the demon of free inquiry. This is not the place to speculate on &iacute;vhat is to come of these internecine struggles. I allude to them merely as an illustration of the state of confusion in which the thinking world is plunged, in all directions but one. For side by side with all this scene of tumult and destruction, there lies that great field of human thought, the investigation of the laws governing the material universe, in which the mind finds lying before it a clear and open track unclouded by storm and mist, revealing everywhere new mysteries of order, and new beauties of never-broken harmony ; and pointing always towards the one central idea of supreme and unchangeable law. It is in this field surely that we must look for the training-ground of the intellect. For even supposing the results of Physical Science to be unimportant, its Method at any rate is at present the only existing type of the process by which truth is to be reached. Setting aside Political Economy,* there is no class of phenomena in dealing with which any approximation has been made to the discovery of scientific laws. Whether the so-called Baconian method can be applied to unravel the complex facts brought about by the action of human wills is just the great problem of the day. For myself, I believe, that there is every ground for a reasonable hope, if not a positive con&not; viction, that the simple canons of Inductive Science, &ldquo; methodized common sense, &rsquo; as they have well been called, will prove in time adequate to the sug&not; gestion and discovery of lavys guiding the history of nations and the march of civilization. But even if this be a chimera, there still remains that considera- ion, of itself sufficient to make Physical Science the greatest of educational instruments : namely, that Comparison and Observation of historical and social facts must be the source of whatever knowledge we can gain about them, and that if so, the surest way to learn to observe and compare rightly is to practise, as it were, on those subjects in which this practical logffi has icen applied with the greatest success. Regarding Physical Science in this aspect, as the great teacher of the art of sound inference , we should have to consider further which department would serve our purpose best Each branch would be found to be, by virtue of some peculiarity in its method, valuable as an illustration of some particular part of the scientific method. Astronomy, for instance, is the Science of Observation and De&not; duction ; Chemistry is the Science of Experiment ; Botany and Zoology,* В is hard for a teacher in our College to mention this subject without some allu&not; mo to the vehement onslaught lately made hy Mr. Ruskin on the Economists But I can only say here, that in common with many who think his viewsThZm lifľ&Auml;se and noble I entirely reject his estimate of the profession and performances of the science ; and that what he has written contains nothing to disturb my conviction that it ought to stand foremośt among the studies of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.
the Sciences of Classification, and so on. The study of Geology has signal advantages which give it perhaps the greatest right to take the lead among the scientific studies of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. This I hope to be able to show in a continuation of this paper. It would be sufficient for our purpose to rest the claim of Physical Science on the pleas I have thus briefly urged. They are, however, immensely strengthened by two other considerations. We may appeal to the immense interest which men are practically found to feel in the investigation of the facts of nature when they have once been induced to take up the pursuit, and to the immense part which this investigation of nature has played and is yet to play in the general history of human progress. Without puzzling ourselves over the abstract question, What is the use of knowledge ? it is enough to know and to feel that an examination of the mysterious laws which govern the world about us, does, as a matter of fact, become to those who try it, a source of the purest and most ennobling pleasure. It is not merely not provable, but it is positively untrue, that Science and Poetry are at war ; that the sense of beauty depends upon ignorance, and is destroyed by knowledge. Does not a flower or a leaf become ten times more beautiful when you are shown all the subtle loveliness of its structure as well as the play of light and colour on its surface ? Does the beauty of the rainbow vanish when you are taught to see in its blended tints the action of the same law which tinges the film of the soap-bubble and gives lustre to the opal ? Must it not be a shallow and sentimental feeling which contradicts itself by saying the world is very beautiful ; but the more know of it the more ugly it will look ? It is a common thing again, to say that Science destroys Reverence, that it makes the mind impatient of mystery, and so dulls the spiritual element in our nature. This, again, is not merely not true, but the very reverse of the truth. It is the scientific mind whose at&not; titude is most habitually one of reverence ; meaning by reverence, not a trembling horror of the unknown, but a deep feeling of the awfulness and the mystery of the great world, of which we are part, a consciousness of man&rsquo;s being but a point in the midst of fathomless Infinities. One finds, indeed, among the students of nature, a certain decent consciousness of the seriousness of things which it is almost impossible to meet with elsewhere. Listen to a geologist discussing the position of a stratum, or an astro&not; nomer giving the details of an observation, and you will see an almost nervous horror of outstepping the bounds of legitimate inference, an ex&not; treme care in distinguishing between hypothesis and proof. But go, for instance, into a church, or take up a Theological review, and you will find a man knocking off a theory of the Universe (as he would have made it) in the space of twenty minutes&rsquo; discourse, or ten columns of print, with the most delightful complacency, disposing of half-a-dozen mysteries in every sentence, and denouncing as a moral offence any doubt you may have of the soundness of his views. This is the sort of irreverence from which science teaches a man to revolt. Slowly, though surely, as the student gathers his knowledge, weighing fact against fact, testing hypothesis by experiment, rejecting plausible fancies when contradicted by crucial in&not; stances, there gradually grows upon him a deep conviction of the enor&not; mous difference between the positive and the speculative, the possible and the real. Mysteries he has to handle, and in plenty ; he sees hundreds of
processes going on, of the reality of which he cannot doubt, but the origin o&iacute; which he cannot fathom ; he has incessantly forced upon him the conviction that he can but study the action of one thing upon another, that the &ldquo; unknown basis of known phenomena 55 is something which he can never grasp. Instead, therefore, of being, as we are told, impatient of mysteries, he in fact has learnt to face them : but he refuses, and this no doubt is the meaning of the vulgar accusation, to believe anything which he is told on the single plea that it must be true because it is unin&not; telligible. He says, in &ldquo; effect,&rdquo; show me that this is true and I will be&not; lieve it, even though I cannot understand it ; but I decline to believe it till I am shown a reason for thinking it true, even though you allege its unintelligibility as a reason for believing it.&rdquo;,Lastly, I would urge that the place which Physical Science has occupied in the intellectual and political history of the world should give it addi&not; tional interest in the eyes of working men. They cannot afford to neglect that which is more than anything else the power that now rules the world. It is by steady additions to the general stock of the world&rsquo;s knowledge that its stock of power is increased. Man&rsquo;s command over nature is in direct proportion to the amount of this knowledge, which bears not only on the satisfaction of his material wants, but upon the moral, social, and political advancement of the race. It is needless to stop to prove&rsquo;this. Indirectly, again, Science has been of vast service to the cause of pro&not; gress by being always the great witness for the right of intellectual free&not; dom ; the great foe of all tyranny, and most of all of the tyranny of au&not; thority over opinion.* It may be safely said that but for Science, the doctrine of Toleration would never have been accepted by the world. Toleration has sprung from the reaction of a strong mass of enlightened opinion standing apart from the profession of any creed.f No religious sect as such has ever yet adopted it. The present age, advanced as it is, cannot afford to dispense with any available aid towards keeping down the still powerful spirit of religious persecution. Considering the preten&not; sions of our generation, the penalties on opinion still retained, or only just i emitted, are as disgraceful as was the witch-burning of our &deg;reat- grandfathers. It will no doubt seem just as monstrous to our great-grand&not; children to be told that we refused to admit certain persons to Parliament because their ancestors came from Palestine, that the most distinguished students of our National Universities ivere denied its highest rewards, be&not; cause they held peculiar views on church government; that the rillit to obtain justice against a thief was forfeited by any man who could not de- clare his belief in the doctrine of everlasting punishment ; that we let millions of children grow up as untaught savages, because we could not agree upon what theological principles to base the teaching 0 f the alphabet. Against all these things science is a living protest, because it familiarizes the mind with the purest aspects of truth, and a kind of truth,+ g * &iacute;ľ P C &igrave; a T &deg;- To&quot;&trade; tlmeS &rsquo; the first French Revolution, is distinctly traceable to the progress of Physical Science. So, too, in the real movement of етап У cipa ion now in progress what has made the ultimate freedom of Italy certain is, that the united mte ligence of Europe is arrayed against her late rulers and chiefly on account of the inexpiable crime of deliberately repressing the intellectual life of the nation. which is unaffected by the arbitrariness of human judgment : and above all, because it makes men liate all falsehood, and therefore all attempts to falsify the profession of opinion by operating on the will. The more the subject is considered, the more evident it will appear that the progress of science is bound up in a hundred ways with the progress of social improve&not; ment. R. B. Furnivall, Esq., M.A., Trinity Hall, Cambridge.,Three Passed with Credit : no Certificate granted.,REVIEWS.,Poetry for School and Home, from the best Authors. Edited by Thomas Shorteb,,Secretary of the (London) &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. London: Allman. ls.6d.,The editor of this pleasant little book says that poetry for school and home &ldquo; need neither be frivolous nor insipid.&rdquo; He might more truly have said, that to be popular it cannot be either. Frivolous and insipid poetry (if such a contradiction were possible) is to be found by scholars and homefolk only in the girls&rsquo; albums, or the corner of the weekly newspaper : and even thence it is slowly disappearing. There are not many penny journals, not many working men&rsquo;s homes, by this time, which do not know Long&not; fellow&rsquo;s verses, and the &ldquo; Three Fishers,&rdquo; and the &ldquo; Sands of Dee.&rdquo; Years ago, in a country village, I knew a crippled old sexton who treasured &ldquo;The Reaper and the Flowers,&rdquo; in his dog-eared album ; and I have seen two errand-like boys reading &ldquo; The Burial of Sir John Moore &rdquo; to each other by the riverside. We are a long way, still, behind the Scotch in this matter ; but we have learnt something, in the last few years&mdash; perhaps because we have had something given us to learn.,This little book contains a good deal of that something,&mdash;of those heartier and more earnest poems which have been written since men began to find out that life was neither beer and skittles on the one hand, nor moonshine and melodrama on the other. It in&not; cludes some of the best and simplest songs of Kingsley and of Longfellow; and its selection from Wordsworth and from Tennyson is judicious, though not, of course, ex&not; haustive. It embalms, too, here and there, rare bits by men who seldom speak in verse, such as Carlyle&rsquo;s &ldquo;Dawning Day;&rdquo; and rescues from the desert air several flowers that well become an English garden, where the editor&rsquo;s tender &ldquo; Sunshine and Shadows,&rdquo; for instance, are by no means out of place. One could wish, indeed, that Mr. Shorter had kept firmer hold throughout of the true principle with which he started, that poetry
read iu youth should be the best poetry of its kind, because its impression on us is the deepest and most lasting : then, perhaps, he would have drained off a few Swains and Bennetts, and given us instead things of stronger body and clearer spirit&mdash;given us more of Vaughan or Herbert, of old Herrick or of Hood. But it is doubtless as hard to edit a perfect selection of poems for school and home, as it would be to say in few words what such a selection ought to be. Looking at the tremendous issues of those years from ten to twenty, at the complex development of the human spirit therein, and the vast im&not; portance of the choice of influences for that development, one may well doubt How best to feed the strongest of those influences, imagination.,Speaking roughly, however, we may at least say, that poems for the young and the homely should be full of reverence ; of love of country, in both senses of that word ; of manly vigour and picturesque humour for the boys, and of sincere and artless pathos for the girls -or rather, for the best of either sex. Tried by these tests, Mr. Shorter&rsquo;s volume not only does not fail, but comes out, in spite of some defects (as they seem to us) of judgment, a worthy and helpful handbook for playground or for fireside. A. J. Price 2d.,None who know Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s writings will need to be told, that this sermon contains many weighty thoughts and wise cautions for Volunteers. Its object is &ldquo;tell, in a few plain words, to what cause the movement is to be attributed, what it means, 5 what will promote or destroy it, what obligations lie upon those who have taken part in it. &rsquo; The following passage is on the relations of the Volunteer force to a standine army ;&mdash; 0,. &ldquo; The feeling has been deepening in all minds, that if we want men whose profession is to be soldiers, we want something continually to remind them that they are not only soldiers, but citizens ; that they are not only men paid for defending our hearths and homes, but that they have hearths and homes of their own to defend. In numbers of our officers and privates both, that belief is alive ; they are, in the truest sense, sous and lovers of the soil. But there is&mdash;there always must be&mdash;a peril of every business de&not; generating into a trade ; there is infinite peril to us all if this business of fighting de&not; generates into a trade.. There is no security for any institution of the land, none for the morality of the land, if it does. The counteraction&mdash;so far as we can see the only counteraction&mdash;to this danger lies in the existence of a bodv of citizen soldiers side by side with the regular soldiery. You must knit together al&igrave; the different classes in the Janci. You must teach all how much greater and nobler it is to be Englishmen than to be any special section of Englishmen. This is a good which we have a right to hope from all the volunteer corps. But I must tell those of you who are here this afternoon that we expect it especially of you. _ You have from the first appealed to the working men of the land for their co-operation. You are set among us to prove that it is not property only or chiefly which gives men a stake in the country; that fathers and mothers, brothers and sisters, the places in which you have grown up, or in which you have worked in which you have formed friendships, in which you have learnt Inv wisdom, bind you to the laud, and pledge you to deteud it as much as it you were poi sessed of thousands of acres, or heaps of gold.&rdquo; J 1,The Bahan Revolution of I860. &Auml; Lecture delivered at the Manchester Workinn Mens CoUege, February 1, 1861. J. A.,Wh March, 1861.,[There must have been countless reviews of Tennyson&rsquo;s poems, among which pro&not; bably some of the earliest would be the most interesting. That in the &pound; Westminster/ on the appearance of the first edition of the * Poems,&rsquo; was remarkable as expressing a decisive opinion that the then unknown writer was destined to take the first place among the poets of the age. The &pound; Times * review of &lsquo; In Memoriam &rsquo; is memorable as a happy specimen of the blundering insolence of a too-clever critic who had not even wit to adapt his verdict to the probable opinion of his readers, in accordance with the usual policy of the paper. The shallow impertinence of this review was most agreeably exposed in oue of those two delightful lectures by Frederick Robertson of Brighton, on the &ldquo;Influence of Poetry on the Working Classes.&rdquo; (The volume, by the way, of &pound; Lectures and Addresses &rsquo; is one which should occupy a prominent place on the shelves of every working man&rsquo;s library.) But by far the best thing written, so far as I know, on Tennyson&rsquo;s poetry, is the paper which the late George Brinsley contributed to the Cambridge Essays. It would be difficult to quote a specimen of more healthy, dis&not; criminating, and sympathetic criticism. The poet himself was, I believe, much pleased wdth it. It may be as well to add that the volume of &pound; Remains,&rsquo; in which this essay is reprinted, contains also an excellent biographical and critical sketch of Wordsworth. In the second volume of the &lsquo; Christian Socialist &rsquo; are to be found some papers by Gerald Massey on Tennyson&rsquo;s poems. Papers on great books or on great authors would be, as you say, an excel&not; lent feature in this Magazine : any such contributions will be welcome to its pages.&mdash; Ed.],NEWS.,Cambridge. &mdash; A soir&eacute;e of members of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; and of the 8th Cambridgeshire Volunteers (the first company of which corps originated with the College) was held in the Town Hall, on 28th February. The evening is reported in the &pound; Cambridge Independent Press &rsquo; to have been a most agreeable gathering, the Programme including the musical artistes and other elements of a conversazione. Among the addresses given was one from Professor Kingsley. On ascending the platform he was greeted with a round of cheers, which were frequently renewed in the course of his speech. In his prefatory remarks he descanted upon what would be the feelings and thoughts of a person looking upon such an assembly as that : they would naturally be those which accorded with his profession, each tending to that course to which he was most addicted to follow. The politician would look upon the bringing about of this movement with the view of in&not; quiring how it would affect the balance of power in Europe, or the fortunes of foreign States. The man of social science would view it as affecting the eternal welfare of the nation, and would look upon that meeting of Volunteers and the movement, as one of the most wholesome ways to bring class and class together, of letting all shades of society join together in displaying their loyalty for the same Queen and the same country. The Professor considered the movement well suited to set aside all those differences w hich had
disgraced England ; although he did not think differences of politics had done any harm in England, because differences of opinion were the best means, when the truth was dis&not; covered, of causing all parties to work together for the good of the country, and therefore it was that he thought it would not be well for a single party to govern, but rather that differences of political opinion should exist. It was, however, clear that something was required to unite us, and we found that in these Volunteer Corps. He spoke from guess, in regard to Cambridge ; but he knew what they were in his own county, and what they were in London ; and he knew enough of the good feeling in Cambridge, to be sure that they were doing and carrying out this movement here with the same good spirit as they were elsewhere. But if he were a soldier, he knew how he should look upon it : he should love every man, work with him, and honour him. He was convinced of what the soldiers themselves thought of these Volunteer Corps : they respect them; they admire them. The officers of her Majesty&rsquo;s service, he knew, including the oldest of them, in&not; stead of looking upon the movement with contempt, they were ready to help it with all their power. They looked upon it in the spirit of brotherhood, as their younger and in&not; experienced brother, as of course a volunteer must be ; but sure he was, they would be delighted to hear, if they could, the general feeling of her Majesty&rsquo;s line&mdash;that of her fine Rifles as well as her Footguards. They would be pleased to hear the free expression of the British army in reference to this movement ; and therefore what an English soldier could say, looking upon his brother, would be, that, some day, he hoped to stand by his side on some glorious field, with the certain assurance that the Volunteer would do as well as the soldier. But he (the Professor) must look upon this movement as a scholar and in that were brought to his mind the memories of old readings and old books He then reviewed the customs of ancient times, some fifteen hundred years ago, when our forefathers held it as a sacred right to carry arms. At that time, the Saxon and the Anglo-Saxon were not looked upon as men if they could not use weapons and had not a sword by their side. _ The two great things recognized, and regarded as a right by the English, from time immemorial, were freedom of speech, and the right to carry arms ihe Professor went into the history of the latter custom (when every man was bound t&oacute; rise as an armed man), and after dwelling upon the proper and improper use of arms ex&not; pressed himself gratified at seeing the old right retained and revived in the present day &middot; but it was in a truer and more peaceful form. He showed that the principle of Volun- teers had been exemplified in the perfect military organization over the whole kingdom which had existed for so mauy centuries.
