Wood, Geography, 3rd Class.,ON LEARNING GEOLOGY,,By Павлу Seelet.,Few men know what learning Geology means : until they know that, methodical study is impossible. The method must be deduced from the matter. Ihe matter must be allowed to consist of all its parts. Until these are considered in their mutual relations, it is impossible there can be any profitable study. This is but another way of putting an obvious truism, &ldquo; &icirc; ou cannot do a thing until you know what is to be done.&rdquo;,The nostrums prepared by a pseudo-geologist may be swallowed, and do great good ; but they are wholly incapable of producing the results they profess. They give no idea of the real nature of Geology, and but a glim&not; mering guide to one of its parts. Even the splendid compilations of Lyell have in a less degree the same defects. They are not manuals of Geology, but only introductory sketches of some of its parts. From this describing a part as the whole, the student fancies that when he has read the Manual, he has read the whole thing ; whereas he has only become acquainted with the conclusions, and knows nothing of the means by which he may test them, or follow them into further detail. Thus it will be seen there are two kinds or degrees of geological knowledge : the one takes a mere surface glimpse at results, accepting everything as incontrovertible ; the other reads the whole thing. In the one case, Geology is an amusement ; in the other, it is a very grand study. Students must determine which they think the more desirable. The Cambridge University Examiners are prepared to certify that a man is distinguished for geological knowledge, if he is acquainted with so much of the subject as will enable him to take an intelligent interest in it. He is at once dubbed a &ldquo; great geologist,&rdquo; and probably becomes a private tutor ! The more I see of the working of this superficial system, the more objectionable does it seem : mental weak&not; ness should not be permitted to pass for mental strength. It is to be hoped that the present race of students will protest against it, as did that which preceded it. &ldquo; Whatever is worth doing, is worth doing well.&rdquo; If we do not do the thing well, we must join the great army of quacks, and be per&not; suaded, or persuade ourselves and others, that we know a great deal, when in truth we are veritable humbugs. I can see no other alternative. It is as though one went into a factory, and in the one case, saw the finished work and the tools ; and in the other, as though he remained there till he became a workman. A man ought to stick to a thing till he has mastered it ; and when it is mastered, he will be able to assist in perfecting it. This is the kind of work best suited to the English character.,I do not wish to be understood to say, that years must be given up to reading, before you can enjoy the delights of the thing; but only that students should know what they are about, and then work methodically
and thoroughly. A little piece of sound knowledge, will bring with it far more pleasure than the most extensive smattering.,Let me now indicate what Geology really is, and how it may be acquired. Geology then consists of two parts : the one, systematic Geology, having for its subject-matter the nature, changes, tenants, and relations of rocks ; the other, applied Geology, which deals with the causes of the different oc&not; cupations of men in different localities, and thus with their mental charac&not; ters. It also indicates the cause of commerce, of the rise of cities, of the limits of language, of the boundaries of nations, etc., and, in short, acts either directly or indirectly upon most things which share our thoughts. It is only with the first part that the general student is likely to be able to deal : at all events, it is with this he must begin ; and, followed into all its details, here alone is work enough for a lifetime. The way to begin is un&not; mistakably suggested by the subject. The essence of Geology consists in the changes and tenants of rocks. Consequently the way to begin is to learn of what rocks consist. I do not mean by this, that the whole subject of mineralogy should be studied. It is quite enough, on commencing, to confine yourself to the few minerals occurring in your own district. Unless this knowledge is gained, all that can be known about the nature of rocks, will be dependent upon the authority of books, in which the student must blindly believe.,Now haying got a groundwork , it will be clear that the foundation of Geology exists in the changes this may undergo : firstly, in its forms ; se&not; condly, in its tenants. Looked at in the quarry, both are equally unintel&not; ligible. They are only to be understood by tracing the present back into the past. These two principles of Geology are respectively named &ldquo; Ter&not; restrial Physics and Physical Geography,&rdquo; and &ldquo; Biology.&rdquo; The one deals with the changes and causes of change in the earth around us ; the other, with the existence and conditions of existence of the life around us. With&not; out being familiar with all the varied means by which rocks are now being formed, it will be impossible to understand the causes of the structures to be seen in the quarry. Without a familiarity with the life now existing in the waters and above them, it will be impossible to interpret the meaning of those relics which indicate the life of ages ago. This is the preliminary training that must be gone through by every one who would become a geologist : without it, you only believe ; with it, you know. But do not suppose it is necessary to go through it all before any of it can be applied. The true principle is to leam what is wanted, as it is wanted. And be assured that the rocks will never be so exacting as to need it all at once. The application of this knowledge to discovering the past history of this little Sphere, is the object of our study. The things actually found out form the first contribution to this grand work ; they are that portion of systematic Geology, a sketch of which is the only part of the study com&not; monly taught. J,dhis then, so fai as it can be said in a few words, is the character of the elementary part of Geological Science. If the sections of it are sepa&not; rated, the student of fossils becomes a Pal&aelig;ontologist ; the student of rocks a Physical Geologist. Neither is what I have defined as a Geologist. One has to elucidate the work of the other. A student of one part o&deg;nly, is comparatively as helpless as a man with one side paralyzed. It is only
the combined knowledge which in the present stage of Geology is likely to permanently benefit it. From the birth of Geology, a combination of two parts, then essential, has been becoming more and more necessary. There can be no Geology without it.,A knowledge of this vast subject is, happily, not to be acquired without great labour. Happily, I say, because, the more labour, the more love. It is a fallacy to suppose the extent of a subject will deter a thinking mind. It no more requires a stout heart to overcome great difficulties, than it does to enjoy the delights which are thus gained. Whatever we read deeply, grows deeply into our lives, bearing precious inward fruit. But it is a special glory of Geology, that, in addition to this character, which it shares with all other studies, it blossoms as it is sown, it finits as it springs up. The smallest piece of real geological knowledge will ever be finding out new pleasures. The earth only appears truly lovely when we know it truly. Ileal poetry is deep truth. To one who has felt this fascination of Geology, obstacles are as little thought of as though they did not exist ; he would, if need were, wade through them to the world&rsquo;s end.,Now as to the method. This I have in a general way already indicated ; logically follow the matter. Obviously no man can study the rocks of the whole world at once ; he can only become familiar with what he can see. Hence to the student, logically following the subject will not only mean first knowing the nature of rocks, then how they may be formed and changed, next the life-remains they may contain, and finally their whole history ; but it will also indicate that these sections should have a relative importance in our reading, corresponding to that which they have in the district of our first investigations. Thus in the country of crystalline rocks, a knowledge of mineralogy would be required, such as would be wholly useless in a tertiary or cretaceous district ; while in a tertiary loca&not; lity, the mammalia would demand a degree of attention corresponding to that given to the reptiles of the secondary rocks. This is the only royal road to Geology. One district should be mastered , and then, as opportunity presents, our acquaintance should be extended to others. After the first general principles are acquired, the student will discard books and endea&not; vour to work out everything for himself ; when he turns to books again, they will be books of details.,In order to make the beginning I indicate, written guidance is of course necessary, and with it oral teaching will be a great advantage. By far the nearest approach to a logical outline of the whole subject, is Professor Jukes&rsquo;s little manual. Lyell&rsquo;s &lsquo; Principles &rsquo; will be the best guide to the Terrestrial Physics section. Many other books treat of portions of this subject in greater detail, and admirably. The student will have no diffi&not; culty in finding them out when they are wanted. In reading Lyell, every&not; thing where possible should be verified by actual observation. A very pleasant outline-sketch of this part of the subject will be found in Professor Jukes&rsquo;s &lsquo;Physical Geology.&rsquo; The other branch of the subject, Palaeontology, is far more extensive, far more difficult, and far more delightful. The only work which can be named as a guide, is the &lsquo; Trait&eacute; de Pal&eacute;ontologie,&rsquo; by Professor Pictet of Geneva. This is a work of most alarming size, consist&not; ing of four thick octavo volumes and a quarto volume of plates. In it are described and figured all the genera of animals which occur fossil. It is
an extremely useful book. However, the student who uses it, should un&not; derstand that what he gains in time by reading it, he will lose in power. He can never be master of the subject unless he is as well acquainted witli the recent as with the fossil forms. The philosophical part of Biology is the genei a. They would seem to be what Edward Forbes has appropriately named them, Divine Ideas.&rdquo; To become a geologist these must be learnt, and not only these, but the larger groups they form. Now learning a genus does not consist in swallowing so many words, or seeing specimens, but, so to speak, in grasping the Divine Idea,&mdash;in recognizing the one thought in all the varied drapings of the species. It is here that the help ot a teacher is most needed.*,This is what has to be done. But notwithstanding the prodigious number of groups of living things, they are easily conquered if attacked methodically. Every group is not wanted at once.&#39; You begin with those most needed. The most constant tenants of rocks are shells ; consequently &ldquo; ls &trade; &iacute; 6 Mollusca we mostly commence. No better Manual exists than Woodward s. The Class with which to start, will depend upon its prevalence in the rock to be studied. As a rule, in a tertiary dis&not; trict it would be right to begin with the Lamellibranchiata and Gaste&not; ropoda; in the palaeozoic and cretaceous rocks, with the Cephalopoda and Brachiopoda ; in the older secondary series, with the Cephalopods and Bivalves. Nothing should ever be learnt without knowing why.,These are the geologist&rsquo;s tools. He must become acquainted with every&not; thing as it comes under his notice. Nothing should be passed by without being comprehended. In all he does, the geologist should remember that &Gamma;&reg; &iquest; &deg;&pound; e &deg; f ť i ie &reg;P ecial devotees of muscular Christianity, doing all he has &iacute;ľ th of mmd and body, with all his might. If he thus attacks the rocks, they will surely yield up to him their treasures and their truths. In proportion to the extent of his knowledge of principles, premising that it is always deep so will be his power of interpreting. It is the work of Geo- logy, not only to assemble facts, but to discover their meaning;. Were it simply a science of observation, preliminary training would be needless&middot; ut being essentially an inductive science, nothing can be done without it! Whde this training is being gone through,&mdash;and it is endless,&mdash;wonders,charms &ldquo;nd a net kveii&quot; ^ The earth ac 4&ldquo; res,I should now like to indicate what this preliminary knowledge will enable us to do but the subject proposed on starting has been already,S &Auml;ff Anything furtber wm more T opri t ly fnrai/h,llarry Seeley.,University Museum, Cambridge, May, 1861.,the* 1Г4 f&mdash;Bg genera on the great advantage that the student gets an idea of IS S v Iri thls the &trade; и aperies. CORRESPONDENCE.,Mv dear L., I regret to find that in your &ldquo; Plea for Geology&rdquo; you have propagated two serious geological errors,&mdash;one of them unique. You say the teeth of the hippo&not; potamus witness to the once tropical character of the Isle of Dogs. This is incorrect, for the climate in which that beast then lived was considerably colder than the present temperature of this our England. This can be demonstrated with as much certainty as you can show that twice two will be four.,The other error is where, having spoken of the Great Sea Dragons of the lias, you say, &ldquo; Ages again before that, glaciers were grinding their yet visible signatures against the cliffs of Snowdon.&rdquo; Let me remind you that these markings are among the most recent of geological productions, belonging, in fact, to the same period as your Isle of Dogs Hippopotamus of the gravel, S.,[The above is from a geological friend, who well knows what he is talking about. The object of the paper (which was at first intended to bear the title &ldquo; Ignoramus on Geology&rdquo;) is as much served by the correction of the mistakes as if they had not occurred. The first blunder arose from a natural association between heat and hippopotami; the second, from thinking or the age of the rocks instead of that of the scratches upon them : but both, probably, would have been avoided, if my worthy critic had returned certain Geological Authorities borrowed from my shelves.&mdash;R. TANSLEY.,Associate&mdash; мг. d. leggatt.,The Council has adopted the following Resolutions:&mdash;,1. The College will be ultimately divided into Six Classes.,2. The First will consist of the general body of merely Matriculated Students,3. The Second Class will consist of Certificated Students, or those to whom at&rsquo;the end of any course shall be awarded a Certificate of Competency in the subject of the course,4. The Third Class will consist of Scholars (in Art, History. Lammave. Lite&laquo;*,,&laquo;.*
Mathematice, or Physica, etc.), or those who prove themselves competent in any one branch of the College Studies.,5. The Fourth Class will consist of those who are thought worthy to be Associates or permanent Members of the College. From these wc require competency not only in some specific study, but in the most important studies of each department of the College. Each student will have an opportunity of answering questions in those subjects in which he takes most interest; but he will be expected to prove that he is acquainted with (1) the Bible History, (2) the History of England, (3) the Principles of English Grammar, and the Elements of (4) some pure Mathematical, and (5) some Physical study, or some branch of applied Mathematics, as Mechanics. The object will be to ascertain that he has the knowledge of those subjects which every person educated in a College ought to have; not to elicit his opinions either in Theology or Politics. The Associate must have attended the College for Eight Terms.,6. The Fifth Class will consist of Fellows : these will be chosen out of the Associates. The Fellows will take part in the education of this College, and may assist in establishing and carrying on other Colleges. They will be chosen by the Council, after due con&not; sideration of what they have done in the Associate Examination, and of what they have been since they entered the College. Moral qualities, a capacity for teaching, and a will&not; ingness to teach, will be at least as indispensable as proficiency in any branch of study.,7. The Sixth Class will consist of the Council. It is intended that the Council be hereafter recruited from the Fellows, to the extent of at least a third of its members. The Council will be prepared to assist in the formation of new Colleges, in London or other parts of England.,EXAMINATION-REGULATIONS.,1. Every teacher shall examine his class at the end of each Term, in the work of the Terra, either in or out of the regular class hours, and either orally or hy writing.,2. At the end of every complete course an Examination shall be held in the subject of the course.,3. A part, at least, of every such Examination shall be oral, and the Council shall seek to procure the services of such Examiners as will undertake to attend personally ; the duty of orally examining to be discharged by the last teacher of the class if no other Examiner can be obtained.,4. At each Examination at the end of a course, certificates of competence shall be awarded to those who shall merit them ; and the names of those who, though not re&not; ceiving such certificates, shall have passed the examination with credit, shall be posted in the hall of the College.,5. Every student attending any course is expected to attend the Examination at the close of that course or the next similar Examination ; and any student having passed any such Examination with credit may offer himself at the next following Examination for a certificate of competence.,6. Special Examinations for certificates of competence shall he discontinued, unless the Council shall at any time order such an Examination after particular consideration of the circumstances under which it shall be applied for.,VOCAL MUSIC CLASSES,,For teaching Sight-Singing on the Old English method, open to persons of both sexes, and of any age, are held as follows :&mdash;,Thursday, 8-9.30. JULY 1, 1861.,A SCOTCH &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,To most of the readers of this Magazine, it will be news, and pleasant news, that a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; exists in the town of Ayr in Scotland. It was opened in November last, and has just completed its first Session ; successfully too, to judge from the information contained in its Report, and the proceedings at its first annual meeting. On reading the account of this latter, it will be evident that the title &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; has in this case not been assumed without due regard to its real meaning. A good name strengthens a good thing ; but there is always the risk of the force of the good name being weakened by being applied to what is not entitled to bear it. The assumption of big titles is often a mere mode of puffery. As you walk along the streets, you may see that not one in half- a-dozen &ldquo; shops &rdquo; has sufficient strength of character to call itself by its right name. It is sure to be either a &ldquo;depot,&rdquo; or a &ldquo;mart,&rdquo; or a &ldquo;ware&not; house,&rdquo; or a &ldquo; manufactory,&rdquo; or an &ldquo; establishment for supplying the public with,&rdquo; etc. etc. So in a column of advertisements, a &ldquo; school &rdquo; is a thing nearly unknown ; even &ldquo; establishments for young ladies &rdquo; have be&not; come scarce. The &ldquo;Boarding-School&rdquo; of Notting Hill has become a &ldquo; Collegiate Institution the schoolmistress and governesses, a &ldquo; lady- principal and &ldquo; staff of professors,&rdquo; with an occasional Saint at their head to give additional dignity to the business. Those who first adopted the title of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, knew well that they would be open to the charge of &ldquo; soiling by ignoble use &rdquo; a &ldquo; grand old name.&rdquo; The expe&not; rience of some years may now be held to have dispelled this suspicion ; and if all who adopt the name, produce as good proofs of its noble use as the first year&rsquo;s work of the Ayr College can show, we may treat it as an idle fear. If our limits allowed it, we might transfer to our pages the report ot the &lsquo; Ayr Observer as it is, we can only refer to the Report, and to a single speech in reply to a Students&rsquo; Address, as capital expositions, in practice and theory, of what a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; should be. The piesent case is also the more interesting, as being the first, apparently, in which a piovincial town of a population not exceeding ten thousand, has established one of these Colleges. The smallness of a place is not wholly a disadvantage ; it is favourable to thorough publicity, and gives the stimulus of local feeling : the real difficulties are usually &agrave; deficiency of teachers, and the disunion frequently caused in a small town by the leading men being separated by sectarian and party differences. The Ayr College seems to be a happy example of the absence&lsquo;of both these drawbacks : may it *0 on and prosper ! Scotland, as Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s letter points out, ought, ac&not; cording to her traditions, to prove a genial soil for the planting of Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s Colleges. No better ground could have been found for the first, than that which the name of an immortal poet has made dear to the rest of the world. The working men of Ayr can now enjoy what vol. in. &bdquo; J
Robert Burns in his youth would have thought a paradise of intellectual luxury. No classes can teach them to produce a &ldquo; Scots wha hae &rdquo; or &ldquo; Auld lang syne;&rdquo; but they will open to the ordinary average of intelli&not; gent working people, sources of enjoyment which eighty years ago were ac&not; cessible ordy to the energy of individual genius.,THE PRESENT STRIKE IN THE BUILDING TRADES.,Whatever be the true solution of the questions raised in this great struggle, the public at least have one thing to complain of: it is that they are not allowed to hear both sides. The c Times 5 rings with denun&not; ciations of the folly, obstinacy, and unfairness of the Union leaders, but we look in vain through the columns of the daily papers for any statement of the operatives&rsquo; case. It is said, on good authority, that they have been refused this privilege. This, if true, is a disgrace to the English press. It would be in exact accordance with the policy of the &lsquo; Times ; 5 but better things might be expected of the more independent journals. No candid reader can peruse the diatribes of the &lsquo; Times &rsquo; without a suspicion that the clients, whose case is advocated with such pertinacious bitterness, can&not; not have all the justice on their side. If they have, why not let their op&not; ponents have their say, that the public may form its own estimate of the controversy? The men are represented as fighting recklessly against the proposed system of hour-payment, simply because this system would re&not; move an old cause of difference, and thus put an end to future agitation. This is a miserably unfair statement of the case. The men have various objections to the system of hour-payment. Some of these are of a kind which it is no use to dismiss without obtaining more trustworthy informa&not; tion. Probably none but those who have an intimate acquaintance with the trade can estimate their true value. But I desire to point out, to those who might be inclined to accept without question the statement of the * Times,&rsquo; that the question of hour-payment, far from being an A B C matter of equity and justice, involves a point which has always been one of the gravest and most difficult subjects of trade disputes. Many long struggles between masters and men have taken place on the question of &ldquo; over-time.&rdquo; It is this same question which is now at issue. It is per&not; fectly certain, that if hour-payment become the recognized system, the duration of a day&rsquo;s labour will be practically unlimited. By the custom of most trades, over-time has hitherto been paid at an increased rate. The equity of the rule is self-evident. The more a man&rsquo;s leisure hours are shortened, the greater the value to him of the leisure that remains. The new system contains no such provision. Whatever extra labour the pressure of individual competition forces a man to give, he must give at the same rate per hour. Any one who thinks over the various steps, must see that in &ldquo; hard times,&rdquo; that is, when work is scarce, capital dear, and labour cheap, we should have a small number in work at long hours, at reduced pay, and a large margin of unemployed. Suppose a man earns 30$. a week by sixty hours&rsquo; labour. The value of labour falls, and the
master says, &ldquo; I can&rsquo;t afford to pay more than 5%d. an hour.&rdquo; The man says to himself, &ldquo;Well, I could scarcely get on on 27&laquo;. 6&iquest; i. a week, but if I work an hour a day longer, I can make up the 30&laquo;.&rdquo; He finds plenty of men about him ready to make the sacrifice, and he gives in. Now if he hadn&rsquo;t the option of working the long hours, either he would make shift to do with sixty hours at 5 \d., and leave the odd six hours&rsquo; work and pay to some other man out of employment (for all his extra work, remember, is taken from somebody else) or he would stand out for his Ы. an hour, and the master would do his best to make the public pay this price. If the natural value of the hour&rsquo;s work was really 5 \d., the public would not pay more, but the workman would at any rate be spared the temptation to take less than the real value, and would have the strongest inducement to ask the old price as soon as the circumstances of the trade justified it. The temptation to accept low pay under pressure, in the hope of being able to make up for it by long hours, would bring into play in the strongest form the competitive principle of individual selfishness. People say, &ldquo; Why should not the industrious workman, if he likes, work twelve hours a day while the lazy one works eight ?&rdquo; Por the simple reason that in the long run the industrious workman will find it the worst for him. If he was the only one thus to strain his energies beyond the natural average, he would gain by it ; but if some hundreds or thousands more compete with him in doing so, this competition will depreciate the value of his labour in exactly the ratio by which he appeared at first to be a gainer. The apparent in&not; terest of a man as an individual is often a very different thing from his real interest as a member of a class. The amount of work waiting to be done is not affected the least by the number of people waiting to do it. The capital that has to provide the labour-payment is at any instant a limited quantity. Any share of the payment taken by one workman is so much taken from the rest. Increasing the workers or increasing the work done by each cannot increase the sum total paid for the work. Here lies the fal&not; lacy of the notion that any good is done to the working class, as a whole, by the employment of women in trades now fully occupied by men. This most fatal piece of ignorant and perverse philanthropy proceeds upon the idea that it is a good thing to &ldquo; find employment &rdquo; for people. So it would be, if it could really be done. If you could increase by any artificial means the fund out of which wages are to be paid, and then call up a new class of workers to take your surplus, you would be doing them some good; if you cannot increase that fund, adding to the number of workers is nothin&laquo;&middot; more than taking wages out of somebody&rsquo;s pocket to put them into some&quot; body&rsquo;s else ; and reckoning the population as composed of families, this means that by setting women to work at what their fathers, husbands&rsquo; and brothers are already doing, you are reducing the value and the remunera&not; tion of every bit of work done in exactly the ratio of the increase of hands. If the system became general, the result would be that a man&rsquo;s whole family would have to work as hard as he previously did himself to get exactly the same wages, and that there would be at any given time a correspondingly increased number of people out of employment. You would have distri&not; buted the labour differently, _ without altering either the amount of work done oi the amount paid for it. The effect of &ldquo; over-time&rdquo; is precisely the same. A man s desire to work twelve hours instead of ten cannot call into,H 3
existence fresli work and fresh wages for the extra two hours. He must take the work and wages which somebody else w r ould otherwise be having. On the whole, therefore, nothing is gained, while on the other hand there is this fatal loss, namely, that the standard of comfort adopted by the class to which he belongs, as the minimum at which it is content to exist, is lowered. To raise this standard by every possible means is the one object to which all efforts at social amelioration should be directed. On the pos&not; sibility of this rests all the hope for the future of the labouring classes. If the enormous increase in industrial forces, steam and machinery, enables us to do such a vast deal more work than formerly, at the same expense of labour, the effect ought to be felt in diminishing the amount of physical or mecha&not; nical toil done by every individual. If we can produce as much in eight hours as our grandfathers did in twelve, we clearly ought not to need to work more on the average than eight hours to their twelve ; and the mar&not; gin ought to contribute to the thousand elements of progress, which are independent of or above the mere mechanical work necessary to keep the world going. The shortening of hours is, therefore, one of the most legi&not; timate objects at which the workmen can aim. Sooner or later, they are entitled to it. The Strike of last year was a perfectly justifiable, though unwise, attempt to secure this boon. A strike is one of those things in which success or failure is really a test of rightness or wrongness. Foi if it suc&not; ceeds, it proves that the value at which the workmen estimated their labour is its true value. A strike is nothing more than a method, baibaious and clumsy indeed, but still effective, of testing the accuracy of the existing scale of labour-remuneration. If the masters had had to give in, it would have proved that the public was willing or able to pay for building in the increased ratio of ten to nine. The result showed that the men were wrono&middot;. They were wrong in not perceiving that their labour is already remunerated at a rate equal to the average value of all other kinds of manual labour, and that any attempt artificially to raise its price above this natural averao-e must fail ; or if temporarily successful, would soon produce a return to the natural scale. So long as the vast mass of the community is con&not; tent with a certain standard of physical comfort and general social ad&not; vantages, it is no use for a section of workers to try to make themselves a select guild, enjoying exceptional privileges. Suppose, for instance, the Building trades had their present wages for an eight-hour day. The necessary result would be an influx of new labourers, which would soon bring wages down to their present level. A lad of eighteen, look&not; ing &deg;out for a trade, would see masons going from work at four o&rsquo;clock on fine summer afternoons, and would say, &ldquo; That s the trade forme! Much better than being sixteen hours on a &rsquo;bus, or fourteen hours in a shop,&rdquo; and in a year or two the competition of new hands would replace the old scale. But if emigration or any other cause should raise the value of home labour, and this increased value should make itself felt to any extent, the workmen would then be not only right but reasonable in saying at once to the masters, &ldquo;The value of our labom has risen ; raise our wages.&rdquo; And the masters would be able to do it, be&not; cause the public would have to pay an increased price tor its buildings. If a baker prices his quartern loaf at 8 d., when it can be got elsewhere tor Hd., no one can say he is doing wrong. He is simply stupid. For either
no one will come into his shop at all, or if his neighbours are so ignorant of the prices in the next street as to pay 8 d. without grumbling, another baker will soon set up next-door and reduce his profits. But if he and the other bakers can, without artificial combinations, keep the price up to 8 d., it proves that this is the natural value of the loaf. The question is not one of rightness or wrongness, but of wisdom and unwisdom.,The present struggle in the building trades however seems to me to have a somewhat new aspect. Beginning by a renewal of the old demands for increased wages in the form of diminished time, it now turns on a dispute as to methods of payment. This question is ten times more difficult than the other. If the men have fair ground for believing that the proposed method will tend to depreciate the value of their labour, they have a per&not; fect right to resist the change ; but how far they should carry this opposi&not; tion is a question of practical policy, which it rests with themselves alone to determine. The &lsquo; Times 5 affects to treat the men as the unwilling victims of a set of agitators. If this is so, the case of these Unions is a singular exception to the general rule. The &lsquo; Eeport of the Social Science Association,&rsquo; which is by far the best authority on the subject, distinctly records it as an ascertained fact, that as a rule, the leaders of these Unions are taken from what is in every sense the best class of workmen. On a question so beset with practical difficulties as that now pending, a differ&not; ence of opinion is very likely to exist. The Societies however have a very strong case in support of the &#39;principle they are asserting : as to the wisdom of fighting it to the death, I should myself have very great doubt. That the hour payment will be maintained, in some form or other, seems pretty clear. If the workmen have forced the masters to a system dangerous to themselves, by their ill-timed agitation for what they could not fairly claim, they must pay the penalty of their indiscretion. But the fair ground to take up now, is to accept the change, subject to the proviso of a recognized maxi&not; mum of hours , and an increased rate for over-time. The masters cannot in common fairness refuse this, if the workmen insist upon it. If they do, they will be belying their reiterated professions of a desire for a just settlement of the case. If these professions are true, they cannot wish to make any&not; thing by the new arrangement, and without such an over-time rule their gain would be certain : first, in quicker returns of interest on invested capital, from the plant being used a greater number of hours per day; and, secondly, in actual saving on men&rsquo;s wages ; and this without reckoning the general chances of depreciation above alluded to. I should presume that the gain thus secured would be more than the loss on the gratuitous half-holiday.* The masters appeal, through their newspaper advocates to the &ldquo; British sense of fair play.&rdquo; This appeal will seem a pitiful sham if it is understood that they are pressing a change which is to transfer money from the workmen&rsquo;s pockets to their own. Their policy will then,* Since writing this I find from an article in the f Spectator &rsquo; of June 29th that my speculation as to the lengthening of the day, and the money gain to the masters is already confirmed by the facts. Messrs. Kelk and Lucas are employing men systemati&not; cally foi twelve and fourteen hours a day, at a saving to themselves of 4a. 7 d and um wards per man per week, after allowing for the half-holiday. This article professes to be the resuit of full inquiry, and fully confirms the truth of what I am here urging, by a refutation in detail ot the mis-statements of the daily press. b 0 J
look like a vindictive attempt to crush the Unions, and the &ldquo; British sense of fair play&rdquo; will never be got to see that an individual workman, ivith the alternative of &ldquo; take what I offer, or starve,&rdquo; can be a match without com&not; bination, for a capitalist who turns over his thousands a week. It is impossible, I think, for any one to read the mass of evidence collected by the Committee above referred to, without seeing that On the whole the Trades&rsquo; Unions have done far more good than harm. They have provided a temporary substitute for the old trade organizations which, under an utterly different economical regime , fostered the early growth of European industry, and they will be replaced, we hope, in their turn, by some better system, combining the elements of order which held together the old guilds, with the principles of freedom and progress by which modern commerce is animated. Before this can come about, the workmen must learn to give up their impotent attempts to raise, by artificial means, the value of then* labour, and to close the access to their trades by a cordon of restrictive enactments. They must learn, too, to reject the childish babble about &ldquo; down-trodden victims of aristocratic oppression,&rdquo; and the like, which forms the staple of so many harangues. Let them quit this stuff, and meet the employers as men and fellow-citizens, and every year that passes will uiake it more and more impossible for any master of respectable cha&not; racter to disregard their fair claims. The tone of both sides is already an immense improvement on what it once was. Give it time, and the English public in the end will get at the rights of a dispute, and see justice done. The mass draw their first impression from the daily oracles, but in time the better judgment of the few who inquire into the subject, throws the weight of honest opinion on the right side. The present dispute could be settled to-morrow, without any more concession than the inexorable facts of the case demand, if each party would agree to consider the question really at issue, without reference to the aggravations of bygone struggles. The men, to hold their ground, must abandon the fruitless demand tor in&not; creased pay, under the disguise of &ldquo; short time,&rdquo; and the masters must admit, if they are decently honest, and look at the case from the men&rsquo;s point&#39;of view, that in- pressing the plan of hour payment, pure and simple, without any restriction as to the length of a day&rsquo;s work, or any provision for over-time, they are forcing on their work-people a system which must inevitably tend to their social degradation, without benefiting in the end either themselves or the public.,Е. B. Litchfield.,LETTER TO THE PROMOTERS OE A &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,Br F. D. Maurice, M.A.,5, Russell Square, Ж. C., June 3.,My dear Sir,&mdash;I have heard, with much interest, of your &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; in Ayr. I wish I could have had the pleasure and the honour which you kindly offered me of being with you at the celebration of your first session&rsquo;s labour. I could have helped you very little ; but your sympathy would have been an encouragement to us in
England. I should have rejoiced to tell the members of our College what you were doing.,In many respects I apprehend Scotland is a more promising field for such an experi&not; ment than South Britain. All your traditions are in favour of a union of classes in edu&not; cation. Money-worship, which has been gradually defiling more and more the schools among us which our ancestors formed, especially for the use of the poor, still encounters a practical as well as a theoretical protest in yours. One great obstacle, therefore, to the idea of a College, in which the professional man and the mechanic shall be fellow- workers, will be absent in your case. You may begin with assuming that that is the natural order of things ; you may not only refer to illustrious precedents and testimonies of a former age ; you may appeal to an experience which has been continued down to your own. Your &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; will not in principle be different from the noblest which exist in the different cities of your land.,I cannot, of course, judge at all of your local circumstances. But if we in the South do not form an exaggerated notion of the diffusion of education, and of the desire for education which has been the result of your parish schools, and of the openings which have been given to poor men in your Universities, there must be not only less novelty in such an experiment as yours when it is commenced among Scotsmen, but a greater faci&not; lity in obtaining the requisite teachers and learners. I do not believe that the difficulty would be as great as many imagine in an ordinary English town ; in many iustancei where men, very little known but thoroughly in earnest, have undertaken the vvork, it has been proved that the busiest schoolmasters, doctors, tradesmen, were ready to con&not; duct classes, and the busiest mechauics to attend them. But you will show us, I trust, in Ayr, that the desire to communicate knowledge and to receive it is keener than we have yet found it to be. If you put us to shame, we shall have great cause for being thankful to you.,In what directions the craving for information is the Strongest among your people, I cannot be a judge. Perhaps, till you have entered on your task, you can hardly be. In many of our anticipations we were mistaken. We were asked to give instruction which we did not suppose would be called for ; some which we offered, and thought likely to be acceptable, was rejected. Experience and humility are needful, that we &quot;may determine how far it is desirable to yield to the pressure from without; how far to maintain onr previous resolutions in spite of it. There are some lessons which I think should be given regularly, even if there is no popular demand for them&mdash;let there be many or few to re&not; ceive them. They express the purpose of whole education; they give it a unity. And you will find it, I am sure, most necessary to show, in the outset, that you do not aim at giving an education which shall fit your pupils for doing certain jobs more effectually. You must appeal to something higher in them than that which craves to get money in one line or another more rapidly. You must tell them plainly that you cannot promise them that they shall be richer men than their forefathers. All the pious frauds which are in circulation about the chances that men have of becoming millionaires if they will only go to school&mdash;all the legends that are told at public meetings to encourage young men in gambling for prizes in the world&rsquo;s lottery&mdash;where the blanks must be ten times as numerous as the prizes&mdash;yon should discard at once and for ever if you really mean to raise your working brethren, or to give them an interest in what concerns all human beings. They should be told that they may have the highest blessings whilst they con&not; tinue in the state into which they were born ; that the best helpers of their kind have been poor men ; that Arkwright, working out a great thought in his barber&rsquo;s shop, had a nobility which never belonged to the same Arkwright when he could command the wor&not; ship of lords. The blessing of a College, it seems to me, is emphatically this, that it gives men a status and a social position which they will not care to abandon for mere accidents of individual superiority ; which also gives a stimulus to their thoughts and energies such as the mere ambition of rising to another grade never gives, I do not want to banish that ambition. It must be; it may be awakened in some by those en&not; couragements to common thought and fellow-work which I regard as the substitutes for it. But if we cultivate it without them&mdash;if we kindle this thought in a working man &ldquo; If 1 am оп1 У diligent, I may some day possess a shop or ride&quot; in a carriage,&rdquo; we are doing our best to make him a flunkey and not a man; he will have a right to&rsquo; curse us hereafter for haying robbed him of substantial blessings which God had given him and ol having led him to pursue and to embrace a vapour.
In my judgment, &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; should mainly propose to themselves the object of awakening that sense of fellowship in labouring men&mdash;that sense of their posi&not; tion as men possessing no special advantages, and therefore obliged to be human or no&not; thing&mdash;which may most counteract the degrading, disorganizing tendencies which the pursuit of money, and the belief that money is the end of existence, inevitably produce. I do not complain of competitive examinations. They are, I believe, doing good ; they are, at all events, a protection against jobbery, which is a terrible evil. But if they be&not; come the soul of education, it must decay; its moral end must be forgotten; it must cease to be humane and become selfish. Without railing against this competition ac&not; cepting all in it which is worthy of acceptance&mdash;you will do much to abate its mischief if you can connect the pursuit of real substantial knowledge with hard honest manual work ; if you can show that there is nothing incompatible in them ; that each explains and dignifies the other; that to lose the reverence for work is to lose the reverence for knowledge, to acquire au exclusive reverence for that which is infinitely less worthy than either.,I have much more which I should like to say, but I should weary you, and I have not much time just now at my command. I will only add, that if we keep the end of a Working College before us, I believe the question that most perplexes education both in England and Scotland may&mdash;not at once, but in due time&mdash;find a solution. &ldquo; How is Christian teaching to be connected with other teaching ? has been the difficulty which no State controversies nor sect compromises have been able to remove. I think it will be found in a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt;, aiming at human fellowship, aiming to overcome the dividing influence of class interest and money-worship, that Christian teaching is needed for just the purpose which it has appeared to hinder, and which, if the Bible is true, it ought specially to promote. I have felt this deeply in our own College. I have invited as many of the students as liked to come to a Bible Class : of course, none were compelled to come. I have told them my whole mind, suppressing nothing, compro&not; mising nothing, listening to all questions and objections, meeting them as well as I could&deg; I have not many pupils in this class. But the good they have done me makes me hope that I may have done some to them, and I do not think the existence of such a class is without significance to the whole College. I hope you may be able to establish such a one, and that whoever undertakes it will not be discouraged if he sees no imme&not; diate fruit from it. , , , &bdquo; , w . ,&middot;,That lesson you will have to learn in all the classes and in all your work. W ishing,vou God&rsquo;s blessing upon it, believe me very truly yours,,F. D, Maurice.,J. S. Henderson, Esq.,,Secy.
