The business of the evening was preceded by tea, and interspersed with mu&not; sical performances by the band of the Ayrshire Militia. The Chairman, Provost Ken&not; nedy, made some introductory remarks, and called on one of the Secretaries, Mr. Hen&not; del son, to read the Report. Before doing this, Mr. Henderson explained that the Council had iuvited Mr. Maurice, the Principal of the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;,
to be present at the meeting. To this invitation he had replied by a most kind and ge&not; nial note, saying that he was very anxious to be present, but was obliged to decline the pleasure. He, however, offered to read a letter in lieu of his presence, which he ac&not; cordingly did. Mr. Henderson then read this letter to the meeting, and after it the following&mdash;,FIRST ANNUAL REPORT.,&ldquo; In presenting their first annual Report to the members of the Ayr &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, the Secretaries feel much pleasure in being able to congratulate them on the suc&not; cessful establishment of the College, and on its gratifying progress during the first ses&not; sion. The measure of that success has certainly far outstripped the most sanguine expec&not; tations of those who took the initiative in organizing the scheme. In order to provide you with a connected narration of the latter, we may be allowed to state here the first steps which were taken to establish the College, and then to detail the results which have followed from it. &middot; Our Report thus naturally branches itself into the two divisions&mdash;1st, Of the history of our scheme ; and 2nd, Of its results, so far as we can yet trace these.,&ldquo; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, as all are now aware, have existed for some time in va&not; rious parts of England, and the testimonies to their beneficial effects have been nume&not; rous and weighty. It has been found that they overtake educationally spheres w&#39;hich, in all probability, but for them would have lain quite barren, and that they are well adapted to supply information and training which may be altogether а -wanting to, or which may be very defective among, the labouring classes. Urged by a desire to extend this class of institutions to Scotland, and also to impart an impulse to education in Ayr, your Secretaries resolved, in October last, to attempt the establishment of an Ayr &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.,&ldquo; Before anything could be undertaken it was necessary to guarantee a favourable con&not; sideration of our idea, and provide a staff of teachers. Naturally our first application was to Dr. Hunter. As we expected from his well-known interest in education, Dr. Hunter entered at once with much enthusiasm into our scheme. We need not stay to tell how r we fared in getting our staff of teachers completed. Suffice it to say that almost without exception our applications were met with great cordiality, and were re&not; sponded to most cheerfully. Being thus provided with a certain amount of teaching- power, our next outlook was for a building where what classes we might form could meet. Of course, the most suitable place in town&mdash;in fact, the only suitable place&mdash;is the Academy, and we resolved to apply to the Directors of the Academy for permission to hold evening classes in the rooms on evenings during the week. This permission, also, was at once, and most cordially, granted ; and we think it only right to express here the sense which the Council of Teachers have of the obliging and friendly disposition which the Directors of the Academy have thus manifested towards them.,&ldquo; Teaching-power and space being ready, the next points for us to consider were&mdash; Whom we should teach&mdash;What we should teach&mdash;and, How, as a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, we should be organized. We had no wish to interfere, in the smallest degree, with the action of local institutions and evening classes which might already be in existence.&rdquo;,The Report proceeded to enumerate some of the rules with which the promoters of the College started&mdash;fixing 16 as an average age-limit, and abstaining from providing classes in reading or elementary writing. This was rendered necessary in order to avoid being a mere elementary night school. The Report mentioned the branches for which classes had been formed, and then proceeded to detail the organization of the College. The classes were to meet in the evenings from 8 to 10 every night except Sunday. The management of the College w r as peculiar, and was composed of a joint Board of Teachers and Students forming the General Council of the College. The latter were elected by the classes, one to represent each class. i\.t present the Council was composed of 17 teachers and 8 representatives, in all 25 members.,The Report continued to narrate the first steps taken to put the scheme on a workable footing&mdash;the preliminary meeting of 26th October, for the purpose of giving explana&not; tions to the public&mdash;and the meeting with enrolled students on 19th November. The greatest number of applicants were for admission to the writing and arithmetic classes, two teachers of the former and five of the latter being thus rendered requisite. The session w-as to occupy seven months, and to be divided into three terms of two months
each ; and in future years the College will be opened earlier than it was last. A fee of l&iacute;. per class per term was imposed, which will, in ordinary cases, meet all expeuses. The College was at length opened on 21st November. All the branches offered at the commencement continued to he taught throughout, with the exception of two, English History and Geography. The Secretaries hoped that these would be resumed next ses&not; sion. The numbers in attendance in others had been very large.,&ldquo; The number of enrolled students&rdquo; (the Report said) &ldquo;during the whole session was 880. Dividing this number by three, there being three terms, we have an average attendance during each term of 293. This number, 293, is the number of enrolled students attend&not; ing all the classes, and is not to be taken as giving the number of individuals&mdash;students in many cases attending two and sometimes three classes. Striking, however, a rough average, we may put down the number of individual students throughout the session at 150. Classifying the averages of each class, we have had attending during the session the following numbers at the following classes :&mdash;French, 34 ; Latin, 28 ; German, 5 ; Grammar, 46 ; Book-keeping, 25; Writing, 43 ; Arithmetic, 83 ; Mathe&not; matics, 18 ; total, 282. Considerable progress&mdash;in some cases very remarkable pro&not; gress&mdash; shas been made in all of these branches by a considerable proportion of the members, aird all of the teachers report to us their great satisfaction with the industry and zeal of the students.&rdquo; (Cheers.),The Report went on to speak of the lecture delivered on 14th May, under the auspices of the College, by Mr. Buckraaster, Government Department of Science and Art, South Kensington, who explained the way in which Government aided science instruction, by giving pecuniary assistance to those who pass the Loudon examinations and become cer&not; tificated teachers, and by adjudging prizes to such as are the best pupils in classes taught by those certificated teachers. The Secretaries strongly urged upon members to prepare for the examination in November next.,&ldquo; On a review of the facts we have given above, we think all will agree that the first session of the Ayr &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; has been eminently successful&mdash;successful in having organized a workable scheme, in having started a number of classes giving in&not; struction in various branches of education, and in having advantage taken of these classes by a large number of students. We are well aware that the novelty of our plan&mdash;a novelty at least in Scotland&mdash;was an attraction to many at first, and that not a few came with very vague and indefinite notions of what they wished, of what the teachers offered, and certainly with no resolute purpose to work. All who were induced to attend by such motives were likely to fall away, and it was our desire that they should. We wish workers, not triflers ; students, not seekers of amusement ; and we do not ask any to come to us who are not resolved to be diligent. The first flush of novelty has now paled ; the nature of the work asked has now become known ; and we may expect that next session will be a testing one for the permanence of the College. A successful esta&not; blishment is one thing ; a permanent continuance is another : we have got the first ; we appeal to those in Ayr who are interested in education to give us the second. We may, we think, have sufficient confidence in the working men of Ayr to anticipate that such an appeal will not be made in vain. Let them, however, clearly understand that the future of the College depends upon them ; and it will be no light sin of which they will be guilty, ii they allow the institution to be wrecked. In expectation that the blight side ol the picture may be the real one, and in lull trust that next session maybe even more prosperous than last, we have many suggestions which the course of the last has offered us. These we need not detail now and here. We believe the General Council and the members will have been equally taught many lessons by our late session ; and next session we will expect to be even more completely equipped as teachers, to be able to organize even more satisfactory arrangements, and to rely still more on the students, on thou powers of learning, and on their zeal for knowledge. In conclusion, we would oul\ express oui hope, as Secretaries, that by-and-by other branches of instruction not yet included in our curriculum may be in introduced. It is very desirable to intro&not; duce such branches as have not merely a direct practical application to the arts and sciences, to the work and business of our everyday life ; but such also as more directly show the dignity of knowledge on its own account, and as impart a true and entire pur&not; pose to educative effort. No true education stops short with Utilitarianism, or is satis&not; fied with enabling men to obtain so much extra &lsquo; praise and pudding.&rsquo; No true educa&not; tion is content with merely teaching men to be more skilful in the use of their tools ;
but what is of infinitely more importance : it aims to teach man his inherent dignity and worth as a human being, by elevating his desires and ends, by widening his mental nature, and by purifying and deepening his moral and religious purposes. We there&not; fore sincerely trust that ere long we may have introduced into our College classes in Mental and Moral Science, and also classes iu Political and in Social Economy, as through the culture induced by the study of these sciences, men are brought to deal more closely with the highest problems of human life, and to grapple with the most tremendous difficulties which human beings have got to solve. There might not be much demand for these at first, but they express, as Mr. Maurice has so finely told us, the purpose of whole education, and they directly ennoble all studies. The worth of education, as we must ourselves learn, and as we are bound under penalties to teach to others, is not to be estimated by its money-value ; but by its influence on the whole nature of the learners. Not what will it put in my pocket, but what will it make me, what effect will it have ou my real self, my inmost being and deepest powers of thought and feeling, is the question of questions for every w r orking man who strives to draw down upon him the glimmering rays thrown across his path by the sun of modern culture. That question asked with all earnestness is sure of an answer, because it drives a man back upon him&not; self, and teaches him that on himself alone, must he rely for all progress. No message needs in these days to be more emphatically proclaimed to working men, as to all other men, than this : that in themselves lie the roots of all true progress, and that they must themselves develope these. We who offer working men instruction, only provide certain conditions to enable them to exercise their own powers. All the rest must be done by each man for himself alone.&rdquo;,&ldquo; WiLLiAM Jack Ί &bdquo; &middot;,Jas. S. Henderson &fnof; &ugrave;ecretanes -,&ldquo;Ayr, June, 1861.&rdquo;,The adoption of this Report was moved and seconded by Mr. Shaw and Mr. D. McK. White. An &ldquo; Address from the Representatives of the Classes to the Principal and Teachers&rdquo; was then read by Mr. Jamieson on behalf of the students. To this Mr. Jack replied nearly as follows :&mdash;,&ldquo; Students,&mdash;In name of the Principal and the Teachers, I thank you heartily for the Address which your representatives have now presented us. I only wish that we could feel in our hearts that we deserved a tithe of the praises you bestow. Indeed, the warmth with which you express your gratitude makes us a little ashamed. It was one of our hopes that our efforts might tend to promote the union of classes. Perhaps the enthusiasm of your spontaneous expressions may help to show others how much some&not; thing has been wanted, and how cordially anything is received that may attempt to supply that. The idea of Plato&rsquo;s Republic was the rigid and logical division of labour. Everybody was assumed to be able to do his special work best ; and his business was to do nothing beyond it. It was necessary to have shepherds who should do nothing but. watch sheep, husbandmen who should till the ground, merchants who should make the link between producers and consumers, and soldiers who should form the outer fence of the State, so as to allow all within it to labour freely at their allotted task. It was an ideal state, but one towards which some economic laws nevertheless tend constantly to carry us. We are always in danger of leaving teaching to teachers, politics to editors, fighting to soldiers, and religion to ministers. But we should make the same mistake as the man who forgets that the bones need more than fit shape to work smoothly in their sockets, and that the most perfect machine will not go right for long without oil. Human affairs go ill where everything is angular and hard, where all divisions are sharply marked, and every profession belongs exactly to some single man. Where the minister is set apart to fulfil religion, the congregation can have little of the power of godliness. Where only editors talk and think politics, we shall have, as we have seen in - America and France, the mob-rule of a passionate and incompetent mob. If our teachers and professors are the only men who teach, instruction will degenerate into pedantry, instead of sitting sovereign over the entire business of life. Where only soldiers can fight, the sober freedom which best suits the peaceful citizen is endangered as much by his defenders as by enemies from without. That is not our ideal of a perfect state. Pro&not; fessional work must be done and well done, but besides his profession every one has his manhood. The mechanic, after his nine or ten hours, as the case may be, leaves his
workshop ; the shopman closes his shop and goes home to his family, and in almost every one s life there is that pleasant period, &lsquo; after office hours.* A state will not work well if that time is taken up by everybody in personal enjoyment. All the smoothing of the world is to be done then. To unite class with class, men must give out of the fund ot their labour, for no reward but the pleasure of giving. The precise way in which we have laboured needs but little defence. We assumed from the experience of others that there was a large number of men sufficiently instructed in the elements of education to take advantage of a little higher learning, and we had reason to believe there were a number in Ayr anxious to do so. On that assumption we acted. We taught all that our special aptitudes, the time at our disposal, the probable demand, and, what we mean never to lose sight of, our ideas as to what ought to be taught to such a class as we aimed to attı act, seemed to demand of us. A great deal of mere elementary work is needed to overtake the educational wants of the working-classes. We do not profess or intend any&not; thing so serious. We have been encouraged by some kind friends, who told us that if we did nothing but keep the working man out of the whisky-shop, we were doing a great deal. We have no such object in view. The class of men who are willing to give part of their hard-earned rest to improve themselves, is not the same that goes to the dram&not; shop. Every effort for good goes to the sum of good, and helps to counteract vicious and coarse indulgences, but we are not servants of the Temperance Society (Applause ) Our work is a small work, easily understood. We want to encourage the love of know&not; ledge. We want to assist those who have had less advantage than we have had in open&not; ing the gates of truth wider to them. We want to introduce them to literature or science which will not merely occupy their idle hours as whisky, toddy, or smokine nnght, but where their highest powers may find a congenial food. One thing is certain that if we give you at all the spirit and temper of the scholar, you will not be slaves of money. Pew of us are apt to undervalue money or the advantages it brings The dis&not; satisfied longing for them turns to poison in the breait when a man spends all his strength in securing the barest necessaries, and finds it impossible to derive either ease or culture m his moments of rest from toil. If we open to you new provinces of thought, we shall have helped you to the most valuable things which money and leisure have to offer. It is an old lesson, but one we all of us need to hear often, that it is not rest or ease that is our ultimate end here or hereafter, but the perfection of those powers,at make us men after the image of God. But above all, you will learn we hone the sacredness of truth. The whole laboured fabric of science is a temple to truth It has been built by the slowly-growing toil of men who have cast aside their prepossessions who have learned to sit as learners at the feet of Nature, and who dare to utter the words they have spelt out laboriously in her book. In our College, as in all colleges, learning,ľvoh&Iacute;tSffi life &raquo; p0SSlble &rsquo; bcCaUSe there are eternal laws which underlie the manifold,The meeting was next addressed by Dr. Hunter, the Principal of the College who in the course of a long and interesting speech, reviewed the work done during the past ses- sioii touching on the various characteristics and advantages of the several studiespursued m the College. He also alluded to the position of such Colleges as part of the general system of national education. &ldquo; After due inquiry, it had been found that day-schools were imperfect agents in the work of education, from no fault of their own, however but from the condition that they lost their pupils before they had had opportunities of making them proficient in the elements of knowledge. The&iacute;e was a necessity therefore for carrying on evening instruction in classes. For some time he had been head master of an institution, now Queens College, Liverpool, in which evening instruction in classes had been given, as we are giving in our College here, and it is known to all ner sons who have read the annual Reports, that the results are declared to be most satisfac&#39; tory Scho ars leavmgthe day-school should be madeto go, according to t&iuml;effi advance&not; ment, directly either to the evening classes or the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; In the evening classes, however, there should be intellectual as well a&quot;s moral trainino&middot;&#39; Tİ &trade; snid by some persons that they had had Mechanics&middot; Institutes, which did alf this bne ago. It was not so. The original design ot Mechanics&#39; Institutes, was not so much to educate as to afford opportunities for self-education Pre simnn^incro fncf&laquo; г т thirst for knowledge which did not eiist, as Lord Brougham had lately acknoSged&ldquo; libraries were instituted,^ reading-rooms opened, courses of lectures provided but as might have been ejected, their success had heen but partial, and their results&#39; nnsatis
factory. Popular lectures had been a complete failure. The usual practice had heen to provide a succession of lectures unconnected with each other. A lecture on history had been followed by one on science, while the scientific had in its turn been followed by one on literature or philosophy. It has been ascertained that such lectures are of no value in an educational point of view ; that they sometimes amuse, but fail to instruct. Fur&not; ther, systematic courses of lectures had been tried in various places, but without suc&not; cess, because, said the Directors, * we found that the persons who attended them were not educated up to the standard which was necessary to qualify them to profit by lectures.&rsquo; &rdquo;,A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the proceedings of the evening.,NEWS.,London .&mdash;On Thursday evening, May 30, the usual General Meeting before the be&not; ginning of a Terra, was held at the College, The preliminary Tea was attended by very few, but among them were the faces of two or three old students, taken from us lately by work in the country and otherwhere, but who had come up for a shake of the hand and a chat with old friends. The speaking was begun at 8.45, by Mr. Maurice, who said that as we Londoners had heard one another&rsquo;s voices often enough, he should call on Mr. Solly, of Lancaster, to say what was going on in the North, where some of our seed had been sown and was bearing fruit. &mdash;Mr. Solly said he had come to listen and learn, not to talk, and he was greatly disappointed not to hear the usual statements of the Teachers as to what had been done in their Classes during the past term, and what they proposed to do in the coming term. The account of vjork was always interesting to him, and he was sorry to put his own words in place of it ; but as he had been called on, his sense of gratitude to the London College would not allow him to refuse the order, and though he had no &ldquo;College&rdquo; to tell about, he would say what had been done in Lancaster. He went there hoping to be able to establish a College, and at first had recourse to the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute. There he could only get Mutual Improvement Classes, and there he found no working men. How to get at the working men was the difficulty, and he advertised a meeting of them, thinking they would come. But some days before it was held, an old workman came to him and said it was sure to be a failure unless he would go to the different large shops and factories, and ask the men at work there to send delegates to the Meeting. This he did ; and a successful Meeting it was : and by way of parenthesis he would recommend anybody wanting to interest working men in any plan, to go to their shops and get their Delegates appointed to hear and re&not; port on it. Well, the Delegates came; the first Meeting went off successfully, and other smaller Meetings were held for six or eight weeks successively, to find out what men most wanted, and how the want could be best met. It was soon found that Classes were not the only want, even if they were the first, but that play was wanted too. The result was that &ldquo; The Lancaster Working-Men&rsquo;s Mutual Improvement and Recreation Society&rdquo; was started. It was not called a College, because the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute men ob&not; jected to the name, and the great wish of him (Mr. Solly) and his delegates had been to effect a union of the two bodies. To their great regret, the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute men, at the last moment, turned round and refused to join them, and so they had to start by themselves. They took a field to play in, then a room at thirteen guineas a year, got thirty books given to them, and provided a stock of leaping-bars and other gym&not; nastic apparatus. Now they had between six and seven hundred volumes in their Li&not; brary ; but let it be remembered that they began with &#39;Recreation : this was the specialty of the Institution. First came a field; then a room and draughts, etc. ; lectures and classes. Mr. Solly here made a warm defence of admitting novels and works of amuse&not; ment into Libraries, even College ones, and instanced the case of a working painter on the North-western Railway, at Carlisle, who told him that hehad belonged to a Working Man&rsquo;s Institute that was gradually failing, and just about to be given up, when some one proposed to lay in a stock of Cooper&rsquo;s and other good novels, etc., and lend them out. They did so, and soon had three hundred members. The second specialty of the Lan-
caster Society was the Discussion Class. This, Mr. Solly maintained, was a good thing, though Mr. Tom Hughes was against it. He did not deny that it might be made an occasion for the display of vanity, but he was quite sure that the good produced by it far overbalanced the evil. You see, said he, we are like the porters of a warehouse; there are the heavy barrels of goods in the street, and there&rsquo;s our warehouse-door. How are we to get the barrels up through it ? Why, by an inclined plane. Well, the Amuse&not; ments, and the Novels, and the Discussion Class, are the inclined plane to hoist our heavy friends into literature by. The Discussion Class, especially, lets men see what stuif their mates&rsquo; heads are made of ; it stirs the listeners up, .makes them think, intro&not; duces them to subjects of study, and then takes them to lectures and classes. A third point to be noticed was, dress. At Brock Street all felt at home in their working clothes. It was not necessary to go home after work and put on black coats. Men could, and did, come straight from their shops ; and they felt this a great boon ; it was what they could not do at the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution. There, they said, they could not stand the well- dressed men coming in, and, as it were, taking the newspapers from them. Nevertheless, he (Mr. S.) felt that the College movement must not be allowed to become a class one, a mere working man&rsquo;s movement ; but due regard must be paid to these little sensitive&not; nesses if working men were to be brought into the fold. The fourth point was, that they looked after the men who left them. Whenever a man discontinued his attendance, some one or two who knew him called on him, and thus showed that they took an interest in him, and would be glad to have him back again among them. They formed a sort of Vigilance Committee, and the plan worked admirably. Lastly, the Lancaster Society did not close its rooms during Vacation. Classes might be given up, as teachers went away for rest, but working men did not get vacations, and so the Library was always open, ready to arford a quiet hour to any one who sought it. Such were the chief topics worthy of note that had occurred to him during his work at Lancaster. He had taken hearty pleasure in it all, and hoped that though the Society was not a College in name, it was one in spirit, all its members working together for the social and moral good of their fellow-men.&mdash;Mr. Maurice said that Mr. Solly&rsquo;s experience, and that of others, had led him to modify his views as to the advisability of Discussion Classes and Amuse&not; ments. He should be much less ready now to say, &ldquo;This or that will not do anywhere,&rdquo; and was convinced that the positive doing of one&rsquo;s own work was better than the nega&not; tive stopping of others&rsquo; plans, so long, of course, as no principle of right was invaded^&mdash; The meeting was afterwards addressed by Mr. Gregg, Mr. V. Lushington, Mr. Roebuck, and Mr. Litchfield, the topics alluded to by Mr. Solly furnishing the chief subject of discussion. J,London .&mdash;The students of this College will be glad to know that their library has just received a most valuable addition by bequest from a late student. Mr. John Colcombe Garrett Williams has left to it by will a collection of a hundred and forty-four volumes, chiefly text-books and treatises of the kind most useful to students. Among them are some of the best books in various departments of science, mathematics, and language, and the collection is altogether of the sort most practically suitable to the shelves of a college library. The following names will serve as a sample :&mdash; De Morgan&rsquo;s Calculus Parkinson&rsquo;s Mechanics, Mill&rsquo;s Logic, Whately&rsquo;s Logic, Humboldt&rsquo;s Cosmos, Smith&rsquo;s Classical Dictionary, Latham&rsquo;s Ethnology, Gregory&rsquo;s Inorganic Chemistry, Woodward&rsquo;s Recent and Fossil Shells, etc. Besides books of this character, there are a large number of the best-known elementary treatises on various subjects.,Cambridge&mdash; On Monday, June 17th, the members of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; made their annual excursion. The places visited were Shortgroves Park and Saffron Walden. The Eastern Counties Railway Company very kindly took us there and back at a greatly reduced fare.,At Shortgroves (the residence of &mdash; Smith, Esq.) we were allowed to ramble at will along the banks of the broad stream which makes its way through the grounds It abounds with fish and waterfowl, and is overhung by groves of cedar-trees and beeches as massive as any in the country. As was to be expected, wherever we went we greatly disturbed the denizens of these parts : the pheasants flew screaming away from us as we passed along, leaving their nests and eggs at our feet; and the half-wild deer scudded through the hay to hide themselves in the wood. After the park, we went through the gardens, orchards, and conservatories. Here the manner of cultivating pines grapes
and peaches, was explained to us by the head gardener. When we were tired, we took our seat in a shady part of the orchard, and were gratefully surprised by having a huge heap of strawberries presented to us. After this we could tramp the two miles to Saffron Walden in good order, though the day was very hot.,Assembled at tea, as on the last occasion, we naturally looked for many of the same faces We particularly missed Mr. R. B. L., from London, and Mr. Macmillan, from Cambridge. Our Secretary, C. Gray, Esq., M.A., Trinity College, informed us, among other things, that the Council of Teachers were going to recommend to us a &ldquo;scheme of studies,&rdquo; which I hope will be soon forwarded to the Magazine. Mr. H. Seeley made an earnest speech, advocating the study of geology, at the same time offering to take a class in that subject during the summer vacation, if a sufficient number came forward &middot; which I think there is great probability of.,After tea, wc visited the remains of the old castle, built soon after the Conquest, the Maze, the Museum, and the church. The Museum greatly interested our party, as it contains a large and very varied collection of English antiquities, and of natural history; all collected and contributed, of late years, by persons who have the good of their town at heart. We spent a considerable time in the large and elegant church and upon its roof. It was built in the time of Henries VI. and VII. Some interesting features of its architecture and ornamentation were pointed out to us by C. Trotter, Esq., Trinity College, one of our teachers. From its roof we had a good view of the hilly country around, and of the common below, studded with cricketers. Here we afterwards had a game ourselves, and finished the day by a moonlight walk to the station. H. J. AUGUST 1, 1861.,LEARNING BY HEART.,I wish in few words to urge that those who come to the College, seek&not; ing education, should consider &ldquo; learning-by-heart &rdquo; as one of the estab&not; lished exercises of the place. At present it is not established. Many persons, as boys, have had enough &ldquo; learning-by-heart;&rdquo; not enough of the right kind, but too much of the wrong kind, or at least of the hard kind; too much Latin syntax, Greek irregular verbs, and such-like ; the taste of which they remember as the champing of iron filings in the mouth. The result of this grim experience, with the help of never-failing indolence, is that now, having reached man&rsquo;s estate, and school-boys no more, they take revenge for the supposed wrongs of their youth, and leave all learning-by-heart behind them, as they do other items of boyhood&rsquo;s Spartan discipline ; they, and reader, I mean we, no longer learn by heart at all.,So it is, undeniably, I think, and a great pity. An excellent, a beauti&not; ful faculty, remains unexercised, unused, ign&ograve;bly idle. Consider for a moment the uses, the services of Memory. In its largest sense Memory is one of the most glorious of the gifts of man, that being of large dis&not; course, who looks before and after; but even in the narrow technical sense in which I now use it, the faculty whereby we possess ourselves of the spoken or written words of other inen, so as to reproduce them at will, its public, historical, and truly educational services have been most illus&not; trious. For instance, for two centuries, the power of memory alone, pre&not; served to the Greek people and the world the poems of Homer; about thirty thousand lines ; some Greeks in those days were willing to learn by heart. Again, through the Middle Ages, the dark ages, when still printing was not, and writing was rare, ballads in every country in Europe passed from generation to generation on the lips of men, a truly noble and truly popular literature. Well, reading has come, and writing, and printin 0- , and cheap printing ; and you may say, What need of such severe labours of memory now? No need of such labours, if you like ; but we are foolish to thrnk that our memory, this literary memory of ours, is henceforth to be idle. The faculty, the power has not faded us. Look at Charles Hall&eacute;, playing sonata after sonata of Beethoven, without book ! But it is not exceptional feats I wish to see, but a general habit and practice Every one can learn by heart ; and I cannot help asking, if good for us in boyhood, in girlhood, why not now ? Are we too old to learn ? or what is it?,As a mere mental gymnastic exercise, a striving with the bare mind re- qurrrng care, precision, and a little honest trouble, the practrce of commit&not; ting to memory, mrght be recommended for educational purposes But far beyond this, it seems to me that a practice of this kind is esneciady (railed for by the circumstances of our time ; and that learning-by-heart so,VOL. III. . 5
far from being superseded by the universality of reading and printing, is for this very reason highly advisable. We read and read, but what? News&not; papers, magazin ss, railway novels, exciting books of travel, as miscella&not; neous and as rapidly succeeding one another as the dishes at a Lord Mayor&rsquo;s dinner. I do not denounce them : I confess I enjoy them, and read the best of them with considerable zest. Perhaps I think breakfast in London without the &lsquo; Times &rsquo; imperfect, and perhaps I like seeing &lsquo; Macmillan&rsquo; early in the month. The world does not want convincing that newspapers and magazines give people much pleasure, and do a deal of good. But nevertheless this &ldquo;Railway Literature,&rdquo; as I will call it, threatens great mischief ; in one word, it threatens to spoil our minds. Imperceptibly we become mere railway-readers, and are content to be so. Railway literature contents us, that is to say, the words of (generally) tenth-rate men, when we might have to speak to us, wise men, the wisest of our own and former times. We read the &lsquo;Cornhill Maga&not; zine,&rsquo; but the great English poets and writers stand dusty on the book&not; shelf. Or, if we do take up a wise book, we do not treat it as such, read&not; ing, marking, learning , inwardly digesting, as the Church prays that the Book may be used ; but we treat it as we do the rest, in true railway style. This, I say, is great mischief, our minds, becoming surfeited with many things, are spoiling. &ldquo; Readers,&rdquo; says a writer to whom I for one am con&not; tinually and most deeply indebted, &ldquo; do not seem to be aware of the fact, but a fact it is, of daily increasing magnitude, and already of terrible im&not; portance to readers, that the first grand necessity in reading is vigilant conscientious selection.&rdquo; Most true, and undoubtedly in these circum&not; stances the best remedy or rather resource is the honest and solid study of sterling literature, reading, if possible, the great works of great men, reading them entire, thinking over them, and traversing the full compass of their minds : this is best ; but greatly serviceable to this end, and in itself most useful, is the minute perusal of their minor works, and even excerpts or fragmentary passages from such authors. And the most thorough form of such perusal is learning-by-heart. The true student will go to work in the spirit, though he need not seek to emulate the life or the exploits of Robert Browning&rsquo;s wonderful grammarian.,&ldquo;&lsquo;What&rsquo;s in the scroll,&rsquo; quoth he, * thou keepest furled?,Show me their shaping,,Their &rsquo;s who most studied man, the bard and sage,&mdash;,Give !&rsquo; So he gowned him,,Straight got by heart that hook to its last page.&rdquo;,Till he has fairly tried it, I suspect a reader does not know how much he would gain from committing to memory passages of real excellence ; precisely because he does not know how much he overlooks in merely read&not; ing. Learn one true poem by heart, and see if you do not find it so ! Beauty after beauty will reveal itself, in chosen phrase, or happy music, or noble suggestion, otherwise undreamed of. It is like looking at one of Nature&rsquo;s wonders through a microscope. Again, how much in such a poem that you really did feel admirable and lovely on a first reading, passes away, if you do not give it a further and much better reading !&mdash;passes away utterly, like a sweet sound, or an image on the lake, which the first breath of wind dispels. If you could only fix that image, as the,.
photographers do theirs, so beautifully, so perfectly ! And you can do so. Learn it by heart, and it is yours for ever.&mdash;I have said, a true poem : for naturally men will choose to learn poetry ; from the beginning of time they have done so. To immortal verse the memory gives a willing, a joyous, and a lasting home. However, some prose is poetical, is poetry, and altogether worthy to be learned by heart ; and the learning is not so very difficult. It is not difficult or toilsome to learn that which pleases us ; and the labour once given is forgotten, while the result remains. Poems, and noble ex&not; tracts, whether of verse or prose, once so reduced into possession and rendered truly our own, may be to us a daily pleasure ; better far than a whole library Moused ! They may come to us, in our dull moments, to refresh us as with spring flowers, in our selfish musings to win us by pure delight from the tyranny of foolish castle-building, self-congratulations, and mean anxieties ; they may be with us in the workshop, in crowded London streets, by the fireside ; sometimes, perhaps, on pleasant hillsides, or by sounding shores, noble friends and companions, our own, never intru&not; sive, ever at hand, coming at our call. Shakspere, Milton, Wordsworth, Tennyson ; the words of such men do not stale upon us, they do not grow old or cold. It was only a short while back that a former member of my class in English composition (now appointed for his own merit to a posi&not; tion of great usefulness and promise), was walking home with me one evening from the College, when he told me he had gained very much from certain short pieces of verse which I had dictated to the class from time to time&mdash;three years before ; and then he began to repeat one to me. This incident perhaps suggested to me to write this paper. It was also suggested to me at one of our most quiet social gatherings ; singing happened to be not feasible, and it then appeared that only two or three of our number knew anything to repeat. Now, reader, if you follow my advice, you will be able to make yourself useful on such an occasion, and to contribute, as no doubt you desire, to the general pleasure. You will have no need, when the independent file-firing from the right begins, to sit mute and impotent, as that musical young lady we know so well in society, who can neither sing nor play, because&mdash;because she has not brought &lsquo; her music with her ! No, out with one of your pieces, like a travelled pilgrim of old, who, in return for hospitality or fellowship, would produce from under his cloak a precious relic, a bit of the true cross, or a rib of St. Andrew. Shakspere our Saint Shakspere&mdash;has ten thousand ribs, every rib multi- pliable without miracle, capable of being in a thousand places at the same time. Your pilgrimage may reach his shrine, his live-long monu&not; ment (there it stands in the College bookshelves).&mdash;Thus others, as well as yourself, will be gainers. Further, though you are young now&rsquo; some day you will be old. Some day you may reach that time when a man lives in greater part for memory and by memory. I can imagine a chance renewal chance visitation of the words long remembered, long garnered in the heart, and I think I see a gleam of rare joy in the eyes of the old man In short, for working men, whose leisure time is short, and precious as scant rations to beleaguered men, I believe there could not be a better ex&not; penditure of time than deliberately giving an occasional hour&mdash;it requires no more to committing to memory chosen passages from great authors l&iacute; working men could be thus daily nourished with a few best words of the,i 2
best English poets and writers ; if the habit of leaniing-by-heart was to become so general that as a matter of course any person presuming to be educated amongst us might be expected to be equipped with a few good pieces well known, 1 believe it would lead, far more than the mere sound of it suggests, to the diffusion of the best kind of literature, and the right appreciation of it ; men would not long rest satisfied with knowing a few stock pieces. And here again the working class might give a lesson to the classes above them ; they have need to profit from the simplicity and earnestness that must belong to the education of working men.,It would be hard to deny that there is not some truth in what I have been saying. The only objection I can conceive is, that it may be said that a relish for higher literature belongs only to the few ; that it is the result of cultivation ; and that there is no use in trying to create what must be in general only a fictitious interest. But I do not admit that literature, even the higher literature, must belong to the few. Poetry is in the main essentially catholic, addressed to all men ; and though some poetrv requires particular knowledge and superior culture, much, and that the noblest, needs only natural feeling and the light of common experience. Such poetry, taken in moderation, followed with genuine goodwill, shared in common, will be intelligible and delightful to most men who will take the trouble to be students at all, and ever more and more so.&mdash;Perhaps also there may be a fragment of truth in what Charles Lamb has said, that any spouting &ldquo; withers and blows upon a fine passage ;&rdquo; that there is no enjoy&not; ing it after it has been &ldquo; pawed about by declamatoiy boys and men. But surely there is a reasonable habit of recitation as well as an unreason&not; able one &middot; there is no need of declamatory pawing. To forswear all reci&not; tation is to abandon a custom which has given delight and instruction to all the races of articulately speaking men. What is public worship itself but the grandest kind of recitation ? If our faces aie set against humbug and vain displav, and set towards rational enjoyment of one anothei, each freely giving his best and freely receiving what his neighbour offers, we need not fear that our social evenings will be marred by an occasional re&not; citation, or that the fine passages will wither. _ And moreover, it is not for reciting&rsquo;s sake that 1 chiefly recommend this most faithful form of reading, learning-by-heart.,I come back therefore to this, that for us learning-by-heart is a good thing, and is neglected amongst us. Why is it neglected P Partly because of our indolence, but partly, I take it, because we do not sufficiently con&not; sider that it is a good thing, and needs to be taken in hand. _ We need to be reminded of it : 1 here remind you. Like a town-criei, ringing my bell in this Magazine, I would say to you, &ldquo; Oyez, oyez ! Lost, stolen, or strayed, a good ancient practice, the good ancient practice of learnmg- by-heart. Every finder shall be handsomely rewarded,&rdquo;,On the practical side of the question little needs to be said. No class is necessary, or perhaps expedient ; nothing but this, Let each resolve to learn by heart some piece or pieces, and to keep them up ; say six pieces. If any ask, &ldquo; What shall I learn ?&rdquo; the answer is, Do as you do with tunes, b&eacute;bin with what you sincerely like best, what you would most wish to remember, what you would most enjoy saying to yourself, or repeating to another. * You will soon find the list inexhaustible. Then &ldquo; keeping up &rdquo;
is easy. Every one has spare ten minutes ; one of the problems of life is how to employ them usefully ; you may well spend some in looking after and securing this good property you have won.,ME. J.
