MILL ON EEPEE SENT ATI V E GOYEENMENT.,[A remarkable book having just been written by a great man, on a great subject re&not; cently discussed in these pages, I prefer to give a sample of its substance to offering a summary in the questionable shape of a review. I have therefore condensed two of the most important chapters, preserving the words of the original except in one or two con&not; necting phrases. All who have read the &lsquo;Logic&rsquo; and &lsquo;Essay on Liberty,&rsquo; not to men&not; tion the &lsquo; Political Economy,&rsquo; will understand why Mr. Mill appears to some of us to be distinguished above all living writers by the union of practical wisdom with the power of philosophical thought. The following extracts will be of more use than most reviews if they induce a few students to make themselves acquainted with one of the greatest minds of our generation.&mdash;R.B.L.],OF TRUK AND FALSE DEMOCRACY ; REPRESENTATION OF ALL, AND REPRESENTATION,OF THE MAJORITY ONLY.,The dangers incident to a representative democracy are of two kinds : danger of a low grade of intelligence in the representative body, and in the popular opinion which con- trois it ; and the danger of class legislation on the part of the numerical majority, these being all composed of the same class. How far is it possible so to organize the democracy as to do away with these two great evils, or at least to abate them, in the utmost degree attainable by human contrivance ?,A completely equal democracy, in a nation in which a single class composes the nu&not; merical majority, cannot be divested of certain evils ; but those evils are greatly aggra&not; vated by the fact, that the democracies which at present exist are not equal, but syste- maticaally unequal in favour of the predominant class. Two very different ideas are usually confounded under the name democracy. The pure idea of democracy, according to its defiuition, is the government of the whole people by the whole people, equally re^ presented. Democracy, as commonly conceived and hitherto practised, is the govern&not; ment of the whole people by a mere majority of the people, exclusively represented. The former is synonymous with the equality of all citizens ; the latter, strangely con&not; founded with it, is a government of privilege, in favour of the numerical majority, who&#39; alone possess practically any voice in the State. This is the inevitable consequence of the manner in which the votes are now taken, to the complete disfranchisement of minorities.,In a representative body actually deliberating, the minority must of course be over&not; ruled ; and in an eqmvl democracy (since the opinions of the constituents, when they in&not; sist on them, determine those of the representative body) the majority of the people, thtough their representatives, will outvote and prevail over the minority and their re- pi esentati ves. But does it follow that the minority should have no representatives at all ? Because the majority ought to prevail over the minority, must the majority have all the votes, the minority none ? Is it necessary that the minority should not even be heard ? Nothing but habit and old association can reconcile any reasonable being to the needless injustice. In a really equal democracy, every or any section would be&deg; repre&not; sented, not disproportionately, but proportionately. A majority of the electors would a ways have a majority of the representatives ; but a minority of the electors would always have a minority of the representatives. Man for man, they would be as fully re&not; presented as the majority. Unless they are, there is not equal government, but a go&not; vernment of inequality and privilege ; one part of the people rule over the rest : there is a part whose fair and equal share of influence in the representation is withheld from them ; contrary to all just government, but above all, contrary to the principle of de&not; mocracy, which professes equality as its very root and foundation. And it is not only the minority who suffer. Democracy, thus constituted, does not even attain its osten-
sihle ohjeet, that of giving the powers of government in all cases to the numerical ma&not; jority. It gives them to a majority of the majority ; who may be, and often are, but a minority of the whole.,The common answer to this is, that as different opinions predominate in different lo&not; calities, the opinion which is in a minority in some places has a majority in others, and thus every opinion obtains on the whole its fair share of representation. But this would be no longer true if the present constituency were much enlarged ; still less, if made co&not; extensive with the whole population; for in that case the majority in every locality would consist of manual labourers ; and when there was any question pending, on which these classes were at issue with the rest of the community, no other class could succeed in getting represented anywhere.,This virtual blotting-out of the minority is diametrically opposed to the first principle of democracy, representation in proportion to numbers. Without the adequate repre&not; sentation of the minority, no real democracy, nothing but a false show of democracy, is possible. For securing this, the best plan yet proposed is that of Mr. Thomas Hare : a scheme which has the almost unparalleled merit, of carrying out a great principle of go&not; vernment in a manner approaching to ideal perfection as regards the special object in view, while it attains incidentally several other ends, of scarcely inferior importance.,According to this plan, the unit of representation, the quota of electors who would be entitled to have a member to themselves, would be ascertained by the ordinary process of taking averages, the number of voters being divided by the number of seats in the House : and every candidate who obtained that quota would be returned, from however great a number of local constituencies it might be gathered. The votes would, at pre&not; selit, be given locally ; but any elector would be at liberty to vote for any candidate, in whatever part of the country he might offer himself. Those electors, therefore, who did not wish to be represented by any of the local candidates, might aid by their vote in the return of the person they liked best among all those throughout the country, who had expressed a willingness to be chosen. This would, so far, give reality to the electoral rights of the otherwise virtually disfranchised minority. But it is important that not those alone who refuse to vote for any of the local candidates, but those also who vote for one of them and are defeated, should be enabled to find elsewhere the representation which they have not succeeded in obtaining in their own district. It is therefore pro&not; vided that an elector may deliver a voting-paper, containing other names in addition to the one which stands foremost in his preference. His vote would only be counted for one candidate ; but if the object of his first choice failed to be returned, from not having obtained the quota, his second perhaps might be more fortunate. He may extend his list to a great number in the order of his preference, so that if the names which stand near the top of the list either cannot make up the quota, or are able to make it up with&not; out his vote, the vote may still be used for some one whom it may assist in returning.,The scheme of which these are the main provisions, secures a number of practical ad&not; vantages which, in my conviction, place Mr. Hare&rsquo;s.plan among the very greatest im&not; provements yet made in the theory and practice of government.,First, it secures a representation, in proportion to numbers, of every division of the electoral body : not two great parties alone, with perhaps a few large sectional minorities in particular places, but every minority in the whole nation, consisting of a sufficiently large number to be, on principles of equal justice, entitled to a representative. Secondly, no elector would, as at present, be nominally represented by some one whom he had not chosen. Every member of the House would be the representative of an unanimous constituency. He would represent a thousand electors, or two thousand, or five thou&not; sand, or ten thousand, as the quota might be, every one of whom would have not only voted for him, but selected him from the whole country.,Of all modes of national representation this one affords the best security for the intel&not; lectual qualifications desirable in the representatives. At present, by universal admis&not; sion, it is becoming more and more difficult for any one, who has only talents and cha&not; racter, to get into the House of Commons. Local influence, lavish expenditure, or the support of one of the two great parties, are the only tickets of admission. On Mr. Hare&rsquo;s system, almost every person in any way honourably distinguished, though devoid of local influence, and having sworn allegiance to no political party, would have a fair chance of making up the quota. Hundreds of able men of indepeudent thought, who would have no chance whatever of being chosen by the majority of any existing con-
stituency, have by their writings, or their exertions in some field of public usefulness, made themselves known and approved by a few persons in almost every district of the kingdom ; and if every vote that would be given for them in every place could be counted for their election, they might he able to complete the number of the quota. In no other way which it seems possible to suggest, would Parliament be so certain of containing the very elite of the country. The minority of instructed minds scattered through the local constituencies, would unite to return a number, proportioned to their own numbers, of the very ablest men the country contains. If the presence of even a few such in the re&not; presentative assembly can be ensured, though the remainder consist only of average minds, the influence of these leading spirits is sure to make itself sensibly felt in the general deliberations, even though they be kuown to be, in many respects, opposed to the tone of popular opinion and feeling. I am unable to conceive any mode by which the presence of such minds can be so positively ensured, as by that proposed by Mr. Hare.,OP THE EXTENSION OP THE SUFFRAGE.,Such a representative democracy as has now been sketched, representative of all, and not solely of the majority, would be free from the greatest evils of the falsely-called de&not; mocracies which now prevail, and from which the current idea of democracy is&quot;exclusively derived. But even in this democracy, absolute power, if they chose to exercise it, would rest with the numerical majority ; and these would be composed exclusively of a single class, alike in Liasses, prepossessions, and general modes of thinking, and a class, to say no more, not the most highly cultivated.,A modern community may be considered as in the main divisible into two sections, which, in spite of partial variations, correspond on the whole with two divergent direc&not; tions of apparent interest. Let us call them (in brief general terms) labourers on the one hand, employers of labour on the other : including however a&icirc;ong with employers of labour, not only retired capitalists, and the possessors of inherited wealth, but all that highly paid description of labourers (such as the professions) whose education and way of lite assimilate them with the rich, and whose prospect and ambition it is to raise them&not; selves into that class. With the labourers, on the other hand, many be ranked those smaller employers of labour, who by interest, habits, and educational impressions, are assimilated in wishes, tastes, and objects to the labouring classes; comprehending a large proportion of petty tradesmen.,ihat the suffrage should be, with certain slight abatements, universal, I regard as ab&not; solutely necessary to an enlarged and elevated conception of good government. Yet in this state of things, the great majority of voters, in most countries, and emphatically in this, would be manual labourers; and the twofold danger, that of too low a standard of political intelligence, and that of class legislation, would still exist, in a very perilous de&not; gree. It remains to be seen whether any means exist by which these evils can be obviated.,They are capable of being obviated, if men sincerely wish it ; not by any artificial con&not; trivance, but by carrying out the natural order of human life, which recommends itself to every one m things in which he has no interest or traditional opinion running counter to it. In all human affairs, every person directly interested, and not under positive tu- telage, has an admitted claim to a voice, and when his exercise of it is not inconsistent with the safety of the whole, cannot justly be excluded from it. But (thouo-h everv one ought to have a voice) that every one should have an equal voice is a totally dif ferent proposition. When two persons who have a joint interest in any business differ m opinion, does justice require that both opinions should be held of exactly equal value? If one of the two is the wiser or better man, he has a claim to superior weight &middot; the diffi- culty is in ascertaining which of the two it is; a thing impossible as between individuals but, taking men in bodies and in numbers, it can be done with a sufficient approach to,To have no voice in what are partly his own concerns, is a thing which nobody willingly submits to ; but when what is partly his concern is also partly another&rsquo;s, and \ e f T&Igrave; S , the other 1 t &deg; understand the subject better than himself, that the other&rsquo;s opinion should be counted for more than his own, accords with his expectations, and with the course of things which in all other affairs of life he is accustomed to acquiesce in. It is only necessary that this superior influence should be assigned on grounds which he can comprehend, and of which he is able to perceive the justice.
Property is a kind of test ; but this is only admissible as a temporary makeshift. The only thing which can justify reckoning one person&rsquo;s opinion as equivalent to more than one, is individual mental superiority ; and what is wanted is some approximate means of ascertaining that. If there existed such a thing as a really national edu&not; cation, or a trustworthy system of general examination, education might be tested di&not; rectly. In the absence of a direct test, such as might be supplied by a really national system of education, the nature of a person&rsquo;s occupation is some test. An employer of labour is on the average more intelligent than a labourer; for he must labour with his head, and not solely with his hands. A foreman is generally more intelligent than an ordinary labourer, and a skilled labourer than an unskilled. A banker, merchant, or manufacturer, is likely to be more intelligent than a tradesman, because he has larger and more complicated interests to manage. The superior function and the successful per&not; formance of it, are tests of the qualifications. A liberal profession, as implying, of course, a still higher degree of instruction, might give title at once a plurality of votes. The same rule might be applied to graduates of universities, and to those who pass &ldquo;local&rdquo; or &ldquo; middle class &rdquo; examinations established by competent bodies. But it is no use to dis&not; cuss here the detailed application of the principle.,Plural voting, though practised in other elections, is so unfamiliar in elections to Par&not; liament, that it is not likely to be soon or willingly adopted : but as the time will cer&not; tainly arrive when the only choice will be between this and equal universal suffrage, whoever does not desire the last, cannot too soon begin to reconcile himself to the former. Until there shall have been devised, and until opinion is willing to accept, some mode of plural voting which may assign to education as such the degree of superior in&not; fluence due to it, and sufficient as a counterpoise to the numerical weight of the least educated class : for so long, the benefits of completely universal suffrage cannot be ob&not; tained without bringing with them, as it appears to me, more than equivalent evils.,So much importance do I attach to the emancipation of those who already have votes, but whose votes are useless, because always outnumbered, that I should not indeed despair of the operation even of equal and universal suffrage, if made real by the propor&not; tional representation of all minorities, on Mr. Hare&rsquo;s principle. But I should in any case still contend for the principle of plural voting. I look upon equal voting as in principle wrong, because recognizing a w r roug standard, and exercising a bad influence on the voter&rsquo;s mind. It is not useful, but hurtful, that the constitution of the country should declare ignorance to be entitled to as much political power as knowledge. The national institutions should place all things that they are concerned with, before the mind of the citizen in the light in which it is for his good that he should regard them : and as it is for his good that he should think that every one is entitled to some influence, but the better and wiser to more than others, it is important that this conviction should be professed by the State, and embodied in the national institutions. Such things con&not; stitute the spirit of the institutions of a country : that portion of their influence which is least regarded by common, and especially by English, thinkers ; though the institutions of every country, not under great positive oppression, produce more effect by their spirit than by any oftheir direct provisions, since by it they shape the national character. The American institutious have imprinted strongly on the American mind, that any one man (with a white skin) is as good as any other ; and it is felt that this false creed is nearly connected with some of the more unfavourable points in American character. It is not a small mischief that the constitution of any country should sanction this creed; for the belief in it, whether express or tacit, is almost as detrimental to moral and intellectual excellence, as any effect which most forms of government can produce.,AN IDLE TOUR IN SURREY AND SUSSEX.,&ldquo; Voyager &agrave; pied, e&rsquo;est voyager comme Hom&egrave;re, Thales, et Pythagore.&rdquo;,It is given, I trust, to most men to put off the &ldquo; Collar of Misery &rdquo; for a few days at least in the year, and to get away from this smoky city to the trees and fields. The fields ! what visions to a jaded Londoner do they not call up ! &ldquo; The walks by silent
scented paths&mdash;pastures besides the pacing brooks&mdash;the rests in noonday heat&mdash;crisp lawns all dim with early dew.&rdquo;,At such times it becomes of course a serious question whither we shall go. Brilliant cartoon-like placards on the hoardings about make enticing offers of cheap trips every&not; where almost. And certainly, if one only had a fair allowance of time, and a reasonable command of funds, there is no lack of choice. But if there should be any &ldquo; forlorn and weary brother,&rdquo; who has only a few days to spare, and&mdash;alas that any good fellow should be so plagued !&mdash;a limited supply of the needful, I would ask if it ever occurred to him to try a knapsack and the Surrey Hills.,Probably very few of those who read this paper do not know Leith Hill. If there should be any such, let them hie away thither, and then, with a week or so&rsquo;s holiday be&not; fore them, see what they think of the sensation for a change. From the top of the hill, it is said, we can overlook an area of eighteen hundred square miles ; and besides&mdash;sight refreshing to a cockney&mdash;with the eyes of faith, may see through Beeting Gap, the sea beyond. It has been my good fortune to make short walking trips in the neighbourhood at divers times, and I am anxious to induce all I can who may be similarly situated with myself as regards time and funds, to try an excursion of the kind in preference to a vapid week at the sea-side. Among the most delightful of these walks was one I made last autumn, in company with several fellow-students and old friends, with some of whom I had tramped many a mile before, through &ldquo; rain and shine.&rdquo; As an inducement to others to go and do likewise, I will endeavour to give a short account of it. The starting-place,was Dorking. This I missed, being detained in town. The others, T-, В -, and,J. В -, accordingly walked on to Guildford, cleanest of country towns, where I met,them on the third morning of their departure. I found them at 9 a.m., comfortably quartered at &lsquo; The Pigeons &rsquo; inn, of that place, breakfasting most solidly off mutton- chops, taking, as they put it, their farewell of butchers&rsquo; meat and civilization for a period ! My early ride had made me peckish, so I gladly joined in. By eleven o&rsquo;clock, having laid in our luncheon, we were fairly on our way over the &ldquo; Hog&rsquo;s-back,&rdquo; and through no end of lovely lanes, to Petersfield, a quaint old place, containing an eques&not; trian statue to King William of blessed memory. It was the crowning effort apparently of the town councillors of the day, for the towu looks in a very sleepy condition now, and not at all up to erecting a statue. The next day we were off to Chichester, getting a distant glimpse of Goodwood by the way. We fortunately went over the Cathedral, and saw its curious Norman monuments, now all dust. Our next resting-place was Bog- nor. We found it very dull and slow. A pull ou the sea was tried, but the tide was coming in with such a heavy swell we were obliged to put back. From here we struck inland to Arundel : on our way there it unfortunately began to rain rather heavily, and,we got benighted, and lost our way, В -, who had the conduct of us, having been,the road before, declares we were not lost ; but I am inclined to think we were, else why did he make so frantic an attempt to get over a gate and wake up some people at a cottage near the road ? However, at last the lights began to twinkle through the trees, and we reached Arundel. We were in a terribly washed-out condition in consequence of the rain, aud sorely afraid the inn people wouldn&rsquo;t take us in. After due deliberation we deputed the smartest man amongst us to make terms, if possible, which, after the landlord had been out to look at us, he succeeded in doing. The inn we found a very comfortable one : the landlord very obliging. The next morning there was a nasty- drizzle on, and we didn&rsquo;t half like the look of it. So while we were making up our minds we went up the town to try and get admission to the Castle, iu which I am sorry to sav we failed. },Ultimately we came to the conclusion&mdash;the wish being of course father to the thought &mdash;that it would hold up, and decided on a pull up the Arun, with the rising tide. Soon after our start the sun came out and away we went, skirting on one side Arundel Park, with its magnificent trees down to the water&rsquo;s edge, and past rich meadows on the other, a scene of beauty of its kind hardly to be surpassed ;. but by-and-by the clouds began t&oacute; gather again, and finally the weather reverted to its original state of drizzle. Mean&not; while we had been growing thirsty. &ldquo;Where&rsquo;s the nearest public?&rdquo; we ask of some men at a limekiln. &ldquo; At Bury.&rdquo; &ldquo; How far ?&rdquo; &ldquo; Half a mile up !&rdquo; As the drizzle is,thickening into a heavy rain we must push on. &ldquo; Pull a longer stroke, J. В _, and,you В -see if you can&rsquo;t keep better time, else we shan&rsquo;t get any beer today !&rdquo; &rsquo; The,cox wain is implored to cut off the corners, for the Arun is a wonderful river, turning and
doubling on itself in an incredible manner. On we go, stimulated by hopes of imme&not; diate beer, steered very cleverly by J. B&mdash;&mdash;- through a queer old bridge absurdly put at a bend of the river, expressly, I presume, to bring amateur boating-men to grief. The coxwain thinks he sees chimneys through the trees. Yes, here&rsquo;s the village at last. Pull bow, steady, hard all ! and we are ashore, the boat being carefully moored np a creek under a small bridge. In our joy at the prospect of &ldquo;quenching,&rdquo; we are almost tempted to sing with the lost Peri,,&ldquo;Joy, joy for ever, the bridge is past,,And we are safe at Bury at last!&rdquo;,But alas ! no signs of a public in this well-regulated village. We were only a mile and a half from the village proper, and have yet a twenty minutes&rsquo; walk up a muddy lane for the long-desired bread-and-cheese and ale. But such ale ! where they got it from I know not, but it was far and away the best I ever drank in my life. Possibly our sen&not; sations had a good deal to do with it ; but we are all ready to declare that we never got anything like it before, nor have we since.,The rain is not abating yet, but the afternoon is getting on and we must be stirring, for we must be off with the turn of the tide, or we shan&rsquo;t be back by dark. Down the lane we go, at a good pace&mdash;damp, but Mark Tapley-like, jolly no end&mdash;&ldquo; Holloa ! what&rsquo;s come to the river ?&rdquo; It&rsquo;s nearly three times the width it was before ! It appears that this is one of the highest tides, and the wind, setting up stream, has sent it half across,the fields on the other side, and nearly up to the ferry door-step on this. A stack of,bricks that was high and dry when we landed, is now surrounded by water. The tide must be near turning, so we must get out the boat ready. T-and J. В -ac&not;,cordingly tucked up their trousers, and leaving their boots in my charge, paddled through the water to the boat. They found the tide had risen so high that the rowlocks caught,the bridge. J. B.-accordingly went across to render further help, and T got,in the boat in order to sink it a bit. Suddenly I heard J. В -singing out to me, in an,excited manner to &ldquo; make haste, for T-is in the river.&rdquo; In he was certainly, and,J. В - was most effectually preventing his getting out by holding on his hand like,&ldquo; grim death.&rdquo; By the time I got over he was back in the boat, all right, and by dint of great perseverance we got the boat out into the stream. But a start was impossible, as the wind was dead in our teeth, and the tide not yet turned. Ashore again we went for shelter from the still heavy rain.,It was a sight to make a hypochondriac die of laughter to see us&mdash;veritable mudlarks&mdash; each with his boots in his hand, and his trousers above his knees, cautiously picking his,way through the water over the pebbles ! J-, who w as in the rear, gives a ludicrous,account of our appearance. The good ferrywoman kindly had compassion on us, and took us in, notwithstanding our draping condition and the damage her bright red-brick floor was likely to suffer therefrom. She soon made up a capital wood fire, and enabled us to dry our clothes. At last the tide did turn, and w r e were speedily pulling down the river. Now and then the rain abated somewhat, but in didn&rsquo;t cease entirely for long to&not; gether. It seemed a long way back ; sometimes we were on the right of the Castle, and sometimes on the left, without apparently getting much nearer to it; but patience, as it ever does, got its reward in the end, and w r e reached Arundel Bridge just before dark, reasonably wet through. The landlord fortunately had a good stock of wearing apparel, which he very liberally lent us ; and after a wash, we sat down to a substantial knife-and-,fork tea, to which, if I remember w ell, we did ample justice. Dear J. В -, neatest and,best of swells, what a Guy he looked in the landlord&rsquo;s clothes, double the size of him !,If he had found himself in that attire in London it would have broken his heart, but down there he didn&rsquo;t seem to mind it. And his bright-blue coat too, a shapeless mass of saturated cloth ! that, he owns, cost him a pang, but &ldquo; not much.&rdquo; This shows, I take it, the strengthening effect on, the nerves of a few days&rsquo; walking. From here we made for Petworth, where we had arranged to meet L. S-and W-, fellow pil&not;,grims in many a long walk before. The way lay through Arundel Park, loveliest com&not; bination of hills and dales, and over the downs beyond, past scores of tumuli of long- forgotten fighting-men. Our instructions w r ere to avoid Dempster&rsquo;s Hotel, at Petworth ; but, as fate would have it, all the other inns were full, and we had no choice. We were there ushered into a large and stylishly-furnished room, with many misgivings on our part touching the reckoning when the feast should be o&rsquo;er. J. В -, whose love of
ease and luxury I have already hinted at, at once rose to the dignity of the position, and proposed to send the &ldquo; boots &rdquo; to the post-office for letters, from which proceeding he was with considerable difficulty restrained. I need hardly say that we passed a very jolly,evening. In the morning we went down to the Station and met L. S-and W-,,who came by an early train. They were dazzled somewhat by the style of breakfast set before them, and we of course had to explain that this apparent extravagance had been caused wholly by the necessity we were under of staying at Petworth to meet them. Our bill luckily proved very considerably more moderate than we had calculated on.,We had met with such good quarters at the &lsquo;White Horse&rsquo; inn, Haselmere, and found the country round about so charming, that after much consultation we determined to go back there and stop a day or two, and explore the environs. We unfortunately omitted to call at Lord Leconsfield&rsquo;s house to see the &ldquo; Turners &rdquo; and &ldquo; Leslies,&rdquo; having been deterred therefrom by the excessively heavy blackmail said to be levied by his servants on all visitors. _We however went through his lovely park, where, by the way, he has set up a thousand-yards range, and it could easily be extended. We were di&not; rected to go through the Deer Park ; the gate of this we found locked, and an old man in charge, who strongly impressed on us that it was at his &ldquo; option &rdquo; to let us through, and sharply rebuked a young woman, apparently his daughter, who put in a word in our favour. After a good deal of cajoling, and a little bribery and corruption, helet us pass, having expressed an opinion that we looked like people who w r ere &ldquo; tramping about,&rdquo; and therefore, I presume, deserving of little consideration from him. The Deer Park is in a very wild state, and swarming with game. This short way, as usual, proved the longest, for we contrived to miss the way ; but that is half the fun of these cross-country jaunts, you get in such out-of-the-way places. The evening, nevertheless, found us at our old quarters at Haselmere, the landlady and her daughters beina; evidently very glad to see us. Here we spent a couple of days, making excursions to the hills about, one of which, Blackdown, is said to be highest in the county. Prom its shmmit you certainly get a view right round, and overlooking an area which I should be afraid to estimate. The Devil&rsquo;s Punch-Bowl near here is a sight by no means to be missed. It lies just off the Portsmouth Hoad, and I suppose is the largest natural amphitheatre in England. A stone is erected on the top, by the road, in memory of a sailor who was murdered on the spot. His murderers were captured and hung in chains hard by. It is a lonely place now, and presents a striking contrast from what it must have been in coaching times. Of the surpassing beauties of the whole neighbourhood I am hopelessly in&not; capable to give the smallest idea. If any of my readers desire to know more, I can only recommend them earnestly to go and see for themselves. The landlady of the * White Horse,&rsquo; and her charming daughters, will be only too happy to make them comfortable at a small charge. One instance of their reasonable charges may suffice. They one day suggested the expediency of making ns, by way of change&quot; (having possibly a quantity of latent power in this line of cookery), an apple pie. The suggestion being highly ap&not; proved by us, they accordingly produced at dinner a pie of marvellous dimensions. J&middot; В -, a connoisseur in pastry, praised it unreservedly. The charge for this was three&not;,pence each. Apple pie practically ad lib. for threepence ! Frequenters of London cookshops can appreciate this.,&Lambda;&lambda; e were all loath to leave so charming a spot, but only a few days&rsquo; more leave remained to us, consequently we had to turn our steps towards London. Very reluctantly we packed up and bade good-bye to Haselmere, hoping, I trust not in vain, to see it again altogether some day. We had had lovely weather while there, but the m ornino&middot; of our departure the rain came down quite heavily. We took the train to Guildford^ hoping the rain would abate, but the fates were pitiless ; for when we had reached Guildford it was coming down as bard as ever. After lunch it was put to the vote whether we should ride on to Dorking, or walk. We were very anxious not to miss the country between, which some of us had not seen, and decided on walking accordingly. We preferred taking the tops of the downs, as affording the only chance of a view, but the dense rain rendered the country scarcely visible at a few hundred yards off. Gradually the rain abated ; and by the time we had.got a few miles from Dorking it ceased. We had a look at Wootton by the way. The humble house at Wootton,&quot; of which John Evelyn spoke with such mock humility, knowing all the time, as Leigh Hunt says &ldquo; that it was the envy of half the people at Court, and not at all a humble one.&rdquo; &rsquo; We reached Dorking in good time, putting up at our old quarters, the Bell Inn, and spent the
next day at Box Hill with some friends who had come down from London for a picnic. It was very nice and jolly, but we couldn&rsquo;t help remembering that it was our last day together, and we were very loath to separate after having spent so pleasant a time in,company. At night we parted, T-and I alone remaining. We two had determined,to have a quiet day for the last in looking up our old haunts. The following morning was fortunately fine, and going through Barday&rsquo;s Wood, we made our way across-country to Leith Hill, and thence through Evelyn&rsquo;s magnificent woods to Wootton Church, which contains John Evelyn&rsquo;s monument, that of his father, and indeed of most of the family since. We returned to Dorking a little &#39;after dark, and after tea took the train to London, all the better, I trust, in every way for our fortnight&rsquo;s tour.,If you sicken of,&ldquo; The dull mechanic pacings to and fro,,The set grey life,&rdquo;,there is no restorative like this. The expense is about five shillings a day, and need not exceed this. A light over-coat is very useful in case of rain, and a few pairs of woollen socks ; but the grand maxim, after all, is to take as few things as possible, for they get terribly heavy under a broiling sun. The boots should be thick lace ones.,The daily march should not exceed fifteen miles or so. Mr. Ruskin says ten or twelve only. His words are, &ldquo; A quiet walk along, of not more than ten or twelve miles a day, is the most amusing of all travelling ; and all travelling becomes dull in exact proportion to its rapidity.&rdquo; But since in any given distance we ordinary mortals could not see one tithe of the beauties he would discover, we may fairly wulk half as far again. There is no way like this to get to know the country, its bye-ways as well its highways. And then consider the mental photographs of bright country scenes you bring home, to be called up at will on all manner of dull London days. For something very considerable I would not be without my small stock. Hear Mr. Ruskin once again : &quot; A man who loves travelling would as soon consent to pack a day of such travelling into an hour of railroad, as one who loved eating would agree, if it were possible, to concentrate his dinner into a pill.&rdquo;,So much for a specimen of a fortnight&rsquo;s country walk ; and now, my &ldquo; forlorn and weary brother,&rdquo; when you next have a few days&rsquo; holiday go and do likewise ; and if you enjoy it as much as we did, you will not regret the few minutes spent in reading this sketch of our operations.,W. The College will be ultimately divided into Six Classes.,2. The First will consist of the general body of merely Matriculated Students.
3. The Second Class will consist of Certificated Students, or those to whom at the end of any course shall be awarded a Certificate of Competency in the subject of the course.,4. The Third Class will consist of Scholars (in Art, History, Language, Literature, Mathematics, or Physics, etc.), or those who prove themselves competent in any one branch of the College Studies.,5. The Fourth Class will consist of those who are thought worthy to be Associates, or permanent Members of the College. From these we require competency not only in some specific study, but in the most important studies of each department of the College. Each student will have an opportunity of answering questions in those subjects in.which he takes most interest; but he will be expected to prove that he is acquainted with (1) the Bible History, (2) the History of England, (3) the Principles of English Grammar, and the Elements of (4) some pure Mathematical, and (5) some Physical study, or some branch of applied Mathematics, as Mechanics. The object will be to ascertain that he has the knowledge of those subjects which every person educated in a College ought to have; not to elicit his opinions either in Theology or Politics. The Associate must have attended the College for Eight Terms.,6. The Fifth Class will consist of Fellows : these will be chosen out of the Associates. The Fellows will take part in the education of this College, and may assist in establishing and carrying on other Colleges. They will be chosen by the Council, alter due con&not; sideration of what they have done in the Associate Examination, and of what they have been since they entered the College. Moral qualities, a capacity tor teaching, and a will&not; ingness to teach, will be at least as indispensable as proficiency in any branch of study.,7. The Sixth Class will consist of the Council. It is intended that the Council be hereafter recruited from the Fellows, to the extent of at least a third of its members. The Council will be prepared to assist in the formation of new Colleges, in London or other parts of England.,EXAMINATION-REGULATIONS.,1. Every teacher shall examine his class at the end of each Term, in the work of the Term, either in or out of the regular class hours, and either orally or by writing.,2. At the end of every complete course an Examination shall be held in the subject of,the course. , n,3. A part, at least, of every such Examination shall be oral, and the Council snail seek to procure the services of such Examiners as will undertake to attend peisonally, the duty of orally examining to be discharged by the last teacher o&iacute; the class it no othei Examiner can be obtained.,4. At each Examination at the end of a course, certificates of competence shall be awarded to those who shall merit them ; and the names of those who, though not re&not; ceiving such certificates, shall have passed the examination with credit, shall be posted,in the hall of the College. . ,,5. Every student attending any course is expected to attend the Examination at the close of that course or the next similar Examination ; and any student having passed any such Examination with credit may offer himself at the next following Examination foi a certificate of competence.,6. Special Examinations for certificates of competence shall be discontinued, unless the Council shall at any time order such an Examination after particular consideration of the circumstances under which it shall be applied for.,VOCAL MUSIC CLASSES,,For teaching Sight-Singing on the Old English method, open to persons of both sexes, and of any age, are held as follows :&mdash;,Thursday, 8-9.30.
