SEPTEMBER 1, 1861.,THE SOCIAL SCIENCE ASSOCIATION AND THE EMPLOY&not; MENT OP WOMEN.,The recent meeting at Dublin has brought round again the annual public discussion of many important social questions. The reports in the daily papers are too meagre to give those who have no time to attend the meeting much notion of the real work done by the Association ; but the impression formed from this source, such as it is, can scarcely but be one of disappointment. The weakness of the Association&rsquo;s work is of the kind indicated by the imperfection of the name. It certainly was not an easy thing to find a description in three words for the miscellaneous operations with which this annual congress busies itself ; but it was a pity that, at all risks, its founders did not avoid the use of a title based upon a fiction. Social Science is, as yet, but a name. The thing is not ; and there is no hope of its ever existing until people are quite convinced that, as yet, it does not exist. The name Science represents the condition of human knowledge when it has reached its most perfect and powerful form, when the entire mass of known facts has been reduced into a state of perfect mutual co-ordination, and when the effects of given causes can be predicted with certainty. To call anything short of this Science, is to play with the word. The misuse of the word would be of no consequence if it was merely a question of terms ; but habits of speech govern habits of thought ; and when people begin to call a thing by a wrong name, they are likely to end by forgetting what the thing really is that the name should mean. Now, there are perfect and imperfect sciences. Meteorology, for instance, is an imperfect science, because, though its fundamental principles are clear, and it presents no fact which it cannot explain, yet the facts have as yet proved too multitudinous and various in their combinations to allow of the prediction of results. We know quite well what produces rain, and yet we cannot tell whether to-morrow will be fine or not. Astronomy we call a perfect science, because there is no known fact about the motion of the heavenly bodies which has not been shown necessarily to follow from the one primary fact of the mutual attraction of matter. &lsquo; But in no conceivable sense can the various subjects whose names give titles to the &lsquo;&lsquo;sections&rdquo; of the Dublin congress,&mdash;Jurisprudence, Education, Social Economy, and the rest,&mdash;be called Sciences. Infinite masses of fact come under discussion, but nobody pretends that, as yet, any considerable group of facts has been completely and satisfactorily explained , or that the results of any given set of causes can be predicted with such certainty as to give a trustworthy basis for future action. The nearest approach to a social science is perhaps what we call &ldquo; Political Economy.&rdquo; The group
of laws implied in that term are at least absolutely and demonstrably true, subject to the correctness of a certain primary assumption. But this last, as we all know, is happily only an approximation to the truth. It is not the fact, of any set of human beings, that each thinks of nothing else than how to get as much as possible out of the rest. Those subjects again, where social results turn on physiological causes, furnish materials for what some day may grow into a science. It is quite certain that dirt and disease and physical misery tend to produce crime and social degrada&not; tion. So far the sketch of a law has been struck out ; but a thousand mo&not; difying causes check its universal action. Some very clean people are very great scoundrels, and some very good people live in nasty cottages. It will be a long time before one universal law can be made to include all these exceptional cases. Meanwhile, the best thing to be done is to work at the drainage, and, if necessary, talk about the best ways of doing it ; only do not let us call this business &ldquo; Social Science,&rdquo; A roomful of mi&not; nisters of different denominations, talking about religious and secular sys&not; tems of education, are not advancing social science, any more than the competitors at a rifle-match are advancing the science of dynamics.,With this reservation, every one must admit that a gathering of those most interested in great social questions, for the interchange of ideas and discussion of projects of reform, must be immensely useful.* Though the oral discussion is very often barren of any useful results, the recorded in&not; vestigations of the members on various subjects may be of great value. The Association would have existed to some purpose if it had only pub&not; lished the &ldquo; Report of the Committee on Strikes and Trades&rsquo; Unions.&rdquo; That volume has ab&rsquo;eady done very much towards dispelling a mass of po&not; pular prejudice. ^,Another subject, in which all ranks of the community, and, most of all, the working-classes, have a most vital interest, is that of the employment of women. To this the Association has given great prominence. They are patronizing or encouraging certain industrial experiments for introducing women to various employments, some of which have hitherto been exer&not; cised by men alone. A correspondent thinks that this subject is one which may be discussed with advantage in these pages. It touches most nearly the interests of working men and their families, as well as the ques&not; tion of female education. Another reason for handling it here is that, as it seems to me, the contra of the question has not been fairly heard. Any objections to the experiment have generally been met with some such off&not; hand reply as, &ldquo; Oh! its only men&rsquo;s jealousy of women&rsquo;s abilities as if it was quite impossible that opposition could proceed from anything else than this idle prejudice. Now, there are serious reasons for thinking that this introduction of women to men&rsquo;s employments is a most pernicious project. The grounds for the experiment are plausible enough, but the arguments against it are overwhelming. One alone ought to be conclusive.,* One might wish certainly that there were a little less personal laudation of the President. The services of Lord Brougham to his country have been too signal to need being recapitulated every year in speeches of some hours&rsquo; duration. Surely he must get tired of going year after year to various parts of the United Kingdom, only to be re&not; assured that he is of &ldquo;the highest order of human intellect,&rdquo; etc. Vide Lord Mayor&rsquo;s speech at Dublin, and the speeches at the &ldquo;Working Men&rsquo;s&rdquo; meeting at Glasgow.
The addition of any number of workers to the ranks of a trade must di&not; minish the average of wages. It would seem absurd that this should need proof, when reason and everyday experience make the fact so mani&not; fest. It would be ridiculous to waste print in proving an apparently self- evident proposition, were it not that the advocates of female labour deli&not; berately deny it. Among the papers read at the Dublin Meeting was one by Miss Emily Eaith full, entitled &ldquo;Women Compositors,&rdquo; reported (appa&not; rently verbatim) in the &lsquo; Daily News,&rsquo; of 19th August.,The principal object,&rdquo; says Miss Faithfull, &ldquo; of this paper is to controvert the opinion, that the result of the introduction of women into the printing trade will be the reduction of the present rate of wages.&rdquo;,dhis is as if a mathemetician should read a paper to controvert the opi&not; nion that two sides of a triangle are greater than the third. The chief difficulty in replying to him would be in knowing where to begin ; whether he would admit, for instance, that two straight lines cannot enclose a space. If Miss Eaithfull is right, every page yet written on political economy is nonsense. The labours of the working class at any moment are paid out of what for shortness is called the &ldquo;wages fund.&rdquo; The more labourers share it, the less each gets. What conceivable answer is there to this ? Take the facts in the mass or in detail, and you are brought to the same result. Eoi example, take the case of a little self-supporting, non-exporting State (say a German Duchy or the republic of San Marino) of a thousand families. Suppose that the labour of the male population gives each an average income of &pound;20 a year. Suppose a man and his family could do twice the work of the man alone. If one family set to work, it would earn &pound;40 ; but if the 999 did the same, they would get&mdash;how much? &pound;40,000 between them, perhaps? But where is it io come from? The wages fund represents the. aggregate of the excess of past labour over past consumption, and only increases slowly by a process of gradual saving. The fact is, the ten thousand families will get just the wages they did be&not; fore (subject to this small gradual increase), the nominal prices of things will be reduced, and the increased labour will give an increased ao-o-reo-ate of commodities to be consumed. The question, then, for the little com&not; munity will then be, Whether the superfluities produced by their women&rsquo;s labour are worth more or less than the work left undone. Whether to have comfortable homes and educated children is better or worse than to turn their country into one big factory, for the admiration of mankind In a commercial countiy like ours, the question is substantially the same If every man now at work in England was to get his wife or sister to work with him, and do as much as himself, the effect would presently be, that all together would be earning just the same as the men alone did before and doing double the work. The first effect would be to increase the number out of employment ; for in most trades the demand for the commodity is united. This excess _ of unemployed would force down wages to their lowest point ; but ultimately the surplus labour would find its way into channels where the demand is capable of extension, and most of the in&not; creased produce would be made away with in an increased export trade Mow during the progress of this change the employer of labour would gam it is scarcely necessary to point out. Part of the present cost of,к 2
labour to employers is the cost of supporting the women and children whom their fathers and husbands at present prefer not making hand&not; workers. If these women and children are set to work, the employer will be getting their work out of them for nothing. He will be only paying them directly what he now pays them indirectly. The question, therefore, is one of choice between two different kinds of work. Are the women you are finding employment for now idle ? Have mothers and sisters and wives nothing to do ? Is the working man&rsquo;s home too happy ; his chil&not; dren too well educated? Has his wife so little to do, that we must send her off to the factory to make cotton prints for the Fans of Central Africa? Which is best, that his children shall learn something to do them good as human creatures, or that the Queen of Madagascar shall have a Birmingham teapot? For a man to complain that a woman comes and takes his wages is, as Mr. Hastings says,* a mean plea. But this is not the complaint. We are jealous not of the women, but for them. We stick to the old tradition, that labour in itself is a curse, not a blessing. When people talk of the blessing of having work to do, they mean that any exertion of moral effort is good ; but there are a hundred better ways of employing moral effort than in providing the mere materials of existence. Not labour, but labour&rsquo;s result, is good ; the process itself we try to cut down to the lowest conceivable minimum.,But let us see by what reasons Miss Faithfull would induce us to ac&not; cept her new doctrine. The following lines comprise all she devotes to this, the principal object of the paper ;&mdash;,&ldquo; The argument that the wages of men will be reduced by the introduction of women into the business was also urged against the introduction of machinery, a far more power&not; ful invader of man&rsquo;s labour than women&rsquo;s hands : but this has fallen before the test of experience. The real and only consequences will be an increase of the productive power of the country, and a slight readjustment of wages ; and while heads of families will be relieved of some of the burdens that now press on them so heavily, there is no ground for the fear that the scale of remuneration earned by them will be really injured : the percentage withdrawn will be so small, that the loss will be proportionally less than the burden from which they will be relieved, for as the percentage destined for the support of such dependants is necessarily distributed to all men indiscriminately, whether their relations in life require it or not, it is inadequate to meet the real burden borne by such as have these said dependants. It has been asserted that the f key-note to the employ&not; ment of women is cheap labour &rsquo;&mdash;that while the professed cry is to open a new and remunerative field for the employment of women, the real object is to lessen the cost of production. It is not necessary to give this statement, so far as the printing is concerned, any further denial than that which is found in the fact that the wages paid to the com&not; positors of the Victoria Press are according to the men&rsquo;s recognized scale. The women work together in companies, with a &lsquo; clicker &rsquo; to each companionship, and they write their bills on the same principle and are paid at the same rate as in men&rsquo;s offices.&rdquo;,The &ldquo; slight readjustment &rdquo; may be granted. The family man on the new system would gain the amount lost by the unmarried : the total re&not; maining the same. But the question as affecting society in the broad must be discussed on the assumption that the community is an aggregate of families, t,So it is on these grounds that Miss Faithfull would have us give up the,* Vide Report of Discussion. f The family&rsquo;s percentage of loss would be in the ratio of available female labour to total labour ; the gain would be in the far smaller ratio of non-family to family labour.
A B C of Political Economy. Can she be serions? Does she really think that the price which the Victoria Press may choose to pay for its female labour, can affect the question of its real value ? Twelve young Avomen working in Great Coram Street, will not, of course, change the average much ; but in the degree in which the movement succeeds, in the same degree it will lower wages. Suppose the current price of potatoes in Covent Garden Market is 1 d. a pound, and to-morrow morning the usual supply is doubled, the price will infallibly fall, though the first few handfuls will be sold at the rate of to-day. In face of this self-evident necessity, what is the use of talking in a weak strain of hopeful conjecture of there being &ldquo; no ground for the fear of a reduction&rdquo; in the scale of remu- neiaţion ? You might as well say that if you had a leg of mutton ready for six people to dine off, and each brought a friend, there would be &ldquo; no ground for the fear of&rdquo; any one getting half his proper share. But in re&not; gard to this matter of wages, though the practice of the Victoria Press does not really affect the main question, it would be interesting to know what the young women actually earn. The statement that they get as much as men of similar skill is in startling contradiction of the &lsquo;hitherto invariable rule that the price of women&rsquo;s labour, other things being equal, is less than that of men. One need not stop to prove this ; it is simply because, on account of our social habits, women can afford to work cheaper than men. Either the printing trade forms a peculiar and unique excep&not; tion or the Victoria Press must be paying more than a non-philanthropic r 61 W0ll ld &pound;&#39;i ve &middot; In the latter case the shadow as well as the substance ot Miss Eaithfulľ s argument disappears. But as the whole question is and will be one of wages, exact information should be given as to what is really being paid. We are told of apprentices getting KB. or ISs. a week. I should like to know how many earn this latter sum; what propor&not; tion get less than 10s,; how much those have been making, who have been, say two or three years at the work. Have any, and if so, what pro&not; portion, of the employ&eacute;es of the Victoria Press been living on their eamino-s? Plain answers to these questions, which, considering that the Victoria Press is a semi-philanthropic and semi-speculative undertaking, are not imper&not; tinent, would be more to the purpose than a general statement about a &ldquo; re&not; cognized scale.&rdquo;*,As to the argument from the analogy of machinery, it is not surprising that an apparent similarity should deceive for a moment, but it is quite wonderful that any one should put such a plea down on paper, and so&not; lemnly read it &ldquo; for the advancement of Social Science.&rdquo; Miss Paithfull must know perfectly well, that the whole argument for and against ma&not; chinery turns on one tact, namely, that it increases the proportion of the produce to the labour expended, and that this is just the one circumstance which is absent &iacute;rom the case she is defending. Machinery is good be&not; cause it saves labour ; because when it is applied to various trades,&rsquo;each class o&iacute; producers gams more by the general cheapening of commodities,* Another interesting inquiry would be,&mdash;what has been the effect of the necessary mixture of male and temale labour ? Has the habitual association of a number of voun^ n omen with the other sex been found to work well or ill ? The experience of th&egrave; Vic? o ia Iress on this head might for special reasons be expected to be favourable If it should be otherwise, the warning would be serious.
than it loses by the cheapening of its labour. Female labour, on the contrary, is not, except in a few rare cases,* more advantageous than men&rsquo;s ; it will therefore not diminish the real cost of things, and will diminish the real value of labour : that is, it will produce permanently the evil which machinery produces temporarily, without the enormous over&not; balancing advantage which makes machinery a social blessing. Machinery reduces the average share of work done by every human being in the com&not; munity. A mere addition to the number of labourers increases this aver&not; age. So much for the analogy of the two cases. The reasoning is not only fallacious in substance, but in form is a curious illustration of the loose way in which people can think and write on subjects demanding the most careful exactness. Miss Faithfull confounds the argument about ma&not; chinery with the facts on which it is based. The argument has indeed been refuted by experience, but no one ever denied the facts. No one will pretend that the hand-loom weavers did not starve, or that the sewing- machine, to take a present instance, has not reduced the value of hand&not; sewing.,It is strictly true, to quote Miss Faithfull&rsquo;s quotation, that &ldquo;the key-note to the employment of women is cheap labour.&rdquo; The phrase &ldquo; increase of productive power&rdquo; practically admits it, unless she can show that this is an increase in proportionable value, and not merely in amount. The great, almost the only hope of social improvement in the position of the working class lies in the gradual raising of the real value of their labour, combined with a simultaneous rise in their standard of social and domestic comfort.f Both these things are lowered by the plan we are discussing. There is already evil enough done in the world by the selfishness of men ; and here we have our philanthropy deliberately setting itself to swell the total, in&not; nocently allying itself with the worst foes of progress. We know what is the commercial ideal of English greatness. This little island is to become in time one huge agglomeration of shops, faetones, and furnaces,&mdash;honey&not; combed with mines and bristling with chimneys, bringing everything to perfection except the bodies and souls of its inhabitants. It is towards the realization of this beautiful picture that Miss Faithfull and her friends are contributing. I read the other day, in a school-inspector&rsquo;s Report, of a girl eight years old (one of many thousands), whose life is consecrated to the business of sitting the whole day in utter darkness in the corridor of a coal-mine, and pulling a string every half hour to open a trap-door for the passage of a truck. One hopes a day may come when a future generation may look back and say, when they are told this, &ldquo; What brutal savages were our grandfathers !&rdquo; But what if they are further told that there were philanthropic associations established purposely to extend the evil, and that this, in the nineteenth century, was called Social Science?,R. B. Litchfield.,* Such, perhaps, as watchmaking, in which a woman is said to produce more than a man in the same number of hours. f Vide Mill&rsquo;s &lsquo; Political Economy,&rsquo; passim.
A PLEA EOE BOTANY.,By G. J. Pearse, M.A.,Dear Mr. Editor, &mdash;I believe my name will appear in the programme of the London College for the ensuing Vacation Terra, as responsible for a botanical class of some sort. I shall be glad if you will allow me a little space in the Magazine for explanation.,In the first place, I learn from Mr. Slade that it is impossible for him to continue his class any longer this year ; or I certainly should not have thought of intruding upon his province. Next, it will probably seem absurd to many to take a botanical class for three weeks (i.e. evenings) only, and beginning so late in the year as the last day of September; for I shall be absent the first week of the term. I must dispose of these as I best can. But I must premise first that the class will be strictly of the &ldquo; Preparatory Order;&rdquo;&mdash;any other I should not at present feel justified in undertaking ; that lam at least a sincere, if ignorant, lover of Nature; and that I would fain stir up others to a similar love, or rather make them fonder, because more intelligent, admirers. I hope, by means of our talk on those three evenings, to enlist a few for next year, not as pupils so much as fellow-students, having myself a very small stock of knowledge to start with, enough to qualify me at least for pupil-teacher. Nearly all that I have, was picked up several years ago from a medical student, who had to take up botany as a part of his professional training ; who taught me something of the principles of classification ; and who, in many delightful country walks that we enjoyed together, told me the names of most of the wild flowers that we found, and by comparing them with the descriptions of them in the books, showed me how to find out others that we did not know. Want or time since has been an excuse to myself for flagging interest ; and I want the encouragement of brother-students, to revive it.,You and others have complained at different times of the neglect of the Natural Sciences at the College ; and have put forward your pleas for geology and chemistry. I cannot pretend to put botany on the same footing : perhaps those who know more of it might do so. Eor though the piesent state of knowledge is the result of much patient research and careful analysis, we find it now reduced to a system of comparatively simplei ules; and our principal business is to learn them, and observe tlieii operation. But if the study of botany, at least under the guidance of an igne&raquo; amus, cannot take its place with other severer studies, as afford&not; ing a fust-rate mental training, there is another educational purpose, which it answers well, that of increasing a man&rsquo;s resources, and givin&deg;&middot; him new enjoyment, not quite of an irrational kind. With a very little labour, one may learn enough of it to take an intelligent interest in it, which is in itself a considerable gain. It is moreover a particularly healthy pm suit foi unf 01 tunates like ourselves, who for our sins are confined to the close streets of a smoky town.&rdquo; Indeed your whole argument for Geology (May No., p. 68) will apply still more strongly to botany; I might appropriate your own words, with one or two trifling changes, such as sandwich-box and Babington for your hammer and Lyell. (I would add
a magnifying glass, especially if my botanist be also a lover of pipes.) Have not I also, in my poor little pressed specimens of cow-wheat and kidney-vetch, woodruff and sundew , memorials of delightful hours spent on Boxhill and Riddle&rsquo;s Down, on Chiselhurst and Swinley commons ? Do not my parsley-fern and pinguicula recall glorious ascents of Snowdon, Cader Idris, and the Brecon Beacons ; my globe-flower, scrambles along the Vales of Neath and Usk and Wye; my Campanula glomerata, strolls over our beautiful Bedfordshire chalk hills ? You need not laugh, for Bedfordshire has its beauties, though Dr. Arnold could not see them. Every flower I have preserved, has its memories of scenery, and of com&not; panions too. Go to, Mr. Editor ! geology is not &ldquo; a long way the whole- somest of the natural sciences that is, if botany be a science. Geology for hard work with a little enjoyment ; botany for much enjoyment, with light work.,Artists tell you that none but an artist can fully appreciate beauties of scenery; though I know at least one man, who cannot draw the simplest figures of Euclid with reasonable correctness, whose thorough enjoyment of scenery of every kind could not be surpassed. This by the way ; I meant rather to cap the assertion than to dispute it. Any one can admire the gorgeous colour of a cornfield full of poppies, of a golden bank of gorse or broom, or of a heath-covered hill in September. Did you ever watch the sun sink behind a Welsh hill, shining through the heather at the sides, and giving the effect of a mass of transparent purple, lit up from behind ? If not, you have yet to see one of the most splendid sights in Nature. Him who cannot admire this I would rather not characterize. But if you wish to see beauties everywhere, lurking in unsuspected places, you must study the minuter details, which go to make up Nature&rsquo;s grander paint&not; ings. You will not find such a thing as an ugly flower. The most in&not; significant weed will prove worth notice, and will well repay a careful ex&not; amination.,But I must rein up my runaway hobby. Enough at present of the pleasures of botany. What of its uses? One great advantage it has, to recommend it to the supporters of natural science generally, that it is an attractive and easy branch to begin upon ; and he who has become familiar with one branch is pretty sure presently to extend his researches in other directions, so surely does the taste grow upon him. This alone however would be but a poor argument for it. The popular notion of a botanist is, I believe, that he is a sham savant, whose science consists in the knowledge of some few hundreds of names of plants. And certainly if that were all, the popular estimation of the study would not be far wrong.,It would be about as useful an accomplishment as learning by heart some dozen pages of dictionary. But it implies something besides this. In Brande&rsquo;s Dictionary, it is divided into Organography, including Morpho&not; logy, Physiology, Taxonomy, Terminology, Phytography, and lastly the Practice of Botany. My space will not allow me now to explain these alarming-looking but harmless names. I hope to do so, when we meet on Monday the 30th instant. We will at the same time see if we can devise the best method of proceeding, and then commence with a little elementary botany, perhaps by talking over the first two chapters of Lindley ; and I hope, by the middle of October, to have got a class at
least in condition for starting in earnest early next year. I need not add that my class for those three nights will be free ; and if any one should appear better qualified than myself to conduct it, I shall be only too happy to resign in his favour.,G. J. Pearse.,TRADE CO-OPERATION: ITS SUCCESS IN PARIS.,The Siede, of a few days back, contained an interesting account of a joint-stock company of operative masons. The success of this society, which from a small beginning thirteen years ago, has by degrees risen to the condition of a large and prosperous commercial body, is particularly worthy of the attention of English working men at the present juncture. It is worth noticing that the rate of wages adopted by the society (exclusive of profit)is something considerably below the present pay of the same trade in England. Pour francs in Paris are practically the equivalent of four shillings in London. The Prench workman evidently reckons to live on this, devoting what he gains in excess either to his extra comforts or to the increase of his capital. But to get a just view of the whole case, it should not be forgotten that the 6f. 25c. a day thus earned by these men (equal to a Londoner&rsquo;s &szlig;s. 3 d.), is a high rate of pay, partly due to the enormous local demand of the last ten years for this particular kind of labour.,&bull; ί J. ľ ? P f &iacute; 1Ve &ldquo;T?&rsquo; Sa ^ S the &lsquo; Si&egrave;cle &rsquo;&rsquo; &ldquo; foun(Ied their association in 1848, but they had the good fortune not to receive any pecuniary aid from the State. They had among them M. Nadaiid, a Representative of the People, who was not well viewed by the authorities. They were re&not; proached with very strong Socialist tendencies. In a word, the Govern- ment refused them the capital which they lent to other companies The masons thought they were lost&mdash;they were saved ! And in fact, they are in existence, they are prosperous, while the companies which were fostered by the State are for the greater part dead, after painfully struggling with the dithcultics which their defective origin had created. In 18 52, after the coup d etat, the operative masons, having confidence in themselves, resolved to reorganize their association. They drew up a very wise code of laws and possessing no capital they observed to one another that they had at all events, their hands their special knowledge, and that with industry&rsquo; order economy and goodwill, they might overcome all obstacles. They set to work, and resolved that each member of the association should deposit in the common chest the tenth of his daily wages. This tenth was intended witV, UP nľl &lsquo; Y łL the T l &deg; tbe Jomt-stock company. During the first year,,kcted on vlil To&#39;, &quot;ΐ ľ- SaCr ; fi T the y &ldquo; &lsquo;bey cob,Iľvľ 1 ly m ;. 10 1 Thejr association at that time consisted of only,nľtP 1&deg; P Г&#39;&rsquo; Y &iacute; &quot; e hands and determined to succeed,They acted so well, and conducted themselves with so much proprietv that,the year following (1853) their capital rose to &pound;171. and in ľsT&Auml;sO In hne, from year to year by dint of saving, by courage and perseverance am thanks, likewise, to the skilful management of their pecuniary affairs
and to the anxious solicitude of some persons in a higher sphere who took an interest in the association, the inventory made in the year I860 demon&not; strated the existence of a capital of &pound;14,600, belonging to 107 associates. During the eight years between 1853 and 1860 the company undertook several considerable works, such as the H&ocirc;tel Eould, in the Rue de Berry ; the H&ocirc;tel Rouher, in the Champs Elys&eacute;es; the H&ocirc;tel Frescati, Rue de Richelieu ; the Square d&rsquo;Orl&eacute;ans, Rue Taitbout ; several small h&ocirc;tels in the Rue de la Pompe Passy ; several station-houses on the Vincennes Railway ; the workshops of M. Godillot, Rue Rochechouart, etc. At present the as&not; sociates are building an h&ocirc;tel for M. de Girardin on the Boulevard of the King of Rome ; an h&ocirc;tel for M. Ars&egrave;ne Haussaye, on the Boulevard Beau- jon ; an h&ocirc;tel for Mademoiselle Allier, on the Boulevard de l&rsquo;Empereur ; and an h&ocirc;tel at Montrouge for M. Pacotte. The year 1860 was less for&not; tunate for the association. Emboldened by their success, the associates for a moment departed from the prudent line they had marked out for themselves, and they suffered a loss of &pound;1600. We would willingly say a fortunate loss. The operatives thus learnt to their cost that success in every affair is to be obtained only by excessive vigilance, great precaution, and the absence of all wild speculation. Notwithstanding that loss the capital of the company rose again at the close of the year 1860 to &pound;14,600, an enormous figure when one considers the trifling sum of &pound;14. 18s. so painfully acquired. Each associate being at the same time a labourer and a shareholder, shared the profit in each capacity. On every settlement the profits are divided into two unequal parts,&mdash;60 per cent, is attributed to labour, and 40 per cent, to capital. That is to say, the associates begin by dividing 60 per cent, in proportion to the number of days of labour supplied. Then they divide 40 per cent, in proportion to the amount of capital possessed by each associate. Thus, to explain, let us take the year 1858, which gave a clear profit of &pound;4000 on a capital of &pound;10,160, &pound;2400 were allocated for labour, the associates having supplied 26,739 days&rsquo; work, for which they were paid, according to custom, every 15 days, and which amounted to &pound;4210. The distribution of the &pound;3400 gave to each of them an addition of 1&laquo;. for each day&rsquo;s work. Let us suppose an operative earning 3s. 3 \d. (4f.) a day in the association, and working 300 days in the year, he will have received &pound;48 for wages. This distribution of the profits gives him an additional sum of &pound;26. 14s. for wages. This is not all. The operative who left one-tenth of his daily wages to constitute a capital for the company became by this fact a shareholder of the company. The portion attributed to capital for the year 1858 having amounted to &pound;1600, the same operative who received an additional sum of &pound;26. 14s. for wages is likewise to receive a share of the &pound;1600 allocated for capital in propor&not; tion to the various suras he paid in to the common chest. The association suppresses undertakers of buddings and all intermediaries. It contracts directly with the proprietors of the soil, and realizes for itself the profits which others receive for themselves under the old system. The operative, thus become powerful by the fact of the association, offers on his part great advantages to those who will employ him. Otherwise such men as MM. Fould, Rouher, Girrrdin, Haussaye, Godillot, and others, would not have entrusted such considerable works to the association.&rdquo;
LANCASTER MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT AND RECREATION,SOCIETY.,First Annual Report.,The half-yearly general meeting of the members took place at the So&not; ciety&rsquo;s rooms, Brock Street, on Saturday evening last ; Mr. Robinson in the Chair.,After the appointment of officers and committee for the ensuing half- year, Mr. Blezard read the First Annual Report, which (slightly abridged) was as follows :&mdash;,The first year of our Society&rsquo;s existence has just been brought to a close. Your Committee have much pleasure in congratulating the members on its great success, both as regards the number availing themselves of its facilities, and the generous aid received from friends by donations in books and money. Your Committee desire to record their great obligations to those kind friends, at the same time they would respectfully solicit a continuance of their support.,In the small space of a year, a library containing 516 volumes (157 the gifts of friends, 859 purchased) has been obtained, together with a news and reading room, various class&not; rooms, and a recreation-room, all nicely fitted up and furnished.,The lending library has been much in request, upwards of 600 volumes having been taken out during the five months since its first opening. Female members continue to join it. Your Committee recommend an effort being made to replenish the library with books of a good class, the great demand for reading requiring an addition to the present stock.,The news and reading room has been well attended. Your Committee approve of its being kept open all day. There are often members out of work, or unwell, to whom a place where they can sit comfortably, and peruse a book or newspaper, is a great boon ; it is also a convenience to several at dinner-hour.,The classes, viz. reading, writing, arithmetic, vocal music, phonetic short-hand, elocu&not; tion, and English history, have been discontinued for the summer season ; your Com&not; mittee recommend them to be re-opened early in the autumn, as well as any other class which may be considered practicable. They also suggest that a paid teacher should be engaged to assist in the three first-named classes, provided sufficient funds can be ob&not; tained.,The Discussion Class is still continued, and is generally well attended ; instructive and important subjects (none embracing political or theological questions) have been, and con&not; tinue to be, brought forward and discussed in a thoroughly friendly manner. Your Committee recommend this department as being beneficially instructive by tendina&middot; to awaken thought and correct false ideas. &deg;,The lectures, your Committee hope, will be recommenced as early in the autumn as may be found convenient. Thirteen have been delivered during the year.,Your Committee still continue to approve of the recreation-room, as being calculated to benefit such as require relaxation from study, or close attention to business or have not a constant inclination for study or reading, or a comfortable and respectable place wherein to spend their evening hours. It brings such into close association with even better things, and attracts them from those which it is well to avoid.,Your Committee deeply deplore the long delay which has ensued in obtaining a field They now heartily congratulate the members on having at length secured a very con&not; venient and extensive one. For this the sum of &pound;16 has been paid. *,The average number of ordinary members during the year has been 265 and at the time of writing this Report many new ones are joining. Besides these we have 44 honorary members (annual subscribers of not less than 5i.) together with several kind friends subscribing small sums.,In conclusion, your Committee beg to state that they have kept in view the great object which led to the formation of the Society, viz. &ldquo; The providing facilities for the muting of all classes m systematic endeavours to promote mutual improvement and re creation.&rdquo; The general unity of all classes towards furthering our objects is not how
ever such as your Committee could wish to see it. Neither are the conveniences and facilities in some departments of the institution such as they could desire, but they are the best which the utmost efforts of your Committee could by any means make attainable, except they had curtailed the usefulness of the Society by abandoning the recreational de&not; partment&mdash;to have done which would have been relinquishing instead of carrying out an important part of the original scheme&mdash;which experience has shown is a public want, and the supplying of which tends to the public good. Your Committee are more and more convinced that in a society established for general improvement and benefit&mdash; temperance, recreation, and education ought to go hand-in-hand, not forgetting religion. But your Committee are of opinion that the last-named object is already amply provided for by an efficient staff of ministers, and every desirable facility amongst the several sects of which our Society is composed, and that, there, it is the right thing in the right place ; but that in our institute it would tend to doctrinal disputes which would end in no good results. Taking all matters into consideration, your Committee advise that we be con&not; tent and work on cheerfully as at present, with the conveniences and facilities already possessed ; but they would add, that they would always be ready to embrace better facilities should they offer themselves, provided the objects uncurtailed could be car&not; ried out.,From the Treasurer&rsquo;s account it appeared that there remained in his hands a balance of &pound;1. 19&laquo;. 7 \d. During&middot; the year &pound;105. 16&laquo;. 7 d. had been expended ; the cost for the recreation field and recreation apparatus had been &pound;31. 4&laquo;. 60?., and the remainder, &pound;75. 13&laquo;. 2d., had been ex&not; pended in new books, and in establishing and carrying out the Beading and Mutual Improvement department. Many of
the London men, tired of the unhealthy condition of their craft, owing to the undue con&not; trol exercised by the paid leaders of the strike, have left the Metropolis, and thus afforded opportunities for new hands to obtaiu employment. Scotland, where building in stone is more common than in England, has sent up a great number of masons, and when first- rate workmen have applied, their expenses to Loudon have been paid by the Duke&rsquo;s con&not; tractors, Messrs. Holland and Hannan, of Bloomsbury.&rsquo; &rdquo;,In reply to this Messrs. Holland and Hannan write as follows. After referring to other statements in Mr. Reid&rsquo;s letter, as to obtaining masons from Scotland they say,&ldquo; With regard to the advertisement in the Scotch papers, the facts are these :&mdash;,&ldquo; A short paragraph appeared in one of the London daily papers, not written by our&not; selves, although partly based on information which had been obtained from us. The paragraph contained some inaccuracies, but as it appeared to us on the whole to be cal&not; culated to assist our agents in Scotland in getting masons, we directed that it should be sent there as an advertisement. We are not responsible for the particular form of the instructions sent with it, but as the course adopted was the one best calculated to obtain for it the attention we desired, we should without hesitation have agreed to a step the iniquity of which we fail to recognize.&rdquo;,It may be as well to mention, that the &ldquo; paid leaders&rdquo; mentioned in the advertise&not; ment are working masons, who get, like the rest of the society-men on strike, lbs. a week from its funds, and l&iacute;. 6d. a day for their work as committee-men ; altogether, 9i. a week less than their usual weekly wages.,LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.,THE SOCIAL SCIENCE ASSOCIATION.,Sie, &mdash; I perceive by the daily papers that several papers have been read at the Social Science Congress on the subject of employments for women, and analogous subjects. Perhaps you would find a page or two in the&#39;Magazine for the discussion of these matters. I have not been able to read the papers referred to above, but (with one exception) I be&not; lieve they all advocated the new doctrine of finding new employment for women, chiefly at the expense of the hardier sex.,Now, I have no objection to women being introduced into a (previous) man&rsquo;s business, merely because it might throw out of employ some of the latter. If 1 did so object, &Iacute; might equally object to the introduction of all machinery.,But I want to know what is woman&rsquo;s sphere. Is it not the domestic fireside ? Her duty is to bring up her children in the way they should go. I strongly object to any married woman going to work at all : her place is at home, and her husband ought to be in receipt of sufficient wages to keep her and her children.,As regards the single women : are there not sufficient employments for them if they are well conducted ? Are not domestic servants advertised for daily ? I firmly believe that this class of females is better off than any other; but women think it is degradino&middot;. I am well aware that needlework is extremely badly paid for. But if there were not so many married women at daily labour (and who ought not to be under the necessity of leaving their children and their homes), the glut in the female labour-market would not be so great, and consequently the wages would rise. As for Miss Faithfull, or any other of the lady philanthropists, trying to introduce females into printing-offices, lawyers&rsquo; offices linendrapers&rsquo; shops, bookkeeping,&mdash;it would only be over-flooding departments of labour that are now brim-full.,But then, some will say, &ldquo; What is to become of our educated ladies ?&rdquo; Iu most cases, if a lady is fortunate enough to possess a moderate education, she expects too great things to ensue from it. If she marries, it must be to a gentleman who can keep her without even doing her own household work, or she will say, &ldquo; Do you think that I who have received a good education, will let myself down to marry a mechanic with 30i. a week ?&rdquo; It is a fact that the certified schoolmistresses are married so fast, that it has become a serious consideration iu the minds of some connected with the education move&not; ment, as to uhat course should be adopted to retain their services a few years, to com&not; pensate the State for the outlay of their training.
How often do you hear of an educated female without the opportunity of marrying:* No, men are too glad to embrace the opportunity of uniting themselves for life with partners with whom they can enjoy some hours weekly of pleasant and instructive conver&not; sation, instead of the next-door neighbour&rsquo;s gossip, to allow such occasions to slip by.,The two principal means by which I think and believe women may be relieved of the burdens that oppress them, are&mdash;,1st. Give men such wages that they may be able to keep their wives at home to attend to the domestic affairs, and their children till they are a reasonable age to work. Cursed be the legislators who make laws regulating how many hours children of eight, nine, ten, or even twelve years of age may work.,2ndly. Educate the female sex, and let them when educated despise not a mechanic.,A Member of the W. M. C.,THE DIFFICULTIES OF A STUDENT.,Dear Sir, &mdash;The discussion that took place at our General Meeting, on Thursday evening last, as to the future welfare of the College, has induced me to take the liberty of addressing you on the subject, and to endeavour to point out what I consider to be some of the defects, and the cause of its not increasing in the way expected.,As an old member (having joined the first week of opening) I have had some ex&not; perience of the classes and teachers, and the reason so many men leave after a short time (though they enter full of the hope of being able to master some of the various matters taught in the College), appears to me to be, that sufficient attention is not paid to the know&not; ledge and ability of the men joining the classes. The procedure is simply this : a man reads the prospectus, fancies he should like to learn this or that subject Mr. Shorter writes out his card, and tells him where to find the class ; the man walks into the class&not; room and seats himself with the rest of the students, and the teacher, without any pre&not; liminary examination of his pupils, commences the teaching, and the new man ti ies his hardest to follow, finds some of the students working very well and following the teacher, hut cannot do so himself, the whole thing is too much for him. One or two of the pupils know a great deal about it; the teacher goes on, and his questions are answered hy the few; but the majority, though striving hard to catch the mean&not; ing of the things taught (evidenced by the many questions they put) cannot get clear notions, and are dispirited ; he sees this around him, and feels it himself, and when the class is finished, goes home cast down to think learning so difficult : he thinks pro&not; bably it must be his own want of ability and inaptitude for learning. When the time comes for the next lesson, just the same thing occurs ; he sees many of the men in the same predicament as himself, and of course, if he has not been able to pick up anything in the first lesson, those that follow are of very little use, as the teacher supposes him to know all about the portion he has been teaching, and acts accordingly. The man after this loses heart, and though he may continue for a term or two, finally drops off in de&not; spair, and falls back upon his old habits. This you may think overdrawn, but I can assure you it is not. I have had men say to me, &ldquo; This is slow work ; I can&rsquo;t make head or tail of what is going on.&rdquo; Working men who have arrived at the age of say thirty, have so become habituated to the indulgence of the pipe and pot after their daily labour, that it requires a very great effort to get away from it all, and particularly to get courage sufficient to sit down to hard study. I have no doubt you have seen many men sitting listless, and amusing themselves by writing their names on the desks, endeavouring to draw, or when once the thread of the teaching has been lost, listening anxiously for the clock to strike the hour for concluding the lesson.,What 1 should suggest as a remedy would be that every teacher at the commence&not; ment of a terra, before starting with his class, should examine the men presenting them&not; selves, as to their knowledge of the subject, and see what they really know ; he could then, if necessary, advise some to join a class where the rudiments are taught, and others who&rsquo;know a great deal about it to join a more advanced class, and so on, leaving only those fitted for the particular work he has in hand. I cannot but think all parties would be pleased with this arrangement. It would, in a great measure, put a stop to that to every one most unpleasant thing, of one or two individuals in a class engrossing all the attention of the teacher and shutting out those who, through not knowing so much at the commencement of the class, are not able to get on, though very anxious to do so. Aug . 7, 1861. Thos &#39; Martin.
LEARNING BY HEART.,My dear Sir, &mdash;Having read in this month&rsquo;s number of &ldquo;our Magazine&rdquo; Mr. V. Lushington&rsquo;s paper on &ldquo; Learning by Heart,&rdquo; I feel impelled to raise some objections to his remarks ; and yet I confess to considerable diffidence in disagreeing with a man whom I have reason to esteem so highly. But 1 only urge objections, and shall be glad to see them elucidated or removed.,lu the second paragraph of his article he says of memory, &ldquo; An excellent, a beautiful faculty remains unexercised, unused, ignobly idle.&rdquo; But I ask, does it do so ? Do we not continually draw heavily on our memory ? Do we not daily add to its stores ? What faculty of mind can so much as memory reject the unsuitable aud accept the con&not; genial P As that involuntary muscle, the heart, performs its life-giving functions with&not; out being bid, so memory, the involuntary muscle of the mind aud the conscience, acts, God be thanked ! independent, aud often in spite of us. I called memory a faculty, but is it not more like a tablet fit only to hold a certain quantity engraven on it, rather than a iaculty capable of indefinite development ? At all events this is, I believe, yet an open though much vexed question. One thing is in favour of the tablet-theory, that old impressions get erased pretty nearly as fast as new ones take their place.,Again, in the same paragraph, Mr. Lushington holds up as examples the Greeks learn&not; ing Homer, aud our medieval ancestors ballads, by heart. He might also have quoted the modern gondoliers of Venice, chanting all, or nearly all, Tasso&rsquo;s &lsquo; Jerusaleip &rsquo; from memory. But remember what help melody and rhythm afford to them. Remember too the occupations of these men. Orestes and Pylades hunting, Greek armies marching, pilgrims in their weary walks, singly, or in company, gondoliers rowing : to all these a song or chanting recitation would be an alleviation or even a positive help ; but imagine a clerk on his much-bothered walks from banks and wharves to counting-house and back, chanting Milton&rsquo;s &lsquo; Paradise &rsquo;! Volunteers, on the other hand, again, begin to feel the* same want of appropriate rhythm for their long marches. I much doubt even if the Greeks of the heroic age ever deliberately sat them down to commit Homer to memory, I had rather believe that of that nation of listeners the most impressible of the memories first caught up, and the most retentive longest preserved, the words of the poet as they fell from the lips of the professed rhapsodists, who of course must be excepted from this statement ; but for their work we have in printed books more than a substitute.,Further on complaint is made, and most justly made, of our Railway Literature, read in railway style. I too plead guilty to that charge, and hope to mend some of these days. I doubt, however, if conscious learning by heart will do much towards remedying this defect. Rather, I would say, that the best authors will be remembered by being affectionately read and re-read, and having favourite passages long dwelt upon, than suppose that they would be read because a few parts have to be remembered for some specific purpose.,I do not deny the desirableness of being able to produce at need &ldquo; a thing of beautv,&rdquo; which &ldquo; is a joy for ever,&rdquo; but, I believe that he that reads most and most earnestly will, c&aelig;teris paribus , possess the greatest number of sacred relics. Our spare time too is &ldquo; precious as scant rations to beleaguered men,&rdquo; and many of us feel that we get enough conscious exercise for our memory, enough mental gymnastics and Spartan discipline, Ъу the declensions, conjugations, lists, tables, historical dates, algebraic formulae, and many other dry skeletons of information, with which we have to cram ourselves.,The watchword that I should like to see passed round us in a College like&#39; ours, is Concentration. Let each of us mainly devote himself to one or two subjects of studv and employ the spare ten minutes in amplifying our information on all subjects col&not; lateral with or subordinate to the Ruling Passion. Sonnenschein.,NEWS.,London.&mdash;-On Saturday the 24th a large party of members of the College Singing Classes and their friends had an excursion to Richmond Park. The intention was to com&not; bine the pleasure of an afternoon in the country with that of a social gathering, and the result in both of these respects was a complete success. Two o&rsquo;clock was fixed for the
start. The party left the Waterloo station under a lowering sky which portended a soak&not; ing day ; a prospect made apparently still more hopeless by the sharp drizzle through which the train dashed on its way to Richmond. But these ill omens gave way pre&not; sently before a burst of sunshine, aud thenceforward (occasional showers excepted) we had a lovely day. At about three we mustered on the &ldquo; Terrace.&rdquo; After enjoying the glorious view over the valley of the Thames to the towers of Windsor, we strolled down the ridge by Earl Russell&rsquo;s grounds, and pitched on a pleasant spot under some magni&not; ficent oak-trees, where, seated on the turf, we had the first part of our al fresco concert. Both classes, for the first time, joined in some familiar choruses and part songs ; and the more &ldquo;advanced&rdquo; of the party then amused the rest by singing a few of their most favourite &ldquo; Old English Ditties.&rdquo; By this time there was a suggestion that it was not too early to go in search of tea. A scramble down a ferny slope brings us to the Peter&not; sham side of the park. But before we have gone far, we are glad to seek a shelter from a passing storm. This we easily find under the dense foliage of some big elms, and the ten minutes&rsquo; delay is wiled away by the exploits of a few vivacious spirits who enter&not; tain the rest with amateur exhibitions a la Blondin and Leotard. The shower over, we pursue our way and descry in the distance a canvas-covered van brought to anchor under the lee of five magnificent lime-trees, and bearing in big letters the name of Mr. Earee, chiefcst of tea-contractors. The chimney of a migratory boiler is smoking at its side, and in a few minutes the interior of the vehicle supplies a complete commissariat, with even the luxurious additions of seats, tables, and tablecloths. The arrival of a few late comers swells the number of our party to about sixty, and the van and boiler are found to be inexhaustible in their supplies of tea, coffee, bread and butter, and cake. While everybody, however, is most busily employed, there is a cry of &ldquo;Here comes the rain!&rdquo; and up goes a panoply of umbrellas ; but the big lime-trees prove a complete protection. In a few minutes the sudden drifting shower is gone as quickly as it came, and the green slope in front of us is lit up by a burst of evening sunlight, reddening the cedars into the brightest contrast with the black mass of the passing rain-cloud. Tea disposed of, we adjourn to another cluster of trees to have a little more singing. Backed by a circle of tall limes, the voices of the choir ring out as freshly as in the best of concert-rooms, and are heard, as some of the wauderers of the party aver, ringing far away in the dis&not; tance. The singing is highly approved, but the rising spirits of the party demand some more exhilarating pastime, and there are regretful sighs for a fiddle and a dance. Fiddle however there is not, but, despite this drawback, a quadrille is improvised. A vigorous chorus of popular airs does the work of a baud, and the &ldquo; all round&rdquo; of the closing figure makes up in its unbounded merriment for any little irregularities in the perform&not; ance. &ldquo; Blind-man&rsquo;s buff&rdquo; followed, the little ones of the party, of whom there were some half-dozen, enjoying it not one whit more than their elders ; and the twilight almost deepened into dark before we could make up our minds for the homeward march. Re&not; turning up the hill through the fern, we just reached the top in time to see the harvest moon rising over the trees on the further side of the park. A lively trot through the town, varied here and there by a stepping chorus, brought us to the station. Seizing, appropriating, aud filling a big open carriage, we had a merry moonlight ride to town, and the proceedings of the day closed by a vote carried by acclamation to the effect that the experiment was one to be repeated. * With the exception of two Whitmonday gather&not; ings in the early youth of the College, and a visit to Mr. Ludlow and Mr. Hughes at Wim&not; bledon some years back, this has been the only week-day excursion-party of any size, and the first to be brightened by the presence of what in mystic college phrase is called &ldquo; G&middot;. and B.&rdquo; An account of this trip is given here to induce its repetition another year on a larger scale. The capabilities of Mr. Earee&rsquo;s van are absolutely unlimited ; it can producerea for two thousand as well as for twenty, and in the long summer days many might enjoy the evening hours by coming at four or five o&rsquo;clock who could not spare the half-holiday.,Cambridge .&mdash;The cricket club has been active aud successful of late. On July 31, it played the University and College Servants&rsquo; Club, and was, by the results of the first inuiugs, the second not being played out, victorious. OCTOBEE 1, 1861.,THE AMERICAN CONSTITUTION AND THE WAR. *,By John Westlake.,No less an event has taken place than the dissolution of the constitu&not; tion of the United States. 1 do not say of the states themselves, for the fortune of war defies calculation, and their union may yet be restored under some other kind of government. But it is an accomplished fact that the constitution of 1787, after being faithfully observed in the main during eighteen presidential terms, now no longer rules in spirit at all, nor in every particular of the letter, even that part of the country which still acknowledges the powers at Washington. No fortune of war tends to close such chinks, when once they have shown themselves in the joints of an old structure ; and the time is therefore now fully come, when a college which reckons history and politics among its subjects should look at this great historical and political fact, to see how it has been brought about, and whether it calls for joy or sorrow.,The most striking thing to a foreigner in the constitution of the United States was the little which the general government had to do with all that he cared about as a member of a nation. The relations of labour to capi&not; tal, including the question of personal freedom ; private law, including questions so vitally affecting society as those of inheritance and divorce ; the relief of the poor, the punishment and reformation of criminals, edu&not; cation, sanitary measures, and a host of other things, were among the at&not; tributes of the several states. The common topics of discussion in news&not; papers, at public meetings, in private conversation, and, in Europe, in supreme national legislatures ; the institutions by which, on crossing a European frontier, the traveller accounts for the differences which strike the eye by far the most of these could not be treated of in Congress. Oui criticism of the arrangement should be sparingly mingled with re&not; proach, as it.arose much less from premeditation than from the impossi&not; bility of getting the contracting states * to cede any larger portion of their separate powers. Here and there, in the early days of the republic, we meet with attempts to find a natural propriety in &lsquo;the limits which had been fixed for the general government, an assumption that to overstep them would be tyrannical as well as unconstitutional : it is common even,* The Republican (or Lincoln) party does not admit that the constitution of 1787 was termed by a contract between states, because it expresses itself to be ordained and es&not; tablished by &ldquo; the people of the United States.&rdquo; But the old Confederation is ad&not; mitted to have been strictly a compact between states. How then were &ldquo; the people of the United States&rdquo; created? If the constitution was not itself a cession by the states which had been parties to the Confederatiou, at least there must have been a previous VOL. III. &gamma;
for &quot;real men to regard with such self-justifying complacency the work which it has been permitted them to do : but, on the whole, the founders of American independence strove to obtain for the general government what they could. Yet, while abstaining from reproach, we must admit that the arrangement violated the principles of humanity. The powers of government are tremendous. Peace and war ; taxation, with the power it gives of affecting every interest and industry ; an immense patronage ; the extremity of the measures by which it may enforce obedience to itself on the part even of virtuous and well-meaning dissentients ; add to these, in the case of the United States, vast tracts of new land acquired for the general benefit. Against the evil which may be done by these means, the ambition and cupidity which the mere possession of them excites, the preventive intended by nature lies in the responsibility which attends all true national action. As agencies held by the wise in trust for the igno&not; rant, by the good in trust for the criminal, by the rich in trust for the poor, by the strong in trust for the weak,&mdash;the central powers of the state are the necessary machinery for working out a national idea of well&not; being. They may not be called into play on every occasion. Often the end itself is best attained by leaving unfettered the spontaneous impulses which tend in its direction. But when such is the opinion entertained of the true purpose of the public arm, we have the best security that can be afforded against its flagrant abuse. When however there exists no na&not; tional unity of sentiment ; no conlmon idea, or standard social condition, which the different parts of the state would all realize if they could ; and there is denied to the central power the bare right of entertaining such an idea for itself, of labouring, even indirectly, towards any project of social amelioration ; one is at a loss to see what justification remains for creating powers, of which the mere existence is as a carcase thrown out to gather together all the vultures of our nature.,If any justification could be found, it would be in the design of secu&not; ring peace at home and power and consideration abroad. How far the former object was likely to be attained by the establishment of such a go&not; vernment, the events which are now occurring bear witness. The lesson is one which we may take to heart in these times, when a blind philan&not; thropy, rushing to its end without duly considering the means, proposes so many schemes for obviating wars by arbitration, or by a federation of states authorized to enforce peace as the first condition, and to settle those questions which tend to disturb the peace only in the second place, if at all. In the language of the interest from which such schemes proceed, the demand for wars must be stopped before the supply of them will cease. In the order of nature, questions must be settled before the differ&not; ences which arise out of their existence can be composed. When the time is not ripe for settling the questions, the peoples which differ on them, if geographically separate, had best also be politically separate, A,and tacit cession by those states, to create the people which ordained the constitution ; and the limits of that previous and tacit cession can only be found in the terms of the constitution which necessitates the hypothesis of such a cession. I have therefore spoken of &ldquo; the contracting states,&rdquo; without reference to a controversy which appears to me to he only of verbal importance. The Republican view is ably stated in Mr. Daniel Webster&rsquo;s speech in the Senate, on January 27, 1830.
forced union multiplies the occasions for collision, without abating the bit&not; terness when such occasions arise; and the closer the union is in form, the more certainly has it that effect. Where the spirit is not one, a Ger&not; man confederation is bad, but an American federal government is worse. And as to power and consideration abroad, my American friends must ex&not; cuse me if I make lighter of this plea than even of that of peace at home : peace, at least, is a legitimate object ; power and consideration, under the circumstances, are not. They are lawfully to be desired only to secure a nation from external interference in a path of progress, moral as &iacute;veli as material, which it has chosen to follow out. An Englishman&rsquo;s attachment to his flag is the symbol and outcome of his attachment to his institutions. Ubi Deus, ibi patria. That was the old spirit of New England also ; and, if we embrace in the phrase all which, besides liberty of worship, or grow&not; ing out of liberty of worship, we now feel to be necessary to the comple&not; tion of humanity, it must be the base of every just claim to assert a posi&not; tion among the nations of the earth. The power of the sword claimed among men on any other foundation tends only to universal empire, or the lust of it, from the Assyrian conquerors and Romulus in the wolf&rsquo;s den, to the latest form in which buccaneering may have been organized. To the absence of such a moral ground for the authority which represented the United States towards foreigners, may be attributed that unhappy tem&not; per which they have displayed in their foreign relations, and which has made it so difficult for us to keep the peace with them. In no point of view was good to be expected from the attempt to erect a national govern&not; ment in disregard of the natural conditions for its rightful existence.,The constitution of the United States was formed by limited concessions from the several states. I have commented on the narrowness of the limits within which the concessions were comprised, a narrowness so ex&not; treme as to be inconsistent with the main design of erecting a national government, and to result, after eighteen presidential terms, either in the ultimate defeat of that design, or in modifications of the constitution with a view to preserve it. I have now to comment on a consequence which would equally have flowed from the fact of limitation, no matter how laro- e had been the concessions. It is that, under the constitution, the majority for the time being does not govern. I do not mean merely the numerical majority, which governs perhaps in no country ; my stress in the phrase lies on the words &ldquo; for the time being.&rdquo; Determine the majority any way you will. take wealth, education, birth, into account : there is always this fundamental difference between a constitution like ours and one like that of the United States, that in the former case there is at every moment, and on every question, some way of marshalling the actual, present forces of the country, so as to get at a national verdict which shall be law ; while in the other case you shall have a decided preponderance in one direction of the actual, present forces of the country, and yet their will, if opposed to the constitution, shall not be law unless the preponderance be sufficient to effect a preliminary change in the constitution ; and a change in the constitution of the United States required certain very large majorities in the preliminary stages, concluding with an approval by three-fourths in number of all the states voting separately. The British constitution is a law only to the judgment and conscience of Parliament : to the judges,L 3
and people of tlie land there is no higher authority upon earth than an Act of Parliament. The constitution of the United States is, to the judges and people of that land, a law higher than an Act of Congress : and if Congress interferes with subjects not submitted to it by the constitution, resistance to such interference may be legally as well as morally justifiable. Only, that every one may not be left to do that which is right in the sight of his own eyes, it will be necessary that the act of the general govern&not; ment, Congress, or President, to be resisted, be declared unconstitutional by the Supreme Court. The constitution may indeed be flagrantly vio&not; lated, and yet we may imagine the Supreme Court to be so flagrantly cor&not; rupt as to uphold the violation : but here at length we have reached the limits of the law; any further resistance must rest on moral grounds, just as it would have to do in this countiy if, as conceivably it might, Parlia&not; ment were to enter on a path of confiscation or other unconstitutional op&not; pression. Or, putting the converse case, the general government may really be acting within its constitutional powers, but the Supreme Court may, by partiality or error, decide its action to be unconstitutional. Then the situation of the general government is just that of a private English&not; man, who has been cast in his lawsuit by a decision overruling what was previously the law, but affirmed by the House of Lords. If either per&not; severes in asserting his or its lights vi et armis , it will depend on circum&not; stances whether there is a moral justification, but a legal justification is already put out of the question by the highest authoritative exposition of the law which is known to the law itself.,The reader is now in a position for understanding the difficulty in which the northern United States were placed. The constitution of the Union, in one point by its plain letter, in another according to the highest authorita&not; tive exposition of it, operated as a direct support of slavery at the South. The former point was the article providing for the restitution of fugitive slaves, carried out indeed in detail by a law of recent enactment, but so express that the enactment of some such law could not constitutionally be evaded. The latter was the permission of slavery in the territories, or ground belonging to and ruled by the general government, pending its formation into states ; which permission, according to the decision of the Supreme Court in the recent case of the negro Dred Scott, it was not con&not; stitutionally open to the general government to refuse. With slavery in the southern states, the North had no desire, as it did not feel that it had the right, to meddle. Its conscience, at least that of most of its citizens, was free, as it had never accepted any unity with the South in domestic questions : its conscience could only have been burdened by availing itself of the external unity, which it had accepted, to put down an institution with which it had expressly contracted not to interfere. But with the two points above mentioned, the case was different. Here the constitutional contract itself was a perpetual burden to the conscience of a very numer&not; ous and influential section of the North, and there was no prospect of ob&not; taining any release from the bargain, or modification of its terms. But the section which felt the burden, strong as it was, was not strong enough to bring the North, as a whole, to adopt its views on the Fugitive Slave Law. In the other case, it formed an alliance with the party which desired to retain the territories as a field for northern settlers ; and on May 17,
1860, the convention of the republican party, assembled at Chicago to adopt a platform and nominate a candidate for the presidency, voted a platform, by the 8th article of which it threw down the gauntlet to the Supreme Court, declaring that there did not in the whole Union exist sufficient authority, not to exclude slavery from a territory, but to legalize slavery in one, and that that position should be maintained &ldquo;by legisla&not; tion, whenever such legislation is necessary.&rdquo; On this platform, Mr. Lincoln was nominated the next day, and in due course he was elected President ; pledged to use the force of the state in support of legislation pronounced unconstitutional, and therefore void, by the Supreme Court. The constitution was at a dead-lock. If Charles&rsquo;s judges could make Hampden pay shipmoney, the Parliament could reverse their decision. But here was authoritative exposition on one side, and force, with no power of altering the exposition, on the other. The constitution said that the majority for the time being should not govern. But the majority for the time being, feeling that they were likely long to continue the majority, without acquiring the relative preponderance necessary for modifying the constitution, had determined to govern.*,If ever there was a time for a nation to confess its sins in sackcloth and ashes, it was this. That the election of Mr. Lincoln upon the Chicago platform was a righteous act, I entertain no manner of doubt ; the posi&not; tion had become intolerable, and a way was&#39; to be found out of it at all hazards, and by cutting through all legal cobwebs. But the new position was one as little to be forced on the South as the old one on the North. If the South accepted it, well : if not, then the test whether the righteous act had been righteously done was to be found in the willingness of the North to permit a peaceable secession. Not that I believe there could in fact have been a peaceable secession. The division of the federal property,* When the Supreme Court operated to consolidate the Federal Government, its authority was written down by the state-rights party, the political ancestors of those who, since the Dred Scott decision, have written it up. Consequently the opinion of Mr. Jefferson, that the judiciary and executive &ldquo; are equally independent in the spheres of action assigned to them,&rdquo; has been quoted since the secession as refuting the South out of its own mouth. I bow to the authority of Mr. Jefferson, but observe that the right of property in a negro is not a question which concerns the executive. The inter&not; pretation which that great statesman put on the constitution was clear and consistent, as will appear from the following extracts :&mdash; &ldquo;The-judges, believing the law constitutional, had a right to pass a sentence of fine and imprisonment, because the power was placed in their hands by the constitution. But the executive, believing the law to be unconstitutional, w-ere bound to remit the execu&not; tion of it, because that power had been confided to them by the constitution &rdquo; (Letter to Mr. John Adams, September 11, 1804). &ldquo; Questions of property, of character, and of crime, being ascribed to the judges through a definite course of legal proceeding, laws involving such questions beloug, of course, to them ; and as they decide on them ultimately, and without appeal, they of course decide for themselves. The constitutional validity of the law or laws, again, pre&not; scribing executive action, and to be administered by that branch ultimately, and with&not; out appeal, the executive must decide for themselves. So also as to laws governing the proceedings of the legislature, that body must judge for itself the constitutionality of the law, and equally without appeal or control from its co-ordinate branches. And in general, the branch w&#39;hich is to act ultimately and without appeal on any law, is the rightful expositor of the validity of the law, uncontrolled by the opinions of the other co-ordinate authorities&rdquo; (Letter to Mr. W. H. Torrance, June 11, 1815),
and liabilities, and of the border states, themselves internally divided in opinion between freedom and slavery, would have been sure to lead to war. That the Union should ever have been formed on such a basis was an error too great to be expiated without suffering. But the North might have s&oacute; dealt with the questions which must have arisen as to preserve, during the secession, the title to admiration which it had won during the election. I cannot say that its conduct has actually been such.
