The unreflecting ac&not; ceptance of secession in the first instance, without any preparation for solving the questions to which it would certainly give birth, but with a hasty snatching, by the Morrill tariff, at the opportunity which the absence of the Southern members presented for pursuing the sectional interests of Pennsylvania and the North-east ; the violent outburst after the fall of Port Sumter, which the North would object to my calling a spirit of conquest, but which, if it was really only a desire to recombine the nation and reani&not; mate the constitution, is hardly to be reconciled either with the gauntlet thrown down to the Supreme Court by the Chicago platform, or with the perseverance in the Morrill tariff, the creature of secession ; the deliberate marching away of regiments on the morning of battle, which, if they were brave, as 1 believe, can only have happened to men who knew in their hearts that they had no cause worth fighting for ; General Premont&rsquo;s pro&not; clamation of liberty to the slaves of the armed enemy, most admirable if that enemy is an acknowledged foreigner, but branding with hypocrisy the pretence of fighting under a constitution which maintains slavery in the states as expressly as it confides the punishment of treason to the general government : all these things are the too natural result of institutions which have connected national action rather with interest and caprice than with social or moral ends. What I find most to be rejoiced at is the manner in which, throughout the North, a definite national policy begins, by the force of events, to shape itself, and of which General Fremont&rsquo;s proclama&not; tion is the most signal instance. 1 have said enough to show that I neither believe such a policy to be compatible with a r&eacute;animation of the constitution, in which it is every month widening the rents, nor regret the incompatibility. Nor, again, do I believe that the North, in this first moment of her repentance for the sins of the old constitution, if indeed that repentance is yet in any degree a conscious one, can claim to be the apostle of liberty to the South. Leaving apart the international rightful&not; ness of such a crusade, the crusaders would have to be far longer and more thoroughly purified themselves before they could undertake it with success. But if the North can acquire such an ideal of national well-being, and of national action to promote it, as she has never till now connected with the sacred name of nation, she will acquire among the nations of the world a respect which will far more than compensate for the curtailed geographical limits of the American state. And in the dignified self-reliance which will be thus engendered, she may perhaps be induced to trust her constitu&not; tional forms to the guarantee of that power by which alone any constitu&not; tion can be worked, the living force of the nation in each successive crisis of its history.,I have said nothing of the conduct of the South. It would be super&not; fluous, inasmuch as no palliations can atone for the fact that she is fight&not; ing in defence of slavery. We may hold an inquest, for the sake of its
political lessons, on the ruins of a constitution which has been shivered by the explosion of the forces it sought to repress : but our sympathies are determined by the nature of those forces themselves, when they stand revealed in the full play of life, irrespective of the cerecloths which bound them for a time. Yet for the South, too, there is possibility, if not hope. If, freed from jealousy as to her rights under an exploded constitution, she should undertake gradually to ameliorate the condition of the negroes ! The prospect is too unlikely to be entertained. Oligarchies have flourished where peculiar circumstances have fostered an esprit de corps , capable of overruling individual ambition. These have chiefly been cases where there was a strong subject population to be dreaded, or where the members of the oligarchy, inhabiting a single city or a small country, have enjoyed that daily contact with each other which moulds men&rsquo;s sentiments to a common pattern. But for proud and wealthy landowners, scattered over half a continent among weak, though sullen, serfs, not very unequal among themselves, and acknowledging only an elective head, the parallel is not to be sought in Sparta or Venice, but in Poland and Spanish America. There is reason to fear that if any better destiny is in store for the South than that internal anarchy which analogy would thus indicate, the rescue must be made by that class of her population with the worth and power of which we are in England the least acquainted, the non-slaveholding whites.,EEPOET OE THE EDUCATION COMMISSIONEES, 1861.,On the 30th of June, 1858, in consequence of an address from the House of Commons, the Queen issued a commission to the Duke of New&not; castle, the Eight Hon. Sir John Coleridge, the Eev. W. C. Lake, late tutor of Balliol College, Oxford, the Eev. William Eogers, the energetic founder of St. Thomas&rsquo;s Schools, Charterhouse, Mr. Goldwin Smith, Professor of Modern History at Oxford, and Mr. Edward Miall, the editor of the &pound; Nonconformist,&rsquo; &ldquo; to inquire into the present state of po&not; pular education in England, and to consider and report what measures, if any, are required for the extension of sound and cheap elementary in&not; struction to all classes of the people.&rdquo;,The Commissioners chose out ten specimen districts, two agricultural, two manufacturing, two mining, two maritime, and two metropolitan, and appointed Assistant-Commissioners to examine them thoroughly. They also sent Mr. Matthew Arnold to Erance, Holland, and Erench Switzer&not; land, and Mr, Maik Pattison to Germany. On their reports, on answers to a paper of questions circulated in proper quarters, on the viva voce testi&not; mony of many experienced witnesses, and other information, the Commis&not; sioners base their Eeport, which is dated 18th of March, 1861, and is con&not; tained in a small Blue-book of seven hundred pages : attached to it are five othei volumes, containing the reports of the Assistant-Commissioners and the evidence. In this paper I propose to give a summary of the Com&not; missione! s Eepoit, so that our readers, to whom the book may be inacces&not; sible, may gain a general survey of the condition of popular education in England.
The first part of the Report is devoted to the Education of the Inde&not; pendent Poor, i.e. the children of those who earn their bread by manual labour. Apart from the endowed schools, which date chiefly perhaps from the seventeenth century, but were never extensively available, there were comparatively few schools at all for the poor in England, till the close of the last century. In 1803, the total number of day-scholars was 524,211 ; there are now 2,535,462. Shortly before 1800, Mr, llaikes of Gloucester invented Sunday Schools; shortly after 1800 came the British and Foreign School Society and the National Society, who planted elementary schools here and there in the land : they are still thriving, powerful institutions. In 1832, Parliament for the first time gave an educational grant of &pound;20,000, the beginning of greater and better things. In 1839 the Committee of Council for Education (a section of the Privy Council) was established as the State Education Department, and the grant increased to &pound;30,000 ; year by year the grant has been in&not; creasing, till it now reaches &pound;800,000 per annum. The principle of the Government grant has always been this, to assist voluntary effort. The State does not educate, it only assists in educating ; generally for every penny it gives, it requires at least a penny of voluntary contribution. The grants at first were for building only; in 1846 came grants of annual pay&not; ments to apprenticed pupil-teachers (nqw the main item) ; then grants to colleges for training masters ; then annual grants to augment teachers&rsquo; salaries; lastly, in 1853, capitation grants, or so much per head for every child attending &ldquo; regularly &rdquo; (176 times in the year) any school under a certificated or registered teacher. Up to 1859, the State had thus spent about four millions and a half. It is very important to note how the State system has thus grown up ; it has grown like a weed, extending itself here and there in a miscellaneous manner, just as opportunity permitted ; it comes of no large pre-ordained scheme. We have not much to thank Parlia&not; ment for, our Collective Wisdom, except that it has given, and does give us this money ; we owe much to the Council Office, and our other admi&not; nistrators, but above all other men, to Sir James Shuttleworth. Mr. Arnold calls him the founder of English popular education.,Day Schools are the schools for the children of the poor, and the exa&not; mination of them constitutes the bulk of the Report ; it will be well there&not; fore to deal with some other matters first.,Sunday Schools are partly educational institutions, but far more prose&not; lytizing engines of the various religious bodies, above all of the Noncon&not; formist societies. There are 33,516 of them, containing 2,388,397 chil&not; dren ; and all conducted by voluntary teachers. They appear to be of doubtful utility ; some persons, many clergymen among them, think that they burden and weary the children, and tie them up so tight in religious learning, that much irr&eacute;ligion ensues in after-life. But the Commissioners, for reasons sufficiently obvious, on the whole approve of them. Sunday schools are popular, that is them great justification ; but the better the day-schools, the less popular they become.,Infant-Schools are nurseries for the poor ; and their true function is a very important one : they start children in the right way. There are many private schools kept by old dames and such-like, in a very grotesque and uncomfortable condition : the public infant-schools are much better. But of these there is a lack, and a still greater lack of trained mistresses.
Night-Schools are of recent formątion, and receive assistance from the Government, if in connection with a day-school. They are for children and adults, and appear to be devoted to elementary subjects. Most ear&not; nest, most hearty, are the scholars ; the difficulty is to get good teachers ; organization altogether is much wanting. At present there are only 2,036 schools of this kind, containing 80,966 scholars; a number totally inadequate for the purpose of carrying on the education begun at the day-school, or supplying the previous want of it. The Commissioners say, &ldquo; If the education of the country were in a good state, night-schools would be nearly universal.&rdquo; The Commissioners recommend a grant wherever an organizing master is employed ; and the new Educational Minute (which has just been issued) appoints a capitation grant on favourable terms, namely, 1 d. per scholar over thirteen years of age, for every atten&not; dance after twelve. But the old condition of connection with a day- school is still maintained (why, I know not), and the grant is only given for results, tested by examination, in reading, writing, and arithmetic; of which more hereafter.,We now come to Lay-Schools. Most children at one time or another see the inside of a school ; &ldquo; it is an axiom that a child left to run about the stieets is ruined. About two-thirds of our children are actually at school, and of the residue two-thirds are at work, and one-third idling, playing, or begging about the streets. &ldquo; There is no large district en&not; tirely destitute of schools, and requiring to be supplied with them on a huge scale, nor is there any large section of the population sharply marked off from the rest, and capable of being separately dealt with, as requiring some special and stringent system of treatment.&rdquo; Differences however between districts seem to be very great : and if our education is ever to become national, some new policy must be struck out.,Day-schools are public or private. The total number of them in England and Wales in 1858 was 58,975, with 2,535,463 scholars on the books; this makes the proportion of scholars to the entire population 1 in 7&lsquo;7, a larger proportion than in any European country, except Prussia. Private day-schools are more numerous than the public ; but they only educate one-third of the children. They are very popular in towns (where they are chiefly situated) ; more so than they deserve. One reason for their popularity is that parents are &ldquo; independent;&rdquo; they do not like accepting what they think a favour from their social superiors : another is that private schools are thought &ldquo;more genteel.&rdquo; Private schools are of all decrees of merit ; some are good, but a majority are wretched in accommodation and teaching : ephemeral speculations, makeshifts by mere miscellaneous creatures for a precarious livelihood. They are unassisted, uninspected &middot; the Commissioners propose that teachers may be examined for a certificate and that schools under teachers so certificated shall receive a capitation grant on a favourable report by the inspector. By the new Minute (if I understand it rightly) private are placed on precisely the same footing as the,pub he schools; that is, grants will be given on the like conditions beino&middot; fulfilled. &deg;,Public Lay.Schools are conducted, or, to use the technical phrase, &ldquo; ma&not; naged, by the great School-Societies, or by committees appointed by the parish vestries, or by committees selected &lsquo;by subscribers. &#39; They are all,L*
marked by some distinguishing religious policy, but the evidence goes to show that this comes from the managers, who are Churchmen, Wesleyans, or Catholics, or whatever it may be : parents seek for the best reading, writing, and arithmetic ; &ldquo; for them,&rdquo; say one or two of the Assistant Commissioners, &ldquo; the religious difficulty does not exist.&rdquo; The State in no wise interferes with the religious conduct of the schools ; it assists (other conditions being fulfilled) all denominations alike, except Secularists ; it assists even Jews. The only religious condition for a grant is that the Scriptures must be read in the school (a condition still retained by the Commissioners and the new Educational Minute) ; this satisfied, the State requires no more religion, and except in Church of England schools the inspector does not even examine the children in religious knowledge. Thus the State avoids the religious difficulty. Mr, Maurice in his answers to the Commissioners gives his opinion that the positive requirement to read the Scriptures should not be retained, and that deserving Secularists should be helped too ; in which many who read this paper will concur; I who wite, most cordially do so.,Public Lay-Schools are assisted or unassisted. There are 15,952 schools unassisted, 6,987 assisted; but the assisted schools contain 917,255 scholars, the unassisted only 654,393. This in itself proves the general inferiority of the unassisted schools, because a small school is both much more expensive, and also disadvantageous for teaching-purposes ; the direct evidence is also conclusive. The reason why so few of the schools receive Government help is that they do not fulfil the conditions on which alone that help is granted. Adequate subscriptions are not forthcoming : land&not; lords and farmers are &ldquo; apathetic,&rdquo; and the children of the poor suffer. Several schemes have been proposed for mitigating the conditions of the Government grants in these apathetic districts, but they are rejected by the Commissioners as inequitable indulgences and as impracticable with the rest of the system.,Assisted Public Pay-Schools derive income from three sources. The Government grant generally supplies in each case one-fourth of the in&not; come ; school-pence constitute about one-third to three-fifths ; the rest (about a third or a fourth) comes from voluntary contributions. Most schools charge about twopence a week, but a child really costs about eight- pence a week. In the towns and manufacturing districts it is thought that the school-fees might be raised with advantage, and that the parents would not be found unwilling. In manufacturing places the employers of labour contribute largely, especially in the mining districts ; in the rural towns and villages the chief burden lies on the clergy, who freely beg and most freely give ; farmers are loath to help schools, and, say the Commis&not; sioners, &ldquo; it is our duty to state that as a class the landowners, especially those who are non-resident (though there are many honourable exceptions), do not do their duty in the support of popular education, and that they allow others, who are far less able to afford it, to bear the burden of their neglect.&rdquo; In this blame the Admiralty and other departments employing numerous labourers, have to take their share ; they do not subscribe to the parochial schools. Dissenting schools are generally in large towns ; they take larger fees from the children, and are supported by numerous small subscriptions. In this latter respect it is much to be wished that others would follow their example.
The Instructors in the assisted schools, are certificated masters, assistant- masters, and pupil-teachers. The system o&iacute; pupil-teachers , i.e. of boys and girls apprenticed under Government regulations to help masters and mis&not; tresses, dates from 1846, the former monitorial system having broken down. Pupil-teachers are selected by the managers with the help of the Inspector, and on passing tlie required examination are then apprenticed to the teacher for five years ; they must be thirteen years old. They teach five hours a day and receive one hour and a half instruction from the master ; during their apprenticeship they receive a stipend from Government, if they satisfy the inspector&rsquo;s examination, increasing annually from &pound; 10 up to &pound;20. Their apprenticeship out, they can obtain a Queen&rsquo;s Scholarship (say &pound;20 a year) at one of the Normal or Training Colleges, and so go on to obtain a teacher&rsquo;s certificate, which they generally do. On the whole the system has worked admirably. &ldquo; Pupil-teachers,&rdquo; says Mr. Arnold, &ldquo; are the sinews of English primary instruction : the grand merit of our English State system, and its chief title to public respect;&rdquo; and all the evidence proves that they have immensely improved the effectiveness of our schools ; the grant for pupil-teachers forms now the bulk of the annual Government grant. The pupil-teachers themselves are an excellent body, but, as might be ex&not; pected, they carry too much information for their intelligence and culture. They are worked too hard, and their pay in some districts (where wages are high) is insufficient.,Before proceeding to Masters, we must interpose some notice of Students in Training Colleges. There are thirty-four colleges for training teach&not; ers, thirteen o&iacute; them Church of England, and nearly all connected with central religious societies. All but two receive Government assistance ; Government pays about sixty-four per cent, of their total income, which is nearly &pound;100,000 : and Government has also paid &pound;101,641 out of the &pound;334,981, the cost of their original erection. The Report says that institutions of this kind cannot be maintained by voluntary effort. In 1858 the colleges contained 2,065 students. Th&egrave; students are re&not; cruited chiefly from the pupil-teachers who have obtained by examination Queen&rsquo;s Scholarships : the students stay two years, undergoing lectures and practising at the elementary schools attached to the respective col&not; leges. The special subjects of work for men are, first year, Euclid, Book 1&mdash;4, and algebra up to quadratic equations ; or Latin grammar of a limited kind. In the second year (1) Physical Science rudiments ; or (2) Mechanics; or (3) Mathematics, Algebra,&#39;and Trigonometry; or (4) English Literature, from Chaucer to Milton ; or (5) Latin. Besides these there is a general progressive course in Scriptural learning and Church History, in Geography, English History,in Arithmetic; continual practice in reading and writing, and an annual examination in school management : an examination also and prizes for drawing and music. The female candi&not; dates have the same subjects for both years, viz. Religious Knowledge, Writing, English Language, Grammar, portions of English Literature, Draw&not; ing and Music (voluntary), School Management, Domestic Economy. At the end of the two years comes the final examination for certificate, which may be first, second, or third class, but the actual certificate is not finally de- tei mined oi issued, till the teacher has been in charge of an elementary school for two fuff years, and his or her capacity proved. The Commis-
sioners approve of both syllabuses, but want to add to that of the men- students a compulsory course of political economy on the principles which regulate employment, wages, and expenditure, also a course of physiology re&not; lating to the laws of health. The result of all this study and system is, on the whole, good, decidedly good; we have trained zealous teachers. Mr. Maurice says, &ldquo; So far as 1 have been able to observe, the training-colleges are the greatest blessings that have been conferred on the land in the last ([Harter of a century.&rdquo; On the other hand, however, it appears quite clear that the students, male and female, are grievously overtasked ; that much of their learning is cram, especially their religious learning ; that the struggle of competition enslaves their daily life ; that vulgar vanity of half&not; knowledge is their besetting sin. This last evil lies deeper than the Com&not; missioners seem to think : it is not confined to schoolmasters, or the lower strata of educated men. On the whole, training colleges may be considered a real effort towards solving that very difficult problem, How to educate the Schoolmaster: but the problem remains yet to be solved. Persevering effort, and the ripe judgments of discerning men, founded on experience, will alone lead us right : the outcry for following common sense, which generally means the first impressions of ignorant and pre&not; suming men, is not much to be heeded.,Teachers trained in the Colleges are, on the whole, as I have said, far superior to the untrained, but the rank of the certificate won by exami&not; nation by no means indicates the actual efficiency as teacher : it is found to be rather the other way ; examination does not disclose what the man really is, but only a part of him. The main charge against the trained teacher is, that he is apt to neglect the lower classes of his school in the elementary subjects of reading, writing, and arithmetic. The Commis&not; sioners think the only remedy for this is to give him a direct personal pecuniary interest in fulfilling this troublesome duty, by absorbing all grants in the capitation grant, and limiting the amount of that by the number of children who pass muster in these subjects. The managers will thus be driven to insist upon these subjects being effectually taught, and will make their bargain accordingly with the master. The Commissioners are glad also thus to put an end to all semblance of teachers being public servants, and making them know that their emoluments and social rank are to be regulated by the open &ldquo; market.&rdquo; Schoolmasters, it seems, think they are undervalued,&mdash;unreasonably, say the Commissioners ; but is this really so ? There is no space for argument, but my belief is, that teachers, in the largest as well as the most technical signification of the term, are always undervalued, uidess established by authority as an honoured class : &mdash;a truth I would illustrate by comparing the social status of artists with that of established ministers of religion. I do not mean by this, that schoolmasters are underpaid ; they ought to be content to be poor men : we ought not to be content with measuring them by their money-wages. To this end the direct connection of the schoolmaster with the Government (central or local) must, in my opinion, be very helpful.,Next, of School Attendance. The inquiries of the Commissioners show that in elementary public schools most of the children, namely, 65*8 per cent., are between the ages of six and twelve ; few go before six, very few before three ; attendance diminishes rapidly after eleven, and ceases almost
entirely at thirteen. More than three-fifths attend for twenty weeks in the year ; lastly, in towns children often migrate from school to school. The Commissioners say, &ldquo; This state of things leaves great room for improve&not; ment, hut we do not think that it warrants very gloomy views or calls for extreme measures.&rdquo; The chief cause for absolute non-attendance (if the school does exist) is the neglect of bad parents ; poverty is rarely a suffi&not; cient cause ; good parents are found to submit to great, even the greatest straits, rather than let their children go without schooling. The chief cause for quitting school at an early age is juvenile labour, juvenile wages. This chapter in the Keport is especially interesting, for it deals, and I am sorry to say, deals in my opinion unsatisfactorily with almost the greatest problem of the education of the English people. It appears that in agricultural, in manufacturing, in mining districts, in large towns, everywhere in short, there is a demand for the labour of children ; and the wages they earn constitute, in many cases, a considerable portion of the family income. The Commissioners acquiesce in this state of things, as if not the best, yet the best possible. They say that &ldquo; neither the Govern&not; ment nor private persons can effectually resist, or would be morally jus&not; tified in resisting the natural demands of labour, when the child has arrived, physically speaking, at the proper age for labour, and when its wages are such as to form a strong motive to its parents for withdraw&not; ing it from school.&rdquo; They accordingly recommend efforts for increasing the regularity of school-attendance, rather than to prolong its duration ; and suggest the great importance of night-schools, and other educational opportunities for adults. All schemes for a general compulsory system of education they reject (quite rightly, I think) as unnecessary, and as doing violence to the habits of our people, and the constitution of our existing schools: and they also quote the evidence of Mr. Pattison and Mr. Arnold, who examined the compulsory educational systems in Germany and Switzerland, to show that the legal compulsion is inoperative, that parents in those countries send their children to school voluntarily. &rsquo; The Commissioners, however, seem to submit to the compulsory education of children employed in factories, which is now enforced by law (convinced, I conjecture, against their will). They do not state whether they desire that the law shall be extended to children engaged in any of the numerous othei similar occupations, as pottery-making, etc. The factory-schools seem to be in a very indifferent condition, simply because any school will satisfy the Act o&iacute; Parliament, and many employers do not care to have a good school* The Commissioners recommend that the factory-inspectors shaff have power to refuse the required certificate of school-attendance, un&not; less the school is &ldquo;fair&rdquo; for the purpose intended. In the coal-mining districts the provisions of the statute seem to be much disregarded, and for the same reason : the Inspectors have not sufficient powers. But on this most important subject of the obligation not to employ children ex&not; cept on condition of education, the Commissioners appear cold, hesitatino- unwilling to speak out their mind. They are happy again when they return,* Sir James Slmttleworth, speaking of an earlier period, says, certain of the schools consisted simply hf the ordinary dining-room which, perhaps, some cripple was put,* with pieces of old newspapers apparatus of instruction than a stick.&rdquo; &ldquo; The apparatus of of the factory, into and with no other
to speak of voluntary effort, and the excellent results which have followed from employers voluntarily providing schools for the children they employ, and insisting upon the attendance of the children, as a condition of em&not; ployment. Nothing, indeed, can be more satisfactory than the Report of the London Lead Company&rsquo;s doings in Middleton ; you will see there an educated people, young and old, comfortable homes, and &ldquo; the sole policeman has a beat of about sixteen miles, and he considers his situation nearly a sinecure.&rdquo;,Prizes for encouraging school attendance, whether money rewards from private purses, or donations of public appointments, are not recommended by the Commissioners ; the first scheme is costly, and neither one nor the other would affect any large number of the children. The Report adds, &ldquo; The tendency of these schemes is to teach the people to value education as the means of rising to a higher station in life. This is of course a rea&not; sonable object in many cases ; but the main object of promoters of educa&not; tion must be to teach the people to value it as a source of morality, enjoy&not; ment, and comfort, in the station in which the great mass of them are necessarily destined to remain.&rdquo;,Each school receiving State assistance, is examined once every year on a preappointed day by the Government Inspector, who is usually a distin&not; guished university man. He examines the premises, the school apparatus, and every class, but he only examines a proportion of the children indi&not; vidually. He founds his report upon his judgment of the school in all par&not; ticulars, and on his report the various grants are made, so much to the master, so much to the pupil-teachers, etc. General testimony agrees that the system of inspection works well ; but that Inspectors are too apt to judge a school by the attainments of the highest class, which three-quar&not; ters of the children never reach. The lower classes are too often handed over to the younger pupil-teachers, and the result is that the children do not learn satisfactorily the elementary subjects; perhaps it is not too much to say that in the lower classes of the majority of schools the spelling is bad, the arithmetic is bad, and above all the reading is bad ; the words so mumbled, guessed at, and skipped, that neither hearer nor reader have any understanding of the thing read. This is the main evil which the Com&not; missioners insist should be remedied. The discipline of the schools seems to be extremely good, and their elevating influence on the after-lives of the children, on the parents, and on all about them is beyond doubt, of which several signal examples are given in the Report. The Commissioners con&not; clude, &ldquo; A set of good schools civilizes a whole neighbourhood. The most important function ot the schools is that which they best peiioim.,The Commissioners before producing their hand of recommendations discuss some general questions. 1st. Ought Government to aid educa&not; tion ? a majority of the Commissioners think Yes, it ought; the minority think No, it ought not ; but in the second resort prefer the scheme pro&not; posed in the Report to the existing methods. 3ndly. Should education be generally compulsory ? This they dispose of summarily on the grounds I have already stated/ 3rdly. Should there be a system of education sup&not; ported by parochial rating ? Several lulls of this kina have been intro&not; duced to Parliament, and there are many strong advocates of this plan, either in whole or in part; amongst them Sir James Shuttleworth and
Dr. Temple. The advantages it offers are, that education will be general, will be cheap, and will be the people&rsquo;s own doing, every parish in the kingdom bearing its own burden. The Commissioners however decide against it, partly because ratepayers would thereby become the managers o&iacute; the schools, and experience shows they would be illiberal and otherwise unfit ; partly because of the religious difficulty, the strife between Church&not; men and Dissenters. 4thly. They consider the pros and cons of the ex&not; isting system. Pros are, it is in possession of the field ; it has succeeded ; in twenty years it has led to the foundation or great improvement of 9,388 schools, encouraging, so they say, local and individual effort ; and, by a system of inspection, pupil-teachers, and trained masters, it has greatly raised the quality of instruction and discipline ; further, it secures or leaves free the religious character of instruction without conflict of religious par&not; ties. Contra : It threatens to be very expensive ; it leaves destitute parishes and almost all smaller schools unassisted ; it requires a complex and costly system of administration in the central office, which mud break down, if the system ever became national ; lastly, the education of the majority of children in the schools is very defective in the rudimentary subjects.,The plan the Commissioners propose is to abolish all grants except a capitation grant, and to have a county rate in aid. (a) The Capitation Grant ; to be conditional on the school being healthy, well drained, venti&not; lated, etc,, and open to inspection, and on the teacher being certificated ; to be so much per child* upon the average attendance of the children, the Inspector having power to choose a higher or a lower sum (within fixed limits), say, 5s. Qd. or 6s., according to his opinion of the general character of the school ; and 2s. 6c?. a child on so many of the average number of children in attendance throughout the year as have been under the in&not; struction of pupil-teachers or assistant-teachers, allowing thirty children for each pupil-teacher, and sixty for each assistant-master. These two sub-grants to be paid in one lump sum to the County Treasurer, upon whom the managers are to draw. The Commissioners thus would do away with the complex system of apportioning the grants in the Council Office, and at the same time would secure the maintenance of pupil-teachers : they would also leave masters, pupil-teachers, etc., free to make their own bargains with managers. (/3) The County Rate : Schoolmasters (an in&not; ferior class to the present Inspectors) to be appointed by a County Board to examine every child in reading, writing, and arithmetic, in any school desiring the county rate in aid ; the county rate to pay 22 s. Ы. or 21s. for every child passing out who has attended 140 times &#39;in the year ; girls also to pass in plain needlework. The effect of this latter plan they think would be, 1st, That many schools would obtain public aid, which now have no prospect of doing so. 2nd, That it would excite local in&not; terest. 3rdly, It would secure general proficiency in reading, writing, and a&iacute; it Kinetic. To pi event too liberal assistance, they propose the capitation grant and the county rate, together, should not exceed 15s. per child of the average number attending, that is to say, on rough estimate, half the cost of their education.,This seems the proper place for noticing the New Minute, or revised code of Regulations, which has caused such a consternation in the educa&not; tional world. It is announced to come into operation on the 81st of
March, 1862. It is founded on the facts brought to light by the Report, and partly, but partly only, on the recommendations of the Commissioners. It discards the notion of a county rate, and abolishing all other grants, establishes a new capitation grant, payable to managers only. Its main purpose is not to reduce the amount of Government assistance, which the Minute calculates will not be diminished, but to pay henceforward only for results in reading, writing, and arithmetic, tested by examination of the children individually. The plan may shortly be stated thus. Condi&not; tions of grant : School premises sufficient, teacher certificated, girls (if any) taught plain needlework, and &ldquo;no objection of a gross kind&rdquo; to the state of the school. Amount of grant : One penny per scholar for every attend&not; ance after the first hundred (there may be two attendances in the day). All children for Avhom head-money is claimed, to be examined annually by the Inspector in reading, writing, and arithmetic, and forfeitures of one- third, two-thirds, or the whole of the head-money for any child, on failing in one, two, or all three subjects. Examinations graduated according to age (items specified in the Minute). No head-money for children over eleven years of age once passed out, or for any child who has not attended sixteen times in the month previous to the examination. The grant may also be reduced one-tenth up to one-half of the whole on the Inspector re&not; porting faults of instruction or discipline, or want of proper furniture, school apparatus, etc.; and by the sum of &pound;10 for every thirty scholars after the first fifty in average attendance, who are without a certificated assistant or pupil-teacher. Total grant not to exceed 15s. a year per scholar in average attendance, or the amount of school fees and subscrip&not; tions. This plan, with a few mutatis mutandis , to extend to all schools, private and public, including factory schools.,The good results aimed at by this new measure are, to simplify the cen&not; tral administration, to enforce the adequate teaching of the elementary subjects, and to extend Government assistance to poorer schools. The objections to it are, 1. It destroys the independent position of the masters, who have hitherto regarded themselves partly as servants of the State, and who became masters on the faith of a Government Certificate having a certain money value. Masters, assistant-masters, and pupil-teachers are henceforth to be left to their bargains with managers. 3. The individual examinations, which are to embrace even children of three years old, may probably be found not to work ; they can hardly escape producing injus&not; tice and bad feeling. An Inspector counts (I believe) on giving three minutes to each child ; in these&#39;three minutes he is to determine whether a year&rsquo;s work on the part of the master is to be rewarded, or to go with&not; out reward. And fancy the condition of the Inspector&rsquo;s mind on examin&not; ing the two-hundredth child ! 3. The rule requiring attendance in the last,month will work unjustly, where the inspection is in harvest-time, etc. 4. Anything higher than the three R&rsquo;s is not only not rewarded, but prac&not; tically discouraged : nothing is to be received for children over eleven, passed out ! Thus the function of the master is lowered (and lowered by an appeal to his pocket) ; and interest in the intellectual progress of the children checked in all, managers, masters, inspectors, and the children themselves. To all this must be added, that the change is so great and so sudden, that it must disturb the arrangements of many schools and
close some altogether. These are certainly objections of the gravest kind ; and they are not objections to matters of practical detail only. The new measure seems to belong in great degree to that order of philosophy, which reduces everything human to a commercial transaction ; declares every human motive is and must be from the purse and for the purse ; and would have human results (even the answers of little children three years old) weighed and measured in brazen scales, and paid for or not paid for accordingly.,The new Minute also deals out hard measure to the Training Colleges. Notwithstanding the statement of the Commissioners that these colleges are effecting great good, and from the nature of them cannot be main&not; tained without powerful Government assistance, the Minute enacts that no fresh grants shall be made for improving premises of Normal Schools, cuts off one-ffith of the Queen&rsquo;s Scholarships, and, as I understand, cuts off also the augmentation of teachers&rsquo; and lecturers&rsquo; salaries, besides other minor grants, which these institutions now receive. It is impossible to say what the effect of this may be.,We now return to the Report, and come upon a sad subject,&mdash;the edu&not; cation of Pauper Children. Pauper children in workhouses are illegi&not; timate, or orphans, or the children of felons, idiots, etc. ; all destitute, all absolutely friendless,&mdash;or worse, with bad friends out-of-doors, who aie allowed to see them once a week. Of such there are now in our six hundred workhouse schools about 37,545 : besides the burden on the parishes for support, they cost the State for education &pound;30,000 a year. In these schools the children mix with the grown-up paupers, the girls with the unmarried women (who have babies). Mr. Cumin, an assistant- commissioner, writes, &ldquo; It seems impossible to exaggerate the spirit of lying, low cunning, laziness, insubordination, and profligacy, which cha&not; racterize the pauper class in workhouses.&rdquo; To this add that the teachers in the schools are uncomfortable, underpaid, and lose instead of gain by improving their schools, that no trade is taught, and boys and girls alike leave the workhouse helpless loons. Tor result, in Plymouth, out of seventy-four girls thirty-seven had returned to the workhouse, and only thirteen were known to be doing well ; of fifty-six boys only eighteen doing : ą^j^is is but a sample. One of the Commissioners reports&mdash; &ldquo; One girl, said the mistress of the Southampton Workhouse, &ldquo; and not a bad specimen of a pauper girl, said to me the other day, &lsquo; My cousin Sally left some time ago, and now she has come back with a baby. I hope soon to go out, and to come back too with a baby.&rsquo; &rdquo; &ldquo; Could anv,of the children,&rdquo; I asked, &ldquo; on their arrival, repeat the Lord&rsquo;s Prayer ? &rdquo; &ldquo; Not one of them had ever heard of it.&rdquo; The great workhouse school of Marylebone seems to be as bad as any : nearly all the girls turn out &ldquo; un- foi tunate ; many of the boys become thieves or paupers. Most wretched most miserable are the workhouse schools, for the most part, but in some the reading and writing is good, accounted for by the regularity of atten- dance, and the teaching of the master himself. &lsquo;,Here however is the remedy,&mdash;District or separate schools. District schools are schools apart from workhouses, in which the pauper children ol several parishes are collected and taught ; recent Acts of Parliament permitting parishes to combine for this purpose. Removed from the de-
praving influences of the workhouse, and taught industrially, the poor children do well in school and afterwards ; &ldquo; failures,&rdquo; i. e. descent into crime or pauperism, are reduced from fifty per cent, to two or three per cent., or even less ; a most notable result. But at present there are only six District Schools in all England and Wales, and they contain only 2,683 children.,The Separate Schools are schools for particular parishes, away from the workhouses. Of such there are nineteen, containing 4,381 children; they too are working excellently. Why, then, not more district schools, why not more separate schools, why any workhouse schools at all? The paro&not; chial guardians are to blame. &ldquo; They care little for education, much for expense they will have no plan which requires immediate outlay. The Commissioners propose that the parishes shall be compelled by law to do their duty, and have district or separate schools. Let us have workhouse schools no more !,The number of children of out-door paupers receiving relief from parishes actually amounts to 388,424, of whom nearly half are dependent on widows. By an Act passed in 1855, parochial guardians may , if they deem proper, grant further relief to provide for the education of such children in schools approved by them ; but such education shall not be imposed as a condi&not; tion of relief. The guardians read &ldquo; may &rdquo; as &ldquo; need not.&rdquo; The whole number of children in all England and Wales so educated is only 6,537 ; and it is calculated that at least 100,000 out-door pauper children are totally uneducated ; perhaps the most miserable creatures in England. The Commissioners propose that the guardians henceforth shall send all children of out-door paupers to district or separate schools, and that they shall impose as a condition of out-door relief, that such education shall be given. Well done, Commissioners !,Ragged Schools , winch we all have heard of, number about 31,000 children. Where no industrial education is given, the Commissioners do not approve of them. They teach little, discipline little, and draw children from the regular schools. They do not receive any Government assistance, and the Commissioners do not recommend any to be given them.,Ragged Industrial Schools, certified or uncertified, receive help from Government, but they are chiefly supported by voluntary subscription. They contain about 5,000 children, who are usually lodged and fed as welf as taught. To the certified schools magistrates may commit vagrant children, and about one-seventh of the children are so committed. The Commissioners propose to extend the powers of the magistrates, and in time to substitute for these schools the parochial district or separate schools. I understand that additional powers have been given by an Act of Parliament passed at the end of last session.,Reformatories , started in 1854. They are private establishments for the reception of convicted juvenile offenders, supported chiefly by the State, partly by subscriptions, and in a small degree by payments from the parents and county or borough rates. The distinctive peculiarity of them is the enforcement of severe and carefully regulated labour : the boys, who are never in for less than two years, dig, trench, make bricks, etc. : two o&iacute; the reformatories are on ship-board,&mdash;the &lsquo; Akbar &rsquo; in Liverpool, the &lsquo; Cornwall &rsquo; in the Thames. Altogether there are forty-seven now, con-
taining 3,594 young convicts ; total annual cost, &pound;72,893. The complete separation from bad parents, strict discipline, and hard work, bring excel&not; lent results ; of the lads discharged, only one in eight has been re-con- yicted ; of the girls, rather a larger proportion : juvenile crime is decreas&not; ing. The Commissioners are so satisfied that they have no recommenda&not; tions to make.,The chapter on the Army Schools is extremely satisfactory. Much is to be done, for our soldiers are from an ignorant class ; but a good deal is doing, and the Commissioners report &ldquo; going on well.&rdquo; Every battalion has a sergeant schoolmaster, every barrack a school ; masters are regularly trained for their work at a good normal school at Chelsea (instituted 1846) ; instruction in regiments is voluntary, but officers and men are beginning to act upon the conviction that the education of the soldier is worth caring for. There are also fair schools attached to regiments for the education of soldiers&rsquo; children, and good schools connected with the Eoyal Arsenal at Woolwich. The Duke of York&rsquo;s school for soldiers&rsquo; children, which is a small nursery for the army, is in excellent condition.,The Naval Schools are far inferior. The * Victory &rsquo; is perhaps the only ship in the service in which there is a really good school ; in most ships the school is only nominal, and the masters are, with scarcely any excep&not; tion, quite inefficient : they are not trained men at all. The Greenwich Hospital Schools were badly worked (except the highest school), but they have been visited by a special Royal Commission, and are in the course of reform. The Eoyal Marine Schools might be as good as the Army Schools, but they are not : the Dockyard Schools for apprentices are also reported as &ldquo; languishing. Altogether the Admiralty needs severe spurring to do their duty in this as in other departments. The Commissioners, amono&middot; other things, recommend the establishment of a normal school at Green&not; wich, and the introduction of a system of pupil-teachership on board her Majesty&rsquo;s vessels, and eten a system of inspection ; and they propose to classify ship-schoolmasters, placing the highest class on a level with third- class warrant officers.,The last part of the Report deals with Charitable Endowments. Foun&not; dation Schools are scattered unevenly over the country ; they abound in the places which were populous and wealthy in the sixteenth and seven&not; teenth centuries, as Coventry ; places of modern growth, like Manchester often remain comparatively unprovided. The endowments are of all di&not; mensions, from &pound;5 a year up to many thousands, and are generally vested m the hands of trustees variously elected. They were intended by the founders for the poor, but not for popular education; the founder desiring to do some lasting good with his money, generally left it, tied up for аП time, to help on, year after year, a few poor people of his own kin or his own parish or county, to a better station in life. One of the chief questions is how far the State ought to interfere with such foundations ; and it is plea&not; sant to find the Commissioners speaking out in the following decisive lan&not; guage By the law of England, and by the law of nature, a man is in- capable of making a perpetual disposition of his property. The State suffers him to exercise an indefinite power over the land for the purpose of his foundation ; and m so doing it is not only entitled but bound to secure the interests of future generations, which can only be done by re-
tabling the poiver of modifying the founder&rsquo;s regulations, when necessary, to suit the requirements of future times. This principle has been acted on ever since the Reformation, but it has never been distinctly expressed.&rdquo; So saying, they proceed to consider the condition of the endowed schools. They find them as a class in a dull lethargic condition ; trustees are in&not; different or job ; incapable worn-out men (sometimes worse) are appointed schoolmasters, and once appointed cannot easily be dismissed (for a tech&not; nical legal reason) ; the instruction is often purely classical, i.e. Latin and Greek, wholly unfitted for the poorer ranks of the people, and so utterly disliked by the neighbourhoods that in some schools no foundation scholars can be got at all !,The Commissioners propose to deal soberly but very resolutely with these foundations, so as to make the most of the &pound;350,000 a year, much of which is now utterly wasted. They also desire that a large propor&not; tion of charity money, which is now doled out by the &ldquo; dead-hand&rdquo; in pa&not; rishes to poor persons, to the great injury of their moral habits, shall be turned to purposes of education, which is the best of all boons to the poor. Their main proposition is to constitute the Committee of Council on Education to be the central authority over all charities, instead of the Charity Commissioners and the Court of Chancery, and to give it really administrative powers. They suggest ordinances, as required in each case, shall be drawn by the Committee, afterwards to receive the sanc&not; tion of Parliament :&mdash;such ordinances to determine the appropriation of noxious miscellaneous charities ; and in endowed schools to regulate the subjects of instruction, the appointment of masters and power of dismissal, and the distribution of the income between the several objects of the foun&not; dation ; to establish a system of annual inspection, to provide for the abo&not; lition or relaxation of present restrictions (religious restrictions excepted) ; and, where necessary, even to change the site of the school, and convert free boarding and clothing schools into ordinary day-schools. These sug&not; gestions are bold, but not over-bold, and I trust may be fully canied out. Amongst other minor matters, grotesque costume is to be abolished. We are to see the bare-headed Bluecoat boy, with his orange stockings, in our streets no more !,I have now concluded this summary (necessarily an imperfect one) of a volume which must for years to come be the handbook of education in England. Much might be said and ought to be said (by some one) upon the multitudinous and most interesting facts here recorded, and upon the various conclusions adopted by the Commissioners, especially, I think, upon their having yielded upon some most important points, as the position of teachers, and the education (or non-education) of children employed in labour, to that which (to use no harsher term) may be called the commer&not; cial temper of the time. Call it this, call it what you will, this it, if bowed to as supreme, is most utterly incongruous to the high, and without any cant at all, sacred cause of Education. Apart from this, which I deeply regret, our warmest thanks are due to the Commissioners for the candid, earnest, and most able manner in which they have fulfilled their hio-h task ; so likewise to the Assistant Commissioners. The Report is the model of what a Blue-book ought to be. Among many thoughts which it suggests is this happiest one, that a great work is going on in
England, in which, we are all, every one of us, in one form or other, called to take a part. It is joy beyond speaking to know, to feel, that this is indeed so. Always, always there will be, in this our native land, children of those who toil for us, boys and girls, growing up to be Englishmen and Englishwomen, and in turn to be the fathers and mothers of new generations. Never shall we be able to do our whole duty of bro&not; therly helpfulness to them. And if there is one conclusion more cer&not; tain than another to be drawn from this Export, it is that all classes must help, and that the hand bearing the lamp of truth and knowledge must be held out to our people from infancy to full labouring man&not; hood. This being so, is it foolish to strive that some part of the good work may be faithfully rendered by &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; ?,Vernon Lushington.,ON THE EMPLOYMENT OE WOMEN.,ADDENDUM to the article in the last number.,I find that I was not quite correct in treating the report in the &pound; Daily News&rsquo; as a complete reprint of Miss Faithfull&rsquo;s Paper read before the So&not; cial Science Association. That report is verbatim, so far as it goes, but omits a few sentences. In addition to what I have quoted as the entire of the argument against the reduction of wages, the complete report (pub&not; lished in the &lsquo;Friend of the People&rsquo; of Sept. 7th, and printed at the Vic&not; toria Press) contains the following sentences :&mdash;,&ldquo; It must be remembered, as is well argued by the author of the &lsquo; In&not; dustrial and Social Condition of Women,&rsquo; that the dreaded increase of competition is of a kind essentially different from the increase of competi&not; tion in the labour market, arising from ordinary causes. Such increase commonly arising from an increased population, either by birth or immigra&not; tion, or a decrease in the capital available for the labouring population. But in the case we are contemplating, this will not occur, since women already form part of the population. Nor will the wages-capital be drawn upon for the maintenance of a greater number of individuals than it now supports.&rdquo;,These sentences, it will be observed, do not&#39; affect anything I have writ&not; ten ; they merely admit the fact which I have stated as one of the grounds of my argument, namely, that the women for whom it is proposed to find mechanical employment are already being supported, indirectly, by the wages paid to men. From this it necessarily follows, as I have said, that they would be doing their new work for nothing. The wages-capital will not be drawn upon for the maintenance of more people, but it will be dis&not; tributed among a greater number ; those, that is to say, who got it before as payment for the mechanical work, and those who previously had trans&not; ferred to them a share of this payment while employed on other work ; which other work they must henceforth give up.,Е. B. L.
NEWS, &amp;amp;c.,London .&mdash;Autumn trips have been the principal feature of the last month. Even before the College closed for the vacation, two parties had strayed off to North Devon, and come back full of the glories of Clovelly and Linton, and the excellent fare provided by Captain Jolly at two shillings a day, beds included. Next went a party of three to Normandy and Paris&mdash;rich and lazy ones&mdash;convinced that roads were made only for diligences, and diligences only to be ridden in, walking being so &lsquo;hot;&rsquo; but they en&not; joyed most thoroughly the beauties of Amiens, Rouen, Caen, Mont St. Michel, etc., and the miscellaneous splendours and gaieties of Paris. The largest party, beginning with walks in Burnham beeches and Taplow woods, with quiet evening pulls on the river, and songs and bits from The Golden Treasury all day, proceeded up the valley of the Thames to Oxford, thence to Woodstock and Stratford-on-Avon&mdash;our best Shaksperians reading four of The Master&rsquo;s plays in and near there&mdash;and then on to Bristol, Clovelly, and Dartmoor. Another party took nearly the Surrey and Sussex walk described by Lieute&not; nant Sutton in the last Magazine, but kept the broken sandhill range to Hindhead, in&not; stead of the chalk. Are not Hindhead and Blackdown the finest bits in their counties, and the walk from Haslemere to Petworth the most charming walk in Sussex ? Can the Petworth pictures&mdash;Claude&rsquo;s best landscape, well preserved, Sir Joshua&rsquo;s Turners, early and late&mdash;be adequately described, or justice done to moonlight walks, and all the joy on and of the sands ? No ; so we let another party of three tell us of their expedi&not; tion to Winchester, Salisbury, Stonehenge, and then to North Devon, Clovelly, and Dartmoor ; and last hear from single travellers of trips to Dublin and Killarney, to New&not; castle, Durham, and Cheshire, the French coast, Brighton, and country homes ; all re&not; turning with browner faces and lighter hearts, to gladden their friends and settle again steadily to work.,LETTER TO THE EDITOR.,DIFFICULTIES OF A STUDENT.,Sir,&mdash;Allow me, as a teacher, to say a few words on the subject of Mr. Martin&rsquo;s in&not; teresting note in the last number. Mr. Martin thinks that the chief cause of the slow rate of increase of the College is the want of a better understanding by the teachers of the denree of the students&rsquo; knowledge when they begin their work. I cannot agree with him in&deg;attributing so much to this single cause. He complains that the teacher begins without any previous examination of his class. Surely if the teaching is carried on, as it ought to be, colloquially, with a constant interchange of ideas between teacher and student, the very first evening&rsquo;s lesson must give the former a pretty good notion of the capacities of the men he has to deal with. For myself, I can say positively that one or two evenings are always sufficient to enable me to guess which are the best in the class in point of ability, which the next best, and so on ; and the opinion thus formed seldom fails to be confirmed by the year&rsquo;s experience. In many subjects the teacher professes to beo-in at the beginning ; in these no examination would do any good. If the students cannot follow the teacher, all they have to do is to ply him with questions. Mr. Mar&not; tin says that the many questions asked evidence an ineffectual struggle to get clear notions.&rdquo; My experience, on the contrary, is that it is very difficult to get a new class into the habit of asking questions. If they will only do this, they can t fail to get clear notions,&rdquo; unless indeed some of them are simply incapable of pursuing the subject, and this happens but very rarely. The various degrees of capacity and previous know&not; ledge among the students constitute a difficulty incident to any and every system of class teaching&middot; vou will find the same in the Professor&rsquo;s lecture-room at the Lmversity; and the only way that I know of meeting it is by supplementing the class-teaching by private instruction. This is what we have done by the &ldquo;Practice-class&rdquo; arrangement and I cannot conceive any plan which can more completely meet the case put by Mr. Martin. If the student finds that he does not understand something which the teacher assumes him to know all about, he has the opportunity of getting the point cleared up by asking the
tutor of the practice-class to explain it ; this will give him the chance of finding ont whether he is fit to continue in the class or not. I do not see how a formal examination would answer the purpose better ; perhaps, indeed, it would be an improvement if the opening lesson of each course were made more expressly introductory, and free to all comers, no one being expected to book himself till the end of the lesson. An hour or two would be well bestowed in giving to the students a general idea of the nature of the subject, and, which is of immense importance, a notion of the amount of time and trouble which it will probably demand. As Mr. Martin evidently describes what he has himself seen, I have no wish to dispute the accuracy of the picture he draws. Many, no doubt, &ldquo; drop off in despair,&rdquo; when they find that what they have undertaken is harder work than they expected; but this is only what happens in a thousand other things besides study. People set about enterprises of which they do not and cannot know the real nature till they have actually tried them. The proportion of men who hold on at our College is certainly as large as at any place of study I know. Take the University of Cambridge, for instance. Not more than one sixth or seventh part of the total num&not; ber who enter, persevere in a course of serious reading for the higher honours of the place, and this notwithstanding the solid prizes open to academical ambition. Of the men who have passed through classes of mine at the College, it is true that only a cer&not; tain proportion have held on till the end of their several courses ; but on consideration I think that the proportion is rather larger than might be expected. In almost every case, when a man drops oif, it is for some assignable reason other than mere disinclina&not; tion or inability to go on ; either he moves away from where he was living, or work carries him out of London, or a special job prevents his coming for three or four nights. The last is the most serious obstacle&#39;: no man&rsquo;s work is quite regular, every employment has its slack and its busy times ; but the Practice Class, or a little extra help from the teacher, will mitigate even this difficulty; or, if necessary, the student can wait and join another class. But after all, the main security for the permanence of a class lies in the existence of personal relations between the teacher and students, and between the stu&not; dents themselves. Whatever difficulties a man encounters in his work will be wonderfully smoothed down when he finds that he is doing it among friends. This indeed constitutes nine-tenths of the reasons for working in classes at all, or having colleges at all. If it was not for the force gained by the union of mutual interests, learning might as well be carried on in solitary rooms. Teachers can do little more than direct people how to study; the actual work they must do for themselves. The more this is acted on, the more stable, and therefore the more progressive, will be our college work. As was&#39;said in the discussion referred to by Mr. Martin, a slight increase in the time each student remains would soon double the present number of three or four hundred students. This progress depends more, as it seems to me, on our keeping more steadily in view the principles on which our College is founded, than on the adoptier of any new plans.,In conclusion, 1 hope this letter will not seem too didactic if I wind it up by a few Suggestions for New Students.,1. Before you begin a subject, get some one who has gone through it before, to tell you what sort of work it is, how much time it takes, and what result it leads to.&rsquo;,2. If you are not blest with a rare genius, or a rare amount of leisure, don&rsquo;t join,more than one class at a time. J,3. Don&rsquo;t miss the Practice Class if you can help it.,4. If you are prevented from coming to the Practice Class, be certain that you do at,least an equivalent amount of work at home. J,5. Make friends with everybody in the class, teacher included.,6. Ask questions ad lib. about everything you don&rsquo;t understand.,A Teacher.

NOYEMBEE 1, 1861.,WOLVERHAMPTON &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,Meeting and Annual Report.,Our College has just entered upon the fifth year of its existence. Our annual meeting was held in St. George&rsquo;s Hall, on Friday, October 4, and in order to make it less formal and more social, it was preceded by a tea, when about a hundred and fifty students, teachers, and friends, partook together of a pleasant, comfortable meal. Tea over, the Rev. J. H. lies, rector of St. Peter&rsquo;s, in the absence of our President, the Earl of Dart&not; mouth, took the chair, and commenced the proceedings by a happy, hearty, practical speech. The Rev. J. H, Sharwood, President of a capital Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College that has been recently started at Walsall, gave us an earnest address ; and several of our teachers and students gave us speeches, short, hearty, and genial. Mr. Till brought a few of his friends, and con&not; tributed some very pretty songs and glees, and a very happy social even&not; ing was spent. The Report, of which the following is a copy, was read by the Hon. Secretary. A great many new students have entered the College, and we look forward to a quiet, hard-working, and successful Term.,Third Report, October 4,1861.,During the past year the following Classes have been conducted :&mdash;,First,Term.,Second,Term.,Third,Term.,Fourth,Term.,Bible Class.,15,14,12,Latiu &mdash;Section I.,4,4,5,_,,, Section II.,11,9,6,_,&bdquo; Section III.,&mdash;,6,5,3,Arithmetic&mdash;Section I.,10,7,4 &#39;,&bdquo; Section II.,10,8,3,__,,, Section III.,15,7,8,13,Bookkeeping.,&mdash;,18,5,Algebra .,9,6,9,_,Mathematics.,5,12,8,English Grammar.,5,6,3,English Literature.,6,6,4,_,Geography.,2,_,French&mdash;Section I.,В,4,4,,, Section II.,2,3,4,Writing.,14,3,8,&mdash;,Total Number of Students.,66,68,52,16,VOL. III.
Of these sixteen Classes, three, viz. the third Latin, the third Arithmetic, and the second French, have been conducted by students, who, having worked successfully in their respective studies, have come forward with&not; out solicitation, almost without notice, to impart to others of what they themselves have received from the College. Two of these Classes, it will be seen, have been continued without interruption during the vaca&not; tion. To these student-teachers the Council offer their warmest acknow&not; ledgments, and recognize in them a valuable proof of the true success of the College.,During the past year the students have formed themselves into a So&not; cial Class, meeting once a week to take coffee together, and to spend a happy evening in songs, recitations, discussions, etc. It is hoped that a large accession of members to this interesting Class will be gained from the new students who may enter the College this next Term.,The College&rsquo;s connection with the Society of Arts is yearly becoming a more important feature. In 1859 one student passed the Society of Arts&rsquo; examination, and gained one first-class and two third-class certifi&not; cates. In 1860 seven candidates passed, and gained two second-cląss and ten third-class certificates. This year seven passed again, gaining eight first-class, one second-class, and two third-class : thus gaining a higher proportion of first-class certificates than any o&iacute; the larger institutions in the,country. , .,As these examinations seemed to be but feebly appreciated m South Staffordshire, Mr. Talbot, the energetic Secretary of Messrs. Chance&rsquo;s Reading Room and Library, at Smethwick (from which institution candi&not; dates have been availing themselves for some time of these examinations), invited the different institutions in the district to form themselves into an Educational Union, in order that the Society of Arts&rsquo; scheme might be more extensively used. The College was one of the first to join this Union, which was formed in the spring of the year I860, under the presidency of Lord Lyttelton. So far the results of this Union have been very gratify&not; ing and only last Monday forty-seven candidates from South Staffordshire received their certificates from the hands of the President at Dudley. Of these forty-seven, Smethwick sent twelve and our College seven.,In consequence of this Educational Union holding its first annual meet&not; ing in this town last year, the Council resolved not to hold any public meeting in connection with the College, but instead thereof they invited the students to a private tea-meeting in St. Peter&rsquo;s school-room, to intro&not; duce to them the Rev. F. D. Maurice, the Principal of the London Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College.,We are now entering on the fifth year of our work. The experience ot the past, though unmarked by any striking features, bids us go on with hope and confidence. We could wish our numbers were larger ; but the spirit and tone of our College life, the hearty goodwill that animates all alike, leaves nothing to be desired. Our oldest students are still our fastest friends. We believe that from many a mechanic&rsquo;s home m this town there often arises the silent thanksgiving for our Working Men&rsquo;s Colleo-e, and that many of us, teachers and students alike, have derived from it a new incentive to duty&mdash;an added blessing to our bounteous store of mercy.
