We notice it here, because it is a book for every &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Library. The total cost of it, in stout half-morocco suited to long service, is &pound;14. 4 s. ; but it is also sold in parts or numbers as required. It consists of eleven great volumes, two devoted to Geography, three to Biography, two to Natural History, and four to Arts and Sciences. No critic can pretend to have read it, but only to have read in it ; all however speak highly, most highly, in its praise: its peculiar merits seem to consist in its con&not; venient divisions, and a large degree of completeness, combined with a very moderate price. I have hardly done more than look into the Bio&not; graphy and Geography, but with very great satisfaction; reading de&not; scriptions of strange places I have visited, and life-stories of memorable men now past away or still living.,Perhaps the first sensation of the reader on opening these massive volumes will be one of bewilderment, and unwillingness to traverse any such vast mountain of knowledge. But on better consideration he will feel two things ; first, that kind of reverence which the spectacle of any great human labour cannot but call forth ; and secondly, that this (or indeed any) Cyclop&aelig;dia is a witness to the inexhaustible interest of reality and simple truth. He will see that it is in fact a record of a thousand thousand conquests over thick night, won in many generations by far-reaching industry, and patient intelligence, in many cases even&mdash;say the discovery of America&mdash;by down- light unmistakable valour: and so gazing on these columns, there mav,m 2
come flashing through his mind something of the exultation with which a people greets a victorious army returning homeward. At least he can&not; not but observe how the age in which we live is assiduously minding and doing her business ; everywhere extending and consolidating positive knowledge; with honest sober eyes scrutinizing the past of human his&not; tory, studying the starry heavens, the solid earth, and all living things, tracking everywhere the dominion of steadfast laws, then recording what is found, for ourselves and for those who come after. A Cyclopaedia wit&not; nesses that all these things are being done.,But let not the reader stop here ! Admiration is good, but not barren admiration. Let the book be really used. A great dictionary of this kind, if within easy reach, should be constantly appealed to. There is no study, no reading, which does not involve local conditions, the history of particular men, the growth of successive efforts, and a variety of other matters which it is well to know, sometimes even indispensable to know, if we would rightly understand the subject in hand. It is here that a Cyclopaedia, the design of which we owe mainly to the great Tien ch&not; inen of the last century, may be of real service to the individual student. It is &ldquo; a teaching all round,&rdquo; a catalogued summary of all knowledge. Under the names of particular men and places, it posts up such infor&not; mation as ordinary inquirers seek for concerning them ; under the titles of subjects or things, it gives short popular treatises, popular in the right sense, which relate the history, and describe the scope of the special matter, whatever it may be : the essence of a good Cyclop&aelig;dia being, as already suggested, that the information shall be easily found, and when found, shall be accurate, clear, and, so far as it goes, sufficient. Thus a Cyclop&aelig;dia is a condensed library ; which omits, of course, a whole world of truth and beauty that lies in &#39;the works of original authors, yet guides us to them, in some measure gives us their results, or at least announces them : it contains also much that is not to be found elsewhere. . Theie might be worse desultory reading than in this big book ; but its true use is to promptly supplement or animate our study of this or that subject, which we are otherwise steadily pursuing; to make our knowledge sure, precise ; a thing of great importance. Therefore, when in doubt, look !,To take the biographical volumes for instance. What interest to those who are studying mathematics or drawing, to look up the biography of Euclid or Titian; to our students of Latin to find a life of C&aelig;sar or Horace or Cicero ready at hand, with some reasonable criticism of then- works as a whole ; to our lovers of music, to read what follows under the names of Beethoven, Mozart, Rossini ! Again, students of the physical sciences will often here find a helper at hand to solve some pressing doubt : often also they are discouraged from attacking scientific books (even if ac&not; cessible) by their bulk and complexity ; in this Cyclop&aelig;dia they will find numerous articles contributed by distinguished professors, short and read&not; able, yet thoroughly trustworthy, which may send them instructed and refreshed on their way.* Or again, to a working man nothing can be more legitimate than to inquire seriously into the really great questions,* Dr. Nichol, one of the best judges, said of the &lsquo;Penny Cyclop&aelig;dia,&rsquo; &ldquo;The cheapest and least pretending, it is really the most philosophical of our scientific dictionaries.
which are trying the time, and which he reads about in the public prints ; especially those which concern his own order, for there his own experience will avail him. At the present moment there is Slavery. The question of slavery really embraces the whole history of the industrial classes, and a consideration of the rightful relations between master and man, here in England as elsewhere : upon it now hangs the destiny of one of the greatest nations, and for it the largest section of our manufacturing popu&not; lation is now suffering, for their grist has been the produce of slave-labour : &mdash;to them the slaves were out of sight, invisible, but what of that ? Our friends in the North would do well to look under the titles of Cotton and Slavery in this Cyclop&aelig;dia ; they cannot but find much there to interest them. Again, to every thoughtful man the history of his own occupation and its processes presents peculiar interest : a banker&rsquo;s clerk may rightly wish to learn something of the history of banking ; a wool-spinner to learn where the wool comes from, and how his beautiful machinery has been produced ; an engineer to read of the labours of Watt and Stephenson, and so on. All such matters are very conveniently studied in a Cyclo&not; p&aelig;dia. Again, for we must not pass over the two noble geographical volumes ; our home, the city or town in which we live, the country round about, the places we visit in our holidays, these we cannot know too much of, and here again the Cyclop&aelig;dia will be our friend.,These slender indications must suffice. It will be observed that a Cyclop&aelig;dia does not dispense with ordinary text-books, and ordinary steady work, still less with poetry and art, and all that supreme class of human utterances which speak directly to the heart of man; but it has a use of its own for every class of students. As such it well deserves a place in a college library.,This notice should not pass without a grateful tribute to Mr. Charles Knight himself. He is not the publisher, but the &ldquo; conductor &rdquo; of this Cyclop&aelig;dia, the publishing part being undertaken by the firm of Bradbury and Evans : they also deserve our thanks, for they take upon themselves a^ heavy money-risk which can only be rewarded in many years. Mr. Knight&rsquo;s publishing days seem now over. He began his career at a time when books were printed for the rich few : he was the first of British publishers who dedicated themselves to the people. He has since been followed by many ; new readers have produced new publishers, and these again new readers ; and so the good work goes on. But Mr. Knight has the credit of leading the way ; he was the first man ; he threw his bread upon the waters. &#39;Hie object of his life has been to bring to the numerous humbler classes sterling English literature and solid information on national and everyday subjects. We cannot remember the first appearance of the &lsquo; ^ епп У Magazine,&rsquo; but it still gives pleasure in many a cottage, and in its day it wrought great things. Since then, Mr. Knight (whose own name is modestly omitted from the Biographies) has brought out good volume upon volume, good series upon series ; himself an author of considerable note, the writer of a history of England and many other books ; and, what is an especial claim of honour, he has done more, as editor and publisher, than any other Englishman for the name and fame and large use of Shakspere.,Cue day his own name will appear in this book, and all his labours be ( u y chronicled ; and he will then show well worthy of comparison with
the illustrious family of the Etiennes, more commonly known by the Latin name of Stephens, the celebrated printers of the sixteenth century, whose lives I have been reading (for the first time) among the Biographies. They dwelt in Paris and Geneva, patronized, in the ancient worthy sense, by princes and wealthy merchants, by Prancis I. and Henry III. of Prance, by the State Council of Geneva, by the munificent Puggers of Augsburg ; and, on the other hand, persecuted and hindered (not very seriously how&not; ever) by Catholic prelates. There they produced grand &ldquo; Hictionariums &rdquo; of Greek and Latin, editions of Greek and Latin authors, editions of the Bible, and theological works ; writing and printing for the scholars of Europe. What could be done for letters in those days they did, and ex&not; cellently well. The same noble enterprise and unwearied industry has marked the career of our English printer in the nineteenth century, in his labours to give to the people of England English secular literature. And here Mr. Knight stands as the representative of the latest&mdash;may we also say, in promise the highest P&mdash;effort of the English printing-press. What a contrast, what a progress between the sacred missal, written by one hand, and tenderly illuminated for the delight of a few high-born eyes, and these stout volumes of secular lore, printed and stereotyped for the service of the million ! Something may be lost, but how great the gain ! Worthy of a &ldquo; Hymn of Praise,&rdquo; such as Mendelssohn actually wrote in honour of Gutemberg, the first printer&rsquo;s anniversary day.,At the same time, this is true and most true,&mdash;that life is a thousand&not; fold more than books ; and especially that no man can live upon a Cyclo&not; paedia. And the service which positive knowledge has to render is but be&not; gun. It has yet to make itself felt as a disciplined orderly whole ; to deal with far higher subjects (a real political economy, for instance) ; and to do what no Cyclopaedia can do&mdash;fashion a methodic education, and reach, in a living form, the great multitude of men. What is especially needed, is that the modern mind should be able to unite itself wholly with the past ; should be able to rise above details&mdash;in history above chronicles, in science above specialities, in life above professional subtleties ; not despising or neglecting these, but subordinating them ; should comprehend the rela&not; tions of the great provinces of knowledge to each other, their office to the individual mind and the social life of men. This seems a gigantic task, almost an impossible one, and, indeed, to the first undertaker it is a work of the first order of magnitude ; but the thing once done or truly conceived, practical success is ultimately certain, and every step gained will wonderfully simplify and illuminate all our conceptions. A right education will, then, aim at communicating this ascertained order as the basis of all knowledge. But for this purpose a Cyclopaedia is not the instrument. To the philosopher it is but a quarry of materials, to us it is and must remain only a discoursing dictionary. Such thoughts are naturally suggested by the spectacle of this vast accumulation of know&not; ledge ; and the question which will be asked, Whither is it all tending P And if, with such great issues before us, involving inevitable large changes of opinion and practice, we cannot but look with anxiety to the future, sin&not; cerity tells us we must on, and is full of noble hope withal. In the early times of maritime discovery, there was an African cape, called Bojador, or the &ldquo; Outstretcher,&rdquo; which the navigator dared not pass ; and rumour said
that those who went beyond would become black men. The cape was passed, and the outward ligure of the world made known ; but the bold Portuguese did not become black !,To go back to Francis I. and the Stephens. Francis, whose face you may read in Holbein&rsquo;s portrait of him in Flampton Court, had faults enough, and an intriguing, warring life with the Emperor Charles V. and even our Henry YIII. ; but he had a genuine love of having gifted men of peace working about him. -&iquest;Thus Andrea del Sarto, &ldquo;the faultless painter &rdquo; (of Browning&rsquo;s admirable poem), the knave also for once, who ran away to Florence with the King&rsquo;s money, describes his former joy&mdash;,&ldquo; In that humane great monarch&rsquo;s golden look,&mdash;,One finger in his heard or twisted curl,Over his mouth&rsquo;s good mark that made the smile,,One arm about my shoulder, round my neck,,The jingle of his gold chain in my ear,,I painting proudly with his breath on me.&rdquo;,And so it is told how Francis would often visit Kobert Stephens in his printing-house, and might be seen silently watching him finish his proof beloie he started upon familiar talk. The English people cannot so visit Mr. Knight ; they are not kings at all ; they can but buy Mr. Knight&rsquo;s books ot lead them, which is what Mr. Knight desires. But many and many a man unknown to him bears him silent gratitude ; and, hereafter, a poor English student asking, &ldquo;How came these good books to me?&rdquo; may have loi answer, By the faithful work of many men; among the foremost, the worthy English printer, publisher, editor, and author, Charles Knight.&rdquo;&rsquo; And so we heartily congratulate him on the accomplishment of this his great work.,V, Lushington.,BIRKENHEAD &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,This is a new addition to the list of active &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. CARLYLE TO A STUDENT.,The following letter was written by Mr. Carlyle, in 1843, to an inquiring student, and was printed in 4 Chambers&rsquo; Magazine.&rsquo; There it was found years after by another student one wet day, in the inn at Beddgelert, and - lie sends it to our Magazine.,&ldquo;Dear Sir, &mdash;Some time ago your letter was delivered to me ; 1 take literally the first half-hour I have had since to write you a word of answer. It would give me true
satisfaction could any advice of mine contribute to forward you in your honourable course of self-improvement, but a long experience has taught me that advice can profit but little ; that there is a good reason why advice is so seldom followed ; this reason, namely, that it so seldom, and can almost never be, rightly given. No man knows the state of another ; it is always to some more or less imaginary man that the wisest and most honest adviser is speaking.,&ldquo; As to the books which you&mdash;whom I know so little of&mdash;should read, there is hardly auything definite that can be said. For one thing, you may be strenuously advised to keep reading. * Any good book, any book that is wiser than yourself, will teach you something&mdash;a great many things, indirectly and directly, if your mind be open to learn. This old counsel of Johnson&rsquo;s is also good, and universally applicable : * Head the book you do honestly feel a wish and curiosity to read.&rsquo; The very wish and curiosity indicates that you, then and there, are lhe person likely to get good of it. * Our wishes are pre&not; sentiments of our capabilities ;&rsquo; that is a noble saying, of deep encouragement to our wishes and efforts in regard to reading, as to other things. Among all the objects that look wonderful or beautiful to you, follow with fresh hope that one which looks wonder- fullest, beautifullest. You may gradually find by various trials (which trials see that you make honest, manful ones, not silly, short, fitful ones), what is for you the wonder- fullest, beautifullest&mdash;what is your true element and province, and be able to profit by that. True desire, the monition of nature, is much to be attended to. But here also, you are to discriminate carefully between true desire and false. The medical men tell us that we should eat what we truly have an appetite for ; but what we only falsely have an appetite for we should resolutely avoid. It is very true: and flimsy desultory readers, who fly from foolish book to foolish book, and get good of none, and mischief of all&mdash; are not those as foolish, unhealthy eaters, who mistake their superficial false desire after spiceries and confectioneries for their real appetite, of which even they are not destitute, though it lies far deeper, far quieter, after solid nutritive food ? With these illustrations I will recommend Johnson&rsquo;s advice to you.,&ldquo; Another thing, and only one other I will say. All books are properly the record of the history of past men&mdash;what thoughts past men had iq them, what actions past men did : the summary of all books whatsoever lies there. It is on this ground that the class of books specifically named History can be safely recommended as the basis of all study of books. Past history, and especially the past history of one&rsquo;s own native country, everybody may be advised to begin with that. Let him study that faithfully ; innumerable inquiries will branch out from it ; he has a broad beaten highway, from \Vhich all the country is more or less visible ; there travelling, let him choose where he will dwell. Neither let mistakes and wrong directions&mdash;of which every man in his studies and elsewhere, falls into many&mdash;discourage yon. There is precious instruction to be got by finding we are wrong. Let a man try faithfully, manfully to be right, he will grow daily more-and more right. It is at bottom the condition on which all men have to cultivate themselves. Our very walking is an incessant falling and catching of ourselves before we come actually to the pavement ! It is emblematic of all things a man does.,&ldquo; In conclusion, I will remind you, it is not books alone, or by books chiefly, that a man becomes in all points a man. Study to do faithfully whatsoever thing in your actual situation, thfre and now, you find either expressly or tacitly laid to your charge ; that s your post : stand in it like a true soldier. Silently devour the many chagrins of it, as all human situations have many ; and see you aim not to quit it without being all that it at least required of you. A man perfects himself by work much more than by reading. They are a growing kind of men that can wisely combine the two things&mdash; wisely, valiantly, can do what is laid to their hand in their present sphere, and prepare themselves for doing other wider things, if such lie before them.,&ldquo; With many good wishes and encouragements,,&ldquo; I remain, yours sincerely,,&ldquo;Thomas Carlyle.&rdquo;,&ldquo; Chelsea, 13 th March, 1843.
TENNYSON&rsquo;S POEMS IN COUNTRY COTTAGES.,In a pamphlet sent to the Editor, called &lsquo;The Post-Revival&rsquo; (Bell and Daldy), occurs the following pleasant experience of reading poetry in the cottages of working men :&mdash; &ldquo; I determined to use English classics and those only. I began with the May-Queen ; and that poem has proved a constant favourite among the working families. It was first read by me to a little party of four, in the cottage of a farm-labourer. There were one or two obstacles at starting. The old wife, after the first verse, interpolated a full account of some May-pole festivities in her own youth ; and her husband, a hale man of sixty, stopped me, a verse or two lower down, to wonder what was become of all the &lsquo; wold-gyems&rsquo; (old games), and to express his conviction that there was much less harm in them than in things that youngsters are up to nowadays. But this was the last interruption. When the second part of the poem began, the wife&rsquo;s knitting-needles moved slowly, and were soon laid in her lap, while the man and two grown lads roused themselves iu their seats, and listened hard up to the last line. When the end came, there was only one remark made, and that was by the old man : &lsquo; Poor thing, I&rsquo;d thought she might have lived ; but there! she died as happy as ever she&rsquo;d been of a May-pole night.&rsquo; The lads said nothing; but they have often asked since for the part &lsquo;where she lies а -dying, and the angels make music to her.&rsquo;&rdquo; The writer mentions several other poems, which he has found very successful; the &lsquo;Lord of Burleigh,&rsquo; &lsquo;Dora,&rsquo; &lsquo;Burial of Sir John Moore,&rsquo; &lsquo;Lay of Horatius.&rsquo; Altogether the pamphlet is the work of one who speaks from actual experience of our real poor, and knows the best way of helping them is to make friends with them. Its special aim is to urge persons to take advantage of the unusually excited minds of &ldquo;the poor proper&rdquo; iu consequence of the late Revivals, the mood nearest akin to intellectual interest of which they are capable&mdash;hence &lsquo; Post-Revival.&rsquo;,A NOTEWORTHY BOOK OF POEMS.*,My dear Editor,&mdash;You have kindly asked me to find some suitable words for intro&not; ducing the readers of our Magazine to a book which, in my belief, is more precious to the individual man than any since &lsquo;In Memoriam. But, even at your request, it is hard to do so. Fresh from a work of high imaginative stress and influence, one has little power or wish to precipitate (so to speak) that vague but glowing sense of benefit re&not; ceived, which seems to exalt the whole nature of him who can hold it in solution. And I think, too, that Poetry, like other spiritual matters, is little helped by even the sin&not; ceres! proselytizing, and is degraded by all but the tendei est, nay the subtlest, ciiticism. If a man finds Shakspeare slow, or writes about Tennysou as the &lsquo; Times &rsquo; has written about him, tant pis your lui; he is beyond the reach of review or panegyric. But let him, or any one, once rise to the height of his best moments and keep there, and you have no need to direct him to such a book as the &lsquo; Golden Treasury.&rsquo;,At least, however, I can state in brief what this &lsquo; Golden Treasury&rsquo; professes to be and is, and why it seems to me to be the best anthology of and iu our language.,It professes to be a collection of our &ldquo; best songs and lyrical poems.&rdquo; Every one sees dimly, in his own reading and recollections, how various and abundant are the songs and lyrics which have been poured out among us for the last three centuries, and especially during the last eighty years. They are of all kinds and degrees of excellence : they affect us in almost every conceivable way. And they crop out so richly in the works of our most familiar poets, that a man may well suspect the existence, too, of hidden trea&not; sures elsewhere, which he ought to know, and does not. Now, to read, mark, and digest all these, the less-known as well as the better ; to judge, finally, which of so much beauty is the best; and, by judicious arrangement, and comments apt though brief, to make of that which is chosen a self-registering barometer (so to speak) both of na&not; tional taste and power, and of each individual mood, from stormy to set fair : would not,* &lsquo;The Golden Treasury of English Songs and Lyrics.&rsquo; Collected by F. W. Paigrave. Macmillan and Co. 4s. 6d.
that he a noble task, and one for which no single man is sufficient ? For, remember, though there have been collectors and selectors enow&mdash;and very capable ones, too,&mdash; they have all, so far at least as I know, either compiled for the benefit of some one class of readers, or wandered vaguely over the whole domain of British poetry, or, like Bishop Percy, selected some province which was not strictly lyrical. I said, no single man is sufficient for the work we are speaking of. The field is too vast&mdash;he may miss something, if ever so comprehensive ; the range is too wide&mdash;his power of comparison may fail, if ever so judicious. And then there is the great bugbear, Taste. Who is to dictate to us all what is best ? Tastes differ widely in contemporary minds, much more widely through the years of ten generations ; and let a man be ever so pure and catholic in this respect, he is either under the influence of his own age, or, if he be himself a great poet, has created that influence. So we must have fer our task a consensus of poetic minds ; each favouring most some one portion of that period which they all traverse too-ether; each taking, it may be, a different view of the comparative value of popular fame^and of the &ldquo; audience.fit though few each representing, but in different proportions, those great constituents Strength, Beauty, Wisdom ; and all harmonized by some one of them (a poet he should be, if possible) who has skill to arrange and learning to explain aud comment, and, above all, firmness and sense to strike the balance true between past and present, and between fleeting popularity and permanent worth. Lastly, if this work were to be done in an age whose poetry is emphatically lyrical, and done, too, with the sanction and help of the chief poet of that age ; then we might expect to see something like a trustworthy collection of the best songs and lyrical poems in the language. What a number of requisitions ! some one may say - what a task it must be to demand such an unprecedented combination of talent ! Marry, and so it is : but if I make requisitions it is only to show that they are already complied with; for, de te fabula narratur. De te, simple little Book, whose very exterior is all refinement, and for whom a place for ever is prepared in my handiest shelf alongside of &lsquo; In Memoriam&rsquo; and the &lsquo; Idylls &rsquo;,Briefly I do for my part think that Mr. Paigrave and his coadjutors have done this great work of selection and comment as wisely and skilfully as the men of any given age can do it ; for none can forecast the judgment of their future. As to selection, there are only two questions to ask Have all the best lyrical poems been admitted? and Have any been let in which are not of the best? This last question is easy to answer. None the other is more difficult. There are several poems which one misses much, and notab&iacute;y the Ancient Manner; but even while regretting their absence, one cannot help seeing that they are only absent because the selectors have rigidly followed out the judicious canons laid down by them for their own guidance, and announced in the editor&rsquo;s preface Moreover there are some poets&mdash;Burns above all others, perhaps&mdash;to whom no selection can possibly do justice.,I ought to mention that the volume is divided into four books, corresponding to the four periods presided over by the names of Shakspere, Milton, Gray (Pope was not much given to lyrics), and Wordsworth. By this means the reader is guided and in&not; deed, forced to compare and contrast the different periods with each other and with our own; to note the changes of style and language, and still more of taste and influence,Z m h &#39;ľ- e hun &icirc; ed ,7Tl &deg;&iexcl; S0&uuml; s ; and t0 observe also throughout the one&not;,ness of the true poetic spirit, the linked sweetness that perfumes the whole.,And now a word as to the comments, which 1 believe are chiefly or wholly Mr Pal grave s own. _ At the end of the volume you will find a summary of the characteristics of each Book , in other words, of the poetry of each period ; and a series of short notes either explaining historical allusions aud difficulties of expression, or (and this is the point) giving succinctly the editor&rsquo;s opinion as to the merits of such and such a poem and its author, absolutely, or as compared with some other author and poem P,^ow, I know that some who not only seem to be pillars, but are so, object to certain of these editorial dicta as being pedantic, dogmatic, superfluous; and I, toQ J have to com plain of Mr. Paigrave for telling us gravely that the phrase &ldquo; Nature&rsquo;s patient sleepless,jľ&ldquo;l BnTfW - S - y /h S S T et &rsquo; &quot;f 1,8 &rsquo; &deg; f aU thi &quot;g s in the &trade;&amp;gt;rld, to the Wandering Jew But theie is in these notes and summaries such a stately purity of lan-ma-e such,tenderness of loving appreciation ; there is so much delicate and subtle criticism Ł much insight into the very heart of the matter, and so much learning well applied that I for one feel too happy in these things to mind being talked to now and then by my cicerone somewhat in Cambyses vein. One hates, indeed, to be told what and when to applaud!
I know a lovely landscape, for instance, whose effect is wholly spoilt because you have to look at it through a doorway in the path, put up just to hide the view until the guide shall choose to admit it ; and I well remember that Sir Bulwer Lytton could not trans&not; late that poem of Schiller&rsquo;s which contains the celebrated It, without adding in a foot&not; note that this It was universally admired. But it is not thus with the notes in the &lsquo; Golden Treasury.&rsquo; Even when you have made up your mind about the beauty of a poem, it is interesting to know why others, and especially keen and competent observers, admire it. And I am sure that for men who have not studied Poetry, but only felt it, and who have had little opportunity of comparing the poets of different nations and dif&not; ferent times, these comments of Mr. Palgrave&rsquo;s will add immensely to the value of a book which in itself is of the highest ; will open their eyes, and suggest opinions if they do not form them.,But I have rambled on long enough, having neither time nor skill to dwell much on this subject now ; and in rambling, I have almost forgotten, my dear Editor, that this letter was addressed to you, or was a letter at all. I will conclude it with an extract from Mr. Palgrave&rsquo;s notes, which is well worth thinking over, and which gives us one reason of many why the contents of this &lsquo; Golden Treasury&rsquo; are fit to be a joy for ever :&mdash;,&ldquo; Great excellence in human art as in human character has from the beginning of things been even more uniform than mediocrity, by virtue of the closeness of its approach to Nature ; and so far as the standard of excellence kept in view has been attained in this volume, a comparative absence of extreme or temporary phases in style, a similarity of tone and manner, will be found throughout&mdash;something neither modern nor ancient, but true in all ages, axrd, like the works of Creation, perfect as on the first day.&rdquo;&mdash;Always yours, A. J. M.,CORRESPONDENCE.,SOCKATES ON THE EMPLOYMENT OF WOMEN.,Some interesting chapters on this world-old problem are to be found in Xenophon&rsquo;s &lsquo; Economicus,&rsquo; chapters vii-x. Socrates, determined to discover what constituted &ldquo; a good man and true,&rdquo; or to use the Greek untranslatable expression, &kappa;&alpha;&lambda;ό&sigmaf; &kappa;ά -yaQhs, (a man &ldquo; beautiful and good,&rdquo;) seeks out an excellent farmer, called Ischomachus, who has this reputation ; and they have a long conversation together in the portico of the temple of Zeus, the god of Freedom. Ischomachus tells him of his well-ordered home and lands, and how, under his guidance, his wife, like a true woman, minds the home. He touches upon the primary necessities of life, the primary gifts and endowments of both sexes. &ldquo; The human race does not live like cattle, in the open air ; they have need of houses. They have to till, and sow, and reap ; to gather necessaries into houses and there take care of them. Both sorts of employment, those without-door and those within, require labour and care, and the God has plainly adapted the nature of the woman for works and duties withiu-doors, and that of the man for works and duties without-doors. To the man he has therefore given the greater strength, endurance, courage ; to the woman a more tender love for children, and prudent anxious mind that shall guard and keep well ; to both equal powers of memory and attention, to both the power of temperance, to both the necessity of mutual assistance. Therefore it is that divine arrangements and human custom agree that it is more becoming for the woman to stay within-doors than to roam abroad, but otherwise of men. The wife is to be the queen-bee, remaining within the hive. She is to superintend those who work within the house, to take charge of all things brought in, to dispense them as required, to keep accounts, to see to the clothing and the provisions, to tend the sick, and especially to educate the servants in their work.&rdquo;,All this seems simple enough, but there lies its virtue. It seems at first to offer no assistance to us with our thronged complex life, our high notions of intellectual improve&not; ment, and with our surplus of half a million women. But in dealing with so funda&not; mental a subject as the employment of women, it is above all necessary to go down to the foundations ; it will not do to give these a look and pass on to the surface evils, which clamour and complain ; we ranst grasp and hold on by the primary truths which
determine the normal and therefore the only sound and happy relations ; or we shall be foolish and utterly feeble empirics, endangering the safety of all, without even suc&not; ceeding in permanently helping the few. This&#39; passage, then, from the old heathen philosopher, may remind us that the office of the man is to sustain the woman, that the office of the woman is in the home. I firmly believe that we must return to this, that we are suffering because we have departed from it ; that the glitter of money won by female labour away from the home must not tempt us, nor the deplorable condition of unprotected girls and destitute widows prevail upon us to turn aside. We must say. This is the law ; it has been forgotten and broken, but it remains the law ; we must go back to it with clear steady resolution. (By no means, reader, abolishing female factory labour at a stroke !) The conditions of women must be studied and remedies for present evils devised by minds really loyal to this law. Rapid abatement of the evils can hardly be, but permanent abatement of them, and fulfilment of all higher hopes for the destiny of women, can only come by obedience to the law, that the woman is to be &ldquo; a keeper of the house.&rdquo; This iş no slavish office : to execute it well, as Socrates says, requires a portion of the divine nature ; and it no more bars the woman from a rightful share in the larger world, than the severe duties of man under the open sky and in the factory bar him from the privileges and blessings of home.,A Teacher.,NEWS.,London. The eighth year of this College commenced as usual by a General Meet ine on October 24th.,The Principal, in his address, referred to the general condition of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, and to the establishment of the &ldquo; City of Loudon College.&rdquo; &#39; With regard to the latter, to the opening meeting of which he had been invited, he said, &ldquo; Though their work is similar to our own, it is possibly better that we should work independently of each other. The more of these institutions the better. It should be a great satisfaction to us that the name College should be adopted and recognized as the inheritance the just possession, of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt;. &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; education is only good so far as it&rsquo;is human culture. The education of the rich will be a dwarfed and heartless thing, unless they understand that of the things we learn, what is common to all is the greatest of all If we have helped the recognition of this name, and of this principle, we should be thankful The working men of Sheffield were the first to make this advance. Now the Bishop of the diocese and leading men of this city have recognized the rightfulness of the claim The new College has abundance of patronage; but by patronage alone nothing lives. It must live by possessing the hearts of those who use it : by their having a conviction of its worth. To ourselves, the promoters of the City College have shown a very friendly feehng Of Colleges more directly allied to us, that at Wolverhampton continues to fiounsh. That at Manchester has been lately amalgamated with Owens College the students at which are chiefly from the middle classes. We have a right to assert with dignity and vehemence, that we are a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ; but we must not be pedantic, and we need not doubt that the Manchester men are right in uniting with the middle class College. The more we believe that our principle is greater than ourselves the more confident we shall be in its diffusion and in its success.&rdquo; The rest of Mr Maurice&rsquo;s remarks were in explanation of the new features of the programme &middot; amon&eacute; other things, of the &ldquo; General Lectures.&rdquo; These would have the effect of keroin&laquo;- in closer connection with the College some of the earliest and most valued amon/ its teachers who had at present no regular classes, and would give opportunities for brina-ine&middot; great subjects bearing on the present life of the world, before the minds of our students 8 Mr Maurice proposed to begin on the 2nd November, taking as his text two recent art.de, m lhe &#39;Times.&rsquo; Mr. Hughes would follow him, perhaps! on the Amerit &Xi; then Professor Brewer and others. Mr - /umivali, and several other teachers, then gave various explanations as to their icspcctive classes, tlie subjects of study and methods of work,,Mr. Cooke, a new teacher, said, in reference to his Botany Class, &ldquo; I was the first to
pass a Government examination to teach botany to working men. This season is not a bad one to begin the study. I once had in the winter a class of gardeners, men as ignorant, by the way, of botany, as any in the world, for they know the Latin names of plants and nothing more. The winter is, in fact, the best time to begin, though not to continue the subject. Before examining numbers of plants, you must know a little of vegetable physiology and the morphology of plants. I have devoted as many as forty evenings to this introduction. I disagree so far with the German botanists, that I think physiological botany more important than what we call &lsquo; hay-making,&rsquo; or collect&not; ing dried specimens. We shall use the microscope, and next spring go out together in the fields.&rdquo;,G. Lushington said that the object of his class was to bring within the reach of working men students, some of the good that was to be got from Latin and Greek authors, without a knowledge of the languages. After thinking much about it, he had determined to devote the term to one great work of a great man. He had chosen the &lsquo; Iliad,&rsquo; and chosen it because Homer was the most thoroughly and simply human of all the poets. He loved fighting men better than working men ; but he loved all forms of active life. He loved to describe men exercising themselves, a ploughman at his plough, a smith at his forge, a woman at the loom. He loved, in fact, men and their works, and if he lived now, would not be far away from the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, and they could not fail to find him interesting.,Mr. Maurice said that he must add to his former remarks, a warm acknowledgment on behalf of the College, of the services of the &ldquo; Finance Committee,&rdquo; whose excellent management had very nearly made the College self-supporting, and was the best proof that the College did not, as the common phrase went, educate men &ldquo;above their work.&rdquo;,Mr. Rawlings acknowledged the Chairman&rsquo;s thanks, and mentioned that for the year ending last June, the legitimate income of the College, from fees, etc., had been equal to the expenditure, leaving the donations and the rent of the adjoining house to be carried towards the liquidation of the mortgage debt.,Mr. Litchfield said that the Singing Class prospered, but would do better if he could get some one to help him in that department, so as to keep a third class always going. As to the certificates, he said that the Geometry Examination had shown the wisdom of making a subordinate distinction of &ldquo; passed with credit.&rdquo; This meant, not incompe&not; tence, but the actual achievement of an amount of work less than that required for the certificate. Those who had, in this instance &ldquo; passed with credit,&rdquo; had done their work in as good style as the &ldquo; certificated&rdquo; students, but had not done enough. They were quite certain to get certificates if they read on. As to Mr. Fletcher, the gainer of the Algebra certificate, he could not help saying that since the opening of the College, no student that he knew had more distinguished himself in mathematics, so far as he had read ; he had a teacher&rsquo;s assistance through only half of his subject, and had done the first of his two papers so thoroughly, that the examiners thought it needless to trouble him with a second.,Mr. Roebuck, after giving a satisfactory account of the Adult School, addressed a few words more particularly to the new students. &ldquo;To-night,&rdquo; he said, &ldquo; closes an impor&not; tant epoch in the career of the College. We have served our seven years&rsquo; apprenticeship. We who are or have been working men, recollect that we thought being &lsquo; out of our time &rsquo; a somewhat tremendous affair. And the College is now &lsquo; out of its time.&rsquo; Of what the seven years have produced, to-night has given us an illustration. That the College has grown, and has in it the material for more growth, is shown by its producing teachers. Of the seven certificates given to-night, four have been to members of one class, a circumstance never before known, and this class is taught by one who was himself a student. One of these certificated students again has been for some time a teacher in one class while a learner in another. This will show how teachers and students are mixed up together. This is the chief feature of the College. After twelve years&rsquo; experience of institutions of various kinds, I can say that I know none in which men get so close together. This has come to pass in a great degree from the example of our teachers. They started with the principle of mutual fellowship. They were themselves of a class better cultivated and better off than ours. We enjoyed being brought into close contact with them, and we have carried out the principle in as many ways as we could. Several things have helped it. We knock each other about for one thing ; we have boxed together, played cricket together, had tea together, and served as Volunteers
together. We consider all this a part of the College work, and we want all those who join us to understand this.,Three hundred of the new Post Office Savings-Banks have heen opened. A depositor may put iu his money any day in the week, and may draw it out at any time and place he chooses ; and his money will always be safe, so long as the Government of England stands. When the system is fully carried out, every working man will have a savings- bank at his own door, open to receive his money at all times. This is a real boon of high value to the working classes, who need, more than others, the habit of reason&not; able providence. The Report of the Registrar of Friendly Societies (Mr. Tidd Pratt) contains an interesting comparison of different nations with respect to savings-banks. Strange to say, Austria contains the greatest proportion of depositors to its population, France the least ; England is low down on the list. The greatest average amount of deposit per head is iu America (&pound;44) ; iu England the average is &pound;24. 14s. 5 d. The Report also announces the establishment of a benefit society for the employ&eacute;s on the London and North-Western Railway Company. The Company are to subscribe annually a sum equal to twenty-five per cent, of members&rsquo; contribution, iu quittance of all other liability ; and membership of the society is henceforth to he a condition of entering the Company&rsquo;s service. In the earlier part of the Report, a disgraceful fact is recorded,&mdash; that children insured in burial-clubs, even in the healthiest districts, die off faster than children in the poorest places&mdash;not murdered, only killed, by a thousand little neglects in consequence of the insurance. &ldquo; If burial-clubs were abolished, society would, in our opinion, he just so much the better.&rdquo; So says the Registrar.,NOTICES TO CORRESPONDENTS.,The article &ldquo; On the Employment of Women,&rdquo; in the September number, has elicited four communications on that subject. Space forbids the printing of more than one. &lsquo;Ricata,&rsquo; a student, advocates strongly the views urged in the article. &lsquo; &Auml; Sub&not; scriber&rsquo; endorses them with some reservations, quoting a passage from Mr. J. S. Mill iu which he reprobates the &ldquo; confining women by custom to one physical function as their means of living and source of influence.&rdquo; There is no passage in Mr. Mill&rsquo;s writings in which he pronounces on the economical results of a great extension of female employments ; but his general drift, as expressed in the passage quoted and elsewhere, (especially in the &lsquo; Essays and Discussions,&rsquo;) seems to be this, that all artificial restric&not; tions on a woman&rsquo;s choice of a pursuit are bad ; that real obstacles, if they exist, will sufficiently make themselves felt without the support of prohibitory rules, either of law or custom. This seems reasonable enough, and leaves untouched the question of the intrinsic fitness or unfitness of a workshop life for women, as compared with a home life. Mr. Mill, too, in these passages, has in view chiefly, if not entirely, the opening to women of the higher social functions, for reasons essentially similar to those urged in the article against their adoption of the lower mechanical employments. The long letter of &lsquo; M.&rsquo; on this subject shall receive a private reply.,Ricata s article on another subject, stands over for next month. THE,&reg;&oslash;rking Щшв (fo&iacute;lege |Шр^шс.,No. XXXVI. DECEMBEE 1, 1861.,A GEEEK PLAT.,I propose giving some account of a Greek Play. I feel sure that I shall have no reason to complain of the indifference of the readers of this Magazine. They have heard, again and again, of the Greek Drama ; of its glorious triumphs and its paramount influence. They know that it forms a most vital part of that great literature that has swayed so boundless an empire over all subsequent literatures, in proportion to whose cultivation the activity of the human mind has seemed to vary. They have listened to its praises many a time, and have noted that those enco&not; miums were pronounced by the lips of the highest authorities ; and they have earnestly desired to gather some knowledge of this universal wonder. They have longed to hear with their own ears the voice that has won such unbounded applause, and to see with their own eyes that sweet singer, on whose brows every age has placed its chaplet.,It will not be possible here to satisfy at all fully these most laudable desires. Long and patient study alone can satisfy them. But something may be done. At least we can glance at one play. We may catch a few notes of that immortal song. We may lay our offering on one of the altars in the temple, if we cannot bow before all the gods whose statues stand there in solemn order.,A very short preface shall be made. Then we will look at the &lsquo; (Edi- pus Tyrannus,&rsquo; one of the greatest masterpieces of antiquity left us.,We must begin by recognizing the radical distinctions existing between the English and the Greek Drama. They will force themselves on our attention presently, as we read Sophocles&rsquo; page. I shall only state here, that in Greece the drama formed part of certain great religious festivals, and was naturally greatly influenced by that connection. Plays, then] were not the amusements of the Athenians every day. They appeared only during certain great seasons, these seasons separated by a consider&not; able interval from each other. The god, whom they thus contributed to honour, was Dionysus. Before his altar, under his especial auspices, the Greek dramatists brought out their imperishable works. This fact must never be forgotten, if we wish to understand those works.,There seem to have been four Dionysiae festivals in a year, and three of them at least were enlivened by theatrical exhibitions. At such times the whole day was given up to the entertainment. The whole city flocked to the theatre, which was of enormous size and open to the sky, abd listened unweariedly to those words that we too, twenty-three centuries aftei, bsten to with so great avidity. The plays came out in sets of four, called tetralogies. Originally the three first component plays were all closely
connected together, forming the acts, so to speak, of one great drama. Afterwards, that coherence was loosened, and the plays were wholly dis&not; tinct. Prizes were given by the state, the competition for which was wonderfully active.,But on these points we may say more perhaps some other time. We want now to read a play.,The play we have mentioned&mdash;the &lsquo; King (Edipus &rsquo;&mdash;was written by Sophocles. He, both by contemporaries and by posterity, was admired as the greatest master of the dramatic art. He was born near the beginning, and died about the close, of the fifth century before the Christian &aelig;ra. Seven of his plays have been handed down to us in tolerable completeness. Of some of the others we have a few fragments. He is said to have written upwards of a hundred.,Now for the &lsquo; King (Edipus.&rsquo; In a few words (Edipus&rsquo;s history is as follows :&mdash; Laius, a king of Thebes, was warned by an oracle that, should a son ever be born to him, that son should kill him. In spite of this awful prediction, Laius became the father of a son. His queen, Jocasta, and he, remembering well those dreadful words, determined to defeat them by having their child at once put to death. They commit it to a servant, with strict injunctions to cany it to mount Cith&aelig;ron, and there leave it to the mercy of the wild-beasts and the storms. The servant&rsquo;s heart is moved with compassion, and failing in his duty, after his master&rsquo;s example, he hands over his innocent burden to the care of a herdsman of one Polybus, Polybus sees and, being childless, adopts the infant, and brings it up at Corinth as his own. And so many years roll on. One unhappy day, when that fatal infant has arrived at manhood, Polybus accidentally excites his suspicions about his parentage. The youth, made restless, sets off to Delphi to resolve his doubts. The oracle answers him strangely, that he shall slay his father, and shall marry his mother. Hor&not; rified by this reply, he vows never to return to Corinth. About that same time Laius too proposes to visit the oracle. In the highway not far from Delphi meet, ignorant of each other, Laius and (Edipus, father and son. Some altercation arises on some trifling matter ; and, unawares, (Edipus fulfils the first part of the oracle. Indifferent to his course, if only Corinth is far away, he turns his feet towards Thebes. At this time, as it hap&not; pened, a monster was afflicting that city, and a proclamation is made that whoso shall free the people from the Sphinx&rsquo;s ravages shall be raised to the throne, now vacant through Laius&rsquo;s death, and receive in marriage Laius&rsquo;s widow, (Edipus delirers Thebes, and receives the much-coveted reward. All men think him happy, and applaud his good fortune; but he is fulfilling the second part of the prophecy.,Could any story be more terrible ?,At this point Sophocles&rsquo; play begins. (Edipus is in all his glory, honoured and envied of all men. But the gods, who see not as men see, visit his kingdom with a sore pestilence. Old men and maidens, young men and children, are falling everywhere before that unseen de&not; stroyer ; and all down the city&rsquo;s streets resound groans and lamentations.,In the first scene a band of suppliants wait upon the King, and implore him to consider his people&rsquo;s dire distress. He has saved them before ; he is great and Avise ; will he not save them now ? (Edipus answers, that
he knows too well their sufferings, that his heart bleeds for them, and, that something might be done to help them, that he has sent to his brother- in-law to inquire of Phoebus the cause, and the remedy of their affliction. While they are yet speaking, some one approaches. It is Creon, the mes&not; senger, and he has brought for his answer a stern command to expel from the country the slayer of its last king. All the energy of (Edipus&rsquo;s cha&not; racter is at once aroused, and he resolves to give himself no rest, until this man of unclean hands, whosoever he is, is driven far away into exile. And so he digs his own grave. He mixes, as he fancies, for some other that terrible cup, that he is himself to drain. He is a kind-hearted, generous prince, and his citizens shall not perish, if he can save them. There are no signs of insolence here. Doubtless he is sensible of his exaltation^ but pride has not frozen his sympathies. His feet are swift to aid and bless his children.,Now is heard a choral strain. The Chorus, composed of the old men of Thebes, invoke the gods to look down on their suffering country. The heart-rending cries of the plague-stricken are ringing in their ears, and they pray high Heaven to remove this stroke away from them.,In the second scene, (Edipus has summoned before him the whole popu&not; lation (whom, in the acting of the play, the audience themselves repre&not; sented), and solemnly curses the murderer, and forbids all men to have any intercourse with him. But who is the murderer ? How shall that question be solved? Happily there is living amongst them an aged seer, by name Teiresias, to whom Ph&oelig;bus oftentimes reveals things secret. The eager King sends for this old prophet, and impetuously urges him to name the murderer&rsquo;s name. But Teiresias only shudders, and entreats (Edipus to send him back to his house, and ask him no more questions. &ldquo; Aye me!&rdquo; he says, &ldquo;how terrible a thing it is to be wise, where wisdom is ruinous !&rdquo; The King grows incensed at his resistance ; the old men too adjure him to denounce the murderer ; but he steadfastly refuses. The King&rsquo;s anger grows hotter and fiercer. He reviles the old man savagely, and even accuses him of having himself perpetrated the deed. Then at last the seer&rsquo;s spirit is roused, and he speaks out : &ldquo; Say&rsquo;st thou so ? I bid thee bide by the proclamation thou hast made, and henceforth from this day address not these old men nor me, forasmuch as &rsquo;tis thou who art the unhallowed polluter of this land !&rdquo; What an incredible tale ! (Edipus &ldquo; the man !&rdquo; The King&rsquo;s fury knows no bounds now. His keen sagacity at once how the case stands. Teiresias is the instrument of some base conspiracy. Who is his accomplice ? Once more, still more plainly, the seer proclaims him, the great (Edipus, the murderer ; and hints manifestly at that other misfortune that has befallen him. But (Edipus&rsquo;s convictions are strengthened. Who can be the lying seer&rsquo;s accomplice ? Can it be Creon ? And the King, whose judgment passion has by this time wholly warped, at once fixes on Creon as the author of the plot, and anathema&not; tizes him and his hireling prophet. Unmoved by his threats and his curses, Teiresias again pronounces his doom, and leaves the scene.,A second time swells the choral song. The Chorus is troubled greatly by the prophet&rsquo;s speech. What may this thing be ? They wonder, and await the issue with fear and trembling.,The third scene introduces Creon to us, anxious to vindicate himself,N 2
from the charge brought against him by (Edipus. The chorus suggests that the King&rsquo;s passion rather than his calm reason preferred it. Suddenly (Edipus himself appears, and inveighs against the supposed traitor in the most intemperate language. Those stern, dispassionate, unmistakable words of Teiresias have not been without their effect upon him for all his scorn and indignation. He tries to drive them from his memory by a wild reckless vehemence of passion, to drown them in a tide of fury. A terrible unquiet possesses him. Him whom a god purposes to ruin, he first bereaves of his senses. (Edipus is now rapidly nearing the brink of the precipice ; but his self-confidence is now most boisterous and overbearing. He will hear no apology. The treason is most glaring, most damnable. The punishment shall not be wanting, shall not delay. Creon can only appeal to time to dissipate the clouds that obscure his &#39;good name. He can see too clearly that (Edipus is not his own master, is estranged from himself, that there is nothing to be hoped from the sobriety of his judgment. In the midst of this wrangling between her husband and her brother, Jocasta enters : and having learnt the cause of the quarrel, and heard Creon assert on oath his innocence, intercedes with the King for him. (Edipus at last, with but an ill grace, assents, and Creon goes his way. The husband and wife, who are also son and mother, talk over together the origin of this outbreak. (Edipus alluding to Teiresias&rsquo;s denunciation, Jocasta takes the opportunity of expressing her contempt for oracles and oracle-mongers, which contempt, it seems, is founded on the fact that an oracle given to her late husband and herself, to the effect that any son bom him by her should kill him, had utterly failed of its fulfilment. What a striking position ! What an awful irony ! &ldquo; She knew not eating death.&rdquo; Jove&rsquo;s right hand is uplifted ; very speedily now the bolt will speed on its tragic errand. Even as this frivolous woman is sneering at the immortal gods, a word she utters in her petty scepticism awakens the most direful associations in her son-husband&rsquo;s breast. A great horror seizes him ; that forking road in Phocis ; the charioteer&rsquo;s insolence ; his own retaliation ; the Prince&rsquo;s interference ; his own furious rage ; and the Prince&rsquo;s silvered hairs defiled with blood and dust; all these things made a ghastly picture to his mind&rsquo;s eye. If that Prince should be Laius ? If Teiresias spoke truly ? If his curse is to fall on his own head P О maddening thoughts ! But the circumstances agree together, too, too well. Only one hope re&not; mains : there is some slight discrepancy in the number of assailants men&not; tioned by Jocasta. Is there no one living who can resolve these horrible doubts ? It appears there is still living one who was in Laius&rsquo;s train on that fatal day. Jocasta promises to send for him. But she cannot com&not; prehend (Edipus&rsquo;s fierce anxiety. She has no doubts, no care, no inquisi&not; tiveness about these matters. Why should he make himself uneasy ?,Now ensues another choral ode, in praise of a calm and dutiful ob&not; servance of the great laws whose only author is Olympus, and in reproba&not; tion of the overbearing and outrageous spirit. Then the Chorus utter the thoughts that arise in them on hearing the Queen&rsquo;s derision of Delphi. Are, then, the oracles of Ph&oelig;bus to return unto him void P Are they to be mocked, spurned, trampled underfoot ? Surely the godly shall be * minished from among&#39;men, if Apollo shall not assert his truth. Let the arod arise ; let his enemies be scattered.
The fourth scene shows us Jocasta supplicating Apollo in (Edipus&rsquo;s be&not; half. &OElig;dipus, she says, is haunted by the most cruel fears, and will not be comforted. As she is praying that his troubles may be lightened, ar&not; rives a messenger to salute &OElig;dipus Prince of Isthmia, in the stead of his reputed father, Polybus, whom death has at last overtaken. Again the unthinking Queen scoffs at the utterances of the Delphic oracle ; for had it not foretold that &OElig;dipus should kill his father, and had not tidings of his father&rsquo;s natural decease but now reached them? Triumphantly she summons her husband. He, too, now suspects the veracity of the Pythian responses, and is tor a brief moment o&iacute; a lighter heart. . This short-lived serenity is but the fickle gleam of sunshine that foreruns the dark unpity&not; ing storm. It is the smile of the bright, still, flowery valley, down whose channel a. deluge of wasting waters shall in but another minute be roaring. &OElig;dipus is thinking to give his cares to the winds ; he has escaped the shipwrecking billows, he thinks, and can now eat, drink, and be merry \ when, from some words the poor wretch utters, the messenger is led&lsquo;to explain that Polybus was not his father at all, and therefore could not have been the person referred to in the oracle. The messenger proceeds to explain how he had himself received him from one of Laius&rsquo;s servants, and had resigned him to the care and nurture of Polybus. That servanti it appears, is still alive. He is to be sent for without delay. The Kin^ is determined to sift this business thoroughly, though he may discover him&not; self to be of the very lowest extraction. He cannot rest now, till the his&not; tory of his infant years is fully investigated. Jocasta would rather that he should look no further into it. The horrible truth seems to be dawnino&middot; upon her. The ghastly phantom already haunts her, and will not be ap- peased. In the first arrives a second messenger, and relates how Jocasta, frenzied by her misfortunes, has sought a release from her shame and sorrow in death. In the last, (Edipus once more appears. He, too, has been maddened by the sense of his calamities, and has put out his eyes. So he appears once more, blind, helpless, utterly wobegone. No man ever trod the earth more wholly abased, more deeply stricken. He can only groan. Most piteous to hear his groans are. He feels himself an abhorrence to gods and men, unworthy so much as to stay in the coun&not; try, fit only to be driven away out of the presence of his subjects. Creon visits him, but not to reproach him. The fallen King implores to be at once ejected from the land of his disasters, having been allowed once more to embrace his children. Those little ones are, accordingly, led to his arms, and as he tenderly places his hands upon them and he blesses them, he prays for them a happier life than had been granted their sire.,And so they pass away from the stage, but not from our remembrance. That -piteous group will ever stand out clear and distinct before us. It is the handiwork of a master sculptor, perfect in its expression, in its dis&not; tinctness, in its reality. With that scene ends the play.,I trust my readers will carefully note the many great differences between this play and any English one. The chorus, for instance, how strange a feature is it ! How is its presence to be interpreted P This and many other questions of great interest I have no time now to discuss.,As to the language, that, too, is very remarkable. It is not designed to attract the spectator&rsquo;s notice to itself. It is designed simply to minister to the drama, not to divert attention by its own charms. The spectator is to be absorbed in the action, not charmed by pretty turns of speech. On the other hand, the choral odes are astonishingly rich and beautiful. They are arrayed in all the magnificence that the most versatile, most gorgeous, most refined language ever spoken could bestow upon them. I believe it is entirely impossible to represent them out of their native garb. They close, at the translator&rsquo;s touch, like the leaves of the sensitive-plant.,But I fear I have already usurped too much space ; and shall therefore come without delay to a conclusion, in spite of a thousand more things that clamour to be written down.,J.
