Hales.,CLOTH AND COEDS.,Polonius, quasi-old-man moralist of any day, discoursing very good second-hand philosophy in manner sententious, asserts, that &ldquo;the apparel oft proclaims the man.&rdquo; Not nearly so often as folks imagine. Sure it may be, at times, when the purse allows one to express his fancy in habits costly, but seldom here even. &lsquo; Le Eolleť leads la foule of fashionables ; it is the taste of the time, not of the individual. But what of our class, where oft the difficulty is to get habits at all, either neat or gaudy ? This however does not prevent us from expressing our fancy with respect to
other people&rsquo;s. To revive a subject broached more than once at our meetings, just a few words on the habits of our students of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, and on the manners dependent thereon. Working men? Ay, there&rsquo;s the rub. I shall not hazard a definition. When we choose, we all know pretty well what is meant by the term Working Man. Рог the nonce we will agree that A., the reader, works in corduroy, with dirty hands, for from &pound;50 to &pound;150 a year, paid weekly ; and that A., the writer, works better dressed, with clean hands, for the same wages per annum, paid quarterly. Are we not both working men, our hopes, our interests the same ? Of these two classes are nearly the whole of our students com&not; posed. Then we are working men, the only difference being one of clothes. &ldquo;Ah !&rdquo; says Corduroy, &ldquo; that is indeed all the difference.&rdquo; Our interests are perhaps the same ; they ought to be. Of your hopes I know little, except that each hopes one day to be an employer&mdash;capitalist I mean. If you talk at all upon subjects bearing upon our positions, you talk as incipient ones, ever taking the side of capital in all disputes between us&mdash;talking of us learnedly as skilled and unskilled labourers. You have used the terms so long that you have forgotten they represent men. Our hopes and interests the same ! It will be a happy day for both when you do&rsquo; know that our interests are the same. Who should be better able to counsel and instruct the poorer paid and more ignorant worker, than his better paid, better educated fellow ? Ay, fellow ; though one work with a trowel, the other with a pen. Who should better know his wants, the rock of error upon which he splits, and will split ? (I speak as a worldling.) Who should be better able to reason with him more freely, putting him more at his ease, than his better educated fellow ? Who could do so with less appearance of patronage? But how does it stand with us here? You call yourselves working men, and we come seeking instruction and fellowship&mdash;it is little of the latter we meet with. We walk into the class&not; room, filled with well-dressed students, chatty and pleasant enough among themselves, but holding themselves aloof from any one in mechanic&rsquo;s work&not; ing clothes, looking down upon him, telling him with their glances that his presence is an intrusion, answering curtly all questions he may dare put to them. Some indeed will speak to him slightly patronizingly when inside the College ; but let them meet you in the streets, they will give you the cut direct. Yet we are all working men, the only difference being clothes.,I, Cloth, know this is in part true. I know also how the ill-dressed man feels under such treatment, having for some short time in my life car&not; ried the whole of my wardrobe upon my back, with little in my pocket, save holes. I know from my experience that a man so dressed and so placed, looks out for such behaviour&mdash;understand me&mdash;he is ever ready to take offence, to feel hurt, at any little apparent slight. I know oft- times he himself is more angry with his own clothes than others are ; they offend him more than they offend others. And let me whisper in thy ear, friend Cords : Were you never angry with another man for being better dressed than yourself ?,In a body of from two to three hundred students, there must of neces&not; sity be men of nearly every disposition, from the respectably snobbish to the very rough. For my part, I divide our men into two groups&mdash;the
non-interested and the interested. The first are those who merely come for what they can get : they pay their subscription, walk into their class&not; room and out again, and beyond their own studies, take no further heed of the College or its work. The second group is composed of men, mostly old students, who have the welfare of the College at heart, men to whom it is indeed a College. They speak of it lovingly, recalling old associations, for it found many of them lone men, to whom London was a &ldquo; city and a soli&not; tude,&rdquo; aiid gave them friends ennobling them. They have worked well at its studies, and prize deeply their well earned-honours ; and now, no longer needing class-teaching themselves, still cling to the place, willing, eager, to welcome, teach, help all new comers, whether in cloth or cords. In fact, I think they have rather a weakness for the latter. I know they are always regretting they cannot get sufficient of the real working wan de&not; vient into the College. Certainly, friend Cords, it was none of these men who treated you in the manner complained of. I fear, indeed, you judged harshly. You came in, and finding us better dressed than you expected, jumped to a foregone conclusion. This is often the way. Who is to blame? Many, very many, poorly dressed mechanics have not the moral courage to sit contentedly in a room with better clothed men. Let them fairly ask themselves Why?&mdash;being heedful not to throw quite all the blame on the shoulders of others.,Come amongst us, and see if you do not find many of the class No. 2. My experience for it, you &iacute;v&uuml;l find the great majority are. Perchance you may stumble against a member of group No. 1, who may stare at you with the nose slightly elevated&mdash;the shame is Ins&mdash;but do not judge hastily, I have been a member of the College since it was first founded, and I have never, personally, heard more than one man accuse us of snob&not; bish behaviour, and his College experience dated back but a few days. We must not judge rashly. A fellow is not necessarily a snob although the apparent end and aim of his existence is to show a differently tinted pair of gloves each time he comes to class ; nor does it follow that he is a boor, though manifestly he does not believe in shirt-collars and con&not; siders soap rather as a luxury than a necessary. A man&rsquo;s dress, his man&not; ner of wearing it, may give you some clue to his character, but the philo&not; sophy of these things is not yet written, and we must not fancy ourselves incipient Lavaters.,Looking not merely within the College w r alls, but far beyond them, con&not; sidering not only the immediate interests of its students, but also the in&not; terests its founders have ever had at heart, and viewing the subject not simply as a student, mindful of his &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;college&lt;/span&gt;, but also as a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;man&lt;/span&gt;, thinking of his order, I do indeed sincerely wish we could induce a grea ter number of the true artisan workers to join us. W orking men are not enough interested with themselves&mdash;they do not understand each other&mdash;there is but little fraternity between the higher and lower grades of workers. To repeat&mdash;ho\v often do you see the clerk and the labourer discussing their common interests ? Does either the one or the other believe their interests are common ? I want them to see that not only their interests, but the interests of higher orders (as things now are) in the social scale are identical. I believe they will only learn this by mingling together, and I know not any place where they have better chances of so doing than at
our College. I say it advisedly. I never knew any mixed body of men &mdash;in fact, any body of men&mdash;where greater social feeling prevailed, and I have an experience of twelve years among institutions of all kinds, from Young Men&rsquo;s Christian Associations to Secular Societies. Still there is something to be done, and an equal willingness to do it. If Mr. Editor deems my College-taught English good enough for our Magazine, I hope in some future pages to show how this good-fellowship has been wrought out, and how, I think, it may best be further developed. For the present I would simply advise each student to look to it for himself.,In conclusion, one or two words to my fellow clean-handed workers. Let me remind you of one of the text-hand copies of our school-boy days : &ldquo; True politeness has its seat in the heart.&rdquo; There is one precept, old as the hills, worth infinitely more than all the formal rules of etiquette : &ldquo; Do unto another as you would be done by.&rdquo; Recollect, in enrolling your name as a student of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, you accept the term &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Man&lt;/span&gt;. Guard against the sin of respectability. The truly polite man is he who places at his ease all approaching him. Do be careful of giving the shadow of offence to any poorly dressed students ; believe me, many of them are unwittingly on the qui-vive for offerees, and will assuredly clutch at the shadow ; and the ghost of an offence is worse than a real one. Recollect that &ldquo; Evil is wrought by want of thought as well as by want of heart.&rdquo; Finally, each of us must ever bear in mind, that the true end of all educa&not; tion is to teach us our duty to our neighbours and ourselves.,Ricata.,SALEORD &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.&mdash;ANNUAL REPORT.,ABSTRACT READ BY THE SECRETARY AT THE GENERAL MEETING ON OCTOBER 38th, 1861.,On behalf of the Council of this College, I have the honour and very sincere pleasure of congratulating you upon the progress and increasing usefulness of this institution. It is but three and a half years ago since several warm friends of education conceived the idea of establishing an in&not; stitution to be exclusively devoted to the education of the working classes of the district. On conferring with the working men, their response was so prompt and decided, that the promoters were enabled rapidly to de&not; velop 6 their views ; generous and highly competent men joined the move&not; ment as unpaid teachers, and the Salford &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, from an idea, became an established fact. Shortly afterwards, this building was presented to the members entirely free from rent-charge, and with a con&not; stitution that placed the chief control in the hands of its members, thereby securing effectually its adaptation to the sympathies and wants of the class for whose benefit it was especially intended. The institution thus inau- guiated began its active career on the 34th of June, 1858, possessing at once a moderate number of students and a highly efficient staff of honorary teachers. Under such auspices, it is not unnatural to suppose that its promoters would be sanguine as to its success and ultimate results, not&not; withstanding that doubts to the contrary were whispered by many who had not learnt the amount of confidence that might be reposed in the
faith, honour, and integrity of the working men to whom the institution was virtually assigned. Without reference to either, I propose simply to lay before you a few brief statistics, from which each person may readily draw their own conclusions. The college has existed during eleven active terms, and in the first term we had 190 students, but, after some fluctua&not; tion, we have now, in the eleventh term, 324 students. The trades and occupations of the students are as follow :&mdash;Mercantile clerks, 63 ; ware&not; housemen, 56; printers, 20; decorative artists, 7 ; shopmen, 11 ; various handicraftsmen, 51 ; labourers, mill hands, and sundry miscellaneous occu&not; pations, 116. There are three female classes, conducted by Mrs. Pochin and Mrs. John Plant, which have been well attended. In addition to the regular classes, the institution possesses a flourishing and ably-conducted essay and discussion society, at which original essays of considerable lite&not; rary ability are from time to time read, and discussions thereon, often of great interest, ensue. (Here follows a list of the papers read at the meetings of the Society.) The Council have also granted the use of the hall on the Saturday evening when not otherwise required, to a number of the members who expressed a strong desire to be drilled and trained, so as to be able to enter creditably at any moment the yolunteer force,&mdash;so that, although not having the timo at their disposal to join the regular corps, they might, under the direction of a competent drill-sergeant, obtain a sufficient amount of in&not; struction and practice within their own college, as would enable them, if their country should need their services, to answer the call made upon them promptly and efficiently, not as raw and useless recruits, but as well- trained soldiers. It was well said at our first social meeting by our much respected president, that this college was intended to supply a want amongst the working classes of a means to carry out the education begun at school, and which is the only basis on which the superstructure of after education has to be reared. Now this idea is essentially the germ from which this institution has sprung, and its prosperity will mainly depend on its adherence to that idea. Yet it must be ever remembered that the construction of this college enables it to adapt itself to the peculiar wants of its members. It was therefore found at a very early period in the exis&not; tence of the college, that men became members who had received no pre&not; vious education, and others whose early education had been so far forgotten by disuse, that elementary classes were obliged to be formed. Therefore in the list of classes will be found several that do not legitimately come within the scope of an institution designated as a college. But it is to the usefulness of the institution that the Council look rather than its name. In April last 24 of the college students volunteered to enter for the Society of Arts examination. The Council secured the co-operation of the Eev. Wil&not; liam Birley, who, as chairman of the local board, most kindly and cour&not; teously gave them the advantage of his great practical experience and abi&not; lity. As the results of this examination, I have the pleasure of recording that to James Brown was awarded a certificate of the first class foi inoi- p-anic chemistry, also another to Walter Dyson, and to James Smethurst a certificate of the third class ; Thomas M&lsquo;Loughlin, a first-class certificate for bookkeeping, Edward Roberts a third-class certificate foi same ; and James M&lsquo;Loughlin a third-class certificate for arithmetic. In May last the members of the chemistry class entered themselves fearlessly for the general
examination under the auspices of the Science and Art Department of the Council of Education ; the results being that the following students, James Smethurst, Thomas Brown, Walter Dyson, Charles Millward, James Atkin White, and Ezekiel Williamson, passed the examination satisfactorily, and Thomas Brown obtained a prize of the second class. At the end of the last term the usual annual College examination took place, and the classes were subjected to a severe competitive examination. The examining board decided to grant a certificate to the pupil who should most distinguish himself in each class, and to honourably mention the second in order of merit. In some classes the competition was very severe, and nice judg&not; ment was required to determine who should be selected for the honours. It is a rule that no member can receive a certificate more than once in the same subject ; therefore, those who obtained certificates last year do not obtain them this, and they are transferred to the next in order of merit. In conclusion, it gives me great pleasure to re- port that m all the classes the very best feeling exists between the students and their teachers; and so long as this is the case, the college is sure to prosper, and without it must soon dedim. On the 29th of September last, after talcing all liabilities into account, and without any reserve, there was a clear cash balance in hand of &pound;30.,CORRESPONDENCE.,A SUGGESTION.,Sir, &mdash;The &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; scattered through the country do not, as it seems to me, know enough of each other. The Magazine does something towards keeping up a certain degree of intercourse between them. It might do more if our country friends would make more use of its pages, which belong to them as much as to us. But per&not; sonal knowledge of each other would do most of all. We have heard at various times from Mr. Hughes, Mr. Maurice, Mr. Furnivall, and Mr. Litchfield, pleasant accounts of their reception, when paying visits to the Country Colleges ; and we have had the pleasure of welcoming, in return, some of the most active Teachers from other places. Mr. Solly, Mr. Plant, and Mr. MacMillan have had opportunities of telling us in the Coffee Room, and at General Meetings, about the Colleges at Lancaster, Salford, and Cambridge, Well, the purport of this note is, to suggest that we ought to have as much as possible of this kind of intercourse, and that, with this end, we ought to make the most of the opportunity afforded by the Great Exhibition of next spring. Whatever may be the good of these decennial &ldquo; World&rsquo;s fairs &rdquo; (and I cannot myself see that they are worth all the talk, trouble, and money which they cost), there can be no doubt that they can be made incidentally useful in many agreeable ways. We shall have no end of country people rushing up to town to see their friends and the Exhibition all at once. Travelling abroad the other day I observed that the affair increased in importance the farther one got from home. If you talked to a man about England, and happened to ask the question, &ldquo;Have you ever been there?&rdquo; the answer was as often as not, &ldquo;No, I&rsquo;m putting it off till the &lsquo; Exposition &rsquo; of &rsquo;62.&rdquo; Great numbers of working men and a great proportion of all the well-to-do people, who can afford it, will doubtless be paying London a visit between the months of April and October. Might not we therefore remind the teachers, students, and friends of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, that they might as well include us in their Metropolitan surveys. I was told the other day of a Leices&not; tershire farmer, who was met by a friend on his return from a visit to London, and asked what he had seen. &ldquo; See&rsquo;d ! well I see&rsquo;d pretty nigh all the sights.&rdquo; &ldquo; Ay, but what were the sights ?&rdquo; &ldquo; Well, I see&rsquo;d the Tower o&rsquo; London, and I see&rsquo;d West&not;,minster Abbey, and&mdash;I see&rsquo;d Ben Caunt !&rdquo; Now we have not, I am afraid, an ex-cham&not; pion among our students, though we do do a little at knocking each other about, and find as was well observed at a late meeting, that an occasional bit of &ldquo; auctioneering &rdquo; with the gloves is an excellent way of cultivating good fellowship ; but let us recom&not; mend ourffrieuds to put the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; at least as high on the list of lions as the Leicestershire man did his model of heroic bravery. We are nothing great to look at certainly ; inside and out, we wear a work-a-day sort of appearance. But such work as we do, we shall be glad to show to those who are doing the like in other places, and talk about our respective successes and failures. Not much good perhaps would be done by having a formal &ldquo; Conference.&rdquo; We should feel cramped and stilted if we had to move solemn resolutions, and discuss amendments in full conclave, but would it not be useful to announce that we propose to devote an occasional evening in the course of the summer to a sort of &ldquo; Conversazione &rdquo; ( Anglico &ldquo; talkee-talkee &rdquo;) for the mutual benefit of ourselves and country friends: Some might then choose such a time for their Loudon trip as would bring them in contact with College people from other parts, whom they would not otherwise see. And besides this we might remind them that on every Wednesday evening we have a social gathering for tea, chat, and general fraternization, at which all strangers are welcome, and especially &ldquo; unknown friends &rdquo; from a distance. Whether we could turn the opportunity to account in a more serious way by discussing matters bearing on the work in which we are engaged, is a point which would be worth alitile consideration. Perhaps, at the next General Meeting, we may hear what the Council and Students think of this. Whatever differences exist between the various
Colleges as to constitution and plans of study, we should at least strive hard after a general unity of purpose, that the common name we have assumed may be made as significant and distinctive as possible, and that we may understand each other on the great principles which underlie all our seeming divergences,,A Teacheb.,&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&#39;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, London , November, 1861.,NEWS.,Cambridge. &mdash;The seventh anniversary of the inauguration of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; was commemorated on Tuesday, October 22nd, by a tea-party in the College Rooms. The Principal, the Very Reverend Dr. Goodwin, occupied the chair, as he had done on every previous anniversary. A large number of the Council of Teachers were present.,The subject of the evening&rsquo;s speeches was the small and fluctuating attendance upon the classes ; reflections of this kind had been often made before, but never in so serious a way as on this occasion : grave doubts seemed to rise in the speakers&rsquo; minds .as to whether the College could continue to live or not. The Committee of Students were earnestly recommended to devise some means of enlarging the attendance, or of making the two ends meet in some other way.,As a set off to these unpleasant considerations, the attention was turned to the suc&not; cesses of those who do attend the classes. The remarks on this subject were more favourable. J J,Salford.&mdash; That this College is flourishing will be evident from the annual renort printed above The account in a local paper of the meeting at which the report was read gives further proof that a hearty and zealous spirit animates all departments of the College The Soiree and Presentation of Prizes was held in the large hall of the building on the 28th of October. The Mayor, E. Weston, Esq., presided, Ind began by asking Mr Near the Secretary, to read the Report. This done, he proceeded to present certi? Acates and prizes to the successful students, each one, as he stepped upon the platform being received with cheers. In the case of Mr. Smethurst, a middle-aged working man who began his education at, and had been connected with the College from its corn&rsquo; me&laquo;* the cheering was very vehement. Elizabeth Collins, also, amidst a perfect furore of applause, came
forward to receive her certificate. The presentations being over, the Rev. W. Birley, Inspector of Church of England Schools, was then called upon to speak, and moved a resolution of thanks to the teachers. In so doing he congratulated the Institution upon the very marked success which had attended those who possessed the confidence to enter the competitive lists for prizes oifered by the Society of Arts, and which prizes they had so nobly and successfully won. Moreover, for the teachers also he must say, that they, equally with those who held the certificates of proficiency con &middot; ferred by the Society of Arts, must participate most honourably in the distinction their pupils had achieved. Among other good results was one on which he went on to dwell, the increase of self-respect and self-control which sound mental training was sure to bring with it. In giving this to the el&aacute;ss who came to the College the teachers were doing a patriotic work, inasmuch as they were helping to make them steadier, better, and more useful citizens. Mr. David Chadwick, in seconding the motion in the capacity of ex-teacher, referring to his recollection of the earlier period of the College, said he had felt nothing more pleasant than to see the faces of those who had achieved success, ^ and to whom the prizes and certificates had been distributed. He would mention one particularly, because he recollected him from the earliest period in the existence of the College. As a perfect stranger he attended their first meeting. He felt a kind of affection for him, because he had, through good and through evil report, stuck to his work as a student. He had seen that man almost crying to think that working men of his acquaintance were so foolish as not to come and be instructed ; and then again full to overflowing with gratitude that he bad had the opportunity of obtaining that education in manhood which he had neglected in his youth. Of course they all knew that he referred to Mr. Smethurst.* He believed that, although not so conspicuous, they had many more Smethursts in the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. This would he an example to others, and proved that although a man might have passed the age of thirty or forty years, that should not deter him from educating himself. This College afforded him the opportunity, and he hoped that those who appreciated it would not keep it to themselves, but would speak within their circle of acquaintance, and try to bring others to share the benefits they enjoyed. He felt convinced that in a few years, if this was done, there would be such an increase in the number of pupils seeking instruction as to render it necessary to turn every large Sunday School in the district into a Working Men&rsquo;s Col&not; lege on the plan of gratuitous teaching so successfully carried out in this College. They had seen elaborate essays written to prove that unpaid teaching must be a failure. They had no occasion to argue the question, hut simply to appeal to the logic of facts, which proved that their instruction had been successful, and the basis of their success was, he thought, the fact that every man who came to receive instruction had a part in the management of the Institution to which he belonged. This, he believed, was the secret of their success. The &lsquo; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Magazine &rsquo; said that they looked to Salford to solve this great problem&mdash;&ldquo; Can &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;colleges&lt;/span&gt; do the amount of good expected of them, and be self-supporting?&rdquo; They heard from the Secretary&rsquo;s Report that the College did fulfil this last important condition.,The resolution was carried with acclamation.,Mr. Plant acknowledged the vote of thanks. He knew that the teachers felt that the best and only reward they could receive for their exertions were the thanks of the pupils. After their long term of lessons, there was some satisfaction to the pupils, and congratu&not; lation to themselves, when there was such a satisfactory result to their labours as the present one ; although mere prizes and certificates were not their ultimate view. No doubt many of the teachers had had harder work, and more difficulties to contend against than he had had, having pupils who came with a good amount of elementary education.,The difficulties that the teachers of the elementary classes must have, in the constant attention necessary, and in the sacrifices of time and absence from home, made it no small thing ; and these gentlemen really did deserve, just as the meeting had volunteered to give them, its warmest thanks. It must be a source of satisfaction to them to see the success which had attended their efforts. They commenced with ten classes, but now they had thirty ; they commenced with nine teachers, now they had twenty-five. Various causes had necessitated some of their old teachers leaving, so that there were but four of,* Mr. Smethurst, it appears, began his education at the College : he had been taught nothing before, and in three years has taken six certificates (Chemistry, Arithmetic, etc.).
the nine still with them. Amongst those who were gone, was the Rev. Mr. Pocklington, who had been with them from the very first, and amongst the four who remained was Mr. Charles Bury, one of the most honoured men they had in connection with the College.,Mr. John Edwards (barrister, and teacher of the History Class) welcomed the large attendance as a pleasant proof of the increased interest taken in the College. He con&not; fessed that he was rather disappointed, that out of the three hundred students attending the College so few had fallen to his share as teacher of the History Class. He did not know how it was, but many of the students were still in attendance upon the Elementary Classes. &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; were instituted with the view mainly of giving work&not; ing men an opportunity of studying those branches of learning which men hitherto had only been able to study at universities. The tendency of the present age was one so practical in its nature, that the aim of every one who attended those classes was to fit himself for rising in the world. He should be the very last person to disparage such a course of action, hut still they might have some working men to look above that, and after perfecting themselves in the elementary classes, fired with a praiseworthy zeal, enter upon those fields of investigation and knowledge which opened so many means of enjoy- ment and pleasure to every man who set his mind upon them. As for himself, he never had greater pleasure in conducting a class than the one which assembled there. He thought that history was a subject which they ought to attend to more than they did. It was not to be taught here as it was to schoolboys. Its philosophical and constitu&not; tional features were words which boys could hardly comprehend, but which nevertheless- formed the principal and most interesting features of history. History ought especially to be an Englishman&rsquo;s study, because our whole laws and constitution were historical. In the administration of our laws, and in making decisions, reference to the past was always made. They had never made a clean sweep of the constitution by the board as some nations had done, but sought rather to add to, or make improvements in, whatever already existed. It was our historical character which had held us up and sustained us so long, and had gained and kept to us our Magna Charta and other rights. This was a reason why working men should take up the study. After some remarks from M. Morian , the teacher of the French Classes, the proceedings of the meeting finished with a vote of thanks to the Chairman. In acknowledging the terms of high eulogy used by Mr. Alderman Harvey in moving the resolution, the Mayor said that Mr. Harvey had flattered him very much, but he knew that it arose from his excessive good nature, and he would say the same for the next Mayor. (Loud laughter, and an expos&not; tulation from Mr. Harvey.) He was, however, exceedingly obliged to the meeting for their kindness. &deg;,The evening wound up by a dance.,Communications for the Editor (including the lists of Classes of the Colleges) to be addressed to him at the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&#39;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, 45, Great Ormond Street W. JANUAEY 1, 1862.,NOTICE TO OUE EEADEES.,The present number closes this series of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Magazine. The circulation being below the number required to pay ex&not; penses, the Council of the London College have determined that it would be unwise to continue it. It is however proposed to replace it by a Quarterly publication of somewhat larger bulk and increased price. If this suggestion, which is now under consideration, be carried out, the first number will appear three months hence, and will be sent to all subscribers to the present magazine.,The small success which has attended the establishment of a printed medium of communication between,the Colleges, shows that the necessity for such communication is not felt to be so imperative as we supposed it to be. But whatever inferences be made from the cessation of the Maga&not; zine, it is at least pleasant to be able to say confidently that it is not an indication of any failure in the main work which it was intended to aid. The London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, not to mention any others, was never in so flourishing a state. It has a larger number of students on its books, with a larger number of class entries, than at any former time. Certain departments, that of Natural History for instance, have never before been so successful ; and for the first time in its history the current income derimi from the payments of students only, has, during the past year, covered the current expenditure.,The Editor.,ON THE WOEKING OUT OF GOOD FELLOWSHIP.,Saith the proverb, &ldquo; Well begun is half done.&rdquo; Assuredly we began under very good auspices. The name itself was good, there was some&not; thing brave in it ; and there was in our Council a steady, earnest wish and will that we should be all that name implied ; that we should be something more than an evening school with a big name ; that we should, in truth, be a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;college&lt;/span&gt;, and that for &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt;. Perhaps, too, our earliest members were, as a body, superior men to those who &#39;have joined subsequently. The Principal s name drew towards him many in&not; dependent thinkers, religious and political, many who had heard his voice beloie on matters concerning their welfare ; his working-men friends, friends of his coadjutors, readers of the &lsquo; Christian Socialist? Nor must İ toiget some few readers of a little tabooed periodical of a very different VOL. in. 1 n
class : I need not give the name ; suffice it to say, the publishing-office was not far from the region of St. Paul&rsquo;s, and that its teachings were, on certain vital topics, not the teachings of Mr. Maurice ; yet was he the Churchman of all Churchmen to ivhora these readers gave most respect. An eminently subtle thinker, but honest withal and kind-hearted, he was just the man these thinkers and thirsters woidd like to listen to. These askers of questions to which they found no response, within or without, would hear him ; his politics were their politics. They might learn more. Add to this, they thought he had been persecuted. I confess it was in the pages of this paper that I first read of the proposed &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. The Inaugural Lecture was delivered, an earnest of the teachings we were to expect. You have seen the men who waited to be taught. Not but that, perhaps, the greater portion of them came for the sole purpose of learning one or two subjects as quickly and cheaply as possible, and who cared for nothing beyond. However great this majority may have been, it was not nearly so great as it has been in later dates. We must keep this fact be&not; fore us. But all men are by nature sociable ; they only want to be ridded of a few class prejudices ; they only want leading. There were leaders enough among us, one woidd have thought, honest, careful, thoughtful, semi-self-taught fellows, who well knew the value of friendship for teaching or learning. Even if it had not been so, there ivas a sufficiency in the Council of Teachers. We soon found in their ranks men who not only earnestly wished and willed that a social, friendly feeling should pervade the College ; but who meant also as earnestly to work that it should be so. With them precepts have followed examples. Their classes, though not always teaching the most social of subjects, have all along been the sources from which, directly or indirectly, many of our College friendships have sprung. Their names, from first to last, have ever been associated with all our inner-life movements. Here, then, in teachers and taught, were the elements of social success. We have succeeded, not equal to our expectations. A large circle of warm friends was soon formed, and still exists ; exists almost at its first limits. We have never succeeded in suf&not; ficiently widening the circle ; one sees in most of its reunions the same old faces again and again. Very pleasant, truly; but it is annoying. We cannot draw other men to us,&mdash;into our inner College-life, for so it is. Are we too much engaged with one another, that we are not careful to bring in fresh blood ? Ho we, when we are together, talk too much of clique sub&not; jects, interesting only to ourselves ? Ho we wait for men to come, instead of fetching them ? You cannot force men to fraternize with you ; you cannot demand of a man that he shall not only learn Latin, but that he shall associate with you, and me, and all of us. The room of some men is preferable to their company. True, you cannot force men to fraternize ; you can almost forcibly lead them, were it needed. A man s friendship may be worth nothing to you, but yours may be of service to him. Good is not conservative, nor needs conserving. Evil spreads because it is so&not; ciable. While we regret that our immediate circle does not satisfactorily increase, we should not lose sight of the fact that other circles are formed one after another. Some of us, in our good-natured pride, forget this. It appears to me the social well-being of the College will be best strength&not; ened by starting new circles ; always taking care they shall ultimately
expand into ours, the circle first formed in this little stream of good-fel&not; lowship, the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;,,Ostensibly my first College social meeting, I think it was the first social meeting in the College, was the Grammar Class tea. I say ostensibly, for in truth each night of teaching was to this class a social gathering. Large- hearted Original, has he not ever taught with a kindness catching, with the little more than equality of an elder brother ? How much of the social element in the College do we owe to him ! Great in the gift of winning hearts, gift to be coveted by all teachers of men. The social value of these &ldquo;teas and talks&rdquo; has always to me seemed very great. There was no&not; thing in the viands calculated to awake, strengthen, or develope sociability, nor was the talk, as talk, often worth much ; but the ice of reserve was melted ; men came with the idea that they were expected to be sociable, and did their best to be sociable accordingly. We are all expected to be sociable at all times. Very good : a time for all things. It is not always convenient to be sociable. One is ever late, running into the class-room to catch the commencement ; another always a little behind in his subject, coaching up privately before the teacher begins ; while a third is compelled to leave as soon as the class is over. During the definite class hours each has generally enough to do to keep pace with the teaching. At a class tea there is plenty of time for all to be friendly. Surely it would pay for most classes to set aside a night for &ldquo; tea and talk &rdquo; at the beginning or end of each term. Failing these, we have our Wednesday evening socials,&mdash;to be safe, I will say we used to have ; for I have not been into the coffee- room lately. An &ldquo; old boy &rdquo; (God bless him !) sat at our head; we had tea, etc., ad libitum; the singers sang; the smokers smoked; we played chess or draughts ; met old friends, sometimes made new acquaintances ; talked of old doings, arranged fresh ones. Did all the students know of these nights P Did they read the notices in the hall ? Did the teachers of classes publish their existence ? If they had, I think we should have seen more new faces, heard more new songs, at this the great social class of the College. Many happy nights have I spent in that coffee-room ; the talk may sometimes have been very small, the song very stale ; there was a charm in old faces, ever new; I had no friendships elsewhere. Others, as I, have no friendships elsewhere.,The great disadvantage of these meetings is, that they are always held on the same evening of the week, whereby many men are prevented from coming up. This is partly remedied by not closing so early as other classes ; the remedy, however, does not help those who have to leave at ten o&rsquo;clock. Their great advantage this, that they are the common rneetin&laquo;&middot;- ground for members, old or new, from all our classes. Men can here con&not; tinue friendships or acquaintances begun in classes that have since com&not; pleted their terms, and but for these Wednesday evenings they might lose sight of each other, unless, perchance, they happened to enter together upon some fresh study. There is a certain wholeness, a certain unity, in these &ldquo; teas and talks &rdquo; that I feel much, but cannot well describe. There is J. R&mdash;, who sat next to me the first night of the Grammar Class, more than seven years ago, and next him L. S&mdash;, one of three of us, left to wait for an extra night to clear up the binomial in the winter of &rsquo;56 ; both are chaffing the worthy teacher of the before-mentioned Grammar Class, his,о 2
long beard bathing in his fourteenth cup of weak tea, very sweet, while he halt leans across the table, accusing J. F &mdash; of stealing fossils at our last geological excursion, quite forgetful, simple man ! that his share of the said fossils are in the museum under his feet, &ldquo;Bah!&rdquo; says S&mdash;, &ldquo;rescued from ignorant destruction for the benefit of science.&rdquo; &ldquo; Thieves all,&rdquo; says J. F &mdash;, the tallest, who will presently sing &ldquo; Come, cheer up, my lads,&rdquo; with great energy, to be followed by other singers with lungs not quite so stentorian, all of whose songs will be applauded good-naturedly, with a tinge of pity, by the singing-class teacher ; the notes, indeed, are not always quite those ivritten by the original composers, their ideas of time might not always coincide. On my left is a middle-aged student, explaining Mill on liberty to two very juvenile thinkers ; opposite, G. T&mdash;, gentlest but heartiest of fellows, talking to D &mdash;, who made my acquaintance last week by knocking me down on scientific principles, leather and horse-hair intervening be&not; tween his hand and my head. Little R&mdash; is bawling lustily for more coffee, when the door opens, and in comes his employer, and takes his seat beside the little man, who is no less noisy for that. Do you get a glimpse of &iacute;vhat I mean by the oneness, the unity of these meetings ? the College at home ? Yes, says the newly-joined student, quite enough to deter me from venturing in alone ; it would be too jolly for me, 1 should feel like a stianger entering a snug evening party. This is a point old students should ever consider. Don&rsquo;t be afraid on any such score, my one-term friend, the patriarchs of the College look upon each student as a member of the family.,I hope I have shown the difference between these two social media, their relative positions and utility. The class tea gives each student an op&not; portunity of fraternizing ivith his fellow class-man. There is no excuse for not attending it, since it takes place upon a class night, in class hours. By means of the Wednesday evenings he can keep up the friendships he may make in any classes he may join ; it is the inner circle fiowiug into the outer one.,I hope it is seen that they are not extraneous, but legitimate, intrinsic, College work. Surely fellowship is one great end of education. For what does a man learn, but that he may reach the level of better men than him&not; self; that he maybe worthy of their society? Not that he should lift himself above his class, out of his class, but that he should raise himself with his class. It is seen by us that not alone money hinders us from fellowship with the more fortunate, it is also a diversity of taste, habits, and education. Education in itself improves the tastes and habits ; how much quicker, more thoroughly, and pleasantly is the change accom&not; plished when the education takes place in the intimacy of men already re&not; fined ! Herein lies the gist of the matter.,My brother Cloth, who has been brought up in a first-rate school, in a home of plenty, who has passed the last few years of his life in counting- houses and drawing-rooms, and who comes to our College to mb up some subject, will see why he should take his seat and learn with a carpenter, or his labourer, if needs be, sinking any superiority except brains&mdash;should that be on his side&mdash;taking his place as an equal, not as a patron, ivith the least shoiv of airs whatever. He will perceive why he should have &ldquo; tea and talk &rdquo; with them&mdash;why they should spend some little of their leisure time together in walking out into the fields and talking by the way. Think
what we are, or ought to be ; a body of men met together for general good&mdash;to use longer words&mdash;a community associated for education in fel&not; lowship. We shall most honestly and readily learn the value of these words by taking up some branch of study that to be best worked must be worked mutually ; all subjects can be worked mutually. Yes. Doubt&not; less subjects are best worked so, after, and in connection with, a class having a good teacher; but I now want specially to enlist you to take up some subject where you cannot well give up all your attention to your text-book and your tutor. I want you to begin some study where you will be led to look to your neighbour on your right hand and your left for ma&not; terial, in which you will be led out together to find material for study with your own hands. Did we learn nothing but geology in those long walks of ours over the chalky downs ? Did we in our work in those still, quiet pits verify nothing but a few scientific facts ? Did we knock out nought but fossils ? Oh, I would that my life among you had been somewhat more staid, that I might add my name to this paper, and weight by so doing. Still the lightest side is most often uppermost&mdash;I am serious enough at this moment. If you would fully know the pleasures of mental education, take up some branch of physical science, say geology or botany, and work at it as I have indicated. The most social, hard-working sets the College has ever had, have been the early mathematical men and the geo&not; logists ; the former especially, I can count up some five or six teachers and some twenty to thirty students to whom nineteen-twentieths of the honest social good-feeling in the College is due ; and most prominent among these students are members of the earlier mathematical classes. Explain it how you will. That they carried their work into each other&rsquo;s homes is certain. We have yet another physical science class that has been productive of much good in the College, by which many of its mem&not; bers have become intimately acquainted. I refer to the Boxing Class. This and the Cricket Club are our chief means for amusement and mus&not; cular education. I ought to have written &ldquo; had&rdquo; for they have both been swamped in the Eifle Corps. However, I do not grumble at this, there are other circles wider by far than those of our College. It would be a poor kind of fellowship which slackened the ties which bind us together as citizens of England. Eicata.,THE LONDON COLLEGE E1ELE COEPS.,Our Eifle Corps has just entered upon the third year of its existence. A little body o&iacute; men, in November, 1859, commenced drilling in the College garden. They, while other corps were seeking out well lighted, warmed halls for drill, Avere obliged to learn their manual and platoon in the open air, on the cold, damp nights of that winter; making the oft-repeated injunction of &ldquo;Do it smartly&rdquo; seem a mockery, when their fingers were benumbed with cold. This*body, mainly old teachers and students, soon attracted others, and the 40 or 50 sw elled into a battalion, threatening to swamp the true work&#39;of the College. We think, however, that such fears have not been realized. The College has resumed its old
appearance. By the kindness of the authorities of University College, the corps has been allowed to drill in their playground, which is much better adapted for that purpose than our garden; and by the removal of the head-quarters to Queen Square, the connection of the corps with the College is more moral than physical. The battalion is now about 750 strong, of which about 180 are members of the College. They form three companies, and it will not be necessary to tell any one acquainted with the College that the names of the Captains (Martineau, Eoebuck, and Furnivall) and of the Lieutenants (Win. Sutton, Tansley, and Pattison) are those of our oldest teachers ; and that the Captain of the first-formed company, now Major Hughes, was for a long time almost a director of the social life of the College. The Ensigns (Barrett, Williams, and Eowlstone), the Quarter-Master, Lieutenant Hooper, and last, though by no means least, our excellent Adjutant, Philip Bead, to whom we owe what military knowledge we have, are or have been all students of the College.,During the past year our corps has steadily drilled, has done a fair amount of shooting, considering the distance of the practice*-ground, at Plumstead, and the uncertainty of accommodation there. Before another year w r e really must have a butt of our own. Tn these contests, the second company (Captain Eoebuck&rsquo;s) has produced the winner (Lieutenant Hooper) of the Corps&rsquo; challenge-cup, and has likewise won the company&rsquo;s challenge-prize against the other companies of the battalion. Numerous contests for company and other prizes have taken place, and although pewter has been the prevailing material of our cups, the winners will prize them no less on that score. Perhaps their value to the winners was in&not; creased by receiving them from the hands of an early friend of the College and Corps, Earl de Grey and Eipon, in Westminster Hall. Not the least significant sight that grand old hall has seen, was the spectacle of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Corps drawn up to receive prizes for skill in shooting. During the summer our corps has taken part in almost all the great gatherings of Volunteers near London, beside several field-days on their own account. They have shown that in excellence at drill they hold a fair rank among Metropolitan battalions. Colonel M&lsquo;Murdo compli&not; mented them in high terms at the official inspection.,So much for the past. Some prophesy that this Volunteer movement will gradually die out. If so, we say it will be to the disgrace of England. If so, it had better never have been begun. We do not fear that our corps will languish. It need not, if the members will look upon the work as a duty, and not as an opportunity of wearing fine uniforms. With some little difficulty we have escaped a good deal of foppery ; we have not been able, however, to dispense with a band. Let us aim to be the best-drilled corps in London ; and remember, that this is not to be attained by attendance ionly on grand parades and marching out, but by hours and half-hours snatched from our evenings for squad and company drill, without the trouble of putting on uniforms, and taken &ldquo; constitutionally,&rdquo; as we should half an hour&rsquo;s boxing, or other physical exercise. We then shall be ready to turn out occasionally to grand reviews with a certainty of knowing our work, and ready to turn out for service should we be required. How then would the man feel who only knew how to form &ldquo;fours right&rdquo; having at-
tended only when his corps marched out ? In conclusion, one word for the companies of the 19th who do not belong to the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. You are &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt;, like ourselves, and have accidentally become associated with our College in an indirect way. Is there nothing in the teaching or social life of that College that is attractive to you ? The College companies would not desire to recruit their strength by inducing you to desert your own company, but they would gladly welcome you as students of the College. If you live far away, and feel that you know each other so well as members of the Corps that you would like to learn other things besides drill toge&not; ther, they would try to assist you in forming classes,&mdash;might perhaps be able to supply teachers ; as members of the Corps you can all do something towards aiding the extension and formation of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, not the least good work of this time, we can assure you.,So the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Eifle Corps enters upon its third year, and looks forward hopefully to the future. Like everybody else, they want more money to spend, and would cheerfully receive it from those who are unable to give personal service. Our work as Volunteers is not yet done, we cannot yet lay down our rifles. Europe, spite of financial difficulties, is still under arms. Police duty becomes irksome at last to the soldier, and he will cry out for war. Woe to the unprepared State, be it England, or Switzerland, or Belgium. The more free its institutions, the better fitted for despot&rsquo;s prey. j g,NEWS.,Cambridge .&mdash;The prize given by students of the Cambridge &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; for the best Essay on &ldquo; Newspaper Literature, considered in its effects on the different classes of readers of the present day,&rdquo; has been awarded by the Council of Teachers to Mr. H. J. Ansell.,London &mdash; Au accidental delay in the issue of this number enables us to chronicle a pleasant festive gathering, with which a large College party have inaugurated their New Year. Mrs. Robinson, of Birdhurst, near Croydon, invited the whole of the Sinsin&raquo;&middot; Class and a number of old students and their friends, to spend the evening of the 1st of January at her house. After the heartiest of welcomes from the hostess and all her family, including Mrs Frank and Mrs. William Malleson (both of whom the College has to thank for similar hospitalities), came an entertainment which, under the modest name of tea was in truth a Christmas banquet in the unmistakeable old English manner suloin and turkey &ldquo;all proper.&rdquo; Songs, choruses, domestic comedy in the shape of cha&not; rades, moving inextinguishable laughter, and relieved by a sober bit of Shakspeare reading made the succeeding hours pass but too quickly ; till the thought of the inexorable rail-&rsquo; way bell hurried the party away, scarcely giving them time to thank, as they would have wished, their warm-hearted entertainers for hospitality so generous. The walk through Croydon town became a quick march under the inspiration of a chorus from Mozart and evei y roadside station was made to ring with the echoes of some lively madrigal.
